### I

# EPISTLE TO DON FREDERICK
# OF ARAGON

<term>Epistle</term> <note>original: "Epistola." In this context, it refers to a formal literary letter that serves as a preface to a collection of works.</note>, <term>Frederick of Aragon</term> <note>Federico d’Aragona (1452–1504) was the son of King Ferdinand I of Naples. Lorenzo de' Medici famously sent him a collection of Tuscan poetry (the "Raccolta Aragonese") to demonstrate the richness and dignity of the Italian vernacular language.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the formal opening for the Epistle to Don Frederick of Aragon, which is the famous introductory letter to a 15th-century anthology of Italian poetry.</summary>
<keywords>Frederick of Aragon, Epistle, Lorenzo de' Medici, Raccolta Aragonese, Italian poetry, Renaissance correspondence</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This is the opening dedication of a collection of poetry, likely the "Raccolta Aragonese" (Aragonese Collection). The author is addressing Frederick of Aragon, the son of King Ferdinand I of Naples.</meta>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS LORD FREDERICK OF ARAGON
## SON OF THE KING OF NAPLES

Thinking many times within myself, my most illustrious Lord Frederick, which among the many and infinite praises of ancient times was the most excellent, I have judged one in particular to be the most glorious and almost unique above all others: that no illustrious or virtuous work, whether of the hand or of the mind, can be imagined for which there were not, in that early age, great rewards and noble honors prepared in both public and private life. For just as all rivers and springs are said to take their beginning from the Great Ocean, so it is understood that all the famous deeds and marvelous works of ancient men derived from this one excellent custom.

Honor is truly that which provides nourishment to every art; nor are the minds of mortals inflamed toward noble works by anything so much as by glory. To this end, therefore, the magnificent <term>triumphs<note>A "triumph" in ancient Rome was a spectacular civil ceremony and religious rite held to publicly celebrate and sanctify the success of a military commander.</note></term> in Rome, the famous games of Mount Olympus in Greece, and the poetic and oratory <term>certame<note>A formal competition or contest of skill, from the Latin 'certamen'.</note></term> among both peoples were celebrated with such great zeal. For this alone the chariot and triumphal arch, the marble trophies, the most ornate theaters, the statues, the palms, the crowns, the funeral orations—for this alone infinite other most wonderful ornaments were established; nor, indeed, did the graceful and high deeds performed with wisdom and [courage...]

<summary>This page serves as a formal dedication to Frederick of Aragon, emphasizing the Renaissance humanist belief that public honor and rewards are the essential catalysts for great achievements in art, intellect, and war.</summary>
<keywords>Frederick of Aragon, Naples, Humanism, Ancient Rome, Greece, Honor, Glory, Triumphs, Olympic Games, Patronage</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory letter (the "Epistola") accompanying the Aragonese Collection, a landmark anthology of Tuscan poetry sent by Lorenzo de' Medici to Frederick of Aragon. The previous page established that glory and honor are the primary motivators for great deeds; this page illustrates that principle through historical examples of leaders who patronized the arts.</meta>

# I - EPISTOLA TO FREDERICK OF ARAGON

and with the sword, and so many wonderful excellences of the valiant ancients, who without any doubt, as our Tuscan poet <note>The "Tuscan poet" refers to Petrarch (1304–1374), the father of humanism.</note> well says, will never be without fame,

->unless the universe first dissolves.<-
<note>original: "se l'universo pria non si dissolve" — a line from Petrarch's *Triumph of Fame*.</note>

These were wonderful and truly divine men who, as they were highly desiring of true and immortal praise, were also kindled with a fiery love toward those who could render the valiant and illustrious deeds of excellent men immortal through the virtue of the poetic style. Inflamed by this same most glorious desire, Alexander the Great, when he arrived at the <term>Sigeum<note>A promontory in the Troad (modern Turkey), traditionally identified as the site of the tomb of the Greek hero Achilles.</note></term> at the most noble sepulcher of the famous Achilles, let out a sigh and uttered that ever-memorable royal voice, truly worthy of himself:

->Oh fortunate one, that you found so clear a trumpet,<-
->and one who wrote of you so loftily.<-
<note>original: "Oh fortunato che sí chiara tromba / trovasti, e chi di te sí alto scrisse." These lines are from Petrarch’s Sonnet 187, paraphrasing Alexander the Great's famous address to Achilles regarding the poet Homer.</note>

And he was, without doubt, fortunate: because if the divine poet Homer had not existed, one and the same tomb would have covered both the body and the fame of Achilles. Nor would this poet, the most excellent above all others, have risen to such honor and fame if he had not been raised up from the earth by a most illustrious Athenian—indeed, almost restored from death to such a long life. For, the sacred work of this most celebrated poet being scattered and almost dismembered in many and various places of Greece after his death, Pisistratus, the Athenian prince <note>Pisistratus (6th century BCE) was a ruler of Athens credited by ancient tradition with commissioning the first definitive collection of Homer's epics, the *Iliad* and the *Odyssey*.</note>—a man most outstanding in many virtues of both mind and body—offered most ample rewards to anyone who brought him any of the Homeric verses. With the greatest diligence and examination, he gathered together the entire body of the most holy poet; and just as he gave that poet perpetual life, so he acquired for himself immortal glory and most clear splendor. Because of this, no other title was engraved beneath his statue except for this one: that he had been the author of bringing the glorious Homeric poem back together. Oh, truly divine men, born into the world for the benefit of mankind!

This distinguished prince knew that his other virtuous deeds, however many and wonderful they might be, were nonetheless all [subordinate] to this one...

<summary>This page uses historical examples like Alexander the Great and the Athenian leader Pisistratus to argue that the greatness of heroes and states depends entirely on the poets who record their deeds. It emphasizes the humanist belief that preserving literature is an act of supreme civic virtue.</summary>

<keywords>Petrarch, Alexander the Great, Achilles, Homer, Pisistratus, Sigeum, immortality of fame, patronage, humanism, Aragonese Collection</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Epistola" or dedicatory letter from Lorenzo de' Medici to Frederick of Aragon. Having previously discussed how Alexander the Great honored Achilles through the poetry of Homer, Lorenzo now transitions to the decline of virtue in recent centuries and his project to revive the glory of the Tuscan language through this collection.</meta>

# I - EPISTOLA TO FREDERICK OF ARAGON

praise, by which he might give birth to eternal life and glory for both himself and others. Such, then, were those first men, whose virtuous deeds are not only inimitable in our own centuries, but are hardly believable. Because the rewards for virtuous deeds have now completely vanished, every benign light of virtue has been extinguished along with them; and, as men no longer do anything praiseworthy, they have also completely despised these "sacred praisers" <note>The "sacred praisers" refers to poets and historians who immortalize great deeds.</note>. If this had not been the case in the centuries immediately preceding our own, the painful loss of so many and such wonderful Greek and Latin writers would not have occurred, which has resulted in our own very great loss. Similarly, in this tempestuous shipwreck <note>The "shipwreck" is a humanist metaphor for the Middle Ages or "Dark Ages," during which much of classical literature was lost.</note>, there were many venerable poets who first began to cultivate the desert field of the **Tuscany language** in such a way that, in these centuries of ours, it is now entirely reclothed in little flowers and grass.

But your kind hand, most illustrious Frederick, which you deigned to offer to these poets after their many and long labors, has finally brought them into port. For when we were in the ancient city of **Pisa** last year, and began to discuss those who had written poetically in the Tuscan language, Your Lordship did not keep your praiseworthy desire hidden from me: namely, that through my effort all these writers might be gathered together for you in a single volume. Therefore, being as desirous in this as in all other things to satisfy your most honorable will, and having found the ancient manuscripts <note>original: "antichi esemplari"</note>—not without very great effort—and choosing from them those things that were less crude, I have collected them all in this present volume. I send this to Your Lordship, greatly desiring that you welcome my work, whatever it may be, and receive it as a reminder and a pledge of my singular love toward you.

Nor should anyone despise this Tuscan language as being poorly adorned or lacking in variety. For if its riches and ornaments are estimated correctly and justly, this language will be reputed not poor, nor crude, but abundant and most polished. No thing noble, flowery, graceful, or adorned; none sharp, distinct, ingenious, or subtle; none high, magnificent, or sonorous; none, finally, burning, spirited, or excited can be imagined...

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici describes how he compiled a collection of Tuscan poetry (the Aragonese Collection) at the request of Frederick of Aragon, arguing that the Tuscan language is as refined and capable as the classical languages.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Frederick of Aragon, Tuscan language, Pisa, poetry collection, literary patronage, recovery of manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Epistola" or dedicatory letter to Frederick of Aragon. On the previous page, Lorenzo de' Medici recounted a meeting in Pisa where Frederick expressed a desire to see a collection of Tuscan poetry. This page transitions from the general history of the language to specific portraits of the early masters, from the origins of rhyme to the "sweet new style."</meta>

# I - EPISTOLA TO FREDERICK OF ARAGON

in which [this language] not only in those first two, Dante and Petrarch, but also in these others whom you, my Lord, have revived, infinite and most illustrious examples shine forth.

The use of rhyme, according to what Petrarch writes in a Latin letter <note>Petrarch discusses the origins of rhyme in his *Epistolae familiares* (Letters on Familiar Matters), specifically in Book XI, letter 4.</note>, was once highly celebrated among the ancient Romans; having then been interrupted for a long time, it began to flourish again in **Sicily** not many centuries ago <note>This refers to the "Sicilian School" of poets at the court of Frederick II in the 13th century.</note>, and, having spread from there through France, it finally arrived in Italy as if at its own home.

The first, therefore, among our own people to set his hand to portraying the lovely image of this <term>new style</term> <note>Known in Italian as the "Dolce Stil Novo" (Sweet New Style), a refined, philosophical movement in 13th-century lyric poetry.</note> was **Guittone of Arezzo**, and in that same age the famous Bolognese **Guido Guinizelli**; both were highly adorned in philosophy, grave and full of wisdom. Yet the first was somewhat rough and severe, and not lit by any light of eloquence; the other was so much more lucid, sweet, and ornate than he, that our honored Dante does not hesitate to call him his father, and the father of those others who:

->better than I,<-
->who ever used sweet and graceful rhymes of love.<-
<note>original: "miglior, che m' / rime d'amore usâr dolci e leggiadre." These lines are from Dante’s *Purgatorio*, Canto XXVI, where Dante pays homage to Guinizelli.</note>

This man [Guinizelli] was certainly the first by whom the beautiful form of our language was sweetly colored, which before him had been only dimly sketched by that rough Arezzo native <note>Referring to Guittone d'Arezzo.</note>. Shifting behind these men shines the delicate **Guido Cavalcanti**, a Florentine, a most subtle <term>dialectician</term> <note>A practitioner of logic and philosophical debate.</note> and the most excellent philosopher of his century. This man, just as he was handsome and graceful in body and of the most noble blood, so in his writings he resembles something even more beautiful, noble, and refined than the others; he was most sharp in his inventions, magnificent, admirable, most grave in his wisdom, copious and elevated in his order, composed, wise, and prudent. All these blessed virtues of his are adorned with a charming, sweet, and rare style, as if with a precious garment. Had he exercised his talents in a wider field, he would have undoubtedly occupied the highest honors; but above all his other works, one <term>canzona</term> <note>A complex song-form or poem; here referring to Cavalcanti's famous and difficult philosophical poem "Donna me prega" (A Lady Asks Me).</note> is most marvelous, in which this graceful poet subtly described every quality, virtue, and attribute of love; for which reason, in his own age, it was judged to be of such great value that

<summary>Lorenzo traces the evolution of Italian poetry from its Sicilian origins to the "Sweet New Style," praising the refinement of Guido Guinizelli and the philosophical depth of Guido Cavalcanti.</summary>
<keywords>Guittone d'Arezzo, Guido Guinizelli, Guido Cavalcanti, Dolce Stil Novo, origins of rhyme, Sicilian School, Dante Alighieri, lyric poetry</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Epistola" or dedicatory letter from Lorenzo de' Medici to Frederick of Aragon. Following the discussion of the "Sweet New Style" and Guido Guinizelli on the previous page, Lorenzo now evaluates other early Italian poets and explains the purpose of the collection he has assembled.</meta>

# I - EPISTOLA TO FREDERICK OF ARAGON

[Guinizelli] was most learnedly commented upon by three of his contemporaries, most excellent philosophers, among whom was the Roman Egidio <note>Aegidius Romanus or Giles of Rome (c. 1243–1316), a famous theologian and philosopher of the Augustinian order.</note>. Nor should Bonagiunta of Lucca and the Notary of Lentino <note>Giacomo da Lentino, credited with inventing the sonnet.</note> be passed over in silence: both were serious and full of maxims, yet so stripped of every flower of grace that they should be content if we receive them among this beautiful company of such honored men. Both these men and Piero delle Vigne <note>The powerful chancellor to Emperor Frederick II, known for his rhetorical skill.</note> were celebrated in the age of Guittone, who himself also composed several works—albeit small ones—not without gravity and learning. This [Piero] is he who, as Dante says:

> ->held both the keys<-
> ->of Frederick’s heart, and who turned them,<-
> ->locking and unlocking so softly.<-
<note>Dante, *Inferno* XIII, 58–60. While the text mentions Guittone just before, these famous lines refer to Piero delle Vigne’s influence over Emperor Frederick II.</note>

After these men shine those two wonderful suns who have illuminated this language: Dante, and not far behind him Francesco Petrarch; regarding their praises, just as Sallust says of Carthage <note>The Roman historian Sallust wrote in *The War with Jugurtha* that it is better to be silent than to say too little about such a great city.</note>, I judge it better to be silent than to say too little.

Onesto of Bologna <note>Onesto da Bologna, a 13th-century poet who corresponded with Cino da Pistoia.</note> and the Sicilians—who were indeed the first poets—just as they are older than these two [Dante and Petrarch], so they would have more need of their polishing file, though it is clear that neither talent nor will was lacking in any of them. Cino da Pistoia <note>A famous jurist and poet (c. 1270–1336) who was a close friend of Dante and a precursor to Petrarch.</note> responds quite well to his reputation, being entirely delicate and truly amorous; he was the first, in my opinion, who began to completely shun the ancient roughness, from which even the divine Dante, otherwise most wonderful, could not protect himself on every side. Following them is a longer flock of newer writers, all of whom have moved away from that beautiful pair by a great distance.

All of these, my Lord, and with them some of our own age, come to give you immortal thanks, for you have been the author of their life, their immortal light, and their form, worthy of much greater glory than that ancient Athenian previously mentioned <note>Referring to Pisistratus, the ruler of Athens who was credited with first collecting the poems of Homer into a single work.</note>.

For while he restored life to one poet, though a sovereign one, you have restored it to all of these. We have also added at the end of the book (as it seemed it might please you) some of our own sonnets and songs, so that, by reading them, there may be renewed in [your memory...]

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici evaluates the merits of early Italian poets, contrasting the "roughness" of the pioneers with the "illumination" of Dante and Petrarch, and concludes by presenting the collection to Frederick.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Frederick of Aragon, Dante Alighieri, Petrarch, Cino da Pistoia, Piero delle Vigne, Sicilian School, Dolce Stil Novo, Raccolta Aragonese, literary history</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedicatory letter from Lorenzo de’ Medici to Frederick of Aragon. After reviewing the history of Italian poetry, Lorenzo humbly presents the collection—which included his own youthful verses—as a gift to the prince, emphasizing their personal bond of friendship.</meta>

# I - EPISTOLA TO FREDERICK OF ARAGON

...your mind my devotion and singular love toward Your Lordship <note>Italian: *Signoria*. A formal title of respect for a high-ranking noble.</note>; which, if they are not worthy to be numbered among such marvelous writings of ancient poets, at least to provide a contrast to the others and make them appear even more elegant by comparison, it will perhaps not have been useless to have joined them together <note>Lorenzo is referring to his own poems, which he included at the end of this collection (the *Aragonese Anthology*) alongside the great masters like Dante and Petrarch.</note>.

May Your most illustrious Lordship, therefore, receive both these works and myself, not only into your home, but into your heart and mind, just as Your Lordship continually and joyfully dwells within our own heart and soul.

*Farewell.* <note>original: "*Vale.*" A traditional Latin closing for a letter.</note>

<summary>Lorenzo de’ Medici concludes his dedication by humbly offering his own poems alongside the masters, framing the collection as a testament to his deep personal friendship with Frederick of Aragon.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de’ Medici, Frederick of Aragon, Aragonese Anthology, dedicatory letter, Italian poetry, Renaissance patronage</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page serves as the title page for the second section of the volume. Following the dedicatory letter to Frederick of Aragon, the text now transitions to Lorenzo's self-authored prose commentary on his own poetic works.</meta>

### II

# COMMENTARY

## BY THE MAGNIFICENT <term>Magnifico: While often translated as "Magnificent," this was a formal title of respect (Magnifico) used for high-ranking citizens in the Florentine Republic who did not hold noble titles. It has since become inextricably linked to Lorenzo’s legacy as a statesman and patron.</term> LORENZO DE' MEDICI

### ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS <note>Lorenzo’s *Comento* (Commentary) is a rare example of an author writing a philosophical and narrative explanation of his own poetry. By doing so, he followed the tradition of Dante Alighieri’s *Vita Nuova*, seeking to prove that the Italian vernacular was just as capable of expressing complex ideas as Latin.</note>

<summary>This title page introduces Lorenzo de’ Medici’s formal prose commentary on his own sonnets, a work where he analyzes his poetry through the lenses of philosophy and personal experience.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Commentary, Sonnets, Italian vernacular, Renaissance literature, Florence, autobiography</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal preface to the *Comento*. Following the title page, Lorenzo transitions into a first-person justification for his work, addressing the potential criticisms of writing about his own poetry and the nature of love.</meta>

I have been very doubtful and hesitant about whether I should undertake the present interpretation and commentary on my own sonnets; and, even when I was occasionally inclined to do so, the reasons listed below occurred to me to the contrary and discouraged me from this work. 

First was the presumption I felt I might incur by commenting on my own writings—both because of the excessive self-esteem I would seem to show, and because I seemed to be taking upon myself a judgment that ought to belong to others. In doing so, I would be marking the intellects of those into whose hands my verses will fall as being insufficient to understand them. <note>Lorenzo is employing a classic "modesty trope." By suggesting he is worried about appearing arrogant, he actually highlights his status as an author-authority, modeled after Dante Alighieri’s own self-commentaries.</note>

Beyond this, I thought I could easily be criticized by some for having poor judgment, having spent my time composing and commenting on verses whose subject matter and theme were largely centered on a **passionate love**. This seems even more reprehensible in my case due to my constant public and private duties, <note>Lorenzo refers here to his role as the "de facto" leader of the Florentine Republic, where his time was occupied by intense diplomacy and statecraft.</note> which should have pulled me away from such thoughts. According to some, these matters are not only frivolous and of little importance, but even harmful and somewhat damaging both to our souls and to our worldly honor. 

And if this is so, then thinking about such things is a great error, and putting them into verse is an even greater one; but commenting on them seems no less a fault than that of one who has formed a long and hardened habit of wicked deeds. This is especially true because commentaries are usually reserved for matters of **theology** or **philosophy**, <term>Philosophy: Specifically Neoplatonism, which was the dominant intellectual framework in Lorenzo’s Florence, often used to explain how human love could lead to a divine understanding.</term> which lead to great results—either for the edification and consolation of our minds or for the benefit of the human race. To this is also added that it will perhaps seem reprehensible to some, even if the subject matter...

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici defends his decision to write a commentary on his own love poetry, addressing concerns that such a task is vain or inappropriate for a man with his significant public responsibilities.</summary>
<keywords>literary theory, modesty trope, love poetry, civic duty, Neoplatonism, Florentine politics, self-commentary</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface of the *Comento*. Lorenzo is enumerating the "three difficulties" that initially discouraged him from writing this commentary. He has already discussed the perceived arrogance of commenting on one's own work and the perceived frivolity of love poetry. He now addresses the third difficulty: the choice of language.</meta>

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

and [even if] the subject matter were worthy enough in itself, having written and made mention of it in our mother tongue and the <term>vernacular</term> <note>original: "lingua nostra materna e volgare"; at this time, Italian was often viewed as inferior to Latin for serious intellectual work.</note>, which—in places where it is spoken and understood—does not seem to escape a certain baseness for being so common; and in those places where there is no knowledge of it, it cannot be understood. Thus, in this regard, this work and our labor seem entirely in vain, as if it had never been done.

These three difficulties have until now delayed what I had proposed a long time ago: namely, the present interpretation. At present, I have nevertheless resolved—conquered, in my opinion, by better reasons—to put it into effect, thinking that if this meager effort of mine is of some value and pleasing to someone, it will be well placed and not entirely in vain. And even if it should find little grace, it will be little read and disparaged by few; and, not being very durable, the reprimand I might incur will likewise be short-lived.

And, responding for now to the first reason and to those who might wish to label me as presumptuous in any way, I say that to me it does not seem like presumption to interpret my own things, but rather taking the burden off others. Indeed, no office is more appropriate for interpreting than that of the person who has written the work, because no one can better know or choose the truth of his own meaning. This is clearly shown by the confusion that arises from the variety of <term>commentaries</term> <note>original: "comenti"; Lorenzo is likely thinking of the endless and often contradictory medieval commentaries on Dante or classical authors.</note>, in which, more often than not, the commentators follow their own nature rather than the true intention of the one who wrote. Nor does it seem to me by this argument that I hold too high an account of myself or take away the judgment of others. For I believe it is the true duty of every man to perform all things for the benefit of humanity, whether for his own sake or that of others. 

And, because not everyone is born capable of performing those things which are held in the highest regard in the world, one must measure oneself and see in which ministry one can best serve the human race and exercise oneself in that. This is because a single thing cannot satisfy both the diversity of human <term>intellects</term> <note>original: "ingegni umani"; the specific creative and intellectual capacities of an individual.</note> and the necessities of our life, even if it were the first and most excellent work men could do. On the contrary, it seems that <term>contemplation</term>, which without controversy is the first and most excellent . . . .

<summary>Lorenzo defends his use of the Italian vernacular and argues that an author is the most qualified person to explain their own work's intent. He concludes that all individuals should contribute to society according to their unique talents, even if their work is not considered the "highest" form of activity.</summary>

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

. . . . <note>original: "Lacuna nel testo"; there is a gap in the original manuscript here.</note>; and for this reason, one concludes that not only many works of <term>ingegno</term> <note>In the Renaissance, *ingegno* referred to a combination of innate talent, wit, and intellectual capacity.</note>, but also many humble services necessarily contribute to the perfection of human life. It is the true duty of all men—in whatever station they find themselves placed by heaven, nature, or fortune—to serve the human generation. I would have greatly desired to be able to apply myself to greater matters; yet I do not wish, for this reason, to fail in what my talent and strength permit for the sake of someone—if not many—who perhaps, more to please me than because my works satisfy them, have encouraged me in this task; their authority and favor carry great weight with me. And if I cannot provide any other benefit to those who read my verses, at least they will take some small pleasure from them, because perhaps they will find some talent proportionate and in harmony with their own. And if someone should even laugh at them, I will be pleased that they draw this pleasure—small as it may be—from my verses. It seems to me that, by publishing this interpretation, I am submitting myself more to the judgment of others; for if I had judged these verses of mine unworthy of being read by myself alone, I would have avoided the judgment of others; but by commenting on them and publishing them, I avoid, in my view, the presumption of judging myself.

Now, to respond to the slanders of those who might wish to accuse me of having spent time both in composing and commenting on things unworthy of effort or time—being <term>passions of love</term> <note>Love poetry was often dismissed by critics as "frivolous" compared to political or theological writing.</note>, etc., and especially among my many necessary occupations—I say that I would truly be justly condemned if human nature were endowed with such excellence that all men could always perform all things perfectly. But because this degree of perfection has been granted to very few, and even to those few only very rarely in their lives, it seems to me one can conclude—considering human imperfection—that those things in the world containing the least evil are the best.

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici argues that all human activities, from menial tasks to intellectual pursuits, serve humanity; he justifies his love poetry as a "lesser evil" in an imperfect human life.</summary>

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

And, judging rather according to common nature and the universal custom of men—though I would not dare to state it as a certainty—I believe that love between people is not only beyond reproach, but almost necessary and a very true proof of <term>nobility</term> <note>original: "gentilezza"; in the Renaissance context, this refers to a nobility of spirit and character rather than just social rank.</note> and greatness of soul. Above all, it serves as a motive to invite men toward worthy and excellent things, and to exercise and bring into action those virtues that exist as potentials within our souls. For whoever diligently seeks the true definition of love finds it to be nothing other than a <term>desire for beauty</term> <note>original: "appetito di bellezza." This is a core concept of Renaissance Neoplatonism, suggesting that the soul is drawn to beauty as a reflection of the divine.</note>. And if this is so, all things deformed and ugly must necessarily be displeasing to one who loves.

And setting aside for the moment that love which, according to Plato, is the means for all things to find their perfection and ultimately rest in the supreme Beauty—that is, God—and speaking of that love which extends only to loving the human creature: I say that even if this is not that perfection of love called the "supreme good," we clearly see it contains within itself so many benefits and avoids so many evils that, according to the common customs of human life, it takes the place of a good. This is especially true if it is adorned with those circumstances and conditions that suit a true love, which it seems to me are two: first, that one loves only a single thing, and second, that this thing is loved always. These two conditions can hardly occur if the beloved subject does not possess within themselves, in proportion to other human things, a supreme perfection; and if, beyond natural beauty, there does not converge in the beloved a great <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "ingegno"</note>, refined and honorable manners and customs, elegant style and gestures, a dexterity of shrewd and sweet words, love, constancy, and faith. All these things are necessarily required for the perfection of love, because although the beginning of love is born from the eyes and from beauty, nonetheless, for its preservation and perseverance, those other conditions are needed. Thus, if by sickness or age or some other cause the face should lose its color and beauty should fade in whole or in part, all those other conditions remain no less pleasing to the spirit and heart than beauty was to the eyes. Nor would such conditions yet be sufficient, if still in

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici defends human love as a catalyst for virtue and excellence, defining it as a desire for beauty that requires both physical grace and intellectual or moral perfection to endure beyond youth.</summary>

And therefore, whoever proposes a true love necessarily proposes great perfection, according to the common custom of men, both in the beloved and in the lover; and, as happens with all other perfect things, I believe that such love has been very rare in the world: which all the more proves its excellence. He who loves one thing alone and always, of necessity places no love in other things, and thus deprives himself of all those errors and <term>voluptuousness</term> <note>original: "voluttá"; here referring to fleeting sensual pleasures or distractions that lead the soul away from virtue.</note> into which men commonly fall; and, loving a person capable of recognition and seeking in every way he can to please her, he must of necessity seek to make himself worthy and excellent among others in all his works, following virtuous deeds to make himself as worthy as possible of her whom he esteems most worthy above all others. It seems to him that, both in public and in private, just as the <term>form</term> <note>original: "forma"; in the Neoplatonic sense, this is the idealized spiritual image of the beloved held in the lover's mind.</note> of the beloved is always present in the heart, so she is present in all his works, which she may praise or reproach according to their fitness, acting as a true witness and presiding judge not only of the deed, but of the thoughts. And so, partly by repressing evil through shame, and partly by stimulating good through the desire to please her, such people—if they do not act perfectly—at least do what the world considers the lesser evil, which, in respect to human imperfection, is chosen by the world as a good.

This, then, has been the subject of my verses; and if, even with all these reasons, I do not answer the objections and slanders of those who wish to condemn me, at least, as our Florentine poet <note>Referring to Francesco Petrarch (1304–1374), whose lyric poetry defined the standards for Italian literature.</note> said, among those who have experienced what love is:

->I hope to find pity, as well as forgiveness,<-
<note>original: "spero trovar pietá, non che perdono." This is a direct quotation from the first sonnet of Petrarch's *Canzoniere*.</note>

the judgment of whom is quite enough for my satisfaction. # II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

it should suffice, and only the praise of high and noble spirits should be sought, caring little for the others, because it is impossible to produce any work in the world that is praised by all men. Therefore, he who has good <term>judgment</term> <note>original: "elezione"; here meaning the ability to choose or discern what is truly valuable.</note> strives to acquire praise from those who are themselves worthy of praise, and they care little for the opinion of others. It seems to me that one can hardly blame that which is natural: nothing is more natural than the desire to unite oneself with a beautiful thing, and this desire was ordained by nature in men for human propagation, a thing most necessary for the preservation of the human species. And in this, the true reason that should move us is not nobility of blood, nor hope of possessions, wealth, or any other convenience, but only <term>natural choice</term> <note>original: "elezione naturale"; the author argues that love should be a spontaneous attraction between two compatible souls rather than a calculated social or economic arrangement.</note>, neither forced nor occupied by any other consideration, but moved solely by a certain conformity and proportion that the beloved and the lover share for the purpose of the propagation of the human species.

Therefore, those who are moved by desire to love things that are outside of this natural order and the true end we have proposed are highly to be condemned; and those who, following this end, love a single thing enduringly and with firm constancy and faith are to be praised. It seems to me that this objection has been answered quite copiously; and, given that this love is good (as we have said above), it does not seem very necessary to clear that part of myself which might perhaps seem more than reprehensible due to my various public and private occupations; for, if it is good, the good has no need of any excuse, because it bears no fault. 

And if, nonetheless, some scrupulous judgment refuses to admit these reasons, let it at least grant this small license to my youthful and tender age, which does not seem so bound to the censure and judgment of men, and in which no error seems so grave—especially because youth is more stimulated to decline from the straight path through lack of experience, and is less able to oppose those things which nature and the common custom of others persuade us toward. This I say in the event that it should be esteemed an error to love with such great sincerity and faith a thing which, by its own perfection, compels the lover's love; a thing which I do not confess <note>The sentence breaks off here as the author prepares to argue that his love is a pursuit of perfection rather than a moral failing.</note>

And if this is so, either for the reasons stated or out of respect for my age, neither the composing nor the commenting on my verses written for this purpose can be imputed to me as a grave error. And granted that it were true that a commentary is not suitable for such matter, because it is small and of little importance for either the edification or the contentment of our mind, I say that if this is the case, the labor of this commentary belongs most properly to me, so that no other genius of greater excellence than mine should have to consume himself or spend time on such lowly things; and if indeed the matter is high and worthy, as it seems to me, clarifying it well and making it plain and intelligible to everyone is very useful; and for this and for what I have said above, no one can do it with a clearer expression of the true sense than I myself. the sweetest and most constant love I feel has given to these [verses], it is impossible for anyone but me to understand it. Because, even if I had narrated the circumstances to someone, it would be as impossible for him to understand them as for me to convey the absolute truth of the experience. And therefore, I return to the aforementioned verse of our Florentine poet <note>Lorenzo refers here to Francesco Petrarch (1304–1374), whose work defined the Italian lyric tradition.</note>, who says that where there is someone who understands love through experience (both this love I have so highly praised, and any specific love and affection toward me):

->*I hope to find pity, if not even pardon.*<-
<note>original: "spero trovar pietá non che perdono"; this is the final line of the first sonnet in Petrarch's *Canzoniere*.</note>

This refers to the spoken Italian of the time, as opposed to Latin, which was the traditional language of scholarship and high culture.</note>, which, according to the judgment of some, is not capable of or worthy of any excellent matter or subject.

And to this part, I respond: a thing is not less worthy because it is more common; on the contrary, it is proven that every "good" is better the more it is communicable and universal, as is the nature of that which is called the <term>Highest Good</term> <note>original: "Sommo Bene." A philosophical term referring to the ultimate source of all goodness, often identified with God in Renaissance thought.</note>; for it would not be "highest" if it were not infinite, nor can anything be called "infinite" except that which is common to all things.

And therefore, it does not seem that being common to all of Italy takes dignity away from our mother tongue; rather, one must think upon the actual perfection or imperfection of said language. And, considering which conditions give dignity and perfection to any idiom or language, it seems to me there are four; of which one, or at most two, are the true and proper praises of the language itself, while the others depend more on the custom and opinion of men, or on fortune. That which is the true praise of a language is its being copious and abundant, and fit to express well the sense and the concept of the mind. And for this reason, the Greek language is judged more perfect than Latin, and Latin more than Hebrew, because one expresses the mind of whoever has spoken or written something better than the other. The other condition that most dignifies a language is the sweetness and harmony that results more from one than from another; and, although harmony is a natural thing, proportioned to the harmony of our soul and our body, nonetheless it seems to me—due to the variety of human geniuses, which, if they are not well-proportioned and perfect, all

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

this is more an opinion than a reason: for those things judged by whether they commonly please or displease seem founded more on opinion than on true reason, especially those whose pleasure or displeasure is proven by no other reason than appetite. And, despite these reasons, I do not wish to affirm that this cannot be a proper <term>praise of the language</term> <note>original: "laude della lingua"</note>; because, harmony being, as stated, proportioned to human nature, one can infer that the judgment of the sweetness of such <term>harmony</term> <note>original: "armonia"; in Renaissance thought, musical and linguistic harmony were seen as reflections of a divinely ordered universe.</note> belongs to those who are similarly well-proportioned to receive it. Their judgment must be accepted as good, even if they are few; because the opinions and judgments of men should be weighed rather than counted. <note>A classical rhetorical principle suggesting that the quality of the judges matters more than the quantity of votes.</note>

The other condition that makes a language more excellent is when subtle, grave, and necessary things for human life are written in it—as much for our mind as for the utility of men and the health of the body. This can be said of the <term>Hebrew language</term> <note>original: "lingua ebrea"</note> for the admirable mysteries it contains, suited, or rather necessary, to the infallible truth of our faith; and similarly of the <term>Greek language</term> <note>original: "lingua greca"</note>, containing many <term>metaphysical</term>, natural, and moral sciences, which are very necessary to the human generation. And, when this occurs, it is necessary to confess that the <term>subject</term> <note>original: "subietto"</note> is more worthy than the language, because the subject is the end and the language the means. Nor can a language be called more perfect in itself for this reason, but rather it indicates a greater perfection of the matter being treated through it. Because for those who have written <term>theological</term>, metaphysical, natural, and moral things, in that part which dignifies the language in which they wrote, it seems the praise remains with the matter, and that the language has performed the office of an instrument, which is good or bad according to its end. There remains only one other condition that gives reputation to a language, and that is when the success of world affairs is such that it makes universal and almost common to the entire world that which is naturally specific to a single city or province alone; and this can be called a happiness and prosperity of fortune rather than a true praise of the language, because the fact that a language is prized and widely celebrated in the world consists in the

the opinion of those who greatly appreciate and value it. Nor can that be called a true or proper good which depends on others rather than on itself; because those who hold it in high regard could easily despise it and change their opinion, and those conditions could shift so that, the cause being removed, the dignity and praise of the language would also easily fail. Such dignity—being prized due to the prosperous success of <term>fortune</term> <note>In the Renaissance, "Fortune" was seen as a fickle force of luck and historical circumstance, contrasted with "Virtue" or intrinsic merit.</note>—is very much attributed to the <term>Latin language</term>, because the expansion of the Roman Empire did not only make it common throughout the world, but almost necessary. For this reason, we shall conclude that these external praises, which depend on the opinion of others or on fortune, are not inherent praises. Therefore, wishing to prove the dignity of our own tongue, we must insist only on the primary conditions: whether our language easily expresses whatever concept is in our mind; and for this, no better proof can be introduced than experience.

Dante, Petrarch, and Boccaccio, our Florentine poets, have shown quite clearly in their grave and most sweet verses and orations that every sense can be expressed in this language with great ease. For whoever reads the *Comedy* <note>original: "Commedia"; later known as the *Divine Comedy*, Dante Alighieri’s epic poem.</note> of Dante will find many theological and natural matters expressed there with great dexterity and ease; he will also find, most aptly used in his writing, those three <term>kinds of styles</term> <note>original: "generazioni di stili"; refers to the classical rhetorical categories of the humble (low), mediocre (middle), and high (sublime) styles.</note> which are praised by orators: namely humble, middle, and high. In effect, in this one man, Dante has perfectly achieved what is found divided among various authors, both Greek and Latin.

Who will deny that in Petrarch one finds a style that is grave, witty, and sweet, and those matters of love treated with as much gravity and <term>beauty</term> <note>original: "venustá"; referring to Venus-like grace or aesthetic charm.</note> as is undoubtedly found in Ovid, Tibullus, Catullus, and Propertius, or any other Latin author? The songs and sonnets of Dante are of such gravity, subtlety, and ornamentation that they have almost no comparison in prose or <term>unbound speech</term> <note>original: "orazione soluta"; a technical term for prose, which is "loosened" from the constraints of poetic meter.</note>. Anyone who has read Boccaccio—a most learned and eloquent man—will easily judge that not only his invention, but his abundance and eloquence, are singular and alone in the world. And, considering his work the *Decameron*, for the diversity of its subject matter—now grave, now middle, and now low, and containing...

all the disturbances that can happen to men regarding love and hate, fear and hope, so many new clevernesses and wits; and since it has to express all the natures and passions of men found in the world, one will judge without controversy that no language is better suited for expression than ours. And <term>Guido Cavalcanti</term> <note>A major Florentine poet and close friend of Dante; he was a leading figure of the *Dolce Stil Novo* (Sweet New Style).</note>, whom we mentioned above, it cannot be said how comfortably he joined together gravity and sweetness, as is shown by his aforementioned song and some of his most sweet sonnets and ballads.

There still remain many other serious and elegant writers, whom we shall omit mentioning more to avoid being long-winded than because they are not worthy of it. And therefore we shall conclude that it is men who have failed the language by not practicing it, rather than the language failing men or the subject matter; the sweetness and harmony of which, for those who have gained some familiarity with it through use, is truly very great and capable of moving one deeply.

These qualities, which are—and which perhaps might even seem to some to be—the specific praises of this language, appear to me quite abundantly in our own; and, regarding what has been treated until now, especially by Dante in his work, it seems to me not only useful, but necessary for their serious and important effects that his verses be read. This is shown by the example of many commentaries written upon his ***Comedy*** <note>original: "Commedia"; the *Divine Comedy*. Lorenzo emphasizes that the poem was so profound it required scholarly explanation even in its own time.</note> by most learned and famous men, and the frequent citations that one hears every day from holy and excellent men in their public sermons. And perhaps subtle and important things worthy of being read will still be written in this language; especially since it can be said that, until now, this language is in its <term>adolescence</term> <note>Lorenzo uses a biological metaphor, suggesting the Italian language is still growing and has not yet reached its full "adult" potential.</note>, because it grows more elegant and refined with every passing hour. And it could easily reach even greater perfection in its youth and adult age; and even more so if some prosperous success and increase should come to the <term>Florentine dominion</term> <note>original: "fiorentino imperio"; referring to the political and cultural influence of the Florentine state. Lorenzo, as its leader, links the language’s success to the city’s power.</note>, which good citizens must not only hope for, but help with all their wit and strength. Yet this—being in the power of <term>Fortune</term> and the will of the infallible judgment of God—while it is not right to affirm it as certain, is also not yet something to be despaired of. ## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...of the language, ours is quite sufficiently rich; nor can we justly complain of it. And for these same reasons no one can reprove me if I have written in that language in which I was born and nurtured, especially since Hebrew, Greek, and Latin were all, in their own time, mother tongues and natural languages, but spoken or written more accurately and with some rule or reason by those who are in honor and esteem than generally by the common folk and popular crowd.

It seems proven with quite sufficient reasons that our language is not inferior to any of the others; and therefore, having demonstrated its perfection in general, I judge it very appropriate to narrow down to particulars and move from the general to some specific property, almost like moving from the circumference to the center.

And therefore, since my primary purpose is the interpretation of my sonnets, I will strive to show—among the other modes of the common and customary styles for those who have written in this language—that the style of the <term>sonnet</term><note>A fourteen-line poem with a specific rhyme scheme, which Lorenzo argues is the most challenging and refined of the "common" poetic forms.</note> is not inferior to the <term>ternary</term><note>Also known as *terza rima*, the three-line interlocking stanza form used by Dante in the *Divine Comedy*.</note> or to the <term>canzone</term><note>A "song" or lyric poem; usually the most prestigious and long-form structure in Italian vernacular poetry.</note> or to any other generation of vernacular style, arguing from its difficulty; because virtue, according to the philosophers, consists in what is difficult <note>original: "circa il difficile"; a reference to a common Aristotelian ethical principle that excellence is found in mastering difficult tasks.</note>.

It is a <term>sentiment of Plato</term><note>Lorenzo refers to the Platonic ideal that true wisdom is expressed with clarity and brevity rather than through excessive ornamentation.</note> that to narrate many things briefly and lucidly seems not only admirable among men, but almost a divine thing. The brevity of the sonnet does not allow for a single word to be in vain; and the true subject and matter of sonnets for this reason must be some sharp and noble sentiment, aptly narrated and restricted to few verses, fleeing obscurity and harshness. This style has a great similarity and conformity with the <term>epigram</term><note>A short, witty poem; Lorenzo argues the sonnet is like a more serious version of the classical epigram.</note>, regarding the sharpness of the subject and the dexterity of the style, but the sonnet is worthy and capable of more grave sentiments, and therefore becomes that much more difficult. I confess the ternary to be a higher and greater style and almost similar to the <term>heroic style</term><note>A reference to the "high" style used in epic poetry (like Virgil’s *Aeneid*), which deals with grand, historical, or mythological themes.</note>, yet it is not more difficult because of this, as it has a wider field; and that sentiment, which cannot be restricted into two or three verses without the writer's fault, can be expanded in the ternary. The *canzoni* seem to me to have great similarity with the <term>elegy</term><note>In this context, a poem expressing sorrow or longing, often related to love.</note>, but I believe—either by the nature of our style or by the custom of those who have...

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...written until now regarding the <term>canzone</term><note>A high-style lyric poem. Lorenzo argues that because it is longer, it is actually easier to write than a sonnet.</note>. The style of the canzone, not without a certain degree of shame, might admit many things that are not only light and vain, but even too soft and lascivious, such as those commonly found written in Latin <term>elegies</term><note>A form of Latin poetry often used for personal or erotic themes; Lorenzo implies the canzone can sometimes be too self-indulgent.</note>. Furthermore, because canzoni have wider spaces in which they may wander, I do not consider their style as difficult as that of the sonnet. This can be quite easily proven through experience: for anyone who has composed sonnets and restricted themselves to a specific and subtle subject, it is with great difficulty that they have escaped the obscurity and harshness of style. There is a great difference between composing sonnets in such a way that the rhymes force the subject matter, and those where the subject matter forces the rhymes.

It seems to me that there is much greater freedom in Latin verses than in our vernacular ones. In our language, besides the "feet" <note>The rhythmic units or meter of a line of poetry.</note>—which must be observed more by nature than by any other rule—there is also the added difficulty of <term>rhyme</term>. As anyone who has tried it knows, rhyme disturbs many beautiful thoughts and does not allow them to be narrated with such ease and clarity. That our verse has its own feet is proven by the fact that one could write many lines containing eleven syllables without them sounding like poetry or having any difference from prose. We shall conclude, therefore, that vernacular verse is very difficult, and among all verses, the style of the sonnet is the most difficult of all; for this reason, it is worthy of being valued as much as any other vernacular style.

By this, I do not mean to imply that my own sonnets possess that perfection which I have said is appropriate to such a style. But, as <term>Ovid</term> says of <term>Phaeton</term><note>In Greek mythology, Phaeton attempted to drive the chariot of the Sun but lost control. Lorenzo uses this as a "modesty trope," a common literary device where an author humbles himself before his audience.</note>, for the present it is enough for me to have attempted that style which is most excellent among vernacular poets. And if I have not been able to reach the perfection required to drive this **solar chariot**, let it at least serve as praise that I had the daring to attempt this path, even if, through some fault of my own, my strength failed in such a great undertaking.

Perhaps someone will judge the beginning of my verses to be inappropriate, starting not only outside the custom of those who have written similar verses until now, but also—as it appears <note>original: "prima facie"</note> **at first glance**—almost perverting the order of nature,

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici argues that the sonnet is the most difficult and prestigious poetic form because its tight structure and rhyme requirements demand more skill than the longer canzone or Latin verse.</summary>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...placing at the beginning that which in all human affairs is usually the ultimate end; because the first four sonnets were composed by me for the death of a lady, who not only wrung these sonnets from me, but also tears universally from the eyes of all men and women who had any knowledge of her. Therefore, despite it appearing a very absurd thing for me to begin with death, it seems to me a very appropriate beginning for the reasons we shall state hereafter.

It is the opinion of good philosophers that the <term>corruption</term><note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "corruption" is the natural process of a thing ceasing to exist in its current form so that a new form can emerge.</note> of one thing is the creation of another, and the limit and end of one evil is the stage and beginning of another. This happens of necessity because, since the form and species are—according to the philosophers—immortal, they must of necessity always move through matter. From this perpetual motion there necessarily arises a continuous generation of new things. Since these exist without any interruption of time and with only a very brief presence of the being of things in that specific quality or form, one must admit that the end of one thing is the beginning of another. And, according to Aristotle, <term>privation</term><note>The state of lacking a quality that could be present; a fundamental principle of change in ancient physics.</note> is the beginning of created things. Because of this, it is concluded that in human affairs, end and beginning are the selfsame thing—I do not mean the end and beginning of the same thing, but that what is the end of one thing is *immediately* <note>original: "immediate"</note> the beginning of another.

And, if this is so, death is very appropriately the beginning of this work of ours; and all the more so because whoever examines it more subtly will find that the beginning of the life of love proceeds from death, because he who lives for love first dies to all other things. And if love possesses that perfection which we have already mentioned, it is impossible to reach such perfection unless one first dies to more imperfect things. This same opinion seems to have been followed by Homer, Virgil, and Dante, of whom Homer sends Odysseus to the underworld, Virgil sends Aeneas, and Dante sends himself to survey Hell, to show that one travels these paths toward perfection. But it is necessary, after gaining knowledge of imperfect things, to die to them; because, once Aeneas has reached the <term>Elysian Fields</term><note>The final resting place of the heroic and virtuous in classical mythology.</note> and Dante is led into Paradise, never again...

<summary>Lorenzo justifies starting his sonnet sequence with the theme of death, using Aristotelian philosophy to argue that the end of one state is the necessary beginning of another. He links this to the "death" of the self required to enter a life of perfected love, citing the epic journeys of Odysseus, Aeneas, and Dante.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, philosophy of change, death and rebirth, poetic theory, Homer, Virgil, Dante, Elysian Fields, courtly love</keywords>

...are recalled from the underworld. And Orpheus would have drawn Eurydice out of hell and led her among those who live, if he had not turned back toward the underworld; this can be interpreted to mean that Orpheus was not truly dead, and for this reason, he had not reached the perfection of his happiness—which was to possess his dear Eurydice. Therefore, the beginning of the true life is the death of the life that is not true. For this reason, it does not seem that placing death at the beginning of our verses was done without some sound consideration.

Perhaps this prologue of ours has been both too lengthy and a greater preparation than the work itself warrants. To me, it seems to have been somewhat copious not without true necessity; and, considering the slightness of these verses of mine, I judged they had need of some ornament, which is appropriate for those things that by their nature are little adorned. Nor was a lesser excuse suitable for the faults that might perhaps have been attributed to me. And so, this part being concluded, we shall come to the exposition of the sonnets, having first provided a brief <term>argument</term><note>In Renaissance literature, an 'argument' is a short introductory summary of the themes or plot that follow.</note> that seems necessary for these first four.

There died, as we said above, in our city a lady who moved the entire Florentine people to general compassion. This is no great wonder, because in beauty and human <term>gentilezza</term><note>original: "gentilezza". This term implies more than "kindness"; it refers to the innate nobility, refinement, and grace expected of a high-born person.</note> she was truly as adorned as any woman who had lived before her. Among her other excellent gifts, she possessed such a sweet and attractive manner that all those who had some personal acquaintance with her believed they were supremely loved by her. Even the women and the young ladies who were her peers not only felt no envy toward her for her most excellent virtue, but supremely exalted and praised her beauty and nobility; thus, it seemed impossible to believe that so many men loved her without jealousy and so many women praised her without envy. And, although her life—because of her most worthy qualities—made her most dear to everyone, the compassion felt at her death was even greater, due to her very tender age and her beauty, which, though she was dead, she displayed perhaps more than any living woman ever had. ...[who had] lived, a most ardent desire for her. And, because she was carried uncovered from her house to the place of burial, she moved a great abundance of tears in all those who gathered to see her. Among those who already knew her, beyond compassion, there arose an admiration that in death she had surpassed that beauty which, while living, seemed unsurpassable. In those who did not know her before, there was born a grief and almost a remorse for not having known such a beautiful thing before they were entirely deprived of it, having only discovered her then to suffer perpetual sorrow. Truly, that which our Petrarch says was verified in her:

->*Death appeared beautiful upon her beautiful face.*<-
<note>original: "Morte bella parea nel suo bel viso." This famous line is from Petrarch's *Triumph of Death*.</note>

This lady having thus died, all the Florentine <term>wits</term><note>The Italian "ingegni" refers to the city's poets, scholars, and intellectuals.</note>, as was fitting for such a public <term>loss</term><note>original: "iattura". A term denoting a great calamity or misfortune.</note>, lamented the bitterness of this death in various ways—some in verse and others in prose—and they strove to praise her according to the faculty of their talent. Among these, I also wished to be, and I too accompanied their tears with the following sonnets, the first of which begins:

->O bright star, who with your rays.<-

It was night, and a very dear friend of mine and I were walking together, speaking of this common loss; and while speaking, the weather being very serene, we turned our eyes toward an exceptionally bright star which was seen toward the west. It was certainly of such splendor that it not only far surpassed the other stars, but it was so luminous that it caused those bodies that stood against its light to cast a shadow. Having been at first struck with admiration, I turned to this friend of mine and said: "Let us not marvel at this, for the soul of that most noble lady has either been transformed into this new star or has joined with it. And if this is so, this splendor does not seem miraculous; therefore, just as her living beauty was a great comfort to our eyes, let us comfort them now with the vision of this most bright star. And if our sight is weak and frail before such light, let us pray to the <term>numen</term><note>original: "nume". Lorenzo uses this classical term to refer to the lady's divine spirit or presiding power.</note>—that is, her divinity—to strengthen them by taking away a portion of such splendor, so that without offending our eyes we may contemplate it for a while." ## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

Truly the flower is of a pale color, because it is yellow and white. I felt compassion for its fate because, as evening was already approaching, I thought that it would soon lose the sweetest vision of its beloved, for the sun was already drawing near to our horizon, which deprived <note>Clytie (Italian: *Clizia*). In Greek mythology, Clytie was a nymph who loved the sun god and was transformed into a flower—historically identified as a heliotrope or marigold—that always turns its face toward the sun.</note> Clytie of her beloved sight. Her grief was even greater because what was denied to her was common to many others—namely, the eyes of those who are called the **<term>antipodes</term>** <note>From the Greek for "those with feet opposite ours." In the Renaissance, this referred to the inhabitants of the opposite side of the Earth.</note>, for whom the sun shines when we are deprived of it, and whose night makes our day. From this thought I entered into another: that although she lost this delightful vision for one night, at least the following morning she was granted the chance to see it again; for as the western horizon takes it from her, the eastern returns it, and the kind **Aurora** <note>The Roman goddess of the dawn.</note>, pitying Clytie’s love, shows it to her once more. And I too thank the eastern horizon for returning it to her, because it is a very natural and human thing to have compassion for the afflicted, especially those who have some similarity of affliction with us. 

This fate of Clytie, shifting and alternating, then made me think how much harder and more unjust was the fate of one who greatly desires to see something, the sight of which is necessarily forbidden to him—not for one night, but forever. I see which dawn returns her sun to Clytie, but I do not know what other dawn might return to the world this other sun—that is, the eyes of that lady. And if this sun cannot by necessity return to the eyes of those who have no other light, it must be forever night; for night is nothing other than the deprivation of the sun’s light. 

Therefore, the harshest fate is that of one who waits with great desire for what he cannot have; nor can such a person have any other refreshment than to remember and keep the eyes of the mind fixed on what he has most loved and what was most dear to him. For, just as I believe happens to Clytie—who remains at evening with her face turned toward the western horizon, which is what took the vision of the sun from her, until the morning when the sun turns her back toward the east—so this **new Clytie** <note>Lorenzo refers to himself as a "new Clytie," a soul permanently oriented toward the memory of his deceased "sun."</note> can have no greater refreshment than to keep his mind and thought turned toward the final impressions and the dearest things of his sun, which are like...

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...of the western horizon, which deprived her of her beloved vision.

We can further say that this final horizon refers to the death of this most noble lady. For "**horizon**" <note>Lorenzo uses the word *orizzonte* here in both a literal astronomical sense and a metaphorical sense to represent the boundary between life and the afterlife.</note> means nothing other than the ultimate boundary beyond which human eyes cannot see; just as we say, if the sun sets, that it is the final place beyond which the sun is no longer seen, and, when it rises, the first place where the sun appears. And therefore we may appropriately call death that horizon which took the sight of her eyes from us. This "new Clytie"—that is, the lover of her eyes—must keep his eyes fixed and steady upon this horizon, coming to the realization that every mortal thing, no matter how beautiful or excellent, must necessarily die. 

And such a reflection is usually a great and effective remedy to console every sorrow and to show men that mortal things should be loved as finite things, subject to the necessity of death. And whoever considers this in others can easily recognize this condition and necessity in himself, observing that most wise saying that was written in the temple of Apollo: "**Know thyself**" <note>original: "Nosce te ipsum." A Latin translation of the famous Greek maxim *gnōthi seauton* inscribed at Delphi.</note>. One should persevere in this thought until death comes, which will return the sun to this new Clytie, just as the dawn returns it to Clytie who has already been transformed into a flower; for then the soul, released from the body, will be able to contemplate the beauty of her soul—a beauty much greater than that which was previously visible to the eyes, because the light of human eyes is like a shadow compared to the light of the soul. 

And just as her death was the horizon for the setting of the sun of her eyes, so the death of this new Clytie will be the eastern horizon that will return his sun to him, just as the dawn returns it to Clytie already transformed into a flower. Therefore, since this thought seemed to me to be a very appropriate subject to put into verse, I composed the following **sonnet** <note>A 14-line poem, a form Lorenzo helped refine during the Italian Renaissance.</note>:

->When the sun descends below the horizon,<-
->I gaze upon Clytie, pale of face,<-
->and I weep for her fate, which has taken from her<-
->the sight of him who shines for others.<-

<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Clytie metaphor, Neoplatonism, mortality, Know Thyself, immortality of the soul, grief, Italian Renaissance poetry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s philosophical commentary on his sonnets. Having established death as the "final horizon" on the previous page, he now presents the concluding stanzas of the sonnet and analyzes the psychological state of the grieving lover, specifically through the lens of Renaissance humoral theory.</meta>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

> Then, when Phoebus <note>original: "Febo," a poetic name for Apollo, the sun god.</note>, turned toward us, ignites
> the grass, the plants, and the flowers with new flame,
> I then offer much thanks to the other horizon
> and to the kind dawn that took him away from it.
>
> But alas! I do not know what new dawn
> might return its sun to the world: ah, harsh fate,
> that willed to clothe us in eternal night!
>
> O Clytie <note>In Greek myth, the nymph Clytie was turned into a flower (a heliotrope or sunflower) that always turns its face toward the sun.</note>, in vain do you hope to see him now:
> keep your eyes fixed until death closes them
> upon that final horizon which took him from you.

It is commonly the nature of lovers, and the food that satisfies their "amorous hunger," to dwell on sad thoughts and **melancholy**, full of tears and sighs; and this commonly occurs even during their moments of greatest happiness and sweetness. I believe the reason for this is that love, which is a singular and enduring state, proceeds from a powerful imagination. This can hardly exist unless the **melancholic humor** <note>In the Renaissance, "melancholy" was considered one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids that governed personality. A person with an excess of melancholy was thought to be prone to deep thought, creativity, and persistent sadness.</note> predominates within the lover. The nature of this humor is to always be suspicious and to transform every event—whether prosperous or adverse—into grief and passion.

If this is the true nature of lovers, then their grief is certainly greater than that of other men, especially when a truly painful and tearful event is added to this natural predisposition. Indeed, nothing can happen to a lover more worthy of grief and tears than the perpetual loss of the beloved. From this, one can imagine how much grief the death of that lady caused those who loved her most deeply—a grief that was reasonably greater than any a man might normally experience. 

It is the nature of those who are melancholic (as we have said lovers are) to seek no other remedy for their grief than the accumulation of more grief, and to hate and flee every kind of refreshment and consolation. Therefore, if sometimes death was placed before my eyes as a potential remedy for this most bitter grief—seeing it as the end of this painful passion—it was nonetheless hated by me. It ought to have been hated all the more because death, having "been in her eyes," could be considered sweeter and more noble. By being connected to such a noble person, death necessarily shared in that quality of excellence which it had found so abundant in her.

<vocab>sonnet, love, Phoebus, Clytie, melancholy, death, grief</vocab>

<summary>Lorenzo concludes his sonnet by lamenting the "eternal night" left by his beloved's death and explains that lovers are naturally inclined toward melancholy, which makes their grief uniquely intense and resistant to comfort.</summary>
<keywords>melancholy, four humors, Clytie myth, Phoebus Apollo, grieving process, Renaissance psychology, amorous hunger</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>The previous page established that the lover’s grief stems from a "melancholic humor," which causes them to find a strange satisfaction in sorrow. Lorenzo now explains how this grief transforms the very concept of death from something fearsome into something "gentle" or noble because it has been sanctified by the beloved's passing.</meta>

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

And, thinking how much death had been made noble <note>The Italian *gentile* here implies more than "kind"; it refers to the Renaissance concept of "gentlehood" or nobility of spirit—the idea that death has become refined and beautiful by touching the beloved.</note> by this, I believed that the immortal gods must change their fate, and that even they would want to taste the nobility of death. And, if this were so, I—desiring by my nature only pain and wishing to taste nothing sweet—chose to continue through these remaining years of life for the sake of greater suffering, so that my grief might be more lasting <term>diuturn [diuturno]</term><note>A formal word meaning long-lasting or enduring.</note>, and so that my eyes might weep for a longer time and my heart might more lengthily sigh for the setting—that is, the death—of my sun. My eyes would be deprived of their sweetest vision and my heart of all its hope and comfort, weeping and sighing in the company of Love, the Graces, and the Muses, for whom weeping and sorrow are as appropriate as they are to my own eyes and heart. 

Because, just as the eyes and the heart have lost that goal to which they were ordered and destined by Love, so Love himself must also weep, because he had placed his empire and his purpose in her eyes; and the Graces placed all their gifts and virtues in her beauty, and the Muses placed the glory of their choir in singing her most worthy praises. Therefore, weeping is fittingly appropriate to all of them; and whoever does not weep with them must be a man entirely devoid of either love or grace. Because everyone ought to weep—some to avoid being, and others to avoid at least appearing, rebels against such nobility—I wished to express these feelings in the present sonnet:

> I would flee the sweet light of life
> for that life which others call "death";
> but death is so noble today and so beautiful,
> that I believe the gods will want to die.
>
> Death is noble, since it has been within her
> who is now the brightest star in heaven;
> I, who wish to taste no sweetness since she
> is dead, will follow these wicked years.
>
> My eyes shall always weep, and my sad heart
> shall sigh for the setting <note>original: "occaso," referring to the sunset or the "falling" of the sun.</note> of its beautiful sun,
> they being deprived of it, and the heart of all its hope.
>
> Love shall weep sweetly with me,
> the Graces and the sisters of Parnassus <note>The Muses, who were said to live on Mount Parnassus.</note>;
> and who would not weep along with these?

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici argues that the beloved’s death has so dignified the state of dying that even the gods might envy it. He concludes that his continued life is an act of devotion, allowing him to prolong his grief in the company of personified Love and the Muses.</summary>
<keywords>Death as noble, Simonetta Vespucci, Lorenzo de' Medici, Sonnet, Muses, Parnassus, personification of Love, Grief</keywords>
