## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

No one will wonder—if their heart has ever been lit by the fire of love—to find in these verses various passions and feelings that are quite contrary to one another. Because love is nothing other than a noble <term>gentle [gentile]</term> <note>In the Renaissance context, "gentle" refers to a state of spiritual nobility and refinement, rather than just being kind or soft.</note> passion, it would be more of a wonder if a lover ever had a moment of peace or a consistent way of life. And so, if in my own and in others' love verses one often finds this variety and contradiction of things, this is the privilege of lovers who are released from all common human qualities; for no reason can be given, nor any method or counsel found, in those matters which passion alone governs. 

The present sonnet seems quite contrary to the previous one because, while that one fled from every kind of consolation and seemed to feed both on present pain and the hope of having even more of it, this one shows that I have searched for various reasons for consolation. It shows that I have tried many things—though in vain—so that this most bitter memory of her death might flee from my soul; and finally, it shows some desire for death, from which the previous sonnet was entirely alienated. 

Anyone who feels excessive grief commonly tries to mitigate it in two ways: either by letting something pleasant, sweet, and delightful soften the pain, or by letting some grave and important thought drive it away. Commonly, one chooses the remedy that is easier and sweeter first. And so, feeling the bitterness of this memory, I went searching for some solitary or shaded place, or the beauty of some green meadow—as the commentary on the second sonnet also testifies—or I would place myself near some clear, running water or in the shade of some small tree. 

But it happened to me as it does to one who is burdened by illness: having a corrupted sense of taste, even if various kinds of delicate foods are served to him, he draws the same flavor from all of them, converting the sweetness of those foods into bitterness. In the same way, the more these diverse and pleasant things should have offered joy to my heart, because my "taste" was corrupted and my soul disposed toward tears, they all multiplied my grief. The memory of her, which was present in every place and at every time, showed me all those things with a much greater bitterness than usual. And, even though this memory was very harsh...

<summary>Lorenzo explains that the contradictory emotions in his poetry reflect the nature of love, which is ruled by passion rather than reason. He describes his failed attempts to find comfort in nature, noting that his grief was so profound it turned even beautiful landscapes into sources of bitterness.</summary>
<keywords>contradictions of love, noble passion, grief, consolation, nature, illness metaphor, Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance psychology</keywords>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS
-> LORENZO THE MAGNIFICENT, Works - I. 3 <-

...and troublesome; yet, as we have said of the sick person <note>The OCR reads "inferno" (hell), but in the context of food tasting bitter, Lorenzo is almost certainly referring to an "infermo" (a sick person).</note>, who although all foods bring bitterness to the taste, yet they nourish him and are the reason he lives; so my life was sustained by this most bitter food of her memory. And in fact, no better antidote or remedy could be found against this evil than the evil itself; nor could that thought be conquered except by the same thought, because no other sweetness remained in the heart but this most bitter memory, and therefore only this was helpful to my malady. 

It being necessary, therefore, to recur to the second remedy, I fled from these delightful places into the straits and storm of civic occupations <note>Lorenzo refers to his demanding role as the leader of the Florentine Republic.</note>. This remedy was also insufficient because, that most noble lady having taken dominion of my heart and once made it hers among all other thoughts, the thought and memory of her stayed in the middle of my heart, and in spite of all other cares, as if it were her own property, she consumed it; because "care" <term>cura</term> <note>Lorenzo is using a popular Renaissance etymology, likely from the Latin *cor urat*, meaning "it burns the heart."</note> means nothing other than "that thing which burns and consumes the heart." And therefore, being unable to remove myself from such bitterness and harshness by either one way or the other, there remained no other remedy and hope than that of death, which hears too late; which can be interpreted either as her not having wanted to hear the prayers of so many who desired life for her, or because the affliction felt after her death, having no other remedy but death, was so great that every delay and postponement of death, however small, seemed unbearable.

> To what place shall I go where I find you not,
> sad memory? into what dark cavern
> shall I flee, where you are not always with me,
> sad memory, who alone aids my illness?
> If in a meadow, which buds with new flowers,
> if to the shade of green shrubs I betake myself,
> I see a flowing stream, I weep with it:
> what thing is there, that does not renew my tears?
> If I return to my unhappy paternal nest <note>A poetic reference to his home or his native city, Florence.</note>,
> among a thousand cares this one sits in the middle
> of the heart which, as its own, it consumes and gnaws.
> What must I do now? in what do I trust?
> Alas! for I can only hope for mercy
> from death, who by now hears too late.

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS
-> [LORENZO THE MAGNIFICENT] <-

Having completed the explanation of the four previous sonnets, and since those which follow are quite different, it seems necessary for greater clarification to first establish a new argument <note>An "argument" here refers to an introductory theme or framing device common in Renaissance literature.</note> which shall be common to all the following sonnets. This is to verify what we stated above: that death was a fitting beginning for this new life, as I shall strive to show hereafter.

All men are born with a natural appetite for happiness, and all human works tend toward this as their true goal. However, because it is very difficult to know what happiness is and what it consists of—and even if it is known, it is no less difficult for men to be able to achieve it—it is sought by various paths. Therefore, since men have proposed this goal to themselves in a general and vague way, they begin to seek it, some in one way and some in another. And so, narrowing down from that generality to something specific and particular, they exert themselves in different ways, each according to his own nature and disposition. From this arises the variety of human pursuits and the adornment and greater perfection of the world through the diversity of things, similar to the harmony and consonance that results from different voices in accord. And for this purpose, perhaps He who never errs <note>A traditional way of referring to God.</note> has made the path of perfection obscure and difficult. Thus we know that our works and human intelligence have their beginning in the most well-known things, moving from those to the less known. Nor is there any doubt that things are more easily understood in general than in their specific and particular forms; I speak according to the discourse of human intelligence, which cannot have a true definition of anything unless universal knowledge of it precedes.

The life and death of her whom we have mentioned <note>Likely a reference to Simonetta Vespucci, whose death in 1476 affected all of Florence.</note> was therefore a universal notice of love and a vague understanding of what the passion of love was. Through this universal knowledge, I then arrived at the particular knowledge of my own sweetest and amorous pain, as we shall say hereafter. For, the lady we mentioned having died, she was both praised and mourned by me in the preceding sonnets as a public loss and a common calamity <term>iattura</term> <note>original: "iattura." A term derived from Latin meaning a grievous loss or misfortune, often used in a legal or commercial sense to describe the throwing of cargo overboard during a storm.</note>, and I was moved by a grief and compassion that moved many, many others to the...

And although in the preceding verses some things are written that seem dictated by a private and great passion, I strove—to better satisfy myself and those who felt a very great and private passion for her death—to place before my eyes the idea that I too had lost a most dear thing, and to introduce into my imagination all those effects that might move me, so as to better move others. And being now almost out of all hope of being able to find her, chance did more in a single moment than my exquisite diligence had done in all that time; and perhaps Love <note>original: "Amore"</note>, to better show me his power, wished to reveal such a bounty to me at that very time when I seemed to be altogether despondent of it.

A public festival was being held in our city, where many men and nearly all the noble and beautiful young women gathered. To this festival, almost against my will—I believe by my destiny—I was led with some of my companions and friends; for I had been for some time quite alienated from such celebrations, and if they had ever pleased me before, it proceeded more from a certain ordinary desire to do as other young men do than from any great pleasure I derived from them. There was, among the other women, one who in my eyes was of supreme beauty and of such sweet and attractive appearance that, seeing her, I began to say: — If this one possessed that refinement, talent, and manner that the deceased lady we spoke of had, certainly in her the beauty, grace, and power of the eyes is much greater. — Afterward, speaking with someone who had some knowledge of her, I found the results to correspond very well,

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...not so common to everyone, especially those qualities revealed by her beauty and most of all by her eyes, in which that which Dante says in one of his songs <note>Dante Alighieri, specifically in the canzone *Donne ch'avete intelletto d'amore* from the *Vita Nuova*.</note> when speaking of the eyes of his lady was truly verified:

> She brings Love there as to its proper place. <note>original: "Ella vi reca Amor come a suo loco"</note>

Truly, when nature created them, it did not merely make two eyes that gazed steadily so that they might be loved or hated by them, but the true place where Love himself resided, and along with him death, or rather the life and happiness of men. At that point I began to love with all my heart that apparent beauty; and regarding that beauty which was not immediately visible, the impression and indication given by such a sweet and rare <term>peregrino: literally "pilgrim-like," used here to mean exotic, rare, or exceptionally refined</term> appearance gave rise to an incredible desire within me. And where before I wondered at finding nothing I judged worthy of a sincere love, I began to feel even greater admiration, having seen a woman who so far exceeded the beauty and grace of the aforementioned deceased woman <note>A reference to Simonetta Vespucci, whose death was the subject of the earlier sonnets.</note>. And indeed, entirely inflamed by her love, I strove diligently to investigate how noble <term>gentile: in the Renaissance, this implies "nobility of spirit" and refinement rather than just social rank</term> and discerning she was in both words and deeds; and in fact I found all her qualities and parts so excellent that it was very difficult to discern which was the greater: her physical beauty, or her intellect <term>ingegno: innate wit, talent, or creative spirit</term> and soul. 

Her beauty was, as we have said, marvelous: of a beautiful and appropriate height; the color of her skin white but not pale, lively but not flushed; her expression serious but not proud, sweet and pleasing, without any lightness or baseness; her eyes lively but not restless, without any sign of either haughtiness or levity. Her whole body was so well-proportioned that, among others, she displayed dignity without anything coarse or clumsy; and nevertheless, in walking and dancing and in those things where it is permissible for women to use their bodies—and indeed in all her movements—she was elegant and charming. Her hands, above all others that nature ever made, were most beautiful, as we shall say regarding certain sonnets for which her hands provided the subject matter; in her dress and bearing she was very neat and appropriately adorned, avoiding all those fashions that do not suit noble and refined women, and observing...

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...gravity and dignity. Her speech was truly very sweet, full of sharp and good insights, as we shall make clear in the course [of this work], because some of her words and subtle inquiries have provided the subject matter for certain of my sonnets. She spoke at the right moment, briefly and concisely, and in her words one could neither desire more nor remove anything; her witty remarks and jokes were sharp and salty <note>A Renaissance metaphor for "pungent" or "spicy" wit.</note>, yet without giving offense to anyone, they bit sweetly. Her intellect was truly marvelous, far more than what is considered customary for women; and this was, however, without ostentation or presumption, avoiding a certain vice often found in most women who, thinking they understand a great deal, become unbearable by wanting to judge everything—those who are commonly called "know-it-alls" <term>saccenti: literally "knowledgeable ones," used pejoratively here for "pedants" or "pretentious people".</term>. She was very quick of mind, so much so that many times, through a single word or a small gesture, she understood the thoughts of others; in her manners she was beyond measure sweet and pleasant, yet without mixing in anything "soft" <note>Implying weakness, excessive sentimentality, or moral laxity.</note> or anything that might provoke others toward some less-than-praiseworthy outcome; in everything she was wise, prudent, and circumspect, yet avoiding any sign of craftiness or duplicity, nor giving any suspicion of a lack of constancy or faith. The narration of all her most excellent qualities would be longer than the present commentary <term>comento: Lorenzo's own prose explanation of his poems, following the model of Dante’s *Vita Nuova*.</term>; and so, we shall conclude everything in one word, and truly affirm that nothing could be desired in a beautiful and noble <term>gentil: in the Renaissance, this implies "nobility of spirit" and refinement rather than just social rank.</term> woman that was not found in her in abundance.

These most excellent conditions had bound me in such a way that I no longer had any thought or limb that was still at liberty. And I can say that, as far as my eyes were concerned, that deceased woman <note>A reference to Simonetta Vespucci, the celebrated beauty of Florence who had recently died.</note>, whom we have mentioned, was like the star of Venus, called *Lucifer* <note>original: *Lucifer*, literally "Light-bringer" in Latin; the name for the morning star.</note> by the Latins; which, preceding the sun, gives way and is completely extinguished when that greater light arrives, almost as if she were ordained to warn men that the sun is coming, rather than to give light to the world. This star dies and is extinguished upon the arrival of the sun's splendor, and nonetheless it is called *Lucifer*, which means something that carries light with it—which light it does not bring into the world except when its own light is extinguished; thus, it seemed to the Latins that even the death of that star...

# II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...at the departure. And therefore, if I found myself in her presence, her face truly appeared angelic to my heart, sweet and proud; sweet, because it truly was so; proud it was made to seem by the previously mentioned doubt regarding her lack of pity <note>original: "pietà" — in the courtly love tradition, this refers to a lady's mercy or favorable response to a suitor.</note>. And thus, at first, I would turn entirely pale, because my heart, being already set on fire and having the doubt we mentioned above, could not help but feel the greatest fear. 

From this fear arose a great distress within the heart, and so the vital spirits <term>vital spirits: In Renaissance medicine, these were believed to be subtle substances that carried life and sensation throughout the body</term>, rushing to aid the heart, left my face without color, pale and lifeless; and together with these spirits—as nature has ordained—a great abundance of blood gathered around the heart. This generated in that place a heat far greater than usual; and since such heat could not escape, the space being too small for such a quantity, there resulted a near suffocation of those spirits and blood. Whereby the blood, being unable to vent, was forced to become numb and cold, as experience shows in those who die of fear, in whom a quantity of cold, coagulated blood is found around the heart, even though some quality of heat remains in their other limbs. 

But then, gazing again upon her face, as there seemed to be many signs of pity there, the heart set aside its fear and regained some courage. Because of this, the vital spirits returned to the place from which they had first departed, and with them returned the strength and color previously lost. This was even more so because, looking into her eyes, I saw Love <note>original: "Amore" — personified here as the god Cupid</note> wrapped in the rays of those beautiful eyes, showing the heart the way it might flee from me into the eyes of my lady. This path may be called blind, because the heart had no certainty other than the words of Love, and therefore it walked through shadows and in doubt of itself—all the more because Love, who was its guide on that journey, is himself depicted as blind. 

And, so that my heart would give him more faith, he swore by the eyes of my lady that the pity they showed outwardly was true; and moreover, that he would always remain in the company of my heart. For where pity and love coincide, there can be no suspicion or fear in my heart; and Love, swearing by her eyes, cannot make a more powerful oath. Because an oath does not

# II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...is nothing other than to produce as a witness for what you affirm that thing by which you swear; because he who swears, for example, by Jupiter, wants Jupiter to be the witness and almost the guarantor <term>guarantor: original "fideiussore," a legal term for someone who takes responsibility for another’s debt or promise</term> of the observance of that thing, and he who breaks a sacred oath becomes a perjurer: he offends the primary thing and holds in contempt that by which he swore. Love, therefore, having sworn by the eyes of my lady, and adding that her eyes are his honor and strength, the heart ought to have believed Love; because it is not to be presumed that he would wish to deceive or provoke as enemies those eyes in which his honor and strength were placed. And therefore my heart did not err in believing him, and completely left my breast and went into those most splendid and amorous eyes.

First impressions in the minds of men are usually very vehement, and it seems a thing very much in accordance with reason that it should be so. Because, our mind being by nature ordained to receive various impressions, and with this natural appetite to not remain empty, it acts like a thirsty person who quenches his thirst with the first thing that occurs to him, suitable for extinguishing it; and he does it all the more willingly, the sweeter that thing is to the taste. # II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...they learn, as first and new impressions are best received in the memory. Having then been assured by Love, and my heart having already fled from me, no troublesome thing remained in my thoughts, as it seemed to me I already saw certain signs of future **compassion** <term>compassion: original "pietá," referring to the lady’s mercy in returning the poet's love</term> in my lady. This generated in me the greatest hope and sweetness; and because one naturally desires what is pleasing, when it cannot be present, memory and thought represent it to us, and more willingly those things which were first, as the beginning or cause of that good which the mind feels. 

Thus, there were almost perpetually present in my memory the attire with which my lady was adorned, and the place and the time when first I gazed intently into her eyes; that is, when, already inflamed by her love, I looked at her with the highest delight. For gazing intently proceeds from only two causes: either to know well that thing which is looked upon, or for the great delight taken in looking at it. The first cause had ceased in me, because I already knew the beauty and power of her eyes; there remained, therefore, only delight as the cause of my intent gazing. 

And I, although I had seen her eyes at other times, not yet having had the grace to know them, I had not yet gazed at them intently. And when first I gazed at them intently, it was after the knowledge of such great good, after which I immediately and necessarily was all inflamed by them, because knowledge precedes and then love follows <note>This reflects the Aristotelian and Scholastic view that one cannot love what one does not first know.</note>. What appeared to my eyes was very difficult for me either to imagine or relate; because her beauties, as **Dante** says, **overwhelm our intellect, like a sunbeam on a fragile face** <note>original: "soverchiono il nostro intelletto, come raggio di sole in fraile viso." Lorenzo is quoting Dante Alighieri to explain how divine beauty blinds human reason.</note>. And therefore that which was impossible for me, I left to **Love**, who, staying always with her and dwelling, as we have said, in her eyes, could both know better and more absolutely express such excellence. 

And beyond this, as I proposed that her beauty, charm, nobility, and compassion were things impossible either to narrate or imagine, and this appearing wonderful and almost impossible to the reader, it seems very appropriate to produce an authentic witness in faith of this; and no one is a better witness than Love, especially having been present...

<summary>Lorenzo explains that memory prioritizes first impressions and that his intense gaze at his lady was driven by delight rather than mere curiosity. He cites Dante to argue that her beauty is so transcendent it surpasses human understanding, requiring Love himself to act as a witness to her perfection.</summary>
<keywords>memory, Love, Dante Alighieri, intellect, beauty, first impressions, compassion, Neoplatonism</keywords>

# II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...and it certainly deserves to be believed, at least by those who have been subject to Love: who, as we said in the preface, must be high and noble minds <term>noble minds: "animi gentili," referring to those with the spiritual refinement to understand true love</term>, among whom it is enough to have faith in such amorous miracles; and if those outside this number should not believe it, it is just as well that coarse, base, and rebellious hearts do not taste such nobility. Having therefore spoken generally of her excellence and how beautiful, noble, and pious she appeared at first sight, it seemed right to mention the three things proposed at the start of the sonnet: the attire, the time, and the place. 

And so, regarding the attire—even if the comparison is less than her excellence—since she was dressed all in white and showing her golden hair upon her head, it seemed to me to liken them to the rays of the sun when they spread over a mountain of white snow; for the hair covered nothing less white than snow, and the hair had no less splendor than the rays of the sun. And if the hair was so radiant, the eyes were much more so. Therefore, as for the time, there is no doubt that it was day, which at least was created by the sun of her eyes. 

And, given that this was so, the place was necessarily paradise, because where there was such splendor, beauty, and compassion <term>compassion: "pietá," meaning the lady's grace or mercy</term>, it can certainly be called paradise. For "paradise," as anyone who wishes to define it correctly knows, means nothing other than a most pleasant garden <note>This refers to the classical "locus amoenus," an idealized place of safety and beauty.</note>, abundant in all things pleasing and delightful: trees, fruits, flowers, living and running waters, the songs of birds, and in effect all the amenities that the heart of man can imagine; and for this reason, it is proven that where such a beautiful lady was, there was paradise, because here was an abundance of every amenity and sweetness that a noble heart can desire.

> ->Carry to her my wretched laments.<-
> ->But, alas! how foolish is my desire,<-
> ->for the heart cannot live without those beautiful eyes.<-

Already, through my eyes, the image of her beauty had descended into my heart, and her eyes had made such an impression upon it that they were always present there. Love—who, as we have said, always lived with them—had also come in the company of those eyes; because of this, the heart was surrounded by so many flames that it seemed impossible to endure the distress arising from its burning desire. 

Thinking of what better remedy could be opposed to this evil, nothing occurred to it of greater efficacy than to make its sorrowful condition and miserable state known to my lady; she alone, as the sole cause of such pain, could provide relief. It seemed necessary in this case to choose a herald <term>herald: "nunzio," a messenger who delivers official or important news</term> and messenger who possessed two qualities: first, that he should be pleasing to her to whom he was sent—because favors are more easily obtained through a gracious person—and second, that the messenger, besides being well-informed of the misery in which the heart found itself, should be believed by her, so that the truth of the suffering might more easily move her to pity <term>pity: "pietà," in this context, refers to the lady’s grace or her willingness to grant the lover her favor</term>. 

Therefore, the heart conceived the idea of praying to my lady’s eyes—which, being inside it, saw its great torment—that they might go and report it to her. Along with them, it would send Love, so that with more intercessors and a greater number of witnesses to its suffering, grace might be more easily obtained through these gracious messengers. For no one ought to be more pleasing or more believed by my lady than Love and her very own eyes. Those eyes, and Love with them, were witnesses to the heart’s pain and also to its complete faith, which was not overcome by the greatness of its martyrdoms <term>martyrdoms: "martíri," a common poetic term for the intense suffering of a lover</term>. For this reason, the heart believed that its state ought to be known to her. Yet, as is seen in the course of the sonnet, it was in great error; for the heart cannot live without those eyes, and since it was alive when it sent these heralds, <note>The text cuts off here, explaining the paradox that the heart needs the lady's eyes to survive, yet wants to send them away to deliver a message.</note>

through his very words it is understood that those eyes had never departed from my heart. And therefore, when my heart says: "Return to her"—presupposing as if they had departed at other times—one sees that the heart is dying from passion; as it also shows, marvelling that my lady does not care for his pain, assuming it is known to her. The heart, therefore, prays to these two messengers <note>referring to the eyes and the personification of Love mentioned on the previous page</note> that they go to appease the harshness of my lady, as the only refuge and sole hope for its salvation. And whoever reads this must assume that the eyes and Love were already on their way to depart, when the heart, realizing its error and that it was impossible for it to live without those eyes, called them back, praying that they stay with him, and charging Love alone to go and plead for him. A romantic passion can be removed from the heart in two ways: either by forgetting the beloved or by appeasing her. My heart tried both paths, and, wanting to cast from itself the eyes of that woman, it made the experiment of putting her into oblivion <term>oblivion: "oblivione," the state of forgetting or being forgotten</term>, because nothing exists in the heart if one does not remember it. It tried this remedy in vain, and therefore resorted to the second: that is, to appease her. This cannot be done except through Love, nor could pity <term>pity: "pietà," meaning the lady's mercy or compassion</term> be born in my lady if Love were not with her, along with the certainty of the pain and the faith of the heart; for love, pain, and faith are the things that move one to pity. The present sonnet, therefore, speaks to the lady’s eyes, which were constant attendants to the heart.

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

<page-num>45</page-num>

It often happens to men that they desire what would be to their gravest harm and hope to obtain those things that are impossible to achieve, moved by presumption <term>presumption: "presunzione," here meaning an arrogant overstepping of human limits</term> and ignorance, which, according to the philosophers, is the mother of all evils. This defect is found most often in those who are placed in the greatest state of desire and passion, in whom the affliction and pain are so great that they attempt every desperate path to free themselves from it. This very error is noted in the above-written sonnet <term>sonnet: "sonetto," a fourteen-line poem with a specific rhyme scheme</term>, which first proposes how grave an inconvenience it is to either desire or hope to possess those things that exceed our strength, to which our nature is not proportioned, being much inferior and less worthy; it then adds two examples in confirmation of this truth.

The first is against those eyes that presume to look toward the sun, which not only fail to see it but lose their vision of all other things because of it. The other example concerns the ears, which are not sufficient to be able to hear the harmony of the celestial spheres <term>celestial spheres: "spere celesti," the concentric shells of the universe in ancient astronomy</term>. And, to better clarify this part, it must be understood that it was the opinion of some philosophers (which Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), Roman statesman and philosopher</note> records in his book titled *On the Dream of Scipio* <note>original Latin: *De somnio Scipionis*</note>) that the motion of the celestial spheres generates various sounds, according to the variety of their faster or slower movements, and from all of them together a most sweet harmony <term>harmony: "armonia," the musical concord believed to be produced by the movement of the planets</term> is made, of such great volume and sound that human ears are not sufficient to hear it, just as mortal eyes cannot look upon the sun. 

He gives as an example those men who are born near the cataracts <note>original: "cateratte," referring here to massive waterfalls</note> of the Nile—that is, where that great river falls from very high mountains to the bottom—who are all deaf because of the great crash and noise. This opinion, not being yet much approved by me, is not presented as a certainty, and therefore I said: “If the thought be true,” etc. From these comparisons of human eyes and ears not being proportioned to see the sun or hear the aforementioned harmony, I then come to show the error of my own eyes and ears, which have been presumptuous: my eyes for looking at the sun of my lady, and my ears for hearing the most sweet harmony of her words. And, if this is indeed a grave error, much greater is that of my thought, and a much greater presumption, desiring that pity <note>In this context, "pietà" refers to the lady’s potential for mercy or compassion toward the poet</note> be added—that is, so much greater strength to the beauties—

<summary>Lorenzo explains a sonnet regarding the dangers of human presumption, using Cicero's concept of the "Music of the Spheres" and the blinding sun as metaphors for his lady's overwhelming beauty and speech.</summary>
<keywords>presumption, ignorance, music of the spheres, Cicero, Dream of Scipio, Nile cataracts, poetic commentary, human limits</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s philosophical commentary on his own poetry. Following the discussion on the limitations of human senses (the eyes blinded by the sun and the ears unable to hear celestial harmony), Lorenzo now provides a Neo-Platonic framework for how he perceives his lady’s beauty. He justifies why his sonnet focuses exclusively on the mind, the eyes, and the ears, excluding the "baser" senses.</meta>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

<page-num>46</page-num>

...of my lady, which, if they were unbearable to my frail and humble senses without this pity <note>original: "pietá"; in the context of courtly love, this refers to the lady’s mercy, compassion, or favorable regard toward her suitor</note>, one can imagine how much my thought desires against itself, wishing to add strength to its own injury. It seems very appropriate to the present matter to explain the reason why mention is made only of thought, of the eyes, and of the ears, and not of any other power or sense; and therefore we shall say hereafter what cause moved us to do this.

According to the Platonists <term>Platonists: followers of the philosophy of Plato, whose ideas on the hierarchy of beauty were central to Renaissance thought</term>, there are three species of true and laudable beauty: namely, beauty of soul, of body, and of voice. The mind alone can know and desire that of the soul; that of the body delights only the eyes; that of the voice, the ears. The pleasures of the other senses beyond these, being base and unsuitable to a noble soul <term>noble soul: "animo gentile," a heart refined by virtue and capable of true love</term>, are rejected. By "thought," then, is meant the mind, which has as its object the beauty of the soul; this beauty consists in the perfection that comes to it from virtue, and it is more or less beautiful and adorned with more or less beauty according to whether it is accompanied by more virtue, both in number and in quantity and perfection thereof.

The beauty of the body and its grace seems to proceed from being well-proportioned, of a pleasing appearance, and in effect from a certain loveliness <term>loveliness: "venustá," a classical term for beauty associated with charm and Venus-like grace</term> and elegance <term>elegance: "leggiadria," a quality of effortless, light grace</term>, which sometimes pleases not so much for the perfection and good proportion of the body, as for a certain conformity it has with the eyes it pleases—a conformity that proceeds from heaven or from nature; and all this is the object and evidence of the eyes. The third beauty, that of the voice, consists of several concordant voices resulting in a concert, which is called "harmony"; and this can proceed as much from different voices, as has been said, as from a sweetness and smoothness of words well-connected and arranged together, which yet cannot be so composed without harmony.

All this beauty refers only to the ears, and for this reason we have set down only these three ways to know my lady. Because by that pity <note>mercy</note> which my thought desired in her, one must understand the beauty of the virtues and gifts of my lady’s soul, desired by our mind. For pity is a most worthy work of the soul moved by justice, because, being placed in a reasonable soul, without some

He defines "pity" or mercy as a virtuous beauty of the soul that the mind naturally desires.</summary>

...rightly so, the beauty of the body loved by my eyes is moved by her eyes. Through her words, which surpass celestial harmony, one touches upon the third beauty—that of voice and harmony—to which my ears alone were intent. Because these three beauties were found in this most noble, most beautiful, and sweetest lady, she was dear to me above all things.

> Eyes, I sigh as Love requires,
> and you find delight through my suffering.

> never reaches the goal it most desires.

> Pray with me to Love that he may be tranquil;
> for if he kindly shows that bright object,
> how much more beautiful your sun <note>original: "sole"; a common metaphor in Renaissance poetry for the lady or her eyes, representing the source of light and life for the lover</note> will be!

If that definition of love we mentioned in the preface is true, then the purpose and intention of this sonnet is also very well-verified. Its aim is to prove through evident reasons that the heart inflamed by love never finds peace; and the lover's eyes are all the happier as the heart's torment grows greater. The definition of love we have given is that love is a desire for beauty; and, if this is so, it can truly be said that love does not possess the beauty it desires. For if it did possess it, the desire for it would be in vain, because one cannot desire that which one already has in abundance. Therefore, we shall say that love is one thing, and the end <term>end: "fine," the ultimate goal or purpose toward which an action is directed</term> that moves it is another. For love desires and is moved by a goal called "happiness" and "bliss," which consists in uniting with that beauty which love hungers for, and remaining with it inseparably. And until this goal of bliss is reached, love is not only not a "good," but is rather a pain...

He concludes with a Neoplatonic definition of love as an unfulfilled desire for a beauty not yet possessed.</summary>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

<page-num>48</page-num>

...and unbearable torment, more or less according to the greatness of love. And therefore, presupposing that the heart has not reached the perfection of this bliss and sweetness, one must confess the heart is gravely tormented, because the heart has as its object that bliss of which it is deprived; but the eyes, whose office <note>The "office" refers to the natural function or duty of a sense or organ.</note> is to see, are that much happier as they see a more beautiful thing; and each thing seems that much more beautiful to the eyes as the love—that is, the heart's desire—is greater. Because, if love is great, it necessarily follows that the beauty either is or appears great to the eyes: otherwise it would not be love, which is the desire for beauty.

Thus it is concluded that, for one and the same reason, the eyes are so much happier as the heart is more miserable, taking these terms broadly: that is, the heart as the seat and place of the <term>concupiscible faculty<note>The part of the soul where basic desires, such as love or hunger, originate.</note></term>, in which all desires are born; and the eyes, not merely as a physical sense—because as a physical and external sense they cannot judge the beauty of one thing or another—and therefore, by "eyes" one must understand the operation of our soul, which acts by means of the eyes, and that contentment and pleasure it feels through the instrument of the eyes when, by their report, it judges a thing to be beautiful and takes consolation and comfort from this.

In the present sonnet, then, the heart speaks to my eyes, showing the affliction and misery in which it finds itself, as Love wills, and the delight that the eyes feel because of its suffering, showing first its own ill and then their delight. The misery of the heart is this: that it always desires what it does not possess, nor does it reach that effect and end which it most yearns for and seeks with an ancient and deep-seated desire. But the eyes not only see their object—that is, the eyes and the beauty of my lady—but they see the most beautiful and excellent thing they could possibly see, namely, my lady, because the heart can desire nothing as much as her. And from its desire is born the greater beauty of my lady, who is all the more beautiful and perfect as the heart’s pain—that is, the desire for her—is greater, for the reasons we have stated. It then responds to a silent contradiction that could...

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS</header>
<sig>4</sig>
<meta>LORENZO THE MAGNIFICENT, Works - I.</meta>

...to be done—that is, that the eyes also sometimes weep. This seems to contradict the happiness which the heart claims is found in the eyes; therefore, he says that if the eyes do weep, this does not stem from any pain of their own, but from the grief and desire of the heart, which vents a portion of its sorrow through the path of tears. Then, turning to them, he prays that they should pray to Love to make our lady merciful. They should be moved to do this not only by compassion for the heart's misery, but also by the hope of a greater good for the eyes; for if mercy is added to my lady, Love will be tranquil—that is, the desire for beauty will be fulfilled, and will no longer trouble the heart. In this case, the sun—that is, the eyes and beauty of my lady—will be much more beautiful to the eyes; they will see as much more beauty as her mercy makes it greater. It seems very appropriate, in confirmation of what we have said—that the heart is the cause of tears—to recount how tears naturally proceed from the heart rather than the eyes, and to understand what cause moves tears, as we shall say below.

According to the physicians <note>original: "fisici." In the Renaissance, this referred to natural philosophers or those who studied "physica" (the natural world and the human body).</note>, all disturbances of joy, grief, anger, hope, fear, and any other passion are born in the heart. Because of a certain connection and conformity between the heart and the brain, all these, having arisen in the heart, are immediately communicated to the brain. Thus it happens that when either grief or joy is communicated to it, the brain—oppressed or rather compressed by one of these passions—contracts almost within itself. Being naturally moist, and contracting like a sponge full of water, it distills <note>A term used here to describe the process of liquid being squeezed out or filtered through the body's systems.</note> through the eyes a part of that moisture, and thus generates tears. These are more abundant in one person than in another, depending on whether the brain is more or less moist or dry. It is a manifest thing that one also weeps as much for joy as for grief; but according to Aristotle, there is this difference between tears that proceed from gladness and those that come from grief: happy tears are cold, and sorrowful ones are warmer. And he gives this reason: joy and grief, being different passions, produce many different effects; for joy...

<vocab>physicians, heart, brain, tears, Aristotle, joy, grief, passions</vocab>

<summary>Lorenzo explains that the eyes weep because of the heart's distress rather than their own, and provides a Renaissance scientific theory—citing Aristotle—on how the brain produces tears like a squeezed sponge in response to emotion.</summary>
<keywords>physiology of emotion, heart vs brain, Aristotle, theory of tears, courtly love, Renaissance science, grief and joy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s scientific and philosophical exploration of the human body’s reaction to emotion, specifically how the "vital spirits" and heat result in different types of tears. <page-num>50</page-num>
<header>II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS</header>

...delights and makes the **vital spirits** <term>vital spirits: In Renaissance medicine, these were subtle, vapor-like substances believed to circulate through the blood to link the soul and the body.</term> more rare <note>original: "rari." In this context, it means thinned out or expanded rather than scarce.</note>, while grief constricts them. Where a greater number of spirits gather, there is necessarily a greater abundance of heat, and vice versa; from this arises the difference between **hot and cold tears**, which are born either from grief or from joy. It is concluded from this that tears have two causes: one, the passion of the heart; the other, the distillation of moisture produced by the brain. And for this reason, the eyes are the path rather than the cause of tears.

We promised in the preface, when we came to the exposition of the present sonnet, to recount how great and malignant was the persecution that I endured at that time, both from **Fortune** <note>In the Renaissance, "Fortune" was often personified as a fickle goddess controlling the luck and tragedies of men.</note> and from men; and nonetheless, I am inclined to pass over it very briefly, to avoid the reputation of being proud and boastful. For recounting one's own grave dangers is difficult to do without presumption or vainglory. And I believe this happens because, when a ship, after a most turbulent storm and many dangers and fears, reaches the tranquility of the port, most of the time the pilot <note>original: "nocchiere."</note> and governor of it attributes it to his own **merit** <note>original: "virtú." While often translated as "virtue," in this period it specifically referred to a man's ability, skill, and power to act effectively in the world.</note> rather than to any kindness of Fortune; and so that his merit may seem all the greater, he increases the past danger all the more, and often beyond the truth, so that his merit might be believed even more than is true. I, against whom these things were directed, was a private young man without any counsel or help, except for that which divine goodness and clemency provided me day by day. I was reduced to such a state that—being at one and the same time persecuted in my soul with **excommunication** <term>excommunication: A formal decree by the Pope excluding a person from the sacraments of the Church. Pope Sixtus IV excommunicated Lorenzo after the Pazzi Conspiracy in 1478.</term>, in my wealth with robberies, in the State through various schemes, in my family and children with new plots and machinations, and in my life with frequent ambushes—death would have been no small grace, a much lesser evil to my desire than any of those others.

Being therefore placed in this darkness of fortune amidst such shadows, sometimes a ray of love would shine through—at times her eyes, at times the thought of my lady. This sweetness and **refreshment** <note>original: "refriggerio." From the Latin *refrigerium*, this refers to a cooling relief or spiritual consolation in the midst of suffering.</note> snatched my life from the hands of death, even though Fortune did not notice this relief of mine, because she did not distinguish the sighs of love from those that proceeded from her own cruelty.

And so I say that, when Love mixed some of his sighs among those given to me by my adverse fortune and hard fate, the sighs of love sweetened and mitigated the others and comforted the heart. And if it happened at times that I saw my lady's face—which at other times had wrested my spirits and strength from the greedy hands of death—at present it still defended my soul against death. And "extorted" <note>original: "estorta"</note> means nothing other than something taken from someone against their will. And death is truly greedy, because there can be no greater greed than that of one who wants everything for himself, just as death wants every mortal thing. <header>II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS</header>

The present sonnet is one of these, as we will make understood hereafter. I was very near her eyes, such that I could see both them and her other beauties from close by; and looking fixedly into them, already fully kindled with hope and filled with sweetness, I would sometimes sigh with deep sighs. This most noble lady <note>original: "gentilissima." This term implies both nobility of birth and a refined, virtuous character, typical of the courtly love tradition.</note>, to whom the desire and state of my heart were already known, asked me with very sweet words how I was content and how I was faring. And when I replied that I could not be more content, nor my heart in greater sweetness, she added: — From where, then, do these sighs of yours proceed? — I, out of shyness and because both her beauty and her words had almost drawn me out of myself, could not answer anything else at the time; but, having later departed from her, I composed the present sonnet, in which I strove to set down the natural causes from which sighs proceed. This present sonnet is made in response to that most noble lady. And therefore it speaks to my lady and says that, if my "fortunate" heart—that is, happy and content (because "fortunate" <note>original: "fortunato." Lorenzo clarifies that he means one who possesses prosperous fortune or divine favor, rather than mere luck.</note> means nothing other than one who possesses prosperous fortune)—sighs at that time when it is closest to my lady, which is to say joined to its bliss <note>original: "beatitudine." A state of supreme, near-divine happiness.</note>, the cause is not some perturbation or thing that offends it, such as disdain, anger, grief, or fear; but, wanting to understand the truth well, the cause is the sweetness that it feels, which is so great that it occupies all the powers and **vital spirits** <term>vital spirits (spiriti vitali): In Renaissance medicine, these were subtle, vaporous substances produced in the heart that traveled through the arteries to animate the body and facilitate its functions.</term>, and leads them away from their natural office to the enjoyment of that sweetness. Since all the spirits are thus intent on this, the natural operations performed through them must cease. Among other natural operations is breathing or, shall we say, respiration, which is also interrupted for the reason we have stated. From this it arises that the heart lacks its accustomed **refreshment**; because, the heart being hot by nature, and even more kindled by the gathering of spirits, it would suffocate and die if it were not cooled by means of that air—which air is continually renewed and cooled through that breathing. From this it arises that Love, seeing my heart in such danger, calls his vital spirits to its aid; and truly Love moves them, because nature, being a lover of the preservation of life,

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS</header>

...suddenly drives the vital spirits <note>In Renaissance medicine, "vital spirits" (spiriti vitali) were believed to be subtle vapors that carried life and emotion from the heart to the rest of the body.</note> into every passion of the heart. These spirits, in order to obey this natural love, run with readiness and speed to its aid. From this it happens that, if the heart previously needed to breathe and cool itself, it has much greater need when so many spirits arrive, which by their nature are hot. And therefore it is necessary to draw a greater quantity of air into the chest to restore the ordinary duty of the breath, which had been interrupted; from this arises the sigh, and thus the heart is refreshed—the heart which, having already forgotten itself, did not care if it died, but rather craved so sweet and so happy a death. We can therefore say the sigh proceeds from every passion of the mind and every labor of the body, provided that the passion of the mind is effective enough to divert or interrupt the natural operations of ordinary breathing, which among the Latins is properly called "reviving" <note>original: "refocillare." From the Latin *refocillare*, meaning to warm or cherish again, often used to describe reviving someone who has fainted.</note>, or as we might say, "breathing." The labor and agitation of the body, as in one who runs or performs some strenuous exercise, also generates sighs, because the natural heat is excited and kindled; nor could the body persevere in that labor if the heart did not cool itself and breathe frequently. I wish I could have better expressed this concept of mine, for it was fitting for such a worthy and noble <note>original: "gentile." In this context, it refers to both the lady's question and the refined nature of the topic.</note> question. Nevertheless, I have chosen rather that the sonnet lack ornament and the true expression of this meaning, than that I should lack a ready will to satisfy what Love desires.

> Since the blessed heart,
> conquered by the greatness of its torments,
> having first sent forth many sighs,
> fled out from the anguished breast,
> it stays in those two beautiful eyes with Love;
> and because they—wherever Love may turn them—
> make noble every thing that looks upon them,
> they have deigned to grant it such great honor.
> The heart, chosen for this blessing by the eyes,
> is made so noble by their power,
> that it no longer craves or prizes any mortal thing.
> And though those eyes have chased out from the breast
> every common and base thought,
> it neither returns to me, nor craves any other sweetness.

## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

As we recounted in the previous sonnet, the heart, reassured by Love <term>Love: Here personified as the Roman god Cupid (Amore), who acts as a guide and protector to the lover's heart.</term>, had already fled from me; and from this, it naturally follows that one would wish to understand where it arrived and in what state it found itself. These things are narrated in the present sonnet, the meaning of which is this: that after my fortunate heart—and this is evident from the conclusion of the sonnet, because one can call "fortunate" that which is noble <term>noble: The original Italian word is "gentile," which in the Renaissance referred to a spiritual nobility or refinement of soul rather than just social class.</term> and perfect, as we shall show in the definition of nobility <note>original: "gentilezza"</note> written below; and therefore he does not say "fortunate" for having been defeated by the greatness of its sufferings <term>sufferings: Lorenzo uses the word "martíri" (martyrdoms) to describe the intense, quasi-religious pains of romantic love.</term>, but for the good that followed from them—I say, then, that after this heart, defeated by sufferings, sighed greatly, it finally departed from my breast. Its sufferings were nothing other than the burning desire for my lady's beauty. Having thus fled, it reached her eyes and was graciously received by them; which can be interpreted to mean that my heart fed both upon the beauty of those eyes and upon the hope it held for future mercy <note>original: "pietá." In courtly love, this is the compassion or favor a lady shows her suitor.</note>, which hope was given to it by Love, who was also within those eyes, and who is never without mercy.

This sweetest refuge, through the power of those eyes, made my heart noble; for, if it is true that those eyes, moved by Love, make noble everything they look upon, they must have much more made my heart worthy of such honor—that is, of nobility—since my heart dwelt within them forever. And to better express the truth and verify what has been said, we shall put it this way: things seen by those eyes become noble when Love moves them; through the eyes, we presuppose a singular beauty, and through love, mercy; and where these two things coincide, there is born in the heart of the beholder a great sweetness and love which, as we have said, is never without nobility. Nor can those eyes, moved by Love (that is, with affection), look upon anything that is not either potentially or actually noble; because affection only extends to that which is pleasing, nor can anything please unless it has some conformity with ourselves; and therefore, presupposing the nobility of those eyes, it is proven that they cannot look with Love upon anything without making it noble. My heart, therefore, having been chosen...

<page-num>56</page-num>

## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...that is, not by any merit of its own, but by the generosity and grace of my lady was it raised to this degree of nobility <note>original: "gentilezza"</note>. It already esteemed itself so highly, and it seemed to have reached such perfection, that it no longer valued anything base or mortal. And so that this does not seem to contradict what we have said—that without some merit nothing can receive this degree of nobility from those eyes—even though I said my heart was chosen for this without merit, I say, confirming the aforementioned statement, that we can call someone "noble" either in act or in potential <term>in act or in potential: These are Aristotelian terms. "In potential" means having the inherent capacity to become something; "in act" means having fully realized that state through action or transformation.</term>; that is, truly noble and possessing all the parts that come from nobility, or having the capacity to become noble. 

We might speak of this as we would a <term>blacksmith</term> <note>original: "fabbro"</note> who, holding iron that lacks any specific shape, can be said to have in his hand a sword, a hoe, or whatever tool he intends to fashion from that iron. At first, my heart was this raw iron, but capable of becoming what those eyes desired. And because it was in their power either to leave it so raw or to make of it one thing or another, it was made noble by the blacksmith's choice. Regarding the choice, it was without merit; regarding its being disposed and capable of being noble, it was not without some merit; and thus this part is resolved. 

I, seeing my heart so noble, began to love it more and to desire that it return to me. And, to move it to this, I purged my mind and my breast of every base and vulgar thing, again by means of those eyes, the perfection of which—carried into me by my own eyes—remained in my imagination. Nor would that most noble form have remained in the midst of all my thoughts if my thoughts had been base and vulgar. And therefore, as goodness naturally does, it first stripped my breast of every evil; and despite this purging <term>purging: Here, the poet refers to a spiritual or emotional cleansing ("purgazione") to make the soul a fit dwelling for high thoughts.</term>, my heart did not wish to return to me, nor did it desire any other beauty than that of those eyes where it remained. And thus it necessarily had to be, since those eyes were most beautiful and the heart already made noble, as we shall better explain in the exposition of that sonnet which begins:

->White, beautiful, and delicate hand.<-

It seems only necessary at present—since this word "nobility" <note>original: "gentilezza"</note> is found so often in our verses—and

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo's philosophical investigation into the nature of nobility. Having previously argued that his heart was made "noble" by the grace of his lady, he now pauses to provide a formal definition of the word "gentile" (noble/refined), contrasting his view with that of Dante Alighieri.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>

## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

... "noble" <note>original: "gentile"</note>, to define once and for all what nobility <note>original: "gentilezza"</note> is according to my opinion. Nor would I have presumed to do this if **Dante**, that most illustrious poet, in that song <note>This refers to Dante’s canzone "Le dolci rime d’amor," the centerpiece of the fourth treatise of his *Convivio*.</note> where nobility is defined, had not restricted himself to the definition of the nobility of man, which he calls "almost excellence" <note>original: "quasi nobilità"</note>. But, since this word—according to common usage—is applied to almost all things, it does not seem inappropriate to me to say what I understand by it, especially because in the sense it is currently used, it is a new and entirely vernacular word, for which there can be no certain property established by definition or by the usage of the ancients. 

It seems to me, then, that this word "noble" <note>original: "gentile"</note> was born from those who were called "**Gentiles**" <note>original: "gentili"</note>—that is, the Romans—who were called "the nations" <note>original: "gente"</note> and then "Gentiles" by both the Hebrew theologians and by the Christians, as can be proven by many examples. And because the Gentiles (that is, the Romans) were considered most excellent in those things which the world honors and prizes, I believe people began to call "noble" <note>gentile</note> anything that possessed some excellence among others, as if it were a work made by the Gentiles or which suited their excellence. 

Usage has since broadened the meaning of the word so much that a definition is very difficult. For one might say, for example, "noble ivory" or "noble ebony," where the former is considered more beautiful the whiter it is, while the latter is more esteemed the blacker it is: things very contrary to one another, and yet expressed by the same word. We shall say, then, that "**noble**" <note>gentile</note> is that thing which is well-suited and disposed to perfectly perform the function <term>function: or "office" (ufficio); the specific purpose for which a thing exists.</term> that is proper to it, accompanied by **grace**, which is a gift of God. 

For example, we would call a "noble racing horse" one which runs more swiftly than others; and to this we would add a beauty that makes it pleasing to the eyes. For, besides running fast, it would not be "noble" if it did not run with its head held high and with a smooth gait, showing little sign of effort or distress. Nor would it be noble if it were not beautiful, possessing a small, lean head, wide nostrils, eyes of suitable size and liveliness, small ears, a thin and slender neck, a chest not overly broad but well-proportioned, hooves of good color, and strong, high, and wide heels, short-pasterned, with legs that are not thick...

<summary>Lorenzo defines "gentilezza" (nobility/refinement) by tracing its etymology to the Romans (Gentiles) and argues that true nobility is the perfect alignment of a thing's form and function, enhanced by divine grace.</summary>
<keywords>gentilezza, nobility, Dante Alighieri, Romans, Gentiles, aesthetics, grace, horse breeding, etymology</keywords>

<page-num>58</page-num>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...nor thin but lean, which emerge equally from the shoulders, having a good proportion from the point of the shoulder to the **withers** <note>original: "guidalesco"; the ridge between the shoulder blades of a horse</note>, a back not too long, double-muscled loins, a small belly that does not sag, and a body longer underneath than along the back, good **haunches** <note>original: "lacche"</note>, straight hind **hocks** <note>original: "falci"</note>, a small tail, and a coat that is pleasing to the eyes with some good markings—as would be, for example, a horse that is entirely **black** <note>original: "morello"</note> with a white **sock** <note>original: "balzano"</note> on its left hind foot and a little star on its forehead. Anyone who wished to praise a warhorse using these traits would be mistaken, because it has a very different **duty** <note>original: "uffizio"; here meaning a specific function or purpose</note>. Therefore, nobility <note>original: "gentilezza"</note> is almost a judicial distinction applied to all things. 

Desiring then to see what my heart was like once it had been made noble, it is necessary to understand the duty of my heart; which—having as its object the eyes and beauty of my lady—seems to me to have three duties: the first to know, the second to love, and the third to experience and enjoy that beauty. And if this beauty is great, as we have said, a great perfection of the heart was necessary to know, love, and enjoy it. We will say no more of this part for the present, because in the following sonnets we will explain this matter much better, and we will show clearly why the heart, once made noble, cannot long for any beauty other than that of my lady.

> ->White, beautiful, and delicate hand,<-
> ->where Love and Nature placed those<-
> ->sweet graces, so noble and beautiful<-
> ->that every other work of theirs seems made in vain,<-
> ->you drew the heart gently from my breast<-
> ->through the wound made by the lovely stars,<- <note>In the Petrarchan tradition, "lovely stars" refers to the lady's eyes.</note>
> ->when Love made them so merciful and sweet,<-
> ->you entered after them bit by bit.<-
> ->You bound my heart with a thousand knots,<-
> ->you formed it anew, and, after it was<-
> ->made noble by you, you broke the bonds.<-
> ->If it has been made noble, it is no longer fitting<-
> ->to seek new knots to bind it again,<-
> ->or to think that any other thing might ever please it.<-

We have said that those things can be called noble which perfectly and with grace fulfill the purpose for which they were ordained.

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de' Medici’s philosophical exploration of "nobility" (gentilezza). Having previously established that nobility is the perfect fitness of a thing for its specific purpose, he now addresses a potential contradiction: how a heart already made "noble" by the lady’s eyes could require a second, "newer" nobility when touched by her hand.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

And for this reason it would seem **at first glance** <note>original: "prima facie"</note> that whatever has been made noble once would have no need of any other thing for its perfection; which seems contrary to what the present sonnet says, the conclusion of which is that the most noble hand of my lady, having drawn the heart from my breast, has made it noble by forming it anew. This same heart had already been made noble by her eyes, as shown in the sonnet already discussed which begins: "**Since the blessed heart**" <note>original: "Poscia che 'l bene avventurato core"</note>. And therefore, before we come more particularly to the explanation of the sonnet, to reconcile this apparent contradiction, we shall say this:

If nobility is that which we have described, then as many things can be noble as there are ends toward which things tend. This is seen by experience in a man; for we will call him in his tender and childhood years a "noble child," then a "noble lad," a "noble youth," a "noble man," and so on, according as his age and nature reveal different ends to him; because different things are appropriate to different ages. 

And therefore, when my heart fled into the eyes of my lady, by which it was made noble, it can be understood that then the heart had as its only object the eyes of my lady and her other attendant beauties, and it fed only upon those by means of the vision of my own eyes; and for this it was made noble—that is, to understand, contemplate, and enjoy that beauty solely through the eyes. 

But afterwards, that most white hand having entered my breast and drawn out the heart, it seems that the heart was raised to a more worthy **duty** <term>uffizio; the specific function or "office" a thing is meant to perform</term>. By this, she demonstrates the **jurisdiction** <note>the legal and moral authority or "right of rule"</note> that my lady held over my heart, and expressly clarifies that she already considered it hers. And, it being her thing by her own choice, she necessarily loved it; and this is shown more clearly by her having begun to make it noble with her eyes—that is, having granted it this benefit—because those things are loved more than others which we consider our own, and which we have begun to benefit as our own. 

The duty of the heart was one thing, then, before my lady made any sign of love toward it; and it is another thing which it had to do after so many kind demonstrations. And therefore, as if for a new duty and end, it was necessary to make it noble, because not only—

<summary>Lorenzo explains that "nobility" is not a static state but evolves as the heart's purpose changes, moving from the simple contemplation of beauty to a higher state of devotion recognized by his lady.</summary>

...was intended for her beauty, but also for the love of my lady—a thing all the more worthy because it is more spiritual and less corporeal, and yet no less desirable a beauty to my heart than her eyes were to my own. It was therefore necessary, as has been said, to make the heart noble once again and to form it for this new object. It seems that this duty was more appropriate to no one than the hand of my lady. It must be understood that this was the left hand, which, starting from the heart, acts as a more certain messenger and witness to the intentions of my lady’s heart..... <note>(1) There is a clear gap here in the original manuscript text.</note>: for it is said that in the **ring finger** <note>original: "dito anulario"</note>—that is, the one next to the finger we commonly call the "pinky"—there is a vein that comes directly from the heart, acting as a kind of envoy for the heart’s intentions. <note>Lorenzo refers to the "vena amoris" or vein of love, an ancient and medieval anatomical belief that a vein ran directly from the fourth finger of the left hand to the heart.</note> We see, then, that of necessity the heart had to be reformed and made noble once more for this new and worthier purpose, and that the true minister of this effect was the left hand, for the reasons stated above.

Now we shall come to a more specific explanation of the sonnet. Certainly, among the other most refined beauties of my lady, her hands did not seem to be human things; and although both were beautiful, the present sonnet, as we said above, speaks to the left hand, which it calls "**white, beautiful, and delicate**" <note>original: "candida, bella e delicata"</note>. This is not because these are the only beauties that hand possesses, but by describing a part of them, the poet wishes the reader to understand every exact perfection that is appropriate to a hand. And that this is true is shown by what follows: that Love and Nature had contributed to it every bit of their nobility, grace, and sweetness—and indeed every kind of ornament—so that it seemed all their other works were made in vain when compared to these beauties. Here it should be noted that all things please for two reasons: either for being perfectly beautiful, or for being greatly loved and desired. For it often happens that one loves a thing that is not considered beautiful; but where love is joined with natural beauty, nothing can please so much. For this reason, it is said that Love and Nature had placed in that hand every ornament that can...

<page-num>61</page-num>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...to interpret the perfection of natural beauty and the great love that did not allow even the smallest part of beauty to be lacking in that hand. This hand, so beautiful, then entered into my chest, which it found open from the wound previously made by her eyes; following behind them, the hand suddenly entered and drew out my heart. My eyes first had the grace to know the beauty of her eyes, and then—as often happens, either while dancing or in some other similarly honorable way—I was made worthy to touch her left hand; for on the **ladder of love** <note>original: "scala d'amore"; a Neoplatonic concept where the lover ascends from physical attraction to spiritual enlightenment.</note> one climbs step by step. This hand, thus touched by me, had such power that it took from me my entire self-dominion and, as we have said, drew the heart from my chest. Being grasped by this hand, the heart was at first bound very tightly, and then reshaped anew and made **noble** <term>gentile: in this context, "gentile" does not mean "kind," but refers to the "Stil Novo" concept of nobility of soul and refinement.</term> by that hand, because "forming" is the proper duty of hands. And being thus reformed and made noble, that hand loosened all the bonds and set my heart at liberty; because, having been made noble, it could not love anything except a noble thing, nor have any object other than the most noble. And it could find nothing more noble than my lady—rather, she is true nobility itself. Therefore, there was no need to fear that the heart would ever leave her again, for it remained there without being tied; nor could one doubt that any other beauty could please it. For if that thing pleases most which is, or appears, more beautiful than others, no thing more beautiful could be found than my lady, of whom it can truly be said (because she is noble and beautiful) what Dante says. Of her, one might say:

> Noble is whatever in a lady is found,
> and beauty is as much as resembles her.
<note>original: "Gentile in donna ciò che in lei si truova / e bello è tanto quanto lei simiglia." These lines are from Dante Alighieri’s "Vita Nuova."</note>

> O my hand, most sweet and graceful,
> mine, because Love—on that day he disdained
> my freedom—gave you to me as a pledge
> of the promises he made to me then;
> my sweetest hand, with which Love gilds
> the arrows by which his kingdom grows:
> with this he draws the bow, for which the target is
> every noble heart that falls in love.

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

->Fair and beautiful hand, you then heal<-
->those sweet wounds, just as the spear<-
->of Pelides <note>original: "Pelide"; a reference to Achilles, the son of Peleus. According to mythology, the rust from Achilles' spear could heal the very wounds it had inflicted.</note> once did, as some say.<-
->You hold my life and my death,<-
->ivory fingers, and the great desire I hide,<-
->which no mortal eye will ever see nor has seen.<-

As we stated in the preceding sonnet, nature and love provide every perfection and ornament. This same truth is confirmed by the present sonnet, which also addresses that most noble hand, calling it "most sweet and decorous": decorous for its natural ornaments and beauties, and most sweet because of the love and desire for it; for, if this love and desire were not present, the hand could not be "sweet," no matter how beautiful it might be. Beyond these two properties, it should be noted that I call it "mine." Because this might seem like an arrogance—since I was not worthy of such a beautiful and noble thing—I repeat the word "mine" immediately in the second verse, and I justify why I call it so by showing that the cause is **Love** <term>Amore: often personified in Renaissance poetry as a powerful deity or force that governs human relationships.</term>, who gave it to me as a pledge of the mercy promised by my lady.

It is a common and ancient custom among men in every pact and transaction, as a more effective sign of our heart and will, to touch with one's own right hand the right hand of the person with whom the pact is made. This is commonly used when peace is reached after a war or after some injury has occurred. Similarly, when in such cases or others an oath is taken, the right hand is the instrument and minister. I believe this custom was introduced for the reason we shall state hereafter: whenever a peace or a similar pact and **faith** <note>original: "fede"; here meaning a sworn promise or trust.</note> given is interrupted or not observed, it must be broken by some new injury. Most often, the beginning and "minister" of such injury is the right hand, as it is the hand that strikes and, for most men, is the most ready and prompt for offense. Therefore, by using the right hand in the aforementioned conventions as a testimony and confirmation of what is done, it seems that one "binds" the very thing that could most easily and primarily violate the pact. Love, therefore, gave me this pledge of his promises on that day when I held in disdain the...

my freedom, that is, that day that he <note>Referring to Love, personified as a god.</note> bound me. Furthermore, it should be noted that this seems contrary to the truth, because on the day those eyes bound me, I had not yet touched this most noble hand. But this must be understood in one of two ways: either that on the day Love bound me, he made this purpose within himself to give me this hand as a pledge, even if the effect was delayed for some time; or rather that I was entirely bound, and completely devoid of freedom, the moment I touched that hand: because, as we said in the preceding sonnet, that hand bound my heart with a thousand knots. And this shows that the heart then remained by force of the bond, and, if perhaps it could have, it would have willingly loosed itself; and therefore it still retained some part of freedom. But, after it was reformed anew and the ties were removed, staying of its own will always with my lady, then it could be called entirely beyond all its former freedom. And that servant of Love held his own freedom in disdain—that is, the freedom the heart had before it knew this new freedom, where Love placed it; because "freedom" can be so called when one can dispose of things at one's own will, as my heart could when it was loosed and free from every bond. And regarding this part, we shall speak more extensively in the exposition of the sonnet that begins: "He who has her sight" etc. He then adds that this truly most sweet hand gilds the arrows of Love, and pulls Love's bow, and wounds all the **noble hearts** <term>gentili: in Renaissance literature, "gentility" or "nobility" refers to a refinement of spirit and capacity for love, rather than just social rank.</term> that fall in love, who are the target and bullseye for Love's arrows, as our **Petrarch** <note>Francesco Petrarca (1304–1374), the foundational poet of the Italian Renaissance whose style Lorenzo is emulating.</note> confirms when he says:

> Love, who sweetly ensnares the noble heart,
> nor deigns to test his power elsewhere.
> <note>original: "Amor che i cor gentil suave invesca, / né degna di provar sua forza altrove." From Petrarch’s *Rime Sparse*.</note>

Here it should be noted that all these are tasks that are performed by means of the hands. And besides this, in saying that this hand gilds the arrows of love, it must be understood that this hand prepares for Love the arrows which cause one to fall in love—which are said to be golden, and not those of lead, which are accustomed to drive away love and give birth to hate.
