means nothing other than the work of hands. It wounds and heals, then—that is, it kindles desire and then fulfills it—just as it is said of the spear, that is, the lance of Achilles, son of Peleus <note>Achilles' spear, the *Pelias hasta*, was said to have been the only thing capable of healing the wounds it inflicted, specifically in the case of Telephus.</note>, which, having two points, the poets say that with one he wounded and with the other he healed the wounds. From this it follows appropriately that, since this hand can both wound and heal, it can also kill and bring back to life. Therefore, it is appropriately said that those **ivory fingers**—that is, those fingers the color of ivory—hold my life and death. And furthermore, this is the proper function of the fingers, because that which the hand grasps, it does by means of the fingers. This hand also holds my great desire, and this most truly for what was said in the preceding sonnet. Because, holding my heart, in which resides the concupiscible faculty <term>virtú concupiscibile: in Renaissance philosophy, the part of the soul responsible for longing and the pursuit of what is perceived as good or beautiful.</term>, that is, desire, it holds my longing, which I hide from the eyes of men, to whom it is entirely invisible. Because, if what we have said is true—that this lady of mine is most noble and my heart has been made noble by her, for otherwise it could not know or love such beauty—the eyes of other men cannot see my most noble desire; not being made noble by her, they are not sufficient.

Now, so as not to leave in confusion anyone who has read in our preceding commentary something that seems *at first glance* <note>original: *prima facie*</note> contrary, we will say the following for greater clarification. We said that this hand, so praised and loved by me, was the left, and all the examples we have given—of the faith I received from Love through it, and of the gilding of the arrows, the drawing of the bow, and the healing—refer more readily to the right hand. To remove this confusion, then, one must understand that naturally the left hand is more worthy and stronger than the right, because it is closer to the heart, which is the giver of virtue and power. It is true that human habit, like many other things, has corrupted this natural power. And therefore, if the right has more dignity or strength, it is rather by custom than by nature. Nor should habit stand in the way of that which is more worthy by nature remaining more worthy. And therefore noble intellects, such as that of my lady, despite the perverse custom, wished in this as in other things to be most excellent

...of the others; and having to testify to my heart of the pity and disposition of her heart, she did so through that means which was most natural and which deserved more trust, being closer to the heart. Besides this, gilding the arrows, drawing the bow of Love <note>original: *Amore*; referring to the personified god Cupid</note>, and healing amorous wounds is the office of the left hand; because, although beauty binds much, the heart of the beloved binds much more, and thus heals much better. And all these manual works, which are to be a sign of the heart, are much better suited to the left hand for the proximity already mentioned. Therefore, it is rather the error that men commonly use <note>Lorenzo refers to the cultural preference for the right hand as the "correct" or dominant hand</note>, than the choice on this part of my lady.

We have concluded above and several times defined "noble" as that thing which, according to human perfection, performs perfectly and with grace the office to which it is ordained. And my heart having reached this perfection by means of that most beautiful hand, the present sonnet makes mention of the manner in which it was made noble, and also of some effects of beatitude and sweetness which the heart feels because of this; for such mention and memory is no less pleasing to the heart than it is for sailors to recount some of their dangerous fortunes once they have achieved the safety of the harbor. This present sonnet, then, speaks to my heart, showing that I bear it envy—not because its well-being displeases...

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s commentary on his third sonnet, which addresses his own heart as a "blessed heart" being shaped by the hand of his lady. He is currently explaining why he feels envy toward his own heart and how the lady's actions refine his soul.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>

...his own, but rather out of a desire to be able to achieve the same good; and, by calling it a "blessed heart," he shows quite clearly the cause of envy, which is—as we have said in this place—the desire for that same good. Envy is necessarily greater and more manifest the greater the good that is seen in others; and no good is greater than being blessed, and that thing is truly blessed which is noble <note>original: *gentile*; in this context, it refers to the "gentle" or noble soul capable of true love</note>; and therefore, by saying "blessed heart," nobility is already presupposed. He then narrates the method that hand took to lead my heart from its natural hardness and baseness <note>original: *viltà*; meaning a low, unrefined, or cowardly state of spirit</note> to the perfection of nobility—that is, by caressing and squeezing it, which can be interpreted as that hand using sometimes pleasant and sweet things with it, and sometimes harsh and strong ones; because, having to fight against two enemies, namely hardness and baseness, one must oppose two contrary virtues: strength against hardness, and sweetness against baseness. 

For whoever thinks carefully about what things stand in the way of anyone wishing to reach perfection will find there are only two. First, a natural inertia and a contrary disposition to the bliss one seeks; and this arises both from a defect of the temperament <note>original: *complessioni*; referring to the balance of bodily humors</note> and organs of the body, and from natural lusts and inclinations toward many errors, since the path of perfection has always been laborious and difficult. Therefore, these contrary things are often such an impediment that they do not even allow one to recognize bliss at times: and this may be called "hardness." The other obstacle is that, even if sometimes this bliss is recognized in a confused way, and being recognized it is desired, men have a natural baseness and lack of confidence, because of which they often despair of achieving it, nor, while attempting the path to go there, can they ever reach it. 

Therefore, strength is needed against that first hardness, and softening and sweetness against baseness, using now one and now the other, according to how powerful the enemies are found to be; because one breaks the hardness, while the other gives hope against baseness. These two effects are shown in the present sonnet by saying "now it caresses, now it squeezes" <note>original: *or mulce, or stringe*</note>, for with these two things it draws from the heart every hardness and baseness, which once removed, the heart becomes noble—that is, it immediately becomes capable of receiving every worthy form and noble impression. It follows...

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s commentary on his sonnets. Having previously described how his lady's hand softened his heart, he now explains the metaphysical process of how "Love" gives form to this "noble matter" and consecrates the heart as a temple to the lady.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...this is because, as soon as the heart has become noble matter <note>original: *materia gentile*; referring to the Aristotelian idea that matter must be prepared to receive a specific "form" or essence</note>, it can no more exist without its noble form than matter can exist without any form at all. And because Love joins matter and form—that is, through a natural desire that one has for the other—so Love, who moved that hand to make my heart noble, also causes it to move again to leave upon it such a noble impression. And, finding my heart without hardness—that is, softened and ready to receive every impression—Love begins with his finger to write within it the name of my lady; that name, I say, to which Love consecrated my heart. For we understand "to consecrate" <note>original: *consecrare*</note> as the dedicating of a temple to a god or a church to a saint, giving it the title of that name so that such a temple or church may be known by it perpetually. Therefore, my heart was truly consecrated, because Love made of it a temple and a dwelling place forever, where that name of my lady might be celebrated and remain. That white finger <note>referring to the hand of the lady guided by Love</note> also paints the appearance of my lady’s face, and those perturbations and passions <note>original: *perturbazioni e passioni*; in Renaissance psychology, these are the movements of the soul or emotional states</note> that are appropriate to a noble lady, such as a certain modest joy and a certain sweet distress. 

And because what is written next seems an impossible thing—that is, that one could describe or paint thoughts, which are not visible to the eyes—one must understand that the passions appropriate to my lady are three: namely, the two we have mentioned, modest joy and sweet distress, and the one added at present, which is love. This love necessarily includes a sweet hope; nor is fear the only one of the four principal perturbations <note>referring to the four Stoic passions: joy, desire, fear, and sorrow</note> excluded, for fear is not becoming to such a noble lady, even though it is common to all people. Therefore, wishing to make mention of this most noble passion of Love, and since thoughts are the true nourishment of Love, we have said that her amorous thoughts are painted in my heart. Furthermore, wishing to relate this painting to the eyes, one must understand that the very face of my lady—which was first painted sometimes happy and sometimes sweetly distressed—was also painted at times as being "in love." For, just as we recognize joy and sorrow through laughing and crying and other signs, so too amorous thoughts are understood through many signs; indeed, they are hidden only with great difficulty from the eyes of those in love. And among the...

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s philosophical commentary on his own poetry. On the previous page, he described how Love, acting through his lady, "consecrated" his heart as a temple and began to paint her image and name upon it. He now moves from the "painting" of the lady's face to the "painting" of her words and thoughts, exploring the Renaissance theory of art and the soul.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...other signs, as also happens with other emotional disturbances <note>original: *perturbazioni*; in Renaissance medicine, these were "passions of the soul" that physically affected the body</note>, are much better understood through words, which are most often the expressions of thoughts. And therefore he adds that the same hand also describes the words of my lady, as messengers of her thoughts and outward witnesses of what the heart does within. One must therefore presuppose that this was a most worthy painting with which my heart was adorned; because three things, in my judgment, are required for a perfect work of painting: first, a good subject <term>subject: here meaning the physical base, such as a wall, wood panel, or cloth</term> or wall, or wood, or cloth, or whatever it may be, upon which the painting is spread; second, a master perfect in both design and color; and besides this, that the things painted be by their nature pleasing and delightful to the eyes. For, even if the painting were perfect, the quality of what is painted might be such that it would not suit the nature of the viewer. Inasmuch as some delight in cheerful things, such as animals, greenery, dances, and similar festivities; others would wish to see battles, whether on land or sea, and similar martial and fierce things; others prefer landscapes, buildings, and foreshortenings <note>original: *scorci*; the technique of depicting an object as having less depth or distance than it actually has</note> and proportions of perspective; others some other different thing. Therefore, if one wants a painting to please entirely, this part must be added: that the thing painted must also delight in itself. 

My heart was matter and a subject very fit to receive every impression; never was a hand so gentle and learned in such painting as that of my lady, nor could more pleasing things be expressed in my heart than the sweetest accidents <note>original: *accidenti*; in the philosophical sense of the time, "accidents" were the non-essential characteristics of a person, such as their movements or expressions, as opposed to their "substance"</note>, and the face, and the name of my lady. And therefore, in the judgment of my heart, this painting was so perfect that it desired to be preserved and to be kept eternally within itself. 

And this is a very natural desire and follows from the principles already stated. Inasmuch as one travels along the path of perfection—which is very hard and laborious—to arrive at bliss <note>original: *beatitudine*; a state of supreme spiritual happiness</note>; and whoever has the grace to be led there has no other desire remaining but to be established and fixed in it, just as my heart still desires. And believing that this was the way to be able to perpetuate itself in such a great good, it desired that the eyes of my lady might have that power and virtue which one reads the face of Medusa once had; and that, just as her look turned men into stone...

He concludes by wishing her gaze could turn him to stone, like Medusa, to freeze him forever in this state of perfection.</summary>

...so that my lady’s eyes, and my heart thus painted and made so beautiful, might be transformed into a hard adamant <term>adamant: a legendary stone, often identified with diamond, known for its unbreakable hardness and permanence</term>. One must therefore understand that by the painting of so many beautiful and sweet things in my heart, I mean the thoughts within it and the imagination of such things. Since these thoughts were full of supreme sweetness, the heart desired that they be preserved and last with the hardness of an adamant, and that new and troublesome thoughts would not follow and drive out those that were sweet—as often happens to lovers, who commonly remain in the same state for only a short time.

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...of my life I could remain for some other time, though brief, thus without seeing her. Since she noticed this—not by visible signs, for that was impossible, but by knowing the great love I bore her, and perhaps feeling in herself how difficult and unbearable the deprivation of her eyes was for my eyes, and being unable to remedy this at the time—she came to the aid of my affliction in the way that was possible then. She took delight by nature, as she did in many other refined <note>original: *gentili*; referring to noble or elegant pursuits</note> things, in keeping certain violet plants in her house in several most beautiful vases, which she herself tended with water against the excessive heat and provided everything else necessary for their nourishment. 

She therefore chose three violets from among the many others she had: those to which either Nature was more favorable, having produced them more beautiful than the rest, or Fortune, which brought them first to that most white hand. These violets, thus gathered, she sent to me as a gift; for truly, apart from her herself, nothing could better mitigate such great grief. The present sonetto <term>sonetto: a fourteen-line poem with a specific rhyme scheme, used here by Lorenzo to express his courtly love</term> therefore speaks to the aforementioned three violets, which, being of marvelous beauty in themselves, and being a gift from my lady and gathered by that most white hand, it was reasonable that they should appear much more beautiful to me than those nature usually produces. 

And for this reason, it is fittingly asked in the present sonetto—as is usually done with all marvelous things—what the cause of such excellence might be. And because the present sonetto seems clear enough on its own, we shall say briefly that in asking the reason why they were so beautiful, it touches upon all the means through which Nature produces plants, shrubs, herbs, and flowers. And because all these causes together did not yet seem sufficient for the novel beauty, color, form, and scent of those blessed violets, it was necessary that some new cause and extraordinary power had produced them; and it was impossible to understand what cause it was, except for one who had seen experience of a similar virtue <note>original: *virtú*; in this Renaissance context, it refers to a specific power, efficacy, or innate strength</note> and power in other things. Having therefore experienced within myself the virtue and force of that most white hand, which, according to the preceding sonetto, had made my noble heart out of something base and most hard, I could believe and affirm that...

...the same hand could have created those violets of such excessive beauty; for it was a greater task to make a crude and common <note>original: *rozza e villana*; terms used to describe things lacking the refinement and "gentleness" of the courtly world</note> thing noble <note>original: *gentile*; in the Renaissance context, this implies spiritual, moral, and social refinement</note> than to make a beautiful thing—as violets are by nature—even more beautiful. For this reason, it is concluded: that hand, which had turned my heart from common to noble, also made those violets of such great value and excellence. Thus, these violets are deservedly the consorts of my heart, because "consorts" is the name given to those subjected to the same fate. Therefore, for their great beauty, those violets should thank neither the sun, nor the earth, nor the air, nor the dew, nor the sunny slope, nor any other natural power that contributed to such growth, but only the power and efficacy <term>virtú: in this context, it refers to a specific power, efficacy, or inherent strength</term> of that most white hand.

Perhaps it is not out of place to see whether the beauty of these violets existed only in my opinion, or if it was possible in fact. And although I cannot judge if it were true in fact—because I can only report what appeared to me as my senses reported it to my judgment—it is difficult for me to understand if those senses were depraved and corrupted, or if they truly brought me the truth. For judgment must judge what the senses bring, and in the manner they bring it. Nevertheless, I confess it is possible that a strong imagination may be the cause of corrupting the senses, as often happens in a delirium <note>original: *farnetico*; a state of feverish delusion or frenzy</note>, where one seems to see what is not there; for the imagination has great power over the senses, as we shall make understood in the exposition of that sonetto <term>sonetto: a fourteen-line poem with a specific rhyme scheme</term> which begins: "Alas, the final glances of my lady." <note>original: *« Della mia donna, omè, gli ultimi sguardi »*</note> And yet, this does not prevent that beauty from being real, or rather, that the cause of it is the power of that hand; for we see—whether by the grace of God, celestial influence, or natural virtue—that different powers and graces are given to different men. One often sees a most learned physician kill a great number of men, while a more ignorant one heals almost all those he treats; some men have a certain personal power to heal specific ills with their presence or a simple touch of the hand; for some, their presence has been more helpful against an assailant than a sword.

It is found in certain authors of astrology that he who has a certain constellation has the power, merely through his presence, to heal those possessed by de— <note>The text breaks off mid-word: *indemo-*, likely *indemoniati* (the demon-possessed)</note>

<header>II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS</header>

...sified. And is the power of words heard by brute animals, plants, and herbs not much greater—as is said of snakes and other animals—and able to make plants and herbs wither? And can we deny that fascination <term>fascinazione: a Renaissance belief in the power of the gaze or spoken charms to cast spells or influence the physical world</term> alone produced so many diverse and great effects, as one reads in Cato and Pliny <note>Cato the Elder and Pliny the Elder, Roman authors who recorded folk medicine, natural wonders, and various "magical" properties of the natural world</note>, and in other most ancient authors worthy of faith and reverence? And what more examples should we seek? Do we not see that human eyes often have greater power, which with a simple glance almost kill and bring back to life, make the blood flee and return, take away and restore strength, and what is more, corrupt the judgment of the human mind?

For this reason, it seems quite possible that a hand could have such power <note>original: *virtù*; meaning an inherent efficacy or spiritual strength</note> as to give—I do not say some new quality—but to those same qualities more beauty and excellence than nature is accustomed to give; and especially the most beautiful hand that nature perhaps ever made. And if I were a suspect judge in this matter, I respond that the beauty of that hand, which is loved excessively, was first judged by me; for of necessity, knowledge precedes will <note>A core principle of Renaissance philosophy: one cannot love something unless the mind first perceives and judges it to be good or beautiful</note>. If, therefore, it appeared beautiful enough for me to love it, it is necessary that I be free from the fault of passion, and that the hand truly was most beautiful. And if this is so, it seems rather impossible that such beauty would not be joined by a marvelous power and potential, such that it is difficult to believe what I write of her.

<header>II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS</header>

Although in the previous commentary I mentioned that I wanted to save the discussion of the imagination's great power over the senses for the explanation of the sonnet beginning "Of my lady," <note>original: *« Della mia donna »*. This refers to a specific poem within the collection.</note> etc., nevertheless, it seems appropriate at present to say something rather of the effect than of the cause. It happens many times, therefore, that when one hears some continuous and non-articulate sound, our imagination adapts that sound to whatever it is most imagining at the time; and through this imagining, that sound seems articulate, giving it meaning and making it say whatever one most desires. Commonly, when bells are ringing or water is continuously falling, it seems that such a sound says the very thing that the person imagining it wants it to say. One also sees, as an example of this, diverse and strange forms of animals and men in the clouds in the air; and, observing certain types of stones <note>Lorenzo refers here to the psychological phenomenon of pareidolia, where the mind perceives familiar patterns in random data, such as seeing "pictures" in the veins of marble.</note> that are full of veins, most of the time whatever pleases the fantasy is formed within them.

This same thing happened to me: finding myself in a most pleasant place <term>amenissimo: a term used in Renaissance literature to describe an "ideal landscape" or *locus amoenus*, usually featuring shade, grass, and water</term> where there was a clear and abundant spring, in which water, perpetually falling from above, made a most sweet murmur; it seemed to me that this murmur continuously spoke the name of my lady, because this was the thing I was imagining most, and that name was what I most desired to hear. Assisting this most sweet deception was the fact that my lady had previously been in this most pleasant place and had looked into the spring, which by necessity had become her mirror; because for some time that beautiful and clear water had retained within itself the most beautiful likeness of my lady.

And so, it did not seem impossible to the credulity of lovers that this water, having fallen in love with such a beautiful face, would from that time on perpetually repeat that most sweet name with its amorous murmuring. For this reason, it seemed fitting that if those waters were in love with such a beautiful face, they should retain it within them forever and never let it leave, just as it seemed to me they perpetually spoke the name of my lady. And one can well believe that the same imagination that made me always hear that name, guided by an amorous simplicity, also led me

...to looking into the water, to see if my lady’s face was still reflected within it; and, not seeing it there, I realized my error. I immediately considered that water cannot receive any such form unless a similar object is present before it, because the nature of water is made so, being a **diaphanous body** <term>corpo diafano: in the science of the time, this referred to a transparent substance, like air or water, that acts as a medium for light and images</term>. But it is certainly permitted for the water, through its murmuring—or so it seemed to me—to recall her name. And because this arose solely from my imagination and my own desire, no one but I heard it, nor did **Love** <note>original: *Amore*. Here, Love is personified as a guiding force or deity who mediates the lover's experience.</note> allow such sweet harmony to reach any ears other than my own enamored ones. 

I then began to compare the happiness of those waters to my own; and, as I seemed to be happier than they, if I had previously felt any envy toward those waters, I turned it into a measure of arrogance. I showed that either my eyes had enjoyed better fortune, or they had been more prudent and wise; for from the first hour that my lady’s beautiful face presented itself to my eyes, they have always preserved that most sweet image within them. Since then, they have never been able to see anything else, neither through the obscurity of darkness or shadow, nor through the light of the sun. This can be interpreted as the "shadow" representing the night and the "sun" representing the day—which is as much as to say that neither day nor night removes those eyes from my sight. 

Or, interpreting more broadly, we can say that two things corrupt human sight and take away the **power** <term>potenzia: the natural faculty or ability of the sense of sight to function</term> of the eyes: namely, a great obscurity—and obscurity is nothing other than the shadow born from the placement of matter between the sun and ourselves—or an excessive light, such as happens to those who look directly at the sun. 

Therefore, that same imagination which made me hear my lady’s name in the falling of the water also made me see that most sweet face at every time and in every place. All of this concept, expressed in this way, is included in the present **sonnet** <note>original: *sonetto*</note>, which is addressed throughout to the water of the aforementioned spring. It remains to clarify more fully that part which says that my eyes were a mirror to my lady’s face, a point we have reserved for the end so as not to interrupt the **meaning** <term>sentenza: the underlying logic or philosophical argument of the poem</term> of the sonnet. And, as it does not seem right to omit it, let us say that, wishing to prove that my eyes were a mirror for her face, one must naturally understand that the...

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo's philosophical explanation of how the human eye acts as a mirror for the beloved's image. He is specifically comparing the physical process of sight to the way his soul retains the memory of his lady.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>

<header>II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS</header>

...eyes see, and how the **visual power** <term>potenzia visiva: in the psychology of the time, this was the soul's faculty or "power" to perceive images</term> is put into action. According to the **Peripatetics** <note>followers of Aristotle</note>, the thing being seen represents itself inside the eyes by multiplying its "species" and form <note>original: *spezie e forma*; the visual essence of an object</note>, until it reaches that part of the eye called the **crystalline** <term>cristallina: the lens of the eye, then thought to be the primary organ of sight</term>. Because it is transparent and diaphanous like crystal, it receives the form of the thing seen just as a mirror reflects whatever is placed before it. This form, once seen by the crystalline lens, is transferred to the **common sense** <term>senso comune: the internal faculty where all sensory information (sight, sound, etc.) is gathered and interpreted</term>, which then judges the quality of that thing. 

According to the **Academics** <note>the followers of Plato; Lorenzo was a key patron of the Platonic Academy in Florence</note>, certain very subtle spirits leave our eyes and travel to the object being seen, and they bring it back through reflection to the eyes. These spirits are, in a sense, "informed" by the shape of that object, which they then present to the aforementioned crystalline lens—as if to a mirror—and from there to the common sense. Therefore, according to either of these two opinions, I have spoken very appropriately in saying that my eyes were a mirror to my lady’s face, because the image of whatever is seen is formed in the eyes just as any opposing form is reflected in a mirror.

...represented to the thought. And because the beginning in all works is the most powerful part, our mind willingly returns through thought—and, if possible, through the senses—to those things that coincided with the beginning, such as the time, place, words, manners, and whatever else might have occurred there. I believe it has already been sufficiently stated how great was the desire to enjoy her most sweet presence; being deprived of this at the time I composed the present sonetto, it was necessary for me to have recourse to the aforementioned remedy: to seek something as similar and as close as possible to the truth that my heart desired. 

And therefore, I first began to remember in my thoughts that most happy beginning, from which so many sweet events have followed. From this thought was born in me a most burning desire to go to that place where my soul first departed from me—together with **my lady** <term>madonna: literally "my lady," the traditional poetic term for the beloved</term> and with **Love** <term>Amore: Love is personified here as a guiding deity or cosmic force</term>—and went far away from me. For little time had passed since her eyes had bound me, when I saw her, both very beautiful and very loving and sweet, in a most pleasant place quite near to our city <note>original: *terra nostra*; referring to Florence or its immediate surroundings</note>. 

After this time, as my fortune willed it, she departed, and I remained for some space of time during which her most sweet vision was forbidden to me; it was during this period that I wrote the present sonetto. Finding myself, then, in this place where I had left my **soul** <note>In Lorenzo’s Platonic framework, the soul of the lover "leaves" the body to reside with the beloved</note>, I searched to see if I could find it again; but, seeing neither my lady nor Love, I immediately thought that my search was in vain and that my soul, together with Love and my lady, had fled elsewhere. This was a clear sign, since I saw there neither my soul nor its company—that is, Love and my lady—all of whom I had left together in that beautiful place. 

This soul was led astray by Love and by the words that my lady spoke with Love; for speaking with Love means nothing other than speaking of things that please the soul and, by pleasing it, bind it more tightly. And certainly it was true that on that day she let me hear many and most sweet words, full of love and **pity** <note>original: *pietá*; in the language of courtly love, this refers to the lady's compassion or mercy toward her lover's devotion</note>. I returned, then, not only to this place, but I also brought back to memory within it both her words and her manners, because I could receive no greater comfort in her absence. This thought and the place, which continually represented to me...

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous discussion of the lover’s separation from his lady. Lorenzo is now beginning a philosophical and medical explanation for why the "illness" of love—and mental anguish in general—becomes more intense during the night, setting the stage for the psychological state described in his poetry.</meta>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS
->78<-

It is common for all bodily **infirmities** <term>infermitá: illnesses or physical weaknesses</term> to increase and afflict the patient more at the onset of night. This happens because, in the absence of the sun’s power—which is favorable to human nature—the **malign humors** <term>umori maligni: harmful bodily fluids believed in Renaissance medicine to cause disease</term> gain greater strength and the body’s **natural power** <term>virtú naturale: the body’s innate ability to heal and sustain itself</term> offers less resistance. This is because night is naturally given for rest, and being more inclined toward repose than the day, the body is not as intent and vigilant in its own preservation.

The same thing occurs with the illnesses of our soul, which are nourished by malign and **melancholic** <term>maninconico: related to "black bile," associated with sadness and deep thought</term> thoughts, just as bodily illnesses are nourished by malign humors. This proceeds perhaps from many other causes, but at present two occur to me: just as bodily infirmity involves both a greater strength of malign humors and a lesser resistance of natural power, so too the diseases of the mind have two causes that make them more powerful at night than during the day.

The first is that the humors of which we are composed naturally move within our bodies at certain determined hours, proportioned to the length or brevity of the day or night. That is, by dividing the night and the day (whether long or short) into twelve parts, and calling each of these parts an "hour," the **melancholic humor** <note>In the humoral theory of the time, the body was governed by four fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile (choler), and black bile (melancholy). Their balance determined health and temperament.</note> begins to move toward evening and consumes a part of the night, while **phlegm** <term>flemma: the humor associated with coldness and passivity</term> occupies almost all the rest.

Furthermore, according to the physicians, the **blood** moves during the last three hours of the night and the first three of the day; the **choler** <term>còllora: yellow bile, associated with heat and anger</term> moves during the following six hours; the melancholic humor moves during the last three hours of the day and the first three of the night; and phlegm moves during the remaining hours of the night. Because the melancholic and phlegmatic humors generate melancholic and sad thoughts in our minds, it is necessary that such thoughts have greater power during the time when those humors are in motion.

The other cause that multiplies the mind's suffering more at night than during the day is that those remedies used against these evils during the day cannot be used at night. Since no better remedy can be found against the malignancy of thoughts than **diversion** <note>Lorenzo suggests that "distraction" or "turning the mind elsewhere" is the best cure for obsessive thoughts.</note> from that specific thought. This comes from seeing, hearing, and practicing various things that withdraw the mind from troublesome reflections;

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici explains the medical and psychological reasons why suffering, particularly the "illness" of love, worsens at night, citing the Renaissance theory of the four humors and their daily cycles.</summary>
<keywords>humoral theory, melancholy, Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance medicine, psychology of love, phlegm, choler, blood, nocturnal anguish</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---

He analyzes the psychological state of the lover caught between jealousy and desire during the final hours of the night.</meta>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS
->79<-

...which can hardly be done at night. From this, it is concluded that nocturnal thoughts are far more vehement, and when they are malign, they are much more troublesome, both because they are more powerful and because there is less resistance and remedy.

It was, therefore, night, and I was so afflicted by my amorous thoughts that I could no longer resist, being entirely deprived of sleep—that is, of that little **relief** <term>refriggerio: a cooling or refreshing comfort, often used in a medical or spiritual sense</term> I might have had. And if I sought to put those thoughts aside, it shows clearly enough that they were troublesome. This distress caused by my thoughts of love could proceed from two things: either from a constant doubt and jealousy which, even if it has no true cause, always follows the mind just as a shadow follows the body. For it is the nature of **melancholics** <term>maninconici: those influenced by "black bile," a humor thought to cause deep introspection, sadness, or suspicion</term>, as we said in the explanation of the third sonnet, to cast doubt even on the clarity of the sun; or else, because thinking of the beauty of my lady kindled within me a marvelous desire. With my heart burning from this desire, I could not help but suffer the greatest passion, supremely desiring that of which I was then entirely deprived.

Whichever of these two causes it was, moved by this distress, in the present sonnet I pray to my **burning sighs**—that is, the sighs born from the aforementioned kindled desire. I also pray to my thoughts, always fixed on that beautiful face—meaning they saw or thought of nothing else but her. I also pray to the tears of my eyes, for all three of these things were troubling me at once, that they might give me peace, so that some placid and sweet sleep might come to my **streaming** <term>rorante: a poetic term for tearful, literally "dripping with dew" from the Latin *ros*</term> eyes—that is, my tearful eyes; for "streaming" is interpreted as what we commonly call **dewy** <note>original: "rugiadoso"</note>.

And to stir compassion in those I was entreating, I show that all other men and brute animals, at the time I was sighing and weeping, were quiet and at rest, without any toil or thought. By then I had spent so much of the night in these troubles that it was time I should rest, because the **horses of the sun** <note>Referring to the classical myth of Phoebus Apollo, who drives the sun across the sky in a chariot.</note> had already been yoked to the solar chariot to bring light to the world; for the escort of the **Phoebean rays** <note>original: "raggi febei"; referring again to Phoebus, the sun god.</note>—that is, the **Dawn** <note>original: "l'aurora"</note> who precedes the sun—was already signaling the coming day to the world. And because...

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

Perhaps it seems improperly said that **melancholic** and **phlegmatic** <term>flemmatici: referring to the humor of "phlegm," traditionally thought to be cold and moist, causing sluggishness or apathy</term> thoughts had such strength at the time of dawn, when we have said that the **blood** <term>sangue: in humoral theory, the humor of blood peaks at dawn, promoting a cheerful and lively disposition</term> begins to move. We must understand that, as we said in the preceding sonnets, lovers are most often either of a **melancholic** nature by birth or become so through their passion. And although they produce thoughts aligned with their **complexion** <term>complessione: the specific balance of the four humors in an individual's body</term> at all times, these thoughts multiply even more when the time of day in which that specific **humor** <term>umore: the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) believed to control health and temperament</term> naturally moves is added to their innate nature.

Therefore, even when that time arrives which seems contrary to melancholy, it happens as it does with a furnace: even if the fire is removed, the heat remains for some time because of the impression the fire has made. Naturally, one cannot pass from one extreme to another without a middle state. The impression made by the melancholic humor is great, and the phlegm that follows is not so opposed to the previous humor that it strips away its strength, because it shares the quality of "coldness" with melancholy. And so, when these thoughts—so fortified by the humors—arrive at the moment when the blood begins to move, they must be reduced, step by step, from the power of the previous humor to the nature of the blood. 

Thus, at the hour already mentioned, the strength of those "malign" thoughts was not yet so diminished that it could bring sleep to my eyes. My prayers were not enough to make my sighs, my thoughts, and my tears grant me an audience. Thinking, therefore, of what more I could do, I realized that the true cause of my ill—the one that moved the tears, the sighs, and the thoughts—was **Love**. Consequently, I began to turn my prayers to him. Having asked those first things for **peace** in vain, I brought myself to ask **Love** for a **truce** <note>original: "triegua"</note>. This was something he ought to have granted more easily, because peace is a perpetual rest, while a truce is temporary. To make him more likely to grant it, I promised Love that even while I slept, I would not rebel against his kingdom; in my dreams, I would still see the face of my lady, I would hear her sweet words, and I would touch that whitest hand. My thoughts while sleeping would be just as amorous as they were in my **waking hours** <note>original: "vigilia"</note>, with only this difference: while waking, whether out of jealousy or desire, the thoughts were most distressing and harsh; while sleeping, they would be sweet and...

<summary>Lorenzo explains how the lingering "heat" of melancholic humors prevents sleep even at dawn, leading him to negotiate a temporary "truce" with Love to find relief in dreams.</summary>

In the preceding sonnet, we confirmed that the thoughts of the night are more intense than those of the day, and when they are harmful, they are much more troublesome. But, even if this is generally the case, thoughts of love, more than any others in my opinion, take strength from the night and are much more unbearable when they are troublesome; nor can they be anything but troublesome, assuming the deprivation of the beloved thing, because all the evils that can befall men are nothing other than the desire for a good of which they are deprived. For he who feels any pain or twisting in the body desires the health of which he is deprived; he who is in prison, freedom; he who is deposed from some dignity, to return to a good condition; he who has lost some faculty or substance, wealth. And from this truly one can conclude that he who were without desire would not be subject to any misfortune; and he who desires most, feels greater...

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...affliction. And if this is true, certainly lovers are, more than all others, wretched, because they have greater desire; and at night they are most wretched, because the desire is greater, and since the other occupations that distract the mind are lacking, they have no other recourse against the thought that afflicts them than that very same thought. They are deprived of the various mitigations their passion might have during the day—such as seeing the beloved lady, talking about her with a friend, seeing one of her intimates, relatives, or servants, or at least seeing the house where she lives. These things, although they are nothing more to a lover than a feverish and thirsty <term>thirsty: The original Italian *siziente* comes from the Latin *sitiens*, describing the parched, intense thirst of a person in the grip of a fever.</term> person washing out their mouth—which actually causes the thirst to grow even more—nonetheless make the time pass with less affliction. One can truly say that lovers live on sweetest deceptions which they create for themselves. Being deprived of these to some extent at night, alone and pensive, they admit neither consolation nor sleep, as the present sonnet shows, which is very similar in sentiment to the previous one.

The sonnet speaks to Sleep, praying that it might deign to come after so many troubles and anxieties to close the fountain of my tearful eyes—a "perennial fountain," that is, living and perpetual—as if to say that if sleep does not close those eyes, *they will never cease to weep*. He then calls sleep *sweet oblivion* and *unique quiet* to restrain desire, because my affliction had these two remedies: either to forget, by interrupting my thoughts, or to mitigate such great desire. 

And, because it seemed impossible to me not only to sleep but even to live without imagining my lady, I pray to Sleep that, in coming to my eyes, it might lead her along in its company—that is, show her to me in dreams—and let me see and hear her sweetest smile; that smile, I say, where the Graces <note>The Graces: In classical mythology, these are the *Charites*, goddesses of charm, beauty, and nature who embody everything that is pleasing.</note> have made their home, which is graceful and noble above all others. This is truly said without any flattery, for my lady possessed such grace in everything, and especially in this. I desired further that her countenance—that is, her appearance—be shown to me by Sleep as merciful, along with *wise speech*, both things being suited to bring some peace to my most burning desire; and therefore it was necessary that her appearance and words be loving...

...and full of hope. For he says that, if sleep consents and chooses to grant my prayers to represent my lady to me as beautiful and merciful, etc., he would desire to sleep eternally without ever waking, always assuming he would see my lady in the aforementioned state. And if this should be impossible, at least let these dreams not be vain and lying, like those that pass through the **ivory gate**. It is found written in the fables of the ancient poets that there are two gates near the underworld: one is **eburnea**, that is, made of ivory, and the other is made of **horn**; and all dreams that reach the human imagination during sleep pass through these two gates, with this distinction: that true dreams pass through the gate of horn, and those that are false and vain pass through the gate of ivory. And therefore, my praying that these joyful dreams do not pass through the ivory gate is the same as praying that those dreams may not be false, but verified, and may have 그hat happy effect that those of the **horn gate** <note>Lorenzo refers here to a classical image found in Homer’s *Odyssey* and Virgil’s *Aeneid*, where the "Gate of Horn" allows true visions to pass to the dreamer, while the "Gate of Ivory" releases deceptive ones.</note> usually have.

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s philosophical commentary on his own poetry. While the previous page discussed the classical imagery of the Gates of Horn and Ivory to distinguish between true and false dreams, this page shifts focus to the psychological nature of desire and how it dictates the intensity of both joy and suffering in the lover’s mind.</meta>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

We have shown quite extensively in the two preceding sonnets how intense nocturnal thoughts are, especially those of an amorous nature. And, having made mention only of the affliction caused by malevolent thoughts, it seems appropriate that the present sonnet should follow the previous two. In its exposition, it is necessary to show how much sweetness amorous thoughts bring when they do not proceed from a troublesome cause. These thoughts reasonably bring greater sweetness than others, if it is true that malevolent thoughts bring greater distress; for the very same causes that create the first excess of unhappiness also produce an even more excessive happiness. This is like a miser who feels as much pain in losing a certain amount of money as he feels joy if he were to earn that same amount. For, if it is true—as we said in the previous commentary—that <term>appetite<note>In Renaissance philosophy, "appetite" refers to the natural drive or desire for something the soul perceives as good or necessary.</note></term> is what subjects us to the accidents of **fortune** and to disturbances, it seems necessary that our good and evil must be measured according to the quantity of that appetite. And since the same appetite is directed toward the same thing, it seems not only true but necessary that the happiness and unhappiness regarding that thing be equal, according to equal degrees—either of the deprivation of that thing or of the fulfillment of the appetite. Therefore, amorous thoughts are most sweet and more pleasant than others when they proceed from a sweet cause, as the present sonnet demonstrates. 

And, because we said before that the unhappiness of amorous thoughts proceeded from the deprivation of the beloved and from the suspicion that commonly accompanies lovers, the happiness of the aforementioned thoughts similarly proceeds from two causes—always presupposing the certainty that lovers may have regarding the faith and love of the beloved. One cause is that, by thinking of some fresh and past happiness and contentment, upon which the thought expands and willingly remembers thing by thing, it seems to the lover that by doing so he almost prolongs that past sweetness. The other proceeds from a hope very close to the effect of a future good, which carries such certainty within itself that it almost makes the good seem present. And, just as the first cause makes the past good more perpetual after the fact, so does near **hope** give it a beginning before the fact.

<summary>Lorenzo explains that the intensity of a lover's joy or sorrow is determined by the strength of their desire (appetite). He argues that happy thoughts arise from two sources: the remembrance of past joys and the certain hope of future ones.</summary>
<keywords>appetite, Fortune, psychology of love, memory, hope, Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

As one sees, for example, that whoever expects such sweetness or whoever has recently experienced it, would wish to distance themselves from all other thoughts. And I have known someone who, receiving a sudden and unhoped-for piece of news and an unhoped-for certainty regarding a near and future good, remains almost stunned, without hearing anything said to them or using any sense, being abstracted by that thought. Therefore, the present sonnet shows these amorous effects; by subordinating to such amorous thoughts all the things that are commonly most pleasing and sweet to men, it clearly demonstrates how great is the sweetness of amorous <term>cogitation<note>original: "cogitazione"; a term from contemplative philosophy meaning deep, concentrated reflection or the focused activity of the mind.</note></term>. 

It says, therefore, to leave to whoever wants them the pomp, high honors, and public magnificence, such as squares, temples, and other public buildings; through this, he denotes the ambitious and those who seek honor with the greatest effort. He says further that whoever wishes should seek civil delicacies, and by this he denotes all human pleasures and <term>voluptuousness<note>original: "voluttá corporale"; the physical or sensory pleasures of the body.</note></term>. He adds treasure, showing the love and pursuit of money, because our appetite extends only to these three things: ambition, bodily pleasure, and <term>avarice<note>original: "avarizia"; the excessive desire for wealth or "utility."</note></term>; for honor, pleasure, and utility impede every other operation of ours. 

He follows this by showing what things help and nourish amorous thoughts: namely, a <term>little green meadow<note>original: "verde praticello"; the use of the diminutive "-ello" emphasizes the humble, peaceful quality of nature.</note></term> full of beautiful flowers, and a <term>little stream<note>original: "rivolo"</note></term> that bathes the flowers and grass around the place where it flows, and the amorous songs of some <term>little bird<note>original: "augelletto"</note></term>. 

And here it should be noted that, in opposition to the pomp, great buildings, and other things described with grand and magnificent words, are set all small things called by diminutive terms, such as "little meadow," "little streams," and "little birds," to better prove that if the aforementioned great things are accompanied by a thousand harsh thoughts and a thousand pains, these small things, on the contrary, must induce more tranquil and quiet thoughts. It follows then that forests, mountains and stones, caves, wild beasts, and some timid nymph are things favorable to these thoughts of love, to show in effect that <term>solitude<note>original: "solitudine"</note></term> and distancing oneself from human company renders the mind more quiet and does not force one's thoughts. And thus, not being forced, they easily return to nature, and immerse themselves all the more in the imagination of what most...

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...desire and love; and then imagination has such power that it shows to the eyes whatever it desires, and to me, the world showed the <note>original: "luci"; literally "lights," a common poetic metaphor for eyes.</note>lights—that is, the eyes of my lady—as if I saw her alive and real. But, in the city, one care after another took this sweetness from me, which truly is very great. And, if this were not proven by any other reason, it is proven by this: that the sweetness of the imagination has some similarity to true <term>beatitude<note>original: "beatitudine"; the supreme blessedness or happiness of heaven.</note></term>, namely that which the soul attains when it is given eternal glory, which is enjoyed in no other way than by imagining and contemplating divine goodness. And, although this contemplation is quite different from human contemplation—because the former contemplates the truth and the latter a vain imagination formed by mortal appetite—nonetheless, one and the other have some small similarity in the world. And, as imperfect as this mortal version is, it is approved as the primary happiness of the world when its object is true perfection and goodness, according to what can be achieved in this mortal life. For this reason, it can be said that the contemplation of any thing that is not troublesome has in itself great sweetness, because it shares some part of similarity with the highest sweetness and perfect happiness. In the present sonnet, it must be presupposed that it was composed in the city, because by saying "Here it is taken from me," etc., as one reads in the last verse, it is necessary to understand "here" as being in the city; one must also presuppose some fresh pleasure, either of contemplation or something else, received in rugged and solitary places, by which comparison one yearns for country villas and holds cities in hatred.

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

> — Therefore, what desire moves you, what fate
> makes you both fear and want that which undoes you?
> Who moves or guides the slow and rare step?
> — Nature teaches us to fear death,
> but Love then wonderfully makes
> sweet for his own that which is bitter to every other.

One reads in Homer, that ancient and most excellent Greek poet, that Jove <note>original: "Giove"; the Roman name for the king of the gods, Zeus.</note>, when he wishes to send to men in the world the fate that is suited to each, has two very large vessels. One of these is full of adverse and unhappy fates; in the other, happy and unhappy fates are mixed together in confusion. And, wishing to send someone an evil fate, he takes from those in the vessel which contains only adverse fates. Wishing to make someone happy, he sends to them from the other vessel, in which the adverse and prosperous fates are mixed. This is to signify that men can easily be unhappy without any share of happiness, but they cannot truly be happy without some share of misery. 

And, if the authority of such an excellent poet—who was called **"divine"**—were not enough to confirm such a true <term>maxim<note>original: "sentenzia"; a formal statement of opinion or a philosophical truth.</note></term>, the experience of human affairs provides abundant testimony. We follow this truth again in the present sonnet. Having verified two <term>maxims</term> in the three preceding ones—namely the happiness and unhappiness of amorous thoughts—it seems appropriate in the present sonnet to show that amorous happiness and unhappiness are very often joined and entangled together. Indeed, they are almost always in company, even if one or the other might have greater power. 

Nor does this occur only in amorous matters, but also in natural ones, and commonly in all events that befall men. Regarding natural things, we see all things that live in the world consist of opposites and live through the <term>conflict of humors<note>original: "contrarietá d'umori"; in Renaissance science, health and life were maintained by the balance between opposing bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.</note></term>. They are composed of elements that, individually, would greatly harm the nature of that specific thing. And, if it were not for the restraint of these contrary humors, nothing would live in this lower world. Therefore, it can be said that all mortal animals—whether vegetative, sensitive, or rational—do not

<summary>Lorenzo uses Homer’s myth of Jove's two jars to argue that human happiness is never pure but always mixed with sorrow, a duality reflected in both the experience of love and the biological "humors" that sustain life.</summary>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s philosophical commentary. Following the discussion of Homer’s jars of fate (where good is always mixed with evil), Lorenzo transitions here from mythology to the medical and psychological theories of his time to explain why human existence is inherently defined by conflict and "contrariety."</meta>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...live by the benefit of the <term>humors<note>The four humors (blood, yellow bile, black bile, and phlegm) were the fluids believed to govern human health and temperament. In Renaissance medicine, health required a perfect balance of these elements.</note></term> of which they are composed, but in spite of them and against their will; because each humor naturally desires to overcome its opposites, and as soon as this natural desire in any one of them takes effect, and one overcomes the other, death necessarily follows; and life is preserved as long as an equal power and the war between one and the other lasts. Therefore, we shall say that our life consists of opposition, contrariety, and various evils, and that death proceeds from peace. It is proven then, by this, that life—which among mortals is considered one of the primary goods—always has this conflict of the elements as a companion.

As for the events of the world and what most often happens to men, it is quite manifest that it is either pure evil without any share of good, or good mixed with much evil. And, although it does not seem to me that this proposition needs any confirmation, nonetheless, by distinguishing human operations into mental and bodily ones, I believe it is easy to understand that our mind and intellect always have the senses and bodily passions as opposites and enemies (as is fitting, since the intellect and the body are by nature very contrary); bodily passions and appetites always have the remorse of conscience, which proceeds from the intellect, as an obstacle. 

And, beyond this, often—indeed almost always—one passion is contrary to another, and one appetite to another; for it must be so, since human passions proceed largely from the humors of which we are composed, which, as we have said, are directly contrary to one another. We see also, in civil, private, and domestic affairs, that the difficulty of making any decision arises from the fact that every choice involves some inconvenience, nor can one find once in a thousand times a true deliberation that cannot be contradicted. And therefore those who are more prudent delay longer in making a decision, and because of this slowness they are called <term>grave men<note>original: "uomini gravi"; describing men of weight, dignity, and serious deliberation whose slow pace was seen as a sign of wisdom.</note></term>. And time is called "most wise," because true wisdom consists in waiting for and using the right opportunity; and this would not be necessary, if not for the great difficulty that the deliberations at hand carry with them. Thus it is verified that every human action is not absolutely good nor sweet without...

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...participation in misery. And this is recognized much more in those things governed by passion and appetite, such as matters of love. For as we said in the commentary on the sonnet that begins: <note>original: "In qual parte andrò io"</note> "In what place shall I go," etc., Love is nothing other than a gentle passion. This same sentiment is confirmed by the present sonnet, which is composed as a dialogue. For in the first <term>quatrain<note>A four-line stanza of a sonnet.</note></term> the sonnet speaks to my tearful eyes; in the second quatrain, which begins: "Woe to us," the eyes respond; then in the first <term>tercet<note>A three-line stanza of a sonnet.</note></term>, "What desire then," etc., the sonnet again speaks to the eyes; in the final tercet which begins: "Nature," the eyes respond once more. 

Returning then to the beginning, it is necessary to assume that my eyes were occupied by heavy and continuous weeping; and for this reason it seemed a marvel, since they were very close to her, and having the angelic face of my lady almost present before them—in the vision of whom their happiness seemed to consist, as we said in the sonnet that begins: <note>original: "Occhi, io sospiro"</note> "Eyes, I sigh," etc. For this reason, it seemed reasonable first to comfort the eyes to put an end to the weeping, because they would soon see my lady, who could be said to be almost present. 

And yet, as the eyes persevered in weeping, it is very appropriately asked why they still weep and for what reason the heart remains in the breast all fearful and full of suspicion. The eyes respond to this proposal, showing that their weeping proceeds from the doubt they have regarding the power of my lady’s eyes, which they call a <term>basilisk<note>Lorenzo uses the archaic spelling "bavalischio." In Renaissance lore, the basilisk was a legendary reptile reputed to kill with a single glance.</note></term>, which is said to have the nature of killing solely with the look of its eyes. 

And therefore, just as he kills with his eyes alone, so my eyes—doubting they can endure the gaze of my lady, who, if she were to look fixedly at them, would either make <term>stones<note>original: "priete," an archaic Tuscan form of "pietre" (stones).</note></term> of my eyes as she would the rest of my body, or it would be necessary for the soul to breathe its last and life to depart—one sees that these two doubts shown by my eyes are founded on the experience of things that have already <term>occurred<note>original: "sute," an archaic form of "state" (been/occurred).</note></term>. For, as for turning to stone, one reads of Medusa, as we have said; as for death, we similarly have the example of the basilisk. Having thus resolved the first doubt and shown the just cause for weeping, another arises. And this is that, granted such suspicion is just, the...

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...my eyes were bound to flee the sight of my lady as a mortal thing <note>In the tradition of courtly love, the lady's beauty is often described as so overwhelming that it "kills" or overwhelms the senses of the beholder.</note>, and yet, continuing on the path to see her, it was necessary for them to justify whether desire or fate led them—fearing and desiring the same thing at once. And in this desire and fear, the aforementioned mixture of bitterness with sweetness is shown; for fear presupposes bitterness, and desire presupposes sweetness. The poet says "desire" or "fate" because men are sometimes moved by their own natural desire, and at other times are forced, as if by destiny. As we read: "The Fates lead the willing, but drag the unwilling" <note>original: "*Fata volentem ducunt, nolentem trahunt*"; a Latin maxim often attributed to the philosopher Seneca, meaning that those who accept destiny go willingly, while those who resist are dragged by it regardless.</note>. Indeed, through experience, we often see men, through their own choices, do many things that go against their own will.

Which desire, then, or which fate moves the "slow and rare step"? In these two descriptors of the step, both will and fear are shown at the same time in the act of walking. For if there were desire without fear, the step would be quick and efficient; if there were fear without desire, there would be no step at all, nor any movement toward the thing that was feared. This is because fear, by its nature, causes one to flee, since we naturally hate what we fear, and we flee what we hate. 

To this point the eyes respond, showing that the cause of their fear is very natural, since by nature everyone fears death. They explain that the cause of their continuing forward nonetheless is **Love**, who—not by any natural reason, but miraculously—makes that which is bitter and most harsh to everyone else seem sweet to lovers. And truly, this is described as "miraculous," because everything that goes against the order of nature is a wonder; and nothing could be more opposite to the order of nature than the desire for death, for tears, for sighs, and for the other passions of love.

We shall conclude from this that lovers are the most miserable of all men. This is not only because of a common fate—which, as we have said, all human things share by always being a mixture of evil—but also for a particular reason: lovers never possess any good, neither through their own nature nor through sharing it with others. This is because the greatest "amorous sweetnesses" do not seem to consist of anything other than what all other men call the "supreme evil" <note>This refers to the "sweet pain" of love, where the lover finds pleasure in the very things (like longing or suffering) that others avoid.</note>. Yet, for lovers, it is enough...

<summary>Lorenzo de’ Medici explains the psychological paradox of love, where the lover is driven by both fear and desire. He argues that Love is "miraculous" because it reverses natural human instincts, leading lovers to find sweetness in suffering and death.</summary>

<keywords>desire, fate, fear, Love, death, paradox of love, bittersweet, nature vs. passion, Seneca</keywords>

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...to taste a happiness that seems their own, because human contentment consists more in appearing than in being. And, if it appears to them that they are happy, they are—though never without a constant mixture of unhappiness and amorous cares <note>The word "cares" (Italian: *cure*) here carries the Latin sense of *cura*, meaning both devotion and the anxious pain that accompanies love.</note>. For this reason, I judge that the sweetness of lovers is rare, and occasionally quite great, but their unhappiness is almost continuous, and their pain, beyond comparison, greater; for pain is often without sweetness, but sweetness is never without pain. And so it must be, where there is infinite passion and insatiable appetite.

Because in the preceding sonnet we made some mention of the miracles of love, I wish I possessed such skill that I could make them believable to anyone, just as they are certain to the most noble minds of those in love. And truly, just as it can be considered a great defect to lightly believe those things which at first glance <note>original: "*prima facie*"; a Latin term meaning "on the first appearance" or "at first sight."</note> seem impossible, so too it does not seem right to approve the opinion of those who grant no faith to anything that exceeds, in some part, either common usage or the natural order. For we have often seen the greatest inconveniences arise from presupposing a thing to be false because it seems nearly impossible, when it nonetheless turns out to be true. And, besides this, just as believing too quickly seems the mark of a superficial man, so to absolutely refuse to believe demonstrates great presumption.

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...because whoever says "this thing cannot be" presumes to know all the things that can be, and the extent of the power of nature. And yet, many different and almost incredible natural effects are seen, which would be unbelievable if they were not very well known to almost everyone. And who would believe that from a tiny grape seed <note>Italian: *acino*. Lorenzo is likely referring to the seed or the potential within the grape from which a whole vine grows.</note>, in which no certain color, smell, or taste is visible, the vine with so many noble qualities would be generated? The same applies to other seeds, which all maintain their own species in different ways; and these things do not seem miraculous only because they are seen at every hour.

And it seems to me that those natural effects seen every hour are a greater marvel than some other things which, because they are very rare and far from our knowledge, seem miraculous. Such as some species of animals that, being unknown to us, we judge almost impossible to exist; and perhaps in the countries that produce them, they are as common as dogs, horses, and other similar animals are to us. One reads of those six wonders that our poet Petrarch places in that song beginning: "The more diverse and new" <note>This refers to Petrarch’s *Canzone* 323, *Qual piú diversa e nuova cosa*, which describes six visions of beautiful but fleeting things, such as a phoenix and a gold vessel, symbolizing the death of his beloved Laura.</note>, found among ancient and authentic authors. And whoever considers well both those and the other things predicted as miracles will see, if one may say so, a much greater effort of nature in these things we have before our eyes every hour than in those which we admire more because they are rare than because they are impossible.

Therefore, amorous miracles should be—if not fully believed to exist—at least believed to be possible. And it seemed right to me to make this preparation in the exposition of the present sonnet, having to narrate something that perhaps seems impossible and yet is true; because the sonnet intends only to prove how the desire for death is the immediate cause of life. 

And, to come to the point, it is necessary to understand that my most gentle lady had the habit of often having "death" upon her lips, and showed in her words a longing for it. I believe, knowing herself to be so noble, that this life seemed tedious to her, nor was it worthy of such a beautiful thing; and, having been present sometimes when she most sweetly called upon death, I felt as much bitterness and pain as the fear of loss <note>Italian: *privazione*, referring here to the ultimate loss of the beloved through death.</note> would give to anyone...

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...of all her well-being. Because it seemed to me that she called her <note>Lorenzo refers to Death as a feminine entity here (*lei*), following the Italian *la morte*.</note> so sweetly and with such effective words, that death could not be denied her; the cause of this desire of hers added more to my grief, which was love, as she called upon death in her amorous sighs. And for this reason, the cause of this desire had to be either a great bitterness and passion or a supreme sweetness. For both of these effects cause similar desires in men: because death is craved either to escape from pain or so that no bitterness may occur to contaminate a supreme sweetness and happiness, following that maxim: *“Then it is beautiful to die”* <note>original Latin: *“Tunc pulchrum esse mori.”* This sentiment suggests that dying at the height of one's happiness preserves that joy from being ruined by future misfortune.</note>.

Whatever the cause was, it gave me the greatest affliction, especially regarding what I might have been blamed for, since Love <note>Italian: *Amore*. Here personified as the force driving the lady’s sighs.</note> was the cause of this desire. And, struggling with this passion, I finally resolved upon a single remedy: that I too would accompany my lady in this most harsh desire for death; and so this desire kindled within me so much that it began to seem sweet to me, in a way that sweetened all my other passions.

And, because one naturally appetites and follows that which pleases most, my heart abandoned all other thoughts and set aside every other desire and care to follow this most sweet and noble desire for death. And, although all thoughts of a single thing, when they are intense and vehement, commonly cause all other cares to be postponed, the thought of death achieves this effect much better: because every other thought sets aside other lesser thoughts, not forever, but for a time, because while living they can return—indeed, it is necessary that they return, at least those brought about by the necessity of life; but the thought of death must alienate the mind from everything else, because after death there is nothing to think about regarding the body and the world. For this reason, it is said that every other desire and passion, and all the torments <note>Italian: *martíri*. In the poetic tradition of this era, these are the "martyrdoms" or intense emotional sufferings of the lover.</note> and anxieties that are felt, were extinguished in the heart upon the arrival of this sweet desire for death; and all these passions being gone, and only the sweet thought of death remaining, life took vigor from it and breathed a little: for it was necessarily fitting that it be so, once they were extinguished...

<summary>Lorenzo explains how his lady's desire for death, driven by love, led him to seek the same fate. He argues that the thought of death is uniquely powerful because it clears the mind of all worldly anxieties and "martyrdoms."</summary>

...its enemies <note>Referring to the conflicting emotions or "passions" within the soul.</note>, and there remained in it only that most sweet desire—that is, a desire that pleased the soul and, in pleasing it, gave it strength and, against its own inclination, prolonged life. I do not mean "against its inclination" in the sense of its natural drive for life, but rather against its specific desire for death. And although this might have caused the soul some distress—being the opposite of the sweetness of that desire—nevertheless, as my lady lived (as we shall explain) and remained alive, for this very reason it caused no distress at all. Instead, it brought greater contentment, because the true desire of my heart was the life of my lady. It is proven, therefore, that the desire for death—which my lady often invoked—thus preserved life within me. This same desire of hers also preserved life in her, since that desire caused her to call upon death with her most sweet words. Death, hearing itself called, did not for this reason close my lady's beautiful eyes, but out of pity for her, prolonged her life; and so life was preserved both in her and in me. And this preservation was caused by a desire contrary to life, namely, the desire for death, which excluded death; that is, in the ways we have described, it caused death to draw back. These miracles and many others we have seen performed by Love, and we believe they will be quite credible among noble hearts <note>Italian: *gentili cuori*. In the courtly love tradition, "nobility" refers to the refinement and sensitivity of the soul rather than social rank.</note>, whose testimony should even be trusted by others.
