<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s prose commentary on his sonnets. Having previously discussed how the "miracle" of Love allowed a desire for death to preserve life, he now transitions into a psychological analysis of how the image of the beloved transforms the lover’s internal state from one of suffering to one of gratitude.</meta>

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

I was all alone and without any company except for my loving thoughts. These thoughts, bothering me as they usually do most of the time, led me to consider making a complaint to Love <note>original: *Amore*. Here personified as the classical deity or the governing force of his emotions.</note>, as he was the cause of my tears and sighs and other pains of love. And, wanting to recount them to him one by one, it was necessary for me to begin with that part which was first and most offended: the heart. Therefore, wanting to describe the affliction of the heart, it seemed necessary to look into the heart, and while looking, to consider its state so that I might describe it. 

And even though many passions and torments were depicted in the heart, the image of my lady’s face had made an even greater impression upon it. Since her face was most beautiful—and as it truly was, very shining and clear both because of its beauty and its light—it drew my eyes and forced them to gaze upon that image, taking away from them the sight of the heart's pains. It seemed very fitting that a beautiful and shining thing should elevate the vision above other things—as is the nature of excessive light—and draw the eyes to itself, as beauty always tends to do. Therefore, as my eyes looked upon this image, instead of pains, it seemed to them very beautiful and sweet—that is, full of pity <note>original: *pietà*. In the courtly tradition, this implies the lady's potential for mercy or kindness toward the lover.</note>. 

And so, if the eyes’ first intention was to see the heart’s affliction (a bothersome and deformed thing) in order to lament, then by seeing my lady’s face—so beautiful, full of pity, and the direct opposite of those afflictions—there had to be born an effect entirely contrary to lamenting. For this reason, the first thought of complaining died in shame and was completely extinguished, and another contrary thought was born: to thank and honor my lady. She was so beautiful and so noble <note>original: *gentile*. Referring to nobility of spirit and spiritual refinement.</note> that, simply by being granted the sight of such a beautiful thing, even if there had never been any pity in her, I could have no reason to complain, but rather to thank her.

The thought of complaining was moved by passion, which blinds the mind and overshadows the intellect with a dark ignorance; but, when the light of truth arrives and these shadows are put to flight, one looks back at the past error not without shame. Thus, the first thought dies in shame, and in its place succeeds the other, truer and more laudable thought: to thank my lady and to exalt her.

<vocab>Love, heart, beauty, pity, intellect, truth, sonnets, commentary</vocab>

<summary>Lorenzo explains how his initial intention to complain about his suffering was abandoned when he looked into his heart and found the radiant image of his lady, which replaced his sorrow with gratitude. He describes this as a victory of the "light of truth" over the "dark ignorance" of passion.</summary>
<keywords>Love, beauty, pity, intellect, truth, sonnets, commentary, Lorenzo de' Medici, courtly love, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s psychological and philosophical commentary on his own poetry. ## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

and praise her; which praises, being directed toward her image that is within my heart, make her appear much more beautiful and more full of pity <note>original: *piatosa*. In the courtly love tradition, "pity" or "mercy" refers to the lady’s willingness to look kindly upon the lover’s devotion.</note>; for so it appears to my thought, which sees nothing except this image. And, because we said above that the eyes see the heart and the things that are within it—which are otherwise invisible—it is said here that thought, which has no power of sight, gazes upon the image of my lady. And, to resolve both of these obscurities, one must understand what is meant by "eyes and seeing" or "thoughts and imagining"; because the eyes, the ears, the tongue, and every sense attributed to the heart are nothing other than thoughts, by means of which the heart—that is, our mind—imagines and acts, just as the body does through its physical senses. And therefore all other bodily operations, such as speaking and hearing, which that internal image performs, must be understood as works of the imagination. And, understanding it this way, what we have said is proven: that by hearing that image being praised, she becomes more beautiful and merciful. For the stronger the imagination is, the more it seems to see what it is imagining; and in imagining my lady as merciful and beautiful, it is necessary that the more one imagines her, the more beautiful and merciful she becomes within the thought. From such an imagination of such great beauty and sweetness is born a most ardent and new desire in the mind to see my lady alive and real. He does not say "new desire" because this is the very first desire I ever had in my heart to see my lady, but he calls it "new" in relation to those other thoughts, as if it were reborn then and there. This new desire, then, moves me to see my lady alive and real, because speaking, hearing, and breathing are the functions of a living animal and not of a thing that is merely imagined. With this desire, then, I return to see the shining and sweet rays of the eyes of my lady; and, by saying "I return," I show that the desire is not new—that is, not the first I ever had to see her—because to "return" to see her presupposes having gone to see her at other times. And, by saying "rays, both shining and sweet," one demonstrates the beauty and pity that was first present in that mental image, which, by its resemblance to the truth, moved me to see that most beautiful thing of which the image was a most sweet example. one effect. First, the thought of grieving, which dying in shame, gives birth to the second: the thought of thanking and praising my lady, imagining her as beautiful and full of pity <note>In the courtly love tradition, "pity" (*pietá*) refers to the lady’s potential to show mercy or favor to her suitor.</note>. From this is born the third thought: to go and see the real woman, based on the likeness of the one I imagined. Following these three thoughts comes the effect of putting into execution what the final thought proposed.

> My Lady, I see in your beautiful eyes
> a longing wandering, sweet and full of love,
> which Love keeps hidden from all others,
> but kindly shows to me.
> This noble desire seems to speak,
> promising my heart peace and rest:
> this is confirmed by a warm and merciful sigh,
> which Love gave as a companion in good faith.
> This sigh carries news to my heart
> of that pity, which from your white breast
> sends it forth as a messenger from your heart.
> Having reached your beautiful mouth, it forms
> pious and beautiful words of such sweet effect,
> that it leaves Love himself—let alone others—stunned in wonder.

Of all our senses, without any controversy, the most noble and esteemed is sight; and this is not only the judgment of men, but also of nature. This is evident because nature has placed the eyes higher than any other sense and closer to the place where the intellect resides <note>Lorenzo here follows the Aristotelian and Neoplatonic tradition which argued that sight is the most "intellectual" of the senses because it functions through light and distance, rather than physical contact like touch or taste.</note>. It is clearly known that the eyes are more necessary to human life than any of the other senses, because it seems that through the knowledge of visible things, one proceeds to the other senses much more easily. The eyes are also the reason we can know the most beautiful thing the senses can perceive, which is light; for neither smell, nor taste, nor any voice or other sensory thing can be compared to light. The eyes also have this privilege and excellence over the other senses: that the heart does not manifest itself through any other sensory medium, but keeps its concepts almost secret from all others, and reveals them only through the eyes. For the eyes very often give signs of joy and sorrow, anger and love, and all the other passions of the heart.

## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...a very clear indication. This sense of sight is so close to the qualities of our soul that, according to Pliny <note>Referring to Pliny the Elder, a Roman author who wrote the *Natural History*. This specific sentiment refers to the idea that the eyes are the "windows to the soul."</note>, anyone who kisses a person’s eyes feels almost as if they are kissing their soul. And, although this occurs in all the passions, it is recognized much better in the effects of love, in which the eyes play a very great part. Because the eyes are the starting point from which Love both exits and enters; they are in themselves the most beautiful part of the human body, and they have beauty as their object. Therefore, as they are the most beautiful feature of a beautiful woman, I believe they are most often the first thing that begins to be loved by the lover’s eyes. And so, if Love enters the heart from the eyes of the loved one and through the eyes of the lover—which confirms that the eyes are both active and passive <note>original: "active e passive"</note> principles of love—then, since Love makes his first impression in the eyes and opens the way to the heart through them, the heart communicates its amorous passions to the eyes much more easily than it does to the other senses. Love has provided this remedy for the affliction of lovers: that, when speech and every other way of understanding one another’s hearts is taken away, they often understand each other through the eyes and through loving gazes.

My lady was, as we have said, beautiful above all others, and therefore one can imagine how beautiful her eyes were, which, as we have said, surpass any physical beauty. And, because our appetite always seeks most that which seems best to it, even though my lady was loved by me in her entirety, my eyes were still drawn to look at her eyes as the greater beauty. I looked fixedly then at her beautiful eyes, and it seemed to me that I saw in them a longing for love full of pity <note>In the courtly love tradition, "pity" (*pietà*) refers to the lady’s capacity for mercy or kindness toward her suitor, rather than sorrow.</note> and sweetness, which through them intended to make me understand her most noble heart. Love showed this sweetest desire only to my eyes, hiding it from others—I believe because the others did not gaze upon them so fixedly; nor was the path between my lady and them so cleared by Love through the eyes as it was between her heart and mine, as we have said above. And, besides this, since it was Love who showed me this desire of my lady, which was...

## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...standing in the middle between her and me, others could not see <note>referring to Love personified, who acts as a mediator visible only to the lover</note>, because Love was not present between them and her to reveal it. It seemed to me that this noble desire spoke to my heart and promised it—after so many anxieties and amorous persecutions—peace and rest. This assumed that the past war was the price for future peace, and that labor and amorous anxieties were the price for rest and quiet; for her eyes displayed all these sweetest effects. 

And, my lady fearing that, based on past examples, I might not fully trust the words her eyes were telling me, she accompanied this merciful desire with a loving <term>sospiro</term> <note>a sigh; in Renaissance poetry, sighs were seen as physical messengers of the soul's state</note>. This sigh, sent as a messenger to my heart, issued forth from my lady’s white breast as a witness to the <term>pietá</term> <note>often translated as "pity," but here meaning a blend of compassion, mercy, and grace shown by the lady toward her lover</note> that was within her—for it was that very same compassion that had placed the loving sigh in her heart. 

I have already explained the natural cause of sighs in the exposition of the sonnet beginning: *“If the fortunate heart”* <note>original: "Se ’l fortunato cor"</note>, etc., so it does not seem necessary to say more on that here. However, one must understand that this sigh was born in the heart, which drew air into itself through the breath to cool itself <note>referring to the Aristotelian and medieval medical theory that the heart is a source of intense heat and requires air to regulate its temperature</note>. Before this breath was exhaled and breathed out, it formed certain sweetest and loving words in my lady’s mouth, so that both the words and the sigh seemed to issue from that beautiful mouth at the exact same time. 

Because my lady felt that perhaps neither the sign of the eyes nor the testimony of the sighs was sufficient proof of her compassion and love, she added the testimony of words. This is a much more effective witness than the previous two; she did this so that my heart might have greater certainty, both through the effectiveness of the testimony and through the sufficient number of witnesses, there being three in total <note>the eyes, the sigh, and the spoken word</note>. 

My lady’s words were so merciful and beautiful, and of such a sweet effect, that <term>Amore</term> <note>Love, personified as a deity</note> himself remained stupefied by them. From this, one can imagine what happened to me. Nor should anyone who believes this be surprised if I do not formally recount the words themselves; for, overcome by the same stupor that conquered Love, I forgot not only the words, but almost myself. 

In my judgment, the progression of this sonnet is quite natural and true to life: for he who loves first makes some sign with the eyes, then by necessity the sigh is born, because the pleasure of seeing the beloved thing and that firm intention of seeing...

## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...generates a sigh for the reasons stated in the previously mentioned sonnet, and sighing demonstrates a greater <term>veemenzia</term> <note>vehemence or intensity</note> of love than merely looking. Words follow the sigh, and they are all the more effective as they bring us closer to the certainty of the matter. While looks and sighs could stem from reasons other than they appear, words reveal the truth more clearly and are driven by a greater force of love. Thus, nature produces its effects step by step.

Having shown in the previous sonnet how excellent the eyes are among the other senses, and what great dignity Love has bestowed upon them—wishing them to be the gate through which he enters and often making them his ministers and messengers of the heart's thoughts—one must confess that lovers derive the greatest sweetness from the eyes. And if this is true, the deprivation of them is an almost unbearable torment for one who loves; indeed, it would be entirely unbearable if Love had not provided a single remedy: to succor the heart in this case through <term>pensieri</term> <note>thoughts; in the Platonic tradition Lorenzo follows, the "thought" or imagination allows the lover to see the beloved's image internally when she is physically absent</note>. This remedy, however, acts no differently than other amorous aids, which are more like fuel and wood for the fire of love than a cooling relief for the heart. ## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

Furthermore, regarding this deprivation of the beloved eyes, the lover has prayed for the help of his thoughts against this suffering. He has placed the image of the beloved thing inside his heart, which represents her to his thoughts when his eyes are deprived of her. Love therefore placed, according to his custom, the beautiful image of my lady in my heart—whether by grace or by a power present in my heart. This was either through a particular grace of love toward him, which made him worthy of such a noble image, or through his own virtue, he having already become <term>gentile</term> <note>refined or noble of heart, a key concept in Renaissance love poetry where "nobility" refers to spiritual merit rather than social rank</note>. When this image entered the heart, it extinguished and drove out all other impressions that might have been in my heart through any other desire, so that only the beautiful image of my lady remained there.

On the day I composed this sonnet, I had spent much time and many steps trying to see my lady's eyes, and certainly in vain, for I never had the grace of seeing them that day. I sought, therefore, with my tearful lights <note>original: "luci"; a poetic term for the eyes</note>, the lights I had lost—that is, the eyes of my lady, which I could not find; because of this, I felt a truly intolerable torment. But, since it was not possible for it to be otherwise, I turned to that unique remedy Love had granted me; and, ceasing to search for my lady with my physical eyes, I took refuge in searching for her with my thought. I asked my thought for my salvation—that is, that it might at least show me my lady, because it was in its power to show her to me, as she never hides from it, for thought sees her always.

My prayers were kindly heard by my thought, and immediately it showed me my lady alone. In the center of my heart there were no other thoughts, as we said above; indeed, there could be no others because, the center of the heart being the foundation of thoughts—just as the center is the foundation of the earth and the whole world—no thought could be established unless it were rooted in my lady. Any other thoughts the heart might have formed, if indeed it could have, would have been like all things that lack a foundation. My lady, then, was in the center of my heart, and around her were all my desires; this confirms that my thoughts thought of nothing else, nor did my desire crave other things. And naturally, the place and source of desires is the heart, through the <term>concupiscible</term> <note>from the Latin "concupiscibilis," referring to the faculty of the soul that deals with longing, appetite, and the attraction toward what is perceived as good or beautiful</note>, which is a virtue and power of the heart.

## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

Love came to the aid of the eyes' deficiency with thought, but this resulted in nothing but an accumulation of pain. For, as we said in the <term>commentary</term> <note>original: "comento"</note> of the <term>sonnet</term> <note>original: "sonetto"</note> that begins: "When I think," etc., the image of the beloved thing multiplies the desire for the real one. This happened again at that time, because from seeing my lady within my heart, a new and greater desire for my lady was kindled. And, because it seems impossible that my heart could resist such fire—that it would not consume itself and turn to ash while burning—the remedy is presented to make these wonders believable: the power of sighs. As we have said, these are naturally generated by the heart for its refreshment and to exhale against the suffocation that harms it due to the rush of the <term>vital spirits</term> <note>According to Renaissance medical theory, these were refined substances in the blood that acted as messengers between the soul and the body, facilitating movement and sensation.</note>.

If I could describe one by one the acts and amorous <term>accidents</term> <note>In this context, "accidents" refers to the specific qualities, events, or outward expressions of her character and beauty.</note> of my lady, certainly this amorous history of ours would receive much greater ornament, and my lady much more praise. Because truly every act of her life, even the smallest, has been worthy of being celebrated by me; and, having omitted a great part of them, I give as the only cause the abundance and plenty of things to describe. For it happened to me as to one who, being in the midst of a most pleasant meadow which produces flowers of various colors, and wishing to pick the most charming ones, does not know to which to first put his hand; because the quality of beauty makes the <term>choice</term> <note>original: "elezione"</note> more difficult, since our appetite is drawn most by those things that please us most. 

Being unable, therefore, to pick all the flowers from the excellent meadow of my lady, nor to pursue all her praises, and not knowing how to choose which first deserved to be gathered and celebrated by me, wandering by chance with my hand, I have gathered those first flowers which fate showed me, making fortune the judge rather than my own choice. And after she had, lying there for a while, contemplated the earth and the place where I was, having had some most sweet and amorous thoughts, and moved by that pity for my affliction, finally overcome by sleep, she drifted off. Sleep was aided by the shade of that oak and a sweet summer breeze which, by moving the branches of the oak and the other nearby trees, further nurtured that most sweet sleep with its murmuring. Understanding this amorous act, I judged it worthy of the two sonnets written above, of which the first contains the idea that, since nature granted sleep to human eyes, no sweeter sleep or more quiet repose ever closed a mortal eye, nor has sleep ever closed more beautiful eyes than those of my lady. 

What made that sleep sweetest of all was the shade, the <term>softness</term> <note>original: "mollizie"; refers to the physical comfort and yielding nature of the grassy spot.</note> of the place where she lay, the sweetness of the gentle wind, the murmuring of the trees—which necessarily arose from those things—and the fatigue she had previously endured; for these are all things that give strength to sleep. As for those eyes being as beautiful as we have said, I can assign no other reason than my own opinion, founded upon the effects they produced in me. And if they were so beautiful, it followed by necessity that **Love** <note>original: "Amore"; often personified as a deity or cosmic force in Renaissance poetry.</note> derived great power from them. And therefore, while they remained closed by sleep, and that amorous light was hidden from the world, the value and power of Love necessarily felt a great loss, because the sight of her gave and took away his strength, just as happens to certain species of flowers, which open when the sun rises and then close again at sunset, so that such plants are in bloom by day and deprived of their floral ornament by night. 

Thus we shall say that <term>noble hearts</term> <note>original: "cuori gentili"; a central concept in Italian lyric poetry, implying a heart refined and made "noble" by the capacity for virtuous love.</note> open themselves to receive amorous influences through the "sun" of the beloved's eyes; when those eyes are missing, the hearts would close. And so that they may never close, the power of Love makes such an impression by means of those eyes that they can say they are never "without the sun." Love, then, who makes his power felt through the eyes, would lose his power if that vision were lacking.

For the oak, being the tree of **Jupiter** <note>original: "Giove"; in Roman mythology, the oak is the sacred tree of the king of the gods, who traditionally strikes it with his lightning bolts.</note>, is struck by his bolts more often than other trees; the place of which, from the time it stood over those beautiful eyes, will henceforth rather be a sanctuary for amorous arrows, since those eyes, pleasing to **Love**, have consecrated it.

# II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...in such high and sweet thoughts, I could call myself most happy, because thinking is nothing other than a silent speaking <note>original: "tacito parlare"; a classical philosophical idea that internal thought is a form of unspoken dialogue with oneself.</note>, for he who thinks imagines those things and calls them by their names within himself; hence one can truly say that thinking is a silent speaking. My thought then wanders to all the other circumstances, such as the breeze—or, we might say, the light wind—and, as if giving thanks to it, shows the effect it produced. Because the breeze moved the branches which, by their position between the sun and her eyes, created shade, the shadows necessarily had to move as well; therefore, those eyes could sometimes see the sun, and sometimes not.

And, since these eyes were of such perfection and beauty that they held mastery over **Love** <note>original: "Amore"; here personified as the god Cupid or the force of desire.</note>, as we have said above, it was a glorious victory for sleep when it conquered such beautiful eyes. So that this victory might be perpetual and memorable, sleep ought to hang trophies there upon the tall oak, along with the spoils of the eyes it had already defeated, just as the ancient Romans used to do. It was their custom, when they conquered some powerful or famous enemy, to take his spoils and clothe the trunk of a tree with them as a memorial of the victory achieved. We must see what the "spoils" of those beautiful eyes were, to understand what sleep used to clothe the trunk of the oak. It cannot be interpreted that my lady’s eyes were clothed in anything other than beautiful and amorous gazes and a loving light, which usually allows itself to be seen only by the eyes of lovers. These gazes and loving light, then, certainly had to remain like marks on the trunk of the oak; sleep despoiled my lady of these as soon as it closed those beautiful eyes, and I believe that oak is still adorned with these spoils. Nor should **Love** be indignant at this triumph of sleep, if what we have said is true: that her eyes mastered **Love**, giving him and taking away his strength, and yet sleep has now overcome those very eyes.

->So many charming beauties has<-
->the noble face of my lady gathered in itself,<-
->that every new occurrence within it<-
->takes from it great beauty and worth.<-

It seems to me a very great proof of excessive power when some virtue <note>In this context, "virtue" (virtú) refers to an inherent power or efficacy, rather than just moral goodness.</note> operates powerfully in things that are contrary and different from each other, sometimes even producing effects that are almost outside the natural order of things. And, because this often happens in the life of lovers, we have called them above "miracles of love" <note>original: "miracoli amorosi"; a common trope in Renaissance Neoplatonism where the beloved's influence defies natural laws.</note>. The present sonnet intends to prove how very great was the power of my lady’s beauty through the diverse and extraordinary effects it had on me. Because, as I contemplated the beauty of her face in various occurrences and passions, it seemed to me that all the passions which appeared or showed themselves in that beautiful face became more beautiful and received more force—that is, they moved others more powerfully to either fear, pity, sorrow, or joy. They moved not only powerfully, as has been said, according to the quality of the passions, but they always maintained beauty and grace. In certain passions, such as fear and sorrow, it seems almost impossible for beauty and grace to be preserved, because whoever fears of necessity hates the cause of the fear. This same thing happens to whoever feels sorrow, because, if they could, they would flee the cause of it, and those things that are fled are not loved. And therefore, the power of this beauty was very great, having the strength, even while moving fear and sorrow, to still be desired and loved during such passions. Therefore, the present sonnet introduces only four passions: namely pity, wrath, sorrow, and joy, which take more strength and beauty from the face of my lady. And, beginning with pity, it shows that when pity comes into that beautiful face, it never found a place or home...

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici introduces a sonnet exploring how his lady's beauty remains perfect even when expressing conflicting emotions like anger or sorrow. He argues that her beauty is so powerful it creates "miracles of love," forcing the observer to love even the things (like her wrath) that should cause fear.</summary>

# II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...anyone, where she might seem more truly to be pity itself, nor where she might appear more sweet and pious. And, since pity is beautiful in its own right, it is enough to mention only the strength it acquires, presupposing its beauty.

Coming then to wrath, it was proper for her to burn with wrath and disdain; for wrath is nothing other than a kindling of anger around the heart, and the effects of wrath are commonly similar to those of fire, which performs its effects quickly, and those who are of a choleric <note>In Renaissance medicine, a "choleric" temperament was associated with the element of fire and a tendency toward anger.</note> and hot nature are more disposed to wrath. Therefore, when that beautiful face burns with wrath, it becomes more beautiful and "wicked" <note>original: "rio"; in this poetic context, it implies a formidable or cruel beauty that inspires awe.</note>—that is, more to be feared—as the following example shows: because Love trembling in her face is a manifest sign of the fear of that wrath's power. Furthermore, the fact that Love does not depart from that face despite his trembling (which shows his fear is very great) demonstrates quite clearly that it is her beauty that restrains him; for if this were not so, fear would drive Love away.

This same thing occurs in the sadness and sorrow of my lady, which moves even Love to tears. As he weeps, he affirms that her face is his kingdom and his empire, thereby showing the same strength and beauty in sorrow that was previously seen in wrath. From these premises, the conclusion of the sonnet follows very naturally; for if the beauty of that face had the power to appear more beautiful during those "accidents" <term>accidents: in philosophical terms, these are qualities or states (like emotions) that can change without altering the essential nature of a person.</term> which usually obscure and diminish beauty, then by strengthening these accidents that are normally opposite to beauty, it can much more easily increase in beauty during those accidents that naturally provide strength to beauty—especially when such accidents are further intensified, as happens with my lady’s joy.

My lady was in herself most beautiful; joy is, in itself, beautiful in any person. If, therefore, she is beautiful by nature and the "accident" is also beautiful, then the result was an excessive beauty when such a beautiful nature and such a beautiful accident were joined together. This assumes that both gained strength through such a union—as we said above regarding the other passions—and that the accident was in itself very strong and almost in its highest degree. This is shown by her laughter, which is the greatest sign of joy that humans can make, just as weeping is the sign of sorrow, which was similarly placed above as a sign of excessive grief. Believing, therefore...

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici provides a Neoplatonic analysis of how his lady's beauty remains supreme even through negative emotions like anger and sorrow, eventually reaching a peak of "excessive beauty" when she smiles.</summary>

...with such beauty and sweetness together, one can say this beauty in the world is not only marvelous, but perhaps never seen before; and therefore the world can truly be called blind <note>The "blind world" is a common poetic trope for those who lack spiritual or aesthetic perception.</note>, and it must produce in whoever sees it what can be called true joy and bliss.

Although in many and various ways my lady gave quite evident proof of her love and pity toward me, as we have already shown in several places, she gave none more effective, nor could she ever give one, than that which this present sonnet contains. Nor could I receive a greater gift from her, because there can be no greater gift than when one gives both what is theirs and what is most dear to the giver, according to Epictetus <note>Epictetus (c. 50–135 AD) was a Stoic philosopher who argued that the only thing truly "ours" is our will or internal judgment.</note>. Therefore, we can call nothing in the world "ours" except our opinion <note>original: "opinione"; in this Stoic context, it refers to the faculty of choice or the internal mind, rather than a casual "viewpoint."</note>, because all other things belong either to fortune or to nature. And that this is true is clear, because both nature and fortune often deprive us of them against our will. And so, without expanding upon many things, as such conclusions are very well-worn and proven, we shall confess that only opinion is ours, as has been said, which is always free and cannot be forced by anything; and, in my judgment, whoever mentions "opinion" necessarily presupposes...

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...the will, which is nothing other than the desire for that good which, in one's opinion, appears to be good. And for this reason it can be said that, although opinion and will are not the same thing, they are so similar, so close, and so necessarily joined to one another, that it is not improper for me to speak of one as the other; for these statements of mine are not formal definitions, but rather words spoken broadly and freely.

If, then, only our opinion and our will are truly our own, whoever gives such a thing gives all that they possess as their own; and whoever gives all they have necessarily gives something that is most dear to the giver, and therefore cannot make a greater gift. In these amorous verses, our "heart" is understood in a broad sense to mean our opinion and our will. Therefore, since my lady made an exchange <term>commutazione</term> <note>The term "commutazione" suggests a reciprocal swap or a mutual transformation, a common theme in Renaissance love philosophy where two lovers become one another.</note> of her heart for mine—that is, taking mine for herself and giving me hers, as the present sonnet shows—she could give me no greater gift, nor provide a more evident sign that I was filled with her grace. 

And because it would seem to be the height of arrogance on my part to persuade myself that this were true, making myself the author of and worthy of such a great good without the testimony of my lady, it is necessary for me to tell the truth of how this amorous process occurred. I do this both to avoid the charge of arrogance and for the contentment that the sweetest memory of that amorous act brings to my heart. 

I was in a place where I found myself quite close to my lady's face, and, gazing fixedly at her, due to the sweetness offered by her eyes—feeling almost worn down and weakened—I was supporting my head with my right arm. She, thinking to give me some comfort, approaching me more closely in a gentle manner, placed her white hand upon the left side of my chest; and, holding it there for some time, I asked her quite timidly what she intended to do. She, with an honest boldness, replied that she was listening to her own heart move. And I said to her: 

— Truly, this and every other thing that lives within me is yours. — 

She, continuing, said: 

— I say truly that this is the heart that once lived in me, which now lives in you, and that which was formerly yours, I keep within my breast. — 

How sweet these words seemed to me and what effect they had upon me, I leave to the judgment <note>The text cuts off here, leaving the reader to imagine the spiritual and emotional impact of this "miracle" of Love.</note>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS III

...to those to whom the flame and force of love are known; because, as Dante says in one of his songs <note>original: "canzona". Likely referring to the canzone "Donne ch'avete intelletto d'amore" from the *Vita Nuova*.</note>:

> No base heart has so high a wit,
> that it could imagine even a little of this.

After leaving her presence, and considering which was greater—the nobility of that speech, or the love she demonstrated through it—I decided to compose the present sonnet, and the two following ones with the same conceit <term>invenzione</term> <note>In Renaissance poetics, "invenzione" refers to the central idea or creative conceit of a poem.</note>, even though they reach different conclusions; for truly that amorous speech and that most noble act were worthy of a tongue other than mine to preserve their memory. I represent <term>fingo</term> <note>Lorenzo uses "fingo" (I feign/I fashion) to indicate he is creating a literary framework for the actual events.</note> therefore, although the history is as stated above, that I myself feel a new motion in my breast, and with some wonder I ask myself the cause; especially since, my own heart having fled from me (as we have said above in several places), the cause of that motion could not be my own heart. The motion, then, and my frequent sighs—which are naturally ordained for the cooling of the heart—showed clearly that a heart must be that which was moving in my breast. This was further demonstrated by the high and sweet thoughts conceived by my mind, which likewise had to be moved by the heart—not as the "place" of thoughts, but as their cause; because, since the heart is that which desires, those thoughts came from the heart, for they were nothing but a desire for my lady. And since the thoughts were high and sweet—that is, more worthy than what was fitting for me—I began to believe within myself that a cause more worthy than my own heart was moving them.

In the midst of these doubts of mine, Love <term>Amore</term> <note>Love is personified here as a guiding deity or force, a common trope in the Dolce Stil Novo and Petrarchan traditions.</note> came to my aid. Having been the one who had emboldened my heart to flee—as shown in that sonnet which begins: *“Alas for me, when I am there where she is,”* etc.—Love truly knew that my heart had fled. And so, with his own mouth, he revealed this truth to me; which, interpreting according to the truth (as we have said), means that Love is my lady, who with her own mouth revealed this amorous miracle to me. The miracle was this: when Love first made a path through the eyes of my lady, through which they entered the heart, then that...

In the courtly tradition, this implies both physical grace and a high-born, virtuous spirit.</note> hand entered behind the eyes into the breast and drew out my heart, as shown in the sonnet beginning: "Fair, beautiful and delicate hand" <note>Lorenzo refers here to Sonnet VII of his collection.</note>, and in place of my heart it placed that of my lady; and, because this seems a wondrous and unheard-of thing, Love added that this was a marvelous work of his power. And, truly considering it, Love is nothing other than a transformation of the lover into the beloved <note>A core idea of Neoplatonic philosophy: the lover's soul leaves their own body to live in the beloved.</note>; and, when it is reciprocal, the same transformation necessarily arises in the one who first loves, who then becomes the beloved, so that the lovers marvelously live within one another, for this exchange of hearts means nothing else.

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS
LORENZO THE MAGNIFICENT, *Works* - I. 8

...it seemed to me a thing far above my merits, and it seemed all the greater for being suddenly exalted from a humble place to such great bliss; and for this I considered myself most happy above all others. For all these reasons, I had to fear and deem it almost impossible to preserve myself for long in this happiness. And although the constancy and faith of my lady gave me no cause for doubt, it seemed to me at every hour that my lady’s heart, which lived in me—because Love had given it to me as a pledge for my own—wished to depart from me and leave my breast all alone. This doubt made me think of calling my heart back to me, praying that it would return; but, having been chosen for a greater good—that is, to stay in the snowy <note>original: "candido". In Renaissance poetry, this implies both physical whiteness/purity and moral innocence.</note> breast of my lady—it had become so worthy and so proud in this way that it held my own breast, where it used to stay, in contempt, nor did it wish to return to me. I, believing that the reason for this was that it believed it could stay in my lady’s breast forever, proposed to my heart, so that it might return, that when my lady’s heart no longer deigned to stay in my breast, her breast likewise would no longer deign to receive my heart; and from this it could happen that my heart would at once be deprived of my own breast by choice, and of my lady’s by necessity, once it were cast out by her. The heart responds to this doubt that, even if it were cast out by her, it will stay in a place from which it cannot be expelled: that is, in my lady’s eyes; because Love and she make it so that those eyes are common to everyone, there being in those eyes no sighs, no words, nor any other sign that proceeds from the heart, but only my lady’s glances, which will often bring me news of my heart, because her eyes will often be seen by me. It is necessary to understand the natural process of this sonnet, with which these amorous fictions must harmonize. Love is born in the lover and goes into the thing loved, and so first the lover’s heart flees to the beloved. Then Love is born reciprocally in the beloved, and then the exchange of hearts <note>original: "commutazione de' cori". This refers to the Neoplatonic idea that lovers literally swap souls or hearts.</note> that we have mentioned occurs. Then jealousy is born, the true misery of lovers, because it is an immortal torment; and then

## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

The doubt arises that the beloved’s heart may return to her, and from this comes a thought of withdrawing one’s love from the beloved, which is to call one’s heart back to oneself. But, because a lively love grows amidst hardships <note>original: "affanni." This term encompasses the anxieties, pains, and labors typical of the courtly love tradition.</note>, the lover cannot succeed in withdrawing his love; instead, out of necessity, he must continue in it. And, although he judges with great certainty that he can have no sweetness—but rather hardships and tribulations—since he is not loved by the beloved nor ever free from jealousy, he is finally driven by necessity to take what he can most easily obtain from the beloved. Not being able to possess her heart, his own heart does not leave the beloved, but instead settles in the beloved’s eyes—that is, he enjoys her external beauties and conforms himself to them, since he cannot dispose of her heart (meaning her love). In that moment, the glances of the beloved's eyes provide a sign of the love within her; for both pity and love, as well as disdain and anger, are sometimes understood through the sign of the eyes. We often have news of this; for the sight of the beloved can hardly be hidden from the eyes or become invisible, and love moves and incites the lover to see the beloved all the more often when other things that used to console the mind are lacking. I wish all these effects were better expressed in the sonnet, to remove every difficulty for those intellects <note>In the Renaissance sense, "intellects" refers to refined, educated readers capable of understanding complex poetic metaphors.</note> who shall deem my verses worthy of their study.

## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

Two contrary sayings are found written, yet both are frequently proven true in human actions. For it is said that the wretched easily believe what they desire, and, conversely, that "a wretched man believes not in great hope." <note>original: "a gran speranza uom misero non crede." This is a common Petrarchan sentiment reflecting the skepticism of the broken-hearted.</note> I believe that this diversity of opinion arises more from the nature of those who hope and desire something than from reason, assuming that either opinion has equal causes that do not, by themselves, incline more to one side than the other. And therefore I believe that those men who are <term>melancholic</term> <note>In the Renaissance, "melancholy" was one of the four humors (black bile). It was thought to make a person prone to sadness but also to deep intellectual insight and intense love.</note> by nature have less hope than others; and this is all the more true the more they have encountered such adverse fortune in their lives that they have achieved few things according to their desires.

We said at the beginning that every powerful love proceeds from a powerful imagination, and that such lovers are melancholic by nature. I confess to being one of those who have loved with the greatest fervor, and therefore, as a lover, I reasonably ought to doubt more than hope. Added to this is the fact that throughout my life—although I have achieved more honor and rank than was my due—I have still seen rare pleasures and few other things according to my desires; I speak of those things that our soul sometimes admits as a relief from public and private labors and dangers, even though I live most contentedly and am much satisfied with my lot.

I ought, then, for the reasons written in the preceding commentary and for these present ones, reasonably to doubt. And since suspicion—that greatest and most intolerable passion—had once been born in my heart, nature taught me to do everything to drive it away from me. And, doubting (as the previous sonnet clearly shows) whether my heart had been chased from my lady's breast, and not knowing well whether it was there or elsewhere, it seemed I ought to hear news of it from those who came from that same place; and, since sighs arise from the very place where their heart resides, they could tell me the truth. And therefore the present sonnet, composed as a dialogue, addresses and speaks to those <term>sighs</term> <note>In courtly poetry, sighs were often personified as messengers traveling between the lover and the beloved.</note> that issued from the lady's breast, which came immediately from my heart, if it was in that breast. And, to avoid confusion, it should be noted that in the first four lines I speak to the aforementioned sighs; in the second <term>quatrain</term> <note>original: "quadernario." The first four-line section of a sonnet.</note> the sighs respond to me; thereafter, everything...

# II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

In the ninth line and the beginning of the tenth—specifically that phrase which says: "From you"—I myself am speaking to the sighs; and in the following phrase where it says: "Yes, certainly," the sighs respond to me. For all the rest of the sonnet, I am speaking, partly to the sighs and partly through narration. Now, returning to the beginning, it should be noted that by speaking to my lady's sighs and calling them "amorous"—meaning moved by Love—there was (or I wished there to seem to be) some hope mixed with doubt, as is still shown. For by asking them to give me news of my heart, which they were sweetly nourishing in her breast, I already held the opinion both that my heart was there and that it was well treated by her.

And truly it is said that her sighs nourished my heart, because it remained in that breast where Love also resided, without whom my heart could not stay there. Therefore, the cause that moved the sighs truly nourished my heart sweetly and preserved it in that breast, because the sighs were moved by Love. The sighs respond that my heart remains happy, peaceful, and full of humility and sweetness, and that it is the cause of many sweet and amorous thoughts in my lady. With these thoughts and with Love, my heart often speaks of many high <term>amorous mysteries</term> <note>These "mysteries" refer to the complex, almost religious devotion and philosophical nuances of the *Dolce Stil Novo* (Sweet New Style) poetic tradition, where love is a path to spiritual refinement.</note> and very noble things.

Through this, it is shown that not only was my heart in that breast, but it already lived there like a member of the household and an intimate, since it understood all the thoughts of its lady—thoughts which others cannot understand, namely those who are not made worthy and <term>gentle</term> <note>original: "gentili." In Renaissance poetry, "gentle" refers to a nobility of soul and refinement of character produced by love, rather than mere social rank.</note> by Love, as my heart was. The sweetness that came to me through this longed-for news was as great as my previous doubt had been, as always happens with any hoped-for joy. 

And, almost not believing that what those amorous sighs had reported was possible, I ask them again if their report is true. They reply in confirmation with a very brief response, namely: "Yes, certainly." Nor could they answer at any greater length, as the remainder of the sonnet shows; because, when I posed a new question to them, the breath of those sighs was not sufficient to allow them to answer any further. And here it should be noted that everything spoken by the aforementioned sighs in this sonnet consists of only as many words as could be spoken...

# II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...naturally, one can comfortably speak with a single <term>spirit</term> <note>In the physiological theories of the time, "spirit" (*spirito*) referred to a subtle vapor or breath that carried life, sensation, and speech through the body.</note>—that is, without needing to catch one’s breath again. Therefore, once the force that carried the spirit of a sigh had spent itself, it was reasonable that no more words should be spoken. And, although I call them "sighs" in the plural—meaning more than one—you must imagine that while my lady’s sighs were many, a single one contained the response. It is the nature of one who has attained some great good to do everything possible to preserve it and make it lasting; and so, having received what I desired concerning the state of my heart, I still wished to understand how long-lasting and enduring this <term>bliss</term> <note>original: *beatitudine*. A term often used by poets of this era to describe the near-religious state of joy granted by the beloved.</note> of the heart was to be. I therefore asked the spirits how long my heart was to remain in that breast. But since, as we have said, that breath had already failed and the sighs had already dissolved into the wind, they could not answer. 

Love then—who, as we said above, was in that place from which the sighs originated—responds in their stead, swearing upon his own breast that my heart shall always remain with my lady and shall never return to me. He reassures me with this oath just as he had reassured my heart at the beginning when it first departed from me, as is shown in the sonnet that begins: "Woe is me, when I am where she may be." <note>original: *Lasso a me, quando io son lá dove sia*.</note>

# II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

It was in the month of April, a time when, according to the common custom of our city, men gladly stay with their families in delightful villas for their consolation, because at that time the year is as much more beautiful as early youth is more beautiful than all the other ages of man. Furthermore, our city has many elegant and pleasant places nearby which, beyond natural habit, sometimes entice one to leave behind civic and private cares and enjoy a bit of <term>rural leisure</term> <note>original: *rusticano ozio*. This refers to the classical concept of *otium*, a productive and restorative peace found in the countryside, away from the "negotium" or business of the city.</note>. At this time, therefore, it happened that my lady, like many others, went to one of her delightful villas, where she stayed for several days, depriving me of the sight of her that I so desired.

During this time, a very close friend, who was aware of my great love for her, said to me: — "Now one ought to be at that villa to see your beautiful lady, for now the birds are singing, now the meadows are renewing themselves with grass and flowers, now the trees are reclothing themselves with leaves; the nymphs, men, and all the animals feel the power of love more strongly at present. And thus, now would be the time for you to see your dearest lady amidst so many natural ornaments." — To him, I replied that my desire to see her neither grew nor could it diminish at any time, and that I believed—even if the whole world were most beautiful and adorned at this time more than any other—that the countryside around my lady must be more beautiful than all others. For where she was, there was no need for the sun, nor the new season, nor any <term>power</term> <note>original: *virtú*. In this context, it refers to an inherent, almost magical efficacy or life-force radiating from the lady.</note> other than her own to make the earth sprout, to make the trees flower and fill with leaves, to make the birds sing, and to produce the other effects that springtime usually brings.

Our conversation ended with such words. Having left the aforementioned friend, filled with those thoughts, I composed the present sonnet, in which I strove to express the effects of my lady's power which she worked in those wild places where she was then staying. I showed first that her eyes had the power of the sun, because wherever she turned them, she caused the earth to produce various colors of new flowers, calling her the beautiful <term>Flora</term> <note>The Roman goddess of flowers and the season of spring.</note> in this part because she made the flowers bloom—that is, the goddess of flowers. She also made the birds sing lovingly, as they were enamored by her song whenever they heard her singing most sweetly; she reclothed with their leaves...

<summary>Lorenzo describes the custom of Florentine families retreating to villas in April and recounts a conversation with a friend about his lady's absence. He argues that her presence alone, rather than the season, brings springtime to the land, inspiring a new sonnet where she is compared to the goddess Flora.</summary>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...the dry branches of those trees, which lose their leaves in winter, [responded] when she spoke sweetly. And here it should be noted that in the singing and the speaking of my lady are comprised three parts, which, according to Plato <note>Lorenzo refers here to the *Republic* or the *Timaeus*, where Plato defines music as a combination of *logos* (speech/reason), *harmonia* (harmony), and *rythmos* (rhythm).</note>, music contains, which are these: speech, harmony, and rhythm. I believe the latter is what we commonly call "rhyme," because "rhythm" is nothing other than speech defined by a certain measure. Just as there are vernacular verses and rhymes, one uses the term <term>musical speech</term> <note>original: *parlar musico*</note> even when it does not have fixed "feet" <note>In classical prosody, "feet" are the rhythmic units (like dactyls or iambs) that make up a line of verse.</note>, provided it is composed in a way that delights the ears, as is seen in those who are called "eloquent." Harmony is a consonance of human voices, or indeed of sounds, as is very well known; we have already said what rhythm is. 

One sees the first species of music—that is, speech—expressed in the verse that says: "Who feel how sweetly she speaks"; the other two—namely, harmony and rhythm—are included in the singing of my lady. It must be presupposed that she sweetly sang certain verses and love rhymes, in which she delighted exceedingly. And I many times heard them sung, both those of others and my own, with such sweetness and nobility that, afterwards, in the mouths of others they could not please me. Since she sang such verses and rhymes with the most suave melody, we have all three aforementioned species of music; and, this being so, the wonder of the effects my lady produced is in some part diminished. 

Because music is common to all things—which could not exist without a certain <term>consonance</term> <note>original: *consonanzia*. In Neoplatonic thought, the entire universe is held together by musical proportions or "the music of the spheres."</note>—it was reasonable that they should be moved by music. We see this when two stringed instruments are tuned to the same pitch and placed near one another: when one is played, the strings of the other also move by themselves without being touched by anyone, solely through the conformity of the tone and the similarity of the voice they have between them. Now, having spoken above of two powers of my lady—namely, her eyes and her harmony, etc.—and having to speak of a more marvelous operation of hers, it is necessary to assign an even more powerful reason for it. For although making the earth germinate, birds sing, and trees clothe themselves in leaves are great effects, these are all natural things; but to place a contrary impression into a subject...

<summary>Lorenzo explains how his lady's voice embodies the three Platonic elements of music—speech, harmony, and rhythm—and uses the scientific principle of sympathetic resonance to explain her influence over nature.</summary>

<keywords>Plato, music theory, rhyme, rhythm, harmony, sympathetic resonance, eloquence, Neoplatonism, vernacular poetry</keywords>

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...it is a greater thing, such as making the timid and chaste nymphs admit some soft and sweet thought of love into the hardness of their hearts, because love is entirely contrary to timidity and chastity. And therefore, a greater cause produces this greater effect, such as the laughter and the sighing of my lady; for when these come from her mouth, they stir amorous thoughts, as we have said regarding the nymphs. 

That this is a more powerful reason is shown by the fact that a cause, in my opinion, is more powerful to move one's feelings when it reveals a greater effect in itself. Laughter and sighing show a greater effect than looking, singing, or speaking—as we shall demonstrate—and the greatest effect of all is shown by touching. Therefore, the sonnet concludes that this [touch] produces an even greater effect than the others, showing that wherever her fair white hand <note>original: *candida mano*</note> touches, such grace and virtue abound that it can neither be described nor imagined. 

In this way, one proceeds by degrees from the less effective things to those that are most effective. For if we presuppose that Love moves all the actions of my lady that we have mentioned—namely, seeing, singing, speaking, laughing, sighing, and ultimately touching—less affection is shown by seeing than by singing, and less by singing than by speaking; and I say the same for all the others up to the sense of touch. 

If we presuppose a lover enamored of this lady, I believe that if she looks at him lovingly, it will be very pleasing to him; if he hears her sing amorous verses, it will seem a still greater sign of love; if he hears her speak with him, he will judge it an even more effective testimony of her love; if he sees her either laugh or sigh for love, it will seem a greater increase of her grace; and the greatest of all would be if she should touch him. Therefore, all these things will produce greater or lesser effects in him, according to the quality of the causes. Thus, the present sonnet includes those "lines"—that is, the degrees of love—which Ovid <note>Lorenzo refers to the Roman poet Ovid and the medieval tradition of the *gradus amoris* (steps of love), usually listed as: sight, conversation, touch, kissing, and the final act.</note>, a most ingenious poet, places in that book where he gives the <term>amorous precepts</term> <note>original: *amorosi precetti*; this refers to Ovid’s *Ars Amatoria* (The Art of Love).</note>.

> My weary heart in the midst of my anguished
> breast convokes and draws these wandering thoughts
> all around itself, and first sighs deeply,
> then speaks with sweet and tender words:

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

> — Even though each of you is filled with love,
> yet the one who speaks to you and gazes upon you created you:
> alas! why then do you wage against me a war so eternal and cruel,
> without granting me even a single moment of rest? —
> One of them replies: — Just as beneath the new sun
> the bees create a sweetness from diverse flowers,
> when the beautiful kingdom of Flora <note>The Roman goddess of flowers and spring, representing the rebirth of nature.</note> appears,
> so do we, from her glances and her words,
> from her manners and her beauty, create
> a certain bittersweetness <note>original: "dolce amar"</note> that sustains you. —

Although in the commentary on the sonnet that begins: "Set a limit to your weeping" <note>original: "Ponete modo al pianto"</note> we said quite enough about how wretched the human condition is, and especially that of lovers, yet—because one can never say so much that there isn't much more left to say—it is necessary in the present exposition to make some new mention of it. Indeed, I do not know what more effective argument could better prove the truth of this matter than considering that in which human happiness consists. I speak broadly and according to the corrupt customs of men, setting aside for now true happiness, which I believe is not to be found in this life.

And therefore we shall say that the greatest happiness is that which is preceded by the greatest desire and ardor; and since every appetite <note>In this context, *appetite* refers to a philosophical "drive" or intense longing for something perceived as good.</note> is a more vehement passion the greater it becomes, one must confess that the foundation of this happiness is a very great misery. That desire is its true foundation is manifest because, when desire is lacking, the will is also lacking. For example, one who has a great appetite for eating feels the flavor of what he eats with more delight; this pleasure lasts as long as the hunger lasts and dies along with the hunger. Indeed, that which is a pleasure while it is desired becomes a bothersome and tedious thing once that desire is quieted. 

For this reason, one can say that such happiness consists more in the privation of that which causes distress than in anything that brings any inherent good with it. It is like a medicine that only removes the ill from the sufferer without then strengthening nature to grant it any further power; as Horace shows in one of his epistles when he says: "Pleasure bought with pain is harmful" <note>original: "Nocet empta dolore voluptas"</note>. Having observed this in all human affairs—in honor,

He argues that human happiness is paradoxically rooted in misery, as pleasure is merely the temporary relief of an intense and painful desire.</summary>

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...in utility or in pleasure, it is necessary to confess that all human life, which depends on these things, is a passion <term>passion: here meaning a state of being acted upon or "suffering" an emotion, rather than just romantic love</term>, and its happiness is always mixed with it; because passion is the only immediate cause of happiness, and it accompanies it as the shadow follows the body. Finding this same effect within myself, and receiving very grave and continuous trouble from my thoughts, yet not seeming able to live without these very thoughts, I composed the present sonnet to express the state of my heart. This heart, placed in the center of my breast, full of anguish and already exhausted by the trouble of his thoughts, calls all the thoughts around him; these, as we have said, are naturally gathered around the heart as the cause of them. From this it happens that the heart sighs, because when various passions compete at the same time, they generate sighs for the reasons already mentioned.

After this sighing, the heart, having turned to the thoughts, begs them with sweet and piteous speech that they should cease bothering him for a while, and make peace in the long and continuous war that they wage against him without intermission. He shows that they ought to satisfy him in this, seeing as they are his children, created and generated by him. Because, even though they are amorous thoughts, and because they speak of nothing but love, the heart has made them amorous; and therefore they should recognize no other father but him, and as children they should not cause him such great trouble.

To this piteous proposal, one of the aforementioned thoughts responds, showing in effect that they are the cause of the heart's life. He makes a comparison: just as the bees <note>original: "pecchie," an archaic Italian term for bees</note> in the springtime, when Flora <note>The Roman goddess of flowers and spring, symbolizing the blooming of the natural world.</note> adorns the world with flowers, make a single sweetness—that is, honey—from diverse flowers, so my thoughts generate a certain sweetness from the various beauties of my lady. This sweetness is mixed with bitterness <note>original: "amaritudine"</note>, upon which the heart feeds and lives. It places my lady’s glances, her words, her manners, and her other beauties like flowers in a meadow; there, feeding upon them in different ways, my thoughts generate this bitter sweetness for the reasons stated above, for no pleasure <term>pleasure: original "voluttá," referring to sensory or aesthetic delight</term> in the world is without an admixture of passion. Even though in amorous thoughts the bitter is seen more distinctly from the sweet, although they are mixed together, it is a very great sweetness to contemplate and imagine so many marvelous beauties in my lady,

# II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...it is a very great torment and bitterness to then desire these things and be deprived of them. And the heart, drawn by the aforementioned sweetness, cannot help but think of his lady; and these thoughts of necessity bring with them also desire—that is, the deprivation of that good. Truly, it is said that the heart nourishes itself on these sweet and most bitter thoughts.

> If I turn my weary eyes now here, now there,
> without seeing that good which alone pleases me,
> misery to them, they never find peace.
> This happens to my thoughts, words, and steps.
> Therefore, for the better, I keep them tearful
> and lowered, and my afflicted tongue is silent,
> and my foot lies still in its first footprint; <note>original: "vestigio" — implying the poet stops in his tracks, paralyzed by his internal state.</note>
> every thought remains confined within the heart.
> Then I see her so beautiful and so noble <term>noble: "gentil," implying not just high birth, but spiritual and moral refinement</term>
> inside my heart, where Love has carved her,
> that I ask for no other good, no other peace.
> Silent and alone, I gaze fondly upon my beautiful heart;
> and in that moment she departs and flees with my life:
> I am left neither alive nor dead then, but worse.

Because I do not believe it has been determined which is the greater unhappiness—to be most unhappy or truly to lose one’s being entirely <note>The author is debating the philosophical question of whether suffering is worse than non-existence (death).</note>—I will leave the truth of this matter to a judgment greater than mine. I will affirm, however, through many experiences, that it often happens to men that they choose by preference <term>preference: "elezione," a deliberate choice of the will</term> to deprive themselves of life rather than endure such things; and even though passion is a reprehensible thing, in these cases it overrides every other better consideration. One also sees many times that men choose rather to deprive themselves for a short time of the use of their senses than to endure the offense caused to them: as we might say of one who shuts his ears to some great and frightening noise, or another who closes his eyes so as not to see something ugly or something else that might move him to compassion or pain, or another who holds his nose against any foul odor. And one must believe that such people would keep these senses closed forever if the things that offend them lasted forever. ## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...deprivation of being than the injury <note>original: "offensione" — here meaning a painful stimulus or harm to the senses</note>. And, because it was a most grave injury to my senses when they were deprived of their true object—that is, my lady—the present sonnet verifies the aforementioned judgment. In such a case, I chose for myself the deprivation of every external operation <term>operation: "operazione," meaning the active use of one's faculties like sight, speech, or movement</term> rather than such injury; considering the deprivation of my lady to be a greater thing than the deprivation of the being of the aforementioned operations. And although it may seem that by depriving myself only of the act, and not of the power <note>This refers to the Aristotelian distinction between "potency" (the capacity to do something) and "act" (the doing of it).</note>, it is not a total deprivation, yet assuming what we said above—that is, that the injury would last forever—one can assert the deprivation of both the power and the act. 

The sonnet says, then, that when it happened that I sought my lady either with my eyes or my steps, with words or thoughts, without finding her, the result was the greatest misery for all these things that sought her. For there is no greater misery than never finding peace or quiet, nor an end to passions, especially when that thing of which one is deprived is greatly desired. Nothing could be more desired or dear than my lady, assuming that she was that good which alone pleased me—which signifies that everything else besides her gave me displeasure and distress. And therefore, since other things are infinite in number, my distress was all the greater the more things were offered before me; thus they were almost infinite distresses, all of them grave, because they all presented me with the deprivation of my lady. 

It happens to our soul that it never rests until it finds that thing which pleases it more than any other; and even though many things may please it, the appetite <term>appetite: "appetito," the natural inclination or desire of the soul toward a perceived good</term> that settles on what pleases it most sets aside all others when it can attain its primary desire. As, for example, if someone delights in various things, such as dogs, birds, or horses, and along with these things is greedy by nature and more drawn to accumulating wealth than to any of those other things: therefore, having set aside the other pleasures, which he still naturally desires, his appetite finds rest only in that which he desires first and most, and every other thing gives him distress. Much greater was my distress, because I desired only my lady, nor was I satisfied by anything else, because the desire for her not only...

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici explains that the absence of his beloved makes all other sensory experiences and actions painful, leading him to prefer total stillness. He uses a psychological analogy of a greedy man to show how a primary desire overrides all other interests.</summary>

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...was my first and greatest desire, but it was alone, unaccompanied by anything else that might delight me; and therefore my distress was very great, both because of the number of troubles and their intensity. I found no better remedy for these things than the aforementioned deprivation, because I would close my eyes, covering them with tears and keeping them fixed on the ground, halting my steps in their tracks—that is, in whatever footprint they happened to be—my tongue kept silence, and my thoughts were pulled back into my heart. And here it should be noted that these thoughts refer to the effort <term>effort: "industria," meaning the active ingenuity or diligence used to achieve a goal</term> which I used to find my lady, contemplating the ways I might find her most quickly. This is distinct from the thoughts we will speak of later, which sought her in a different way and in another place. Upon finding her, because of this calming of external operations, the internal thoughts and the imagination <term>imagination: "fantasia," the mental faculty of creating images within the mind</term> gained all the more strength as the distraction of the senses decreased. 

And so, almost by necessity, my thoughts, confined to my heart, contemplated my lady, who was sculpted in my heart by Love <note>The idea of the beloved's image being "engraved" or "sculpted" in the lover's heart is a common trope in Renaissance Neoplatonic poetry, suggesting that love physically transforms the lover's internal being</note>; there they saw her as both most beautiful and noble, as she truly was. And then, with the eyes of my thoughts, I gazed longingly upon my truly beautiful heart, since my beautiful lady was sculpted within it. And my imagining was so strong that, by imagining within myself, I then received the same pleasure as if my eyes had seen the real woman. But because a strong imagination can rarely endure—except in a very few and chosen individuals—upon realizing this **sweetest deceit** <note>original: "dolcissimo inganno" — the pleasant but fleeting illusion of the mind</note>, I felt as if I were waking from a sleep. Finding myself without my lady, I remained in a state of great passion, because of which my heart departed from me, leaving me almost lifeless, silent and alone. 

For the beauty of my lady, which showed itself to my thoughts in my heart, gave birth to a desire for the real woman, as we said in the commentary on the sonnet that begins: "When I think to grieve," etc. And that desire caused not only my thoughts but nearly all my spirits <term>spirits: "spirti," in Renaissance medicine and philosophy, these were subtle vapors or fluids that carried life, sensation, and motion through the body</term> to depart from that imagined form and go toward the real one, because thoughts could only remain where my lady was. Thus, they stayed within me only as long as they saw her within me; and when that image departed, they too abandoned me. Then I remained neither alive nor dead,

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...because, as the heart—the seat of life—departs, I could call myself dead. However, because some vital force still remained, I could neither call myself dead, nor entirely alive. And if those things we said in the exposition of three sonnets regarding the **exchange of the heart** <note>original: "commutazione del core." This refers to the Neoplatonic concept where lovers' souls or hearts are literally swapped, meaning the lover lives only in the beloved and vice versa</note> are true, he who lives in another, as lovers do, cannot call himself alive in terms of himself; nor yet dead, if he lives in some place. One cannot interpret the state in which I remained as anything other than the first state shown in this sonnet: that is, in that distress of searching with my eyes, with words, and with my steps, etc., without finding my lady. Thus, what we proposed at the beginning of this commentary is verified: that the **deprivation of being** <term>deprivation of being: "privazione dell'essere," a philosophical state where one feels they have lost their very existence or essence</term> sometimes seems a lesser evil than a most heavy distress, since I remained in a worse state than if I had been either completely alive or completely dead. And because death includes this kind of deprivation, both of action and of potential, it seemed to me a lesser evil than the misery of that most unhappy state.

->Alas, now what does my beautiful lady do?<-
->Where does she sit? What does she think or say?<-
->Whose eyes or whose hand does she make happy?<-

->send out sad and unhappy tears:<-
->some reach the chest, some are allowed to go further,<-
->they bathe the earth, there they stop and lie.<-
->My heart sends out many sighs then:<-

->comforts the ready thoughts for the beautiful journey.<-
->These go to her, and she makes them fall in love,<-
->so that none brings back the news;<-
