## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...love, that is, the heart of the beloved. For there are two things in the lover that must be nourished and fulfilled: the senses, through which one recognizes both visible beauties and the sweetness of speech, along with other sensory ornaments, whether **natural or accidental** <term>natural or accidental: in Renaissance philosophy, "natural" refers to inherent traits like eye color or grace, while "accidental" refers to non-essential qualities like clothing, jewelry, or acquired skills</term>; and the heart, which, taking pleasure in these things until it is transformed into the other (as we have said), feeds upon the **reciprocal transformation** <note>This "transformation" refers to the Neoplatonic concept of "exchange of hearts," where the lover's soul leaves their body to dwell in the beloved, and vice-versa, creating a shared life</note> of the beloved's heart into the lover. 

If these, then, are the joys of lovers, then unhappiness consists in their deprivation, which can only occur through the **jealousy and absence** already mentioned. Therefore, it is no wonder if a lamentation of absence is frequently found in these verses of mine; for, as passion dictates the verse, a greater passion inspires a greater number of verses. And since the absence of the beloved is a most profound passion, my heart turned to the remedy of poetry all the more often because my absence occurred frequently, always causing me the greatest sorrow. 

Finding myself, therefore, distant from my lady's eyes—both for a long time and by a great distance of place—I began to wonder within myself, not without intense feeling, what my lady was doing at that moment: where she sat, what she thought, and who might be so worthy of such a blessing or so favored by fortune that, being seen by her beautiful eyes or touched by her hand, he would be most happy. Being unable to learn what I desired from anyone but Love himself, I questioned him; but since he refused to give me any answer, I pondered who could bring me some news. 

No messenger seemed more ready for my tearful eyes than the **tears** themselves that flowed from them. However, they were unable to reach the place where my lady was, because their journey ended upon my chest where they fell, or at the very furthest upon the ground, which my tears soaked. The heart then, seeing the plan of the eyes come to nothing and that the tears could reach no further, decided to send her many **sighs**, simply to hear some news of her. 

And here we see the truth of what was said above: using the "eyes" to represent all the sensory means that have external beauty as their object, and the "heart," which had as its object the heart of the...

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s commentary on the psychological and philosophical effects of love. Having previously discussed the pain of absence, he now describes the internal movement of the lover's faculties—eyes, heart, and thoughts—toward the beloved, leading into a classic philosophical definition of the soul's powers.</meta>

## II - COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...my lady; and the eyes are the first to move, and the heart follows them, because, once the exterior beauty is approved, there immediately follows the heart's desire not only for that beauty but for the beauty of the beloved heart. The heart then sent many sighs after the tears of the eyes, the journey of which was not much longer than that of the tears, dissolving into wind and air, just as they were when they became sighs. The heart, therefore, being defrauded of this hope, turned to **thoughts**, comforting them so that they might go to find my lady, for, being most swift and ready, they could go quickly even if the journey were long. 

The thoughts immediately go to find her, and they find her so beautiful and full of such sweetness that they fall in love with her, nor can they depart from her; and, not remembering the misery in which they had left me, they give me no answer or news at all. For this reason the heart—as we have said elsewhere—nourished only by these thoughts and living by their example, departs from me and, weeping, leaves me wretched and disconsolate without it, and it too goes away to my lady. Nor did I, in my weeping, complain of anything but my adverse lot and destiny, which had not made me so agile and ready that I could transfer myself to my lady along with my heart and thoughts. 

And, because we have many times mentioned this flight and departure of the heart, and its transformation, and the fleeing of life, it seems necessary to **demonstrate how this may be** <note>Lorenzo is addressing a common poetic trope: if the "heart" or "soul" leaves the body to be with the beloved, how does the lover remain physically alive?</note>, showing specifically how it is that sometimes the heart and the sight depart, and yet life remains in me, as the last verse of the preceding sonnet shows. And so we shall say that in our **soul** <term>anima: in this context, the "soul" is the animating principle of the body, divided into different functional levels</term> there are three powers, or we might say three kinds of life: 

The first, through which we merely live, nourish ourselves, and grow without any sensation, in the manner that trees and plants live, which is called **“vegetative”**; the other, through which we see, smell, and use the other senses, as the brute animals do, which for this reason is called **“sensitive”**; the third, through which we understand beyond the senses, and with reason approve that one thing is better than another, discerning the causes of things, which is called **“rational”**, which is held in common with the **angels** <note>original: "angioli"</note>, and is that part of us which...

<summary>Lorenzo describes how the lover's heart and thoughts desert the body to remain with the beloved, and begins a philosophical defense of this poetic idea by categorizing the soul into its vegetative, sensitive, and rational powers.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, three powers of the soul, vegetative soul, sensitive soul, rational soul, psychology of love, sighs, heart and thoughts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s philosophical exploration of the soul’s three powers—the vegetative, sensitive, and rational—and how love affects them. Having previously discussed the movement of the heart and thoughts toward the beloved, Lorenzo now explains how the lover can remain physically alive even when their higher faculties have "departed" to be with the beloved.</meta>

## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...is said to be immortal, because the first two <note>Referring to the sensitive and rational powers of the soul.</note> are seen to fail and die. Therefore, whoever falls in love with these three powers transforms two of them into the beloved: namely, the sensitive and the rational. This occurs because all the forces of our intellect, and all that is known through the senses, are surrendered to the power of the beloved, who disposes of and governs them in her own way. And so it necessarily follows that, since the freedom of the will <term>arbitrio: the capacity for free choice and voluntary action</term> (which is the principle of every action within us) submits itself voluntarily, all subsequent actions must follow that same principle, for without it they could not exist. 

There remains, therefore, in the lover only that part of life through which we simply live—as we have said—in the manner of plants <note>This refers to the "vegetative soul," the most basic level of existence responsible for growth and nutrition, which remains even when the mind and heart are consumed by love.</note>. In this way, the departure of life and the heart (meaning the rational and sensitive powers) is confirmed, yet without life itself failing, because the vegetative power remains within the lover.

> Alas, I no longer see those holy eyes,
> The peace and true object of my grieving ones;
> And because I hold in contempt whatever else I see,
> Pitying Love covers my own with weeping.

> The tears that fall down before me
> Waken the heart by bathing the chest from without;
> The heart asks Love what harsh effect
> Makes the eyes so moist and dewy.

> For the eyes, and toward my damp face it sends,

> 
> O sweetest Sun, O my only good,
> Show yourself a little and drive away this mist:
> My eyes have no more tears, nor my heart more sighs.

It does not seem necessary to say much in the exposition of the present sonnet, as its subject is very similar to the two preceding ones. It intends only to describe the misery of the state of love when one suffers the deprivation caused by the absence of the beloved. And, because the passions of love are commonly vented in three ways when they result from absence—namely tears, sighs, and thoughts—I believe these same things are repeated many times with some indulgence, though in different ways; because...

<meta>This page provides the prose commentary for the sonnet fragment introduced on the previous page. Lorenzo explains the psychological state of the lover who, unable to see his beloved, finds relief in tears provided by the personified figure of Love. ## II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

If this passion—as frequently occurs to lovers—has no other remedy, it is necessary to repeat the same things often. This present sonnet shows that when my eyes are deprived of the most sweet eyes of my lady—their sole and true object <term>obietto: in Renaissance philosophy, the specific thing a sense or faculty is designed to perceive</term> and repose—they look with disdain upon everything else they see. 

Love, moved by pity for the misery of the eyes, covered them with tears so that, occupied by weeping, they might at least be free from the sight of other things that caused them distress; for eyes overflowing with tears see with difficulty. 

When these tears fall upon that part of the chest beneath which the heart is placed, they awaken the heart, which feels the outer chest being struck by the falling tears. This demonstrates the abundance of the weeping, which awakens the heart—that is, rouses it from a sweet thought that previously held it occupied. Because of this new disturbance of the tears, much like someone sleeping who is woken by a new and harsh voice, the heart asks Love, who was present, for what reason the eyes weep so intensely. 

As Love explains the cause of the weeping, he must tell the heart that the pity my wretched eyes moved in him has caused him to provide them with these tears. Since the eyes are deprived of their lady and despise everything else, if Love cannot grant them their desired vision, he can at least help them to escape what they hate. 

For two remedies are found for misery: the first is making a miserable person happy (which is the most perfect remedy), or truly removing the misery—that is, taking away the evil without necessarily granting a positive good. Consider a beggar in need of everything: if someone were to remove the necessity of those things he cannot do without, and merely gave him enough to suffice, they would pull him out of misery and from that great evil which is the lack of all things. But whoever made him extremely wealthy and abundant in all things would not only remove the evil of misery but would grant him a positive good, making him rich. 

Love, therefore, produced this effect upon the eyes, granting them the lowest degree of good by removing the thing that offended them: namely, the sight of other things. This was done because there were two causes of grief within them: the desire to see my lady,

<summary>Lorenzo explains how tears serve as a psychological "remedy" for the lover; while they cannot bring the beloved back, they blur the vision so the lover does not have to look at an unwelcome world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the prose commentary from page 130, where Lorenzo describes the physiological and emotional effects of a lover's grief. He explains how the heart creates a "mist of sighs" to protect the eyes from seeing anything other than the beloved.</meta>

### II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...as their primary happiness and ultimate good, and the fear of the offense proceeding from the vision of other things. The heart, sensing the cause of the weeping, moved by the same compassion that moved Love, aids in the obscuring of the eyes—which began with tears—with a great number of sighs. It opposes the "mist of sighs" to the eyes so that, added to the tears, they might still defend the eyes and remove from them the vision of other things. 

And it is naturally called a "mist of sighs" which ascends and rises to the face, because the sigh carries with it a certain air, more vaporous and thick, almost in the manner of smoke and mist; naturally these go upwards toward the eyes, sent there by the impetus that arises from the lowest part of the chest <note>Lorenzo is referencing Renaissance medical theory, which held that strong emotions in the heart produced physical vapors that rose to the head</note>. 

But, because all these remedies were not enough for such great misery—as the loss of the vision of other things was not the sole and true beatitude <term>beatitudine: a state of supreme blessedness or the highest possible happiness</term> of the eyes—all the desires of my heart turned to praying to the eyes of my lady that they might show themselves somewhat and let themselves be seen by my own. And since the tears are like falling rain and the sighs like mist—just as there is no power more effective for dissipating mist and water than that of the sun—so no better remedy could be found to remove the tears and sighs than the light of my lady’s eyes. To this light, as the unique remedy, one recurs, praying to it, as we have said, to show itself. 

For, if it were to delay or hide its light and power <term>virtú: the inherent capacity or active influence of a thing to produce an effect</term> for some time, the eyes would return to even greater misery; for not only would they be deprived of this sun, their true beatitude, but they would be forced to see those other things which we have said are supremely loathsome to them. This is because the tears and sighs could not occupy their sight for long; it seemed impossible that the fountain of tears would not eventually be dammed up or run dry, or that the seat and place of sighs would possess such an abundance that this compassionate supply would not eventually fail.

> I return to you, O bright and beautiful lights,
> to the sweet radiance, to the infinite beauty,
> from which every noble heart <term>cor gentile: "noble heart," a concept from the Stil Novo poets implying a heart refined by love and virtue</term> in the world draws life,
> as from the sun the other stars draw their light.

It is a very great misery for anyone who suffers for the desire of a thing, which then—even when there is the greatest hope of obtaining it—does not lack its initial misery, fearing that, upon obtaining it, they might still remain miserable. And, because this often happens in the <term>accidenti amorosi: the various chance events and emotional circumstances of a love affair</term>, the life of lovers can be called miserable above all others; for, whether having or not having what they want, their unhappy fate never changes, even if the causes of the misery change. 

This effect is expressed in the present sonnet, because having been (as we said above) distant from my lady for some time with much affliction, and being already on the way to return to her so-desired presence, and near the vision of her beautiful eyes, as if I were almost present, I direct these words to them. I show that I am returning to see again the sweetness of their light and their infinite beauty, from which every <term>cuor gentile: a "noble heart," a central concept in Italian poetry referring to someone whose nobility comes from virtue and sensitivity rather than birth</term> must recognize its life, just as the stars of heaven recognize the source of their light from the splendor of the sun. 

To prove this truth—that the life of noble hearts proceeds from this infinite beauty—one must presuppose that beauty is without end. Therefore, it would be not only the greatest beauty, but as much beauty as can possibly exist, for every infinite thing is such. And since supreme beauty, supreme goodness, and supreme truth are one and the same thing, according to Plato, goodness and truth are of necessity so annexed to true beauty that one is converted into the other <note>Lorenzo is referencing the Neoplatonic idea that Beauty, Truth, and Goodness are different aspects of the same Divine reality</note>. And understanding "noble hearts" to mean elevated and perfect souls, as we have said, it must be true that every noble heart...

### II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS 133

...alive with infinite beauty, because the beautiful, the good, and the true are the object and goal of every reasonable desire, giving life to those who crave them; because he who turns away from the beautiful, the good, and the true can be said not to live, for outside of these perfections, nothing is said to exist. Therefore, just as the sun with its rays makes the stars shine without any diminishing of its own light, so this supreme beauty pours grace—that is, a spiritual light—into <term>gentili cuori: noble hearts, those refined by virtue and sensitive to love</term> like rays, through which they live and shine spiritually. And, although the subject matter of our verses is not of such perfection, nevertheless the <term>errori amorosi: "loving errors," referring to the delusions or obsessive misjudgments caused by being in love</term> make one believe that what is found in oneself can be found in others. 

And so, living by the light of those beautiful eyes, their beauty seemed so marvelous to me that I thought it must please everyone just as much as it pleased me; hence I affirmed of all others what I felt in myself. Returning, then, to this infinite beauty, without which I judged myself most wretched, and being full of various thoughts—and more confused within myself the closer I drew to it—it must be considered a great unhappiness of mine that, in the very good I was seeking, I feared there was evil. 

The variety and confusion of thoughts was such that one part of them persuaded me that I would find my lady full of love, pity, and sweetness; another part dismayed me, persuading me of the contrary. In this way, I was afraid within myself to hear the true news because of the distress it would have brought to my heart had I heard I was cast out entirely from my lady's grace. This caused my steps to slow, and it was a most powerful cause: since, though I desired the eyes of my lady above all else, I delayed my step to see her. 

Love came to the aid of this very difficult perplexity: because a loving thought brought back to my memory some words my lady had said to me when parting from her—words full of hope, affirming that in every place and time I would always be full of her grace, and assuring me of her faith and constancy. Love carved these words inside my heart with his own hands; this sweet memory made me believe what Love further adds, showing that every other thought, every disdain and anger, had been drawn out from the heart of the

Plato, a most excellent philosopher, posits two extremes: namely, knowledge and ignorance. Knowledge is like a light that shows us what is truly and perfectly real; ignorance is like a dark obscurity which deprives us of the cognition of those things that exist, and remains only in that which is not. And, because there must always be a middle ground between extremes, he places "opinion" between knowledge and ignorance. Opinion, by being sometimes true and sometimes not true, seems in a certain way to participate sometimes in knowledge and sometimes in ignorance. Not that it can ever truly be knowledge—even if the opinion is true—regarding the things that are; but that opinion can certainly be ignorance regarding that which is not. Knowledge comprises things that are certain and clear; ignorance comprises nothing; opinion comprises those things which sometimes are and sometimes are not, and which have the potential to be or not to be. And for this reason, opinion is always anxious and restless, because our soul is never contented except by that which is true and...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Plato's concept of "opinion" as the middle ground between knowledge and ignorance. Lorenzo is explaining why we perceive beauty through contrast and comparison, rather than as an absolute truth.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
# II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS

...is, since opinion cannot have any certainty, it does not rest, but judges things more by comparison and relative measures than according to the truth. For example, I might say: "Such a man is tall," because he exceeds by some amount the height of three cubits <term>braccia: literally "arms," a Florentine unit of measurement approximately 23 inches</term>, where the stature of men commonly ends. And if men were four cubits tall, one who was three and a half cubits would be considered small. Among the Ethiopians, who are black by nature, one who is less black than the others will be called white; and among these Westerners, one will be called black who, among the Ethiopians, would be very fair. You might say: "Such a person is good," even though, according to the prophet David, "there is not even one" <note>original: "non est usque ad unum"—a reference to Psalm 14:3, suggesting that absolute moral perfection is not found in humans</note>; but he is called good in comparison to the malice of others. One person today is extremely wealthy in Venice, in Florence, or elsewhere, who—with the same resources in the time of the Roman Empire—would have been a beggar in comparison to many other greater fortunes.

And therefore we shall say that, according to human opinion, there can be no certain knowledge of anything, but the best is judged to be that which most approaches the good, or rather, that which is most distant from its opposite. If, for example, a pearl appeared much more beautiful to someone the clearer and whiter it was—that is, the closer it approached true and perfect whiteness—he would want to see it against a black field or some dark color, so that the comparison with its opposite would show the pearl approaching more closely to true whiteness. And although the primary intention is this whiteness, he mixes in the black color which is its opposite, deceiving himself and believing that this gives it strength, because in fact that pearl is no whiter against black than it would be against white. 

From this arises beauty, which proceeds from the variety and distinction of things, because one gains strength from the other, and it seems to approach more closely to its perfection. Because, if our opinion understood the truth, we would choose only those things that are most beautiful, without the mixture of other less beautiful things; whereas in human life we commonly seek variety for the sake of supreme beauty, if we understood perfectly, we would flee from variety before anything else.

This whole discourse has seemed necessary, as we are treating in the present sonnet the supreme beauty that appeared in the face of the...

<header>II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS</header>

...my lady <note>donna mia: This refers to Lucrezia Donati, the woman to whom Lorenzo addressed much of his early poetry in the courtly love tradition</note> through a circumstance that in others usually hides and extinguishes beauty, but in her, it multiplied it. I was walking, then, alone along a very solitary path, full, however, of amorous thoughts; and being beyond any expectation of being able to see my lady in such a place, I suddenly encountered her, and she was already very close to me when I saw her. This unexpected vision and the sudden assault of her eyes upon mine caused almost all my strength and the color of my face to depart from me at once; and, gazing at her face, it seemed to me similarly adorned with a lovely and most beautiful pallor—not, however, of a deathly hue, but one that leaned toward whiteness. 

And at first it seemed to me a great presumption for that pale color to have come upon so beautiful a face. But, thinking better on it later, I saw that it had added strength to her other beauties, just as green grass usually makes flowers more beautiful, and the sky shows the stars more clearly by distinguishing them with its color and serenity: even though the flowers are more beautiful than the grass, and the stars more beautiful than the field of the sky, the grass made the flowers appear more beautiful than if the entire meadow were flowers and they were not set against the green of the grass. 

Similarly, the sky does the same for the stars, not only by the power of variety, but because opposites, when placed near one another, take on greater strength and reveal themselves better. Nor were the beauties of my lady fewer in number than the flowers of the meadows and the stars of the sky. Those beauties were therefore in the midst of that pale color, like flowers in the middle of the grass and stars in the middle of the color of the sky. 

Among so many flowers, Love <note>Amore: Lorenzo personifies Love as an active artist or creator throughout this commentary</note>—that most beautiful flower—was also in the midst of this face, and among so many stars was similarly the star of Love. Love was at the same time joyful and full of wonder, having performed such a noble and beautiful work: joyful, because she was most beautiful, and full of wonder, because the thing he had done was great and very new, having added so much ornament by means of that pale color which, as we have said, usually disturbs other faces and makes them ugly. If Love was full of wonder at being the author of such a beautiful work, one can imagine that I remained astonished and full of stupor, and that all my faculties, overcome by this excessive and new beauty, departed from me for some time...

<summary>Lorenzo describes a sudden encounter with his lady, arguing that her unexpected pallor actually enhanced her beauty through the principle of contrast, much like green grass heightens the color of flowers.</summary>

> Alas, I no longer know what I should do,
> when I am there, where my beautiful lady is;
> if I gaze at one and then the other bright star <note>bright star: A poetic metaphor for the lady’s eyes, a common trope in Renaissance love poetry.</note>,
> I see my death flashing within them.
> If it happens that I flee and seek my help
> now from this beauty and now from that,
> now from her manners, now from her sweet speech,
> I find no place where I might see myself safe.
> If I touch her hand, she has deprived me
> of life, and holds tightly in a beautiful bundle
> my heart and my thoughts, once ready and happy.
> From such and so many sweet enemies of mine
> I have a thousand sweet offenses, and still I await
> so sweet a death, that thinking of it, I live.

All human affections <term>affetti: emotions or feelings that "affect" the soul</term> are, without controversy, passions; and the causes that move the affections of men are two: anger <term>ira: the irascible power, often linked to the drive to overcome obstacles</term> and desire <term>concupiscenzia: the concupiscible power, or the drive toward physical or sensory pleasure</term>. Because these are very different passions, according to some, they have different places and seats in our body: for the irascible power is generated in the heart, and the concupiscible in the liver; according to others, both are in the heart. That they are different powers and distinctions is shown by the effects that proceed from these causes, of which one part—that is, those proceeding from anger—are most often troubles to our soul; those arising from desire are more often pleasing and sweet. 

And all these affections being, as we have said, passions, it is necessarily concluded that every desire, even if it is for something sweet and pleasing, is still a passion. Rather, as we said at the beginning, in the definition of Love and in the exposition of the sonnet that begins: "Set a limit to your weeping, my weary eyes," every appetite <note>appetite: Here used in the philosophical sense of *appetitus*, the natural longing or drive for what a person lacks.</note> shows the deprivation of that which is desired, which is the height of unhappiness; for he who cannot quiet his appetite and restrain it lives in continual passion. And so, at one and the same time, the same thing is sought and fled from, because he who greatly desires to quiet a great appetite has great...

<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de' Medici's philosophical commentary on his sonnet from the previous page. He is exploring the paradox of desire: how seeking relief from the sight of his lady only intensifies his longing, creating a cycle of "sweet" suffering.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS</header>

...desire, and he who does not seek to quiet it likewise possesses a great appetite. But he makes a greater error who seeks to quiet the appetite for a thing by adopting remedies and methods that instead multiply it and increase its restlessness. This is precisely what happened to me; for, thinking of my lady’s beauty, I felt a very great desire for it, and believing I could quiet this feeling, I went to see her. But as I began to look upon her eyes, they seemed so beautiful that my desire only grew; this was the exact opposite of what I intended. 

Therefore, not finding my peace in her eyes, but seeing my own death—that is, Love <note>original: "Amore"</note>—shining and flashing within them, I fled from their sight. I hoped to find the rest I had failed to find there in some other of the many beauties that appeared in my lady. And so, seeking my relief—meaning the aforementioned quiet—sometimes from her most noble manners, contemplating them with the greatest attention, and sometimes by listening to her most sweet and varied speech, I found Love truly armed and prepared for my death. For it is the true office of infinite beauty to kindle infinite desire; and we may say the same in proportion to every beauty and desire. 

Despairing, then, of finding quiet through the beauties and adornments I continually saw with my eyes, I thought I might find peace if I could only touch her whitest hand. Yet, remembering that it was this hand that had taken my life and held my heart and all my thoughts tightly within it, I despaired of this as well. For if my thoughts were happy while being held in that hand, it was impossible for them to depart from such happiness, toward which all things naturally run. And I could not find quiet without my thoughts, because thoughts are the principle of every human action; they precede our works, and nothing can be done without first being thought. Therefore, if thought is lacking, works are lacking as well. 

Unable, then, to obtain my salvation <term>salute: in this context, both "health" and "spiritual salvation," referring to the resolution of his inner turmoil</term>—meaning the quieting of desire—but rather finding it growing more each hour, necessity showed that I must endure these most sweet offenses. I was forced to love such sweet enemies as her eyes, her words, her manners, her hand, and the other beauties of my lady. They were truly sweet, because there was great sweetness...

<vocab>desire, quiet, Love, sonnets, beauty, thought, appetite</vocab>

<summary>Lorenzo explains the psychological paradox of his love: attempting to satisfy his desire by looking at his lady only increases his passion, leading him to conclude that her "sweet" beauty is an inescapable source of both joy and suffering.</summary>
<keywords>desire, beauty, Love, psychology of emotion, Petrarchan paradox, the lady's hand, power of thought</keywords>

considering such great beauty, they were truly enemies, being the cause of further multiplying my desire—that is, my passion. I believed then that not only was that beauty present, but also the hope of a much sweeter death, <note>In the Neoplatonic tradition used by Lorenzo, "death" often signifies the ecstatic state where the lover's soul "dies" to itself to live in the beloved.</note> which I awaited with the greatest longing from the aforementioned enemies, by means of these amorous injuries. For the greater the injuries were—meaning the desire for such beauty—the sweeter death became. And so the hope of this death filled my heart with such sweetness that my heart already fed and lived upon it: understanding this death in the manner we have described lovers dying, when they are entirely transformed into the beloved object. This implies nothing other than the fulfillment of desire, which is achieved when the lover is transformed into the beloved. And therefore this death is not only sweet, but it is the highest sweetness that human <term>desire<gloss>original: "concupiscenzia"; a strong longing or appetite for beauty or pleasure</gloss></term> can possess. For this reason, I awaited it with the greatest sweetness and longing as the only remedy for my salvation, like the true end of all my desires.

->My beautiful lady is not alone<-
->far from my aching and weary eyes:<-

As happened many other times, as we have already said, I was far removed from the eyes of my lady at the time I composed this sonnet. And among the many harsh thoughts that made this absence most troublesome, one marvelously wounded my heart. --- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues Lorenzo de' Medici’s commentary on a sonnet where he addresses the physical absence of his beloved. He is exploring the Neoplatonic idea that the mind and spirit can provide companionship even when the body is alone, transitioning from the concept of a "sweet death" to the nature of true solitude.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>II — COMMENTARY ON SOME OF HIS SONNETS</header>

Because the deprivation of her sight generated diverse passions in me, I began to think that these same things must similarly cause her great distress; and therefore, to the sorrow I felt for my own misfortune, this was also added: my heart was struck by pity for her grief at being alone and without me. And since nature—and every good physician who imitates nature—first applies a remedy to that which most harms life, my amorous thoughts, the only medicine for this sweetest of maladies, first considered the remedy for what most distressed me: namely, pity for the solitude of my lady. They showed, in effect, that she was not alone, even though she was far from my sorrowful and tearful eyes, because in her company were love, hope, and faith, and with them, all my thoughts.

She was not, then, alone, even if there were no other person in her company and she was deprived of the conversation of others. This is attested by the <term>maxim of Cato<gloss>Referring to a sentiment attributed to Cato the Elder, often quoted via Cicero: "Never am I less lonely than when I am alone."</gloss></term>, who said he was “never less alone than when he was alone.” Similarly, the city of Jerusalem was called “solitary” <note>original: "sola"; a reference to the opening of the Book of Lamentations 1:1, "How solitary sits the city that was full of people!"</note> by Jeremiah, even though it was full of people; for true solitude is being deprived of those things which please us.

And one is said to be alone even in the midst of many enemies; because when the true purpose for which a thing is intended is missing, that thing is necessarily no longer itself. For example, we call a man “rational” <note>Lorenzo draws on the Aristotelian definition of a human being as a "rational animal."</note> because he is directed toward the end of reason; when he lacks this, he can no longer truly be called a man. The society and company of men with one another is ordained by nature so that all the comforts necessary for human life, which cannot be found in a single person, may be obtained from many. And if this is the goal of company, whenever a great number of people exists only to harm an individual, that cannot be called “company,” but rather “enmity.”

If, then, the conversation of men was bothersome to my lady, and she took pleasure only in love, hope, faith, and my thoughts, then without these things she would be in extreme solitude even among many; whereas with them, even if she were in the deserts of sandy Libya, she could be called “accompanied.” And that she was not alone is further demonstrated by her speaking and grieving with this company. She grieved, then, so sweetly that Love marvelously...

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici redefines solitude, arguing that his lady is never truly alone because his thoughts and the virtues of Love, Faith, and Hope accompany her. He utilizes classical philosophy and biblical imagery to explain that true companionship is found in what the soul desires, not in a mere crowd of people.</summary>
<keywords>solitude, Cato, Jeremiah, Jerusalem, rationality, human nature, Libya, Neoplatonism, companionship vs. enmity</keywords>

[Love] took pity on her, and, compelled by this compassion, he wept in her eyes. And having said that the seat of Love <note>The personified figure of Cupid or Amore.</note> and his true place was in her most beautiful eyes, it followed that he necessarily wept in those eyes. Because of this weeping, and because the eyes themselves were cast down, overcome by grief, their brilliance was somewhat diminished—not that the eyes became less beautiful through this obscuring, but they shone to the eyes of others as the sun usually does when a cloud comes between them; I say this according to how it appears to our eyes, not that the sun loses any part of its light. <note>Lorenzo uses a common Neoplatonic metaphor: the lady's beauty is like the sun, which remains perfect even if clouds (sorrow) temporarily block our view of its full radiance.</note>

And, because what has been said seemed a marvelous and almost incredible thing, it was necessary to make an author of this <note>Meaning, to provide a witness or authority for the claim.</note> who had been present, as one of my thoughts had been; since all my thoughts were there, he was necessarily there as well. For, as we said at the beginning, this remedy came from amorous thoughts; and, for confirmation of this truth, he brought with him proof of his company—that is, the other thoughts of Love, along with faith and hope, a truly sweet and beautiful company.

Indeed, human life has no other good nor greater sweetness; and if love and faith were truly in my lady, then compassion for my absence necessarily existed there, and the thought, with these witnesses, had to be believed. This faithful messenger with these tidings, on one hand, filled my heart with sweetness, thinking that not only was my lady not alone, but accompanied by such beautiful company. On the other hand, hearing that my lady grieved and wept, my heart was kindled with the greatest <term>pity<gloss>Italian: "pietá"; here meaning a profound empathetic sorrow rather than condescension.</term>, so much so that I would truly have died from that sweetness and pity if hope had not aided me with the promise of soon seeing her eyes, which my heart always saw. And because the eyes of the heart are thoughts, it is verified that my thoughts were always with my lady. . . . . . . . . . . .
. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .

# I

->[May Love inspire his lady to have compassion for him.]<-

The first wound was so cruel,
so fierce and so vehement the first arrow,
that, were it not that hope nourishes and gives wings to the heart,
death would have already seemed sweet to me.
And my tender age does not refuse
to follow Love, but rather cares for it more each hour;
it willingly follows its joyful suffering, <note>original: "giocondo male." A classic Petrarchan oxymoron describing the bittersweet nature of desire.</note>
since it has received such a destiny by fate.
But you, O Love, since under your banner <note>original: "insegna."</note>
you want me so soon, act in such a way
that I do not serve as a warning <note>original: "insegna." Lorenzo uses an equivocal rhyme here, using the same word to mean "banner" (noun) and "teach/show" (verb).</note> to others through my pain.
Have mercy upon your servant,
and in that haughty lady make such a fire reign
that she might recognize the suffering of another.

<term>Lorenzo the Magnificent</term>, <term>sonnet</term>, <term>Love</term>, <term>wound</term> <note>original: "feruta."</note>, <term>hope</term> <note>original: "speme."</note>, <term>equivocal rhyme</term> <note>A poetic device where two words that sound identical but have different meanings are used as rhymes.</note>

<summary>This page features the opening sonnet of Lorenzo de’ Medici’s collection, depicting the overwhelming power of a first love. The poet entreats the god of Love to instill reciprocal feelings in his beloved so that his suffering might be understood and shared.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, sonnet, Cupid, courtly love, unrequited passion, Petrarchism, poetic destiny</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

## II

->[He fell in love with his lady in springtime.]<-

It was in that beautiful season, when Titan <note>A mythological name for the Sun. In Renaissance poetry, the "annual labor" of the sun refers to its journey through the zodiac.</note>
had already completed the third of his annual labor,
and with his rays struck with a bit
of such heat, that was not yet harsh;
every mountain and plain was seen to be green,
and every meadow shone with flowers,
every shrub still held its new leaves,
and Philomela <note>A mythological reference to the nightingale, whose song marks the arrival of spring but carries a tone of ancient grief.</note> weeps and grieves in vain;
when I, who previously would not have feared
even if Hercules <note>The Greek hero known for his immense strength; the poet suggests that before falling in love, he felt invincible.</note> had returned to life,
was captured by a graceful and beautiful gaze.
The path was easy and sweet at the entrance;
now this labyrinth has no exit,
and I am in a place where I always burn.

---

## III

->[“Let every man look well before he is moved.”]<-

Already seven times has Titan circled
our hemisphere and our heavy mass <note>Refers to the Earth, which in the Renaissance geocentric view was the "heavy" center of the universe.</note>:
for me no sun has been on the earth,
for me no light or splendor has gone forth.
Hence it is that all my joy is converted
into dark weeping, and, what pains me most,
is seeing Love, who at the beginning is usually
appearing appeased, becoming more cruel every hour.
A sad beginning is this for our love,
and already I repent the first undertaking,
but only now, when I can no longer help myself;
for I feel the torch burning in the middle of my heart,
and by now this fuel <note>original: "ésca." This can mean both "tinder" for a fire and "bait" for a trap, reinforcing the idea of being lured into a painful situation.</note> is too far ignited.
Therefore, let every man look well before he is moved.

<term>Titan</term> <note>In classical mythology, the sun was often personified as the Titan Helios or Apollo.</note>, <term>Philomela</term>, <term>Hercules</term>, <term>Love</term> <note>Capitalized here to represent the personified god Cupid or the force of Amor.</note>, <term>sonnet</term>, <term>springtime</term>

<summary>These two sonnets describe the onset of the poet's love during the beauty of spring and his subsequent realization, seven time-cycles later, that he is trapped in an inescapable and increasingly "cruel" passion.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, sonnet, spring, mythology, Titan, Philomela, Hercules, unrequited love, labyrinth metaphor</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

### IV

->Sonnet written for a lady who had gone to the countryside.<-

Happy country houses, fields, and you, sylvan
woods and fruitful trees and the uncultivated ones,
grasses, shrubs, and you, thick and harsh thickets,
and you, laughing meadows favorable to my love;

slopes, hills, high shady mountains, alpine,
and rivers where the beautiful springs are gathered,
you, domestic animals, and you, free
nymphs, satyrs, fauns and earthly gods;

from now on, cease to honor Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt and the wilderness, traditionally the "queen" of the woods.</note>,
because another goddess has arrived in your realms,
who also has her bow and her quiver.

She captures wild beasts where Pan <note>original: "Pana". The ancient Greek god of the wild, shepherds, and nature.</note> does not reign:
and anyone who is once struck by her,
like Medusa <note>A figure from Greek mythology whose gaze turned people to stone. Here, the poet suggests the lady’s beauty has a similarly paralyzing, "petrifying" effect.</note>, she turns into stone.

### V

->[Deprived of the sight of his lady, he has lost every good.]<-

Eyes, since you are forever deprived
of seeing that Sun <note>The poet’s lady is frequently compared to the sun as the source of his light and life; without her, he is in darkness.</note> which illuminated
your dark path, and comforted
your sight, now weep eternally.

The happy spring into harsh winter
has now turned, and the time that I expected
to be most happy, and for which I longed,
is now most painful to me: now I discern what Love is.

And if the first arrow seemed sweet to me,
and if the first blow was gentle,
and if in my first service <note>original: "milizia". The "militia of love" is a common poetic trope where the lover is seen as a soldier serving under the command of the god Love.</note> I found much good,

every joy has now turned to ill,
and through a pleasant path into a blind pit
I have fallen, where I must burn.

<summary>In these sonnets, the poet first celebrates the countryside for hosting his beloved, comparing her to a goddess superior to Diana, before lamenting the darkness and metaphorical winter that consumes his life when he is separated from her.</summary>
<keywords>sonnet, Diana, Pan, Medusa, Love, rhymes, pastoral imagery, courtly love</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Rime" (Rhymes) sequence. In the previous sonnet (V), the poet lamented being blinded by the absence of his lady. # III - RHYMES

### VI

->[Happy the land where his lady dwells.]<-

Happy land, where she dwells,
who holds my heart in her hands,
whereby at her will I feel both pain and pleasure,
and I die a thousand times, and live, within the hour.
Now she gives me anguish, now she restores me:
now joy, now sadness comes to the soul;
and thus she maintains my wavering heart
in joys and in tears: now it must live, now die.
Truly, above other lands you are happy,
since you see two Suns rise each day,
but one so bright that the planet <note>The sun itself. The poet suggests the lady's beauty outshines the literal sun.</note> is envious of it.
I have seen six moons return <note>Six months have passed.</note>
without seeing the light that brings me peace,
but I will follow my Sun, like a phoenix <note>A mythical bird that burns in a fire and is reborn from the ashes. The poet suggests he is consumed by the lady's "heat" only to be reborn to follow her again.</note>.

---

### VII

->[His lady gives peace to others, and war to him.]<-

My eyes could not yet endure
the rays of her face so shining;
my sight could not remain composed
before what it saw issuing from her two beautiful eyes.
But it seems against reason if I marvel at this,
because she is a thing divine and so excellent
that she does not permit the human mind
to be able to fully enjoy her great beauty.
She, a celestial being, not of the earth,
is given to men as a supreme and unique gift,
and has come to dwell upon the earth.
Every soul that sees her becomes serene;
and yet I, for certain, remain unhappy,
for she gives peace to others, and to me only war <note>A common trope in Renaissance poetry where the lady's beauty "assaults" the lover's heart, creating an internal conflict or "war" of emotions.</note>.

<summary>In these two sonnets, the poet praises the divine and solar qualities of his lady, noting that while her presence brings peace to others, it leaves him in a state of emotional turmoil and longing after a six-month absence.</summary>
<keywords>rhymes, sonnets, love, Italian poetry, lady, sun, phoenix, emotional conflict</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Rime" (Rhymes) sequence. In the previous sonnets, the poet established the divine nature of his lady and the emotional turmoil her absence causes him. Sonnet VII concluded with the idea that her presence brings serenity to all who see her, though it leaves the poet in a state of "war" or inner conflict. This page shifts toward nautical metaphors for his struggle and reflections on art's ability to capture her essence.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### VIII

->[*He who perseveres wins in the end.* <note>original: "Colui alfin vince, che la dura." This is a common Renaissance proverb emphasizing that endurance is the key to victory.</note>]<-

My weak, tiny, and fragile boat
is so oppressed by the sea waves
that so much water already overflows within,
it shall perish, so burdened is it by heavy thoughts.

Since it has lamented in vain for so long,
and Neptune <note>The Roman god of the sea, representing the unpredictable and often cruel nature of the world or the lady’s favor.</note> seems to hide from its prayers
among the rocks and where the water is deepest;
let everyone now consider with what safety it sails.

I see the winds becoming ever more fierce toward me,
but Fortune and Love, who stands at the helm,
told me that having fear is of no use;

for it is better to hope in every adversity.
And it seems that reason also supports this,
because he who perseveres wins in the end.

---

### IX

->[On a statue of his lady.]<-

Since Fortune, an enemy to my prayers,
did not wish to make me happy—though she had the power—
nor did she see fit to raise me above the common crowd,
because she wishes no one to be called truly blessed;

she <note>Referring to the lady herself.</note>, in whom Nature spent such great effort
to clearly demonstrate to me
that one who appears mortal to my sight
does not entangle herself in earthly affairs.

Whatever likeness the master’s skill <term>magistero: the technical mastery or specialized skill of an artist</term> was most able
to draw from her living and natural form,
is now here before us: it only lacks that she might breathe.

But if he who captured her true face
could also show the virtue that dwells within her,
what would become of Phidias, Polykleitos, and Praxiteles? <note>These were the three most famous sculptors of ancient Greece, representing the pinnacle of artistic achievement. The poet suggests that if the lady's inner spirit could be sculpted, this artist would surpass the greatest masters of antiquity.</note>

<summary>In these two sonnets, the poet uses the metaphor of a storm-tossed boat to describe his emotional struggle, and praises a statue of his lady, claiming her inner virtue is so great it would outshine the works of the greatest Greek masters.</summary>
<keywords>sonnet, nautical metaphor, Neptune, Fortune, Love, sculpture, Phidias, Polykleitos, Praxiteles, artistic mastery</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

## X

->[For a portrait of his lady.]<-

In the little temple, adorned by you alone,
noble lady, more excellent than any other,
whether among modern or ancient people,
yet deprived of every good thing since your departure,

being carried along by my fortune
to comfort my afflicted, grieving soul,
a ray so bright appeared to my eyes
that everything I look at afterward seems dark.

The cause—since I could not look steadily—
I thought to be the splendor of a diamond <note>original: "adamante." In Renaissance poetry, the diamond often symbolized both brilliant light and indomitable virtue.</note>
or of some other stone more bright and beautiful,

placed there to adorn her, she being adorned by it;
but then I changed my mind, and the radiant
ray I recognized: it was your beautiful face.

---

## XI

->Sonnet composed at Reggio, upon my return from Milan, where I found news<-
->that a lady was ill.<-

Fearing the sister of the Thunderer <note>Juno (Hera), the wife and sister of Jupiter (the "Thunderer"), known for her jealousy regarding her husband's many affairs.</note>,
lest Jove be inflamed by a new love;
and Cytherea <note>An epithet for Venus (Aphrodite), the goddess of love, referencing her birth near the island of Cythera.</note>, lest elsewhere her fierce
Mars, her ancient and dear lover, should find love;

the chaste goddess of the woodland plants <note>Diana (Artemis), goddess of the hunt and chastity.</note>,
envious of these modest and new beauties;
and Pallas <note>Pallas Athena (Minerva), goddess of wisdom and war.</note>, that there be found in the world
a mortal woman more chaste and more excellent than she,

all made those holy limbs grow weak,
which are worthy of celestial honor, not of illness.
Ah, Envy, even in heaven your roots take hold!

You, fair Apollo, if you still remember
your first love <note>A reference to Daphne, whom Apollo loved and who was transformed into a laurel tree to escape him.</note>, and if pity is not
extinguished within you, make me happy, for you have the power. <note>As the god of healing and medicine, Apollo is the only one who can cure the lady and thus restore the poet's happiness.</note>

<summary>Sonnet X describes the dazzling effect of the lady's portrait on the poet, while Sonnet XI uses a mythological conceit to explain the lady's illness as the result of the goddesses' jealousy.</summary>
<keywords>portrait, light, diamond, Reggio, Milan, mythology, Juno, Venus, Diana, Athena, Apollo, illness, envy</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

## XII

->[ "Often one loses where one gains." ]<-

I often return to that desired place,
from which my afflicted soul never departs,
which used to give me rest and calm,
once the bait, but now the fuel for my fire.

And this was the reason that little by little
I set my shoulders to the amorous burden, <note>original: "amorosa salma." This could refer to the physical remains of a loved one or, more metaphorically, the heavy weight of love's obligations.</note>
to acquire the desired palm, <note>A classical symbol of victory or spiritual triumph.</note>
for which, in asking, I have already become hoarse.

Through reflection, the holy rays used to shine;
I once saw it adorned and glowing with splendor,
such that mortal sight failed within it.

If it gave me pleasure then, now it gives me woe,
finding it deprived and lacking of every good:
thus, often one loses where one gains.

---

## XIII

->[ Deprived of his lady, he cannot live. ]<-

Will your weeping, eyes, ever have an end?
Will the river of tears ever run dry?
I know not; but from what my heart now presumes,
I fear not; Fortune has turned her cloak. <note>original: "vòlto ha Fortuna ammanto." A metaphor suggesting that Luck has changed its appearance or turned its back on the poet.</note>

I used to live happily in sweetness, feast, and song,
because the holy light
of my beautiful Sun <note>A standard Petrarchan metaphor where the lady is compared to the sun, providing light, life, and guidance to the lover.</note> and that celestial deity
was favorable to me, which made me so glad.

Now, since the holy light is taken from me,
which showed the way through the labyrinth, <note>original: "ambage." Derived from Latin, referring to a winding path, a maze of circumstances, or ambiguous speech.</note>
I see myself left confused in the shadows.

And if such a path leads me to death,
it is no wonder, for my own destruction
I cannot see, because the truth is hidden from me.

<note>Vocabulary: Rhymes, sonnet, Fortune, love, weeping, soul, light, death</note>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (XII and XIII) expressing the poet's grief and disorientation. Sonnet XII reflects on the irony of losing one's peace while striving for love, while Sonnet XIII laments the "darkness" and despair the poet feels after being separated from his lady.</summary>

<keywords>Petrarchism, Fortune, mourning, Sun metaphor, labyrinth, Italian lyric poetry, courtly love</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Rhymes" sequence. The previous page (151) featured Sonnets XII and XIII, where the poet mourned the loss of his lady's presence, comparing her absence to a darkening of the sun. This page moves into Sonnet XIV, which uses the myth of Apollo and the laurel to reflect on the fragility of happiness, and Sonnet XV, which utilizes classic Petrarchan paradoxes to describe the internal turmoil of unrequited or conflicted love.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### XIV

->[ “It is a light thing to change joy into horror.” ]<-

The tree that once so greatly pleased Phoebus, <note>original: "Febo." Referring to Apollo, the god of the sun and poetry, who loved the nymph Daphne. She was transformed into a laurel tree to escape him.</note>
more joyful or more happy than other plants
both for itself and for its dear lover,
shady and green it once lay upon the earth.

And then, I know not by whose fault it arose,
that Phoebus turned his holy eyes away
from the happy plant and its beautiful form,
which is the cause of many tearful waters.

The joyful and green fronds changed color,
and the laurel, <note>original: "lauro." The laurel is a symbol of both poetic glory and the poet’s beloved.</note> which was before shady and flourishing,
was transformed as the Phoebean rays changed. <note>original: "febei raggi." The light or favor of Apollo/the sun.</note>

Pains are always ready and fertile:
it is a light thing to change joy into horror,
whence it is necessary that all hope should fall.

### XV

->[ He lives in perpetual contradiction with himself. ]<-

I follow with desire what most displeases me,
and for more life I often crave my end,
and to escape from death I call upon death,
I seek quiet where there was never peace.

I go after that which I flee and which undoes me,
and I love my enemy much more than myself,
and I am not sated by a bitter food,
I want liberty and servitude pleases me.

Ice amidst the fire, and spite within pleasure,
life amidst death, and war within peace
I seek, and to flee from where I bind myself.

Thus in a murky sea I steer my vessel:
it knows neither how to stay among the waves nor go to land,
and fear has driven out too much suspicion. <note>This concluding line suggests a state of total emotional paralysis, where the poet is so overwhelmed by the "fear" of his situation that even the "suspicion" of other dangers no longer affects him.</note>

<note>Vocabulary: Phoebus, laurel, Phoebean, sonnet, rhymes, contradiction</note>

<summary>Sonnet XIV uses the classical imagery of Apollo and the laurel to meditate on how quickly divine favor and happiness can turn to sorrow, while Sonnet XV explores the psychological state of the lover through a series of dramatic contradictions and oxymorons.</summary>
<keywords>Phoebus Apollo, laurel, poetic transformation, emotional paradox, Petrarchan style, internal conflict, Greek mythology in Italian poetry</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page concludes the current selection of the "Rhymes" sequence. It follows Sonnet XV, which ended on the previous page with a series of Petrarchan paradoxes regarding the speaker's internal turmoil. Sonnet XVI continues this theme of emotional suffering, focusing on the poet’s resignation to his fate and his willingness to embrace death as a final act of devotion.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### XVI

->[ He would die for her with joy. ]<-

Full of bitter sighs and of grief,
full of various thoughts, afflicted and sad,
I go passing the rest of my life,
as it pleases him who is my lord. <note>original: "mio signore." In the tradition of Petrarchan poetry, the poet refers to Love (Amor) as a sovereign ruler who dictates his emotional state.</note>

And, as Fortune follows her usual course,
I fear that I may soon come to that end
where she who is the cause of this will feel pity,
only when I am finally free from so many pains. <note>The poet suggests that his lady's compassion will come too late—only after his death has ended his suffering.</note>

Thus, among these sighs and tears of mine,
I shall nourish my life, until it seems to Clotho
and her sisters that the thread should snap. <note>In Greek mythology, the three Fates (Moirai) control human destiny. Clotho spins the thread of life, while her sisters Lachesis and Atropos measure and cut it.</note>

But my heart is emptied of every sorrow,
if through death I obey those holy lights; <note>original: "lumi santi." A common poetic metaphor for the beloved's eyes, suggesting they possess a divine or celestial power over the lover.</note>
for it will be life to me to be removed from her.

<summary>In Sonnet XVI, the poet expresses a weary resignation to his suffering, finding a paradoxical sense of peace in the idea that death—decreed by his lady's "holy lights"—would be a relief from his earthly pain.</summary>
<keywords>Sonnet XVI, Petrarchan paradox, Fortune, Clotho, Fates, holy lights, devotion, martyrdom of love</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Rhymes" section, transitioning from the sonnets to the first Sestina. While the previous page (Sonnet XVI) saw the poet resigned to death as a final act of devotion, this page explores the cyclical nature of his despair through the rigid structure of the sestina form.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### SESTINA I

->[ Bereft of hope in Love, he awaits death. ]<-

->1<-

How many times because of my excessive hope,
since I first came under the yoke of Love, <note>original: "giogo d' Amore." A classic metaphor in Renaissance poetry, depicting Love as a master who binds the lover like an animal to a plow.</note>
have I bathed my breast with bitter tears!
And how many times, still hoping for peace
from those holy lights, have I desired life, <note>original: "santi lumi." A recurring metaphor for the beloved's eyes, suggesting they are divine or celestial orbs that guide the poet's soul.</note>
and then, to avoid a worse evil, have I called for death!

->2<-

And now I am reduced to this: that unless death
comes soon, I have no other hope in the world,
so unhappy and wretched is my life.
Are these then your promises, Love?
Is this then the longed-for peace?
If one must call these sad tears "peace."

->3<-

Whoever hopes for anything from Love other than tears,
or a life that is less cruel than death,
or to ever taste even a single hour of peace,
that person lives in vain and in false hope;
because one does not become a servant of Love
without experiencing a thousand deaths while still in life.

->4<-

There was a time when my life was tranquil;
but one cannot know what tears are,
unless one is first a servant of Love.
Nor can one know what it is to live without death,
or how vain all human hope is,
nor will he ever be content who desires peace.

<term>Sestina: A highly structured poetic form consisting of six-line stanzas. Instead of traditional rhymes, the sestina relies on the repetition of six specific end-words (here: hope, Love, tears, peace, life, and death) that rotate in a mathematical pattern throughout the poem.</term>

<summary>In the opening stanzas of Sestina I, the poet reflects on the obsessive and painful nature of his devotion, using the repetitive structure of the poem to mirror his inability to escape the cycle of hope and despair.</summary>
<keywords>Sestina I, Love's yoke, holy lights, Petrarchan imagery, emotional cycle, martyrdom, poetic form</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Rhymes" section, specifically the conclusion of Sestina I. The previous stanzas established the poet's entrapment in a cycle of suffering, where the traditional "end-words" of the sestina—hope, Love, tears, peace, life, and death—are woven together to show his lack of resolution.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

->5<-

He who defined the human way of living took all peace
entirely from our mortal life,
and left hope as the cause of every ill.
This hope makes us endure sad weeping,
makes others bear a thousand deaths,
and, what is worse, makes one a servant of Love.

->6<-

Love is not born in a noble heart <note>original: "gentil core." A central concept in Italian Renaissance poetry, implying that only a refined or "gentle" soul is capable of experiencing true, elevated Love.</note>
before the hope for peace joins with desire—
a hope that does not depart until death arrives.
I do not speak of that dying one does while still in life, <note>A reference to the "living death" or "sweet agony" common in Petrarchan poetry, where the lover feels as if they are dying while remaining physically alive.</note>
but of that death which ends all mortal weeping,
and which remains the firm hope for a better life.

->7<-

I, who have no hope of finding Love favorable,
but must dwell in weeping and without peace,
shall await death for the sake of a better life.

**Vocabulary:** Love, peace, death, hope, weeping, life.

<summary>This page concludes the poet's first sestina, a complex poem where he argues that hope is a deceptive force that leads only to servitude and suffering. He ultimately concludes that physical death is the only hope for a "better life" and a release from the emotional turmoil of love.</summary>

<keywords>Sestina, noble heart, Petrarchism, unrequited love, living death, suffering, hope</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

# SONG I

->[He well knows that Love has made him a slave; but he neither knows how nor wishes to free himself from his chains.]<-

Love, I see that you are still not satisfied
with my ancient sufferings,
for other snares and chains
you go on fashioning, ever more bitter and strong
<margin>5</margin> than your usual ones; so that all my hope
of any favorable outcome
is now carried away by the wind,
nor do I hope for liberty except through death.
O blind, O little-aware
<margin>10</margin> minds of wretched lovers!
Who, in those beautiful holy lights <note>original: "be' lumi santi." In Renaissance poetry, the lady's eyes are frequently referred to as "lights" or "stars" that guide or blind the lover.</note>
would have nevertheless expected such bitterness?
Nor did it seem that her features
promised us such harshness.
<margin>15</margin> Thus I have surrendered myself to another’s power,
nor do I hope ever to be who I once was.
I recognize now my former liberty,
and the honest and happy time,
and my quiet state,
<margin>20</margin> which my kind fortune once gave me.
But then, as every good thing turns back,
she <note>referring to Lady Fortune, the personified force of fate often depicted as fickle and turning a wheel.</note> became my enemy,
and to give me trouble,
Love and she conspired as one;
<margin>25</margin> as if one part of such great force were not enough
for someone who, of his own accord, strives
to bind himself ever more and more tightly,
and like a simpleton,
not looking further than the surface, <note>original: "scorza." Literally "bark" or "rind," here referring to the outward physical beauty of the lady which hides the pain her rejection will cause.</note>
<margin>30</margin>

<vocab>Love, Song, Rhymes, lyric, liberty, fortune</vocab>

<summary>This page begins a "Canzone" (a structured Italian lyric poem) in which the poet laments his total surrender to Love's power. He reflects on his lost freedom and describes how both Love and Fortune conspired to lead him into a state of emotional servitude.</summary>

# III - RHYMES

with my own hands I helped them fashion the snares,
so that he might make me more and more a slave.
A little bird or a simple animal,
if there is revealed to it
<margin>35</margin> a trap that certainly
shows itself to be a disturber of its peace,
keeps its eye wider open the second time;
for it is natural reason <note>original: "ragion naturale." The poet contrasts the basic survival instinct of animals with the irrational behavior of a human lover.</note>
that everyone should flee from their own harm;
<margin>40</margin> and I, who see that it deceives and destroys me,
am nevertheless pleased to follow
the path in which I see
past harm and worse to come,
as if I did not have a hundred examples.
<margin>45</margin> But in such a way has
Love extinguished in me every sign of reason,
that I would not want to find a remedy or restraint
that would take from me the desire to burn forever.
So much power have the amorous deceptions had,
<margin>50</margin> and my ancient martyrdom, <note>original: "martirio." In the tradition of courtly love, the lover’s suffering is often described in religious terms as a form of martyrdom for his "faith" in the lady.</note>
that I have no greater enemy
to all my peace than myself:
nor do I seek anything else, nor for anything else do I labor,
except for how I might deceive myself;
<margin>55</margin> and I add to my own losses,
and call my manifest ill my salvation.
I rejoice if it is granted to me
to dwell in sighs and in grief:
I hold in hatred whoever strips me
<margin>60</margin> of my servitude, and seeks to make me free;
and, seeing myself being bound,
it seems to me that whoever does it <note>referring to Love or the personified force of his passion.</note> wishes to give me liberty.
Thus I rejoice in my harm and grieve at my good,
and that which I seek, I then take away from myself.
<margin>65</margin> Thus Fortune and my enemy Love,
amidst dark and uncertain hopes,

<summary>In this section of the poem, the speaker describes the paradox of his desire: unlike a simple animal that learns to avoid traps, he willingly helps Love "fashion the snares" that bind him. He admits to being his own worst enemy, choosing to find "salvation" in the very suffering and servitude he endures.</summary>

# III - RHYMES

clear and manifest pains
have held me and passed a full five-year period; <note>original: "lustro." A lustrum is a period of five years, suggesting the poet has been suffering for a specific, measurable length of time.</note>
and under a thousand skins and wicked disguises
<margin>70</margin> the flower of my youth
under a cruel master <note>The poet refers to the personified Love (Amor), often depicted in this era as a tyrannical lord who demands absolute service.</note>
I have consumed, and I hope for joy no more.
Love, you indeed know the truth
of my complete faith,
<margin>75</margin> for which I should, as a reward, <note>original: "merzede." In the tradition of courtly love, "mercy" or "merzede" is the favor or pity the lover expects in exchange for his unwavering service.</note>
have shown at least some small sign;
now I am so near to the kingdom
of her whom whoever believes they can flee is a fool, <note>This "kingdom" belongs to Death; the poet acknowledges he is reaching the end of his life.</note>
that, even if the rest of my life were happy,
<margin>80</margin> as much as it can be, it will not pay for the past.
My song, keep your laments to yourself,
and our bitter grief
you shall not show in any place;
but hide your bitter torment so much,
<margin>85</margin> that Love, Death, or Fortune may provide a remedy.

<summary>The poet laments five years of wasted youth spent serving a cruel Love and expresses that even a happy future cannot compensate for his past suffering. He concludes by instructing his song to keep its sorrows private, hoping for an end brought by Love, Death, or Fate.</summary>

# III - RHYMES

### XVII

->[His life, previously happy, Love has made similar to death.]<-

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I do not know what cruel fate, or what wicked lot,
what adverse destiny, or gloomy planet, <note>In the Renaissance, "planets" were believed to govern individual destinies through astrological influence.</note>
has made my life—which was, as it should be, happy—
so very much like death.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Love well knows that I always stood firm,
stronger than adamant, or any harder stone: <note>original: "adamante." Adamant was a legendary mineral of extreme hardness, often used as a metaphor for an unbreakable heart or will.</note>
if a false opinion forbids me from my beloved, <note>original: "mio ben." Literally "my good," a common poetic term for one’s lover or source of happiness.</note>
it seems I bear the loss without any fault of my own.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But cruel Fortune cannot be so much
opposed to me, that excessive indignation
could turn me even one step from my original path.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I would sooner choose to remain in sorrow and in weeping
under my ancient lord and my first banner, <note>The "ancient lord" is the personified god Love. The "first banner" (segno) refers to his initial vow of loyalty to his lady.</note>
than to rejoice under another, weary of weeping.

### XVIII

->[Love and Fortune fight within him: and he does not know who will emerge victorious from the struggle.]<-

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Love promises to give me peace one day
and keep me content within his kingdom:
then Fortune breaks every plan,
and brings my every hope to shame.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A beautiful face, adorned with pity,
ensures that I go contentedly to my death;
Fortune, who holds all my well-being in contempt,
yet leaves me surrounded by my accustomed sighs.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Thus, I do not know what the end of this long war
will be, or whose prey I shall become,
after so many hopes and so many anxieties.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;One, I know, has already conquered heaven; the other governs
the earth alone: hence I must believe <note>The poet contrasts Love, a divine force that "conquered heaven," with Fortune, who rules the unpredictable material world of Earth.</note>
that one day I will be satisfied with my losses.

<term>Love</term>: personified as a powerful deity or lord.
<term>Fortune</term>: the personification of luck or fate, often depicted as a fickle woman turning a wheel.
<term>Sonnet</term>: a fourteen-line poem with a specific rhyme scheme, used here for personal reflection.
<term>Rhymes</term>: the general title for a collection of lyric poetry.
<term>Lyric poetry</term>: poetry expressing the writer's emotions, usually briefly and in stanzas.

<summary>In these two sonnets, the poet describes his life as a battlefield between the divine power of Love and the cruel whims of Fortune. Despite his suffering, he remains loyal to his "ancient lord," Love, even as Fortune thwarts his every hope for peace.</summary>
<keywords>Love, Fortune, Fate, suffering, loyalty, sonnet, Renaissance poetry, internal conflict</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

### XIX

->[The sun itself is struck with wonder as it admires the lady’s beauty.]<-

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Love, from whom jealousy never departs,
who guides my every thought and my slow step,
had led me to the place where once
I was content, though now he no longer wishes me to be.
While I turned my weary eyes,
I saw the golden hair that was scattered in the wind,
and the beautiful planet, <note>In the Renaissance, following the Ptolemaic system, the Sun was considered a "planet" that circled the Earth.</note> gazing so intently,
that it checked the course of its path.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I, as a lover, heading toward the greater harm,
thought at first that he wished to return to the fire: <note>The "fire" refers to the sun’s own heat or perhaps a desire to compete with the lady’s radiance.</note>
but his hidden desire did not remain so for long.
His appearance showed me clearly that he was not
my rival, for he passed on, after staying a while—
I know not if stupefied or envious.

### XX

->[The sun shines more brightly, fearing it might be surpassed by the radiance of his lady’s eyes.]<-

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Since the sun has returned to its ancient course,
Phoebus <note>Phoebus Apollo is the classical god of the sun.</note> resumes his accustomed light,
and now one sun, then the other, shines so much
that already the rigid winter has become sunny and mild.
If the first sun [the lady] be propitious and friendly to me,
as that one which lights the world shows himself to be,
my soul may expect quiet from its pains,
and from this cruel, wicked, bitter, and hostile life.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If Phoebus has made himself so much brighter
than usual, and shines now more than he is wont to do,
and if he has further rekindled his little flames,
he did so because he feared the two stars <note>In Petrarchan poetry, the lady's eyes are frequently compared to "two stars" (duo stelle).</note>
might surpass the flame of the sun,
and that there might be in the world a blessing as rare as he.

<vocab>Rhymes, Love, jealousy, planet, Phoebus, sun, winter, sonnet</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (XIX and XX) that use solar imagery to praise the lady's beauty, suggesting her eyes and hair are so radiant they cause the sun itself to pause in wonder or compete in brightness.</summary>
<keywords>sonnets, solar imagery, Phoebus, beauty, eyes as stars, Renaissance poetry, Love and Jealousy</keywords>
