# III - RHYMES

### XXI

->[His only consolation is that Love, in all that he has done to him,<-
->has done so unjustly.]<-

Alas, already the sun has completed five cycles, <note>The "five cycles" refer to five years having passed since the poet fell in love.</note>
since Love first held me in his snares,
and the amorous thought entered my soul,
and Fortune continues to do what she is accustomed to do.
Tears, prayers, sighs, verses, and words,
which could not be written even with a thousand pens,
and the hope that once sustained my heart
I see brought to nothing, as my destiny wills.
Nor does there remain for me any but a single comfort,
because every other thought induces me to crave death:
that whatever Love has done to me, he has done unjustly. <note>In the courtly love tradition, the lover finds moral superiority in being an innocent victim of an unrequited or "unjust" passion.</note>
There is no happier fate in the world
for a noble soul, <note>original: "gentil alma." In Renaissance poetry, a "gentle" or noble soul is one refined by love and virtue, regardless of social rank.</note> if it sees clearly
that it has used its brief hours well.

---

### XXII

Sonnet composed for a certain occurrence that revealed itself every day in a thousand ways.

Fortune, as is her custom, still mocks me,
and forever encumbers me with vain hopes:
then she changes in an instant, and shows that a shadow
is all that mortals can think of or see.
Now she becomes kind and now she grows harsh,
now she fills me with thoughts, and now she clears them away,
and she makes the frightened soul shy away, <note>original: "aombra." This term literally refers to a horse taking fright or bolting when it sees a shadow.</note>
nor does the soul yet seem to realize its own misfortune.
Our nature fears, hopes, rejoices, and grieves
a good thousand times a day:
often misfortune makes it glad, and prosperity makes it sad.
It hopes for its own harm, and is afraid of what is good:
so short-sighted is our mortal life.
In the end, every thought and care is in vain.

<sig>11</sig>
<meta>LORENZO THE MAGNIFICENT, Works - 1.</meta>

<summary>In these two sonnets, Lorenzo de' Medici reflects on the five-year duration of his unrequited love and the psychological instability caused by the whims of Fortune. He concludes that human perception is flawed and mortal efforts are ultimately fleeting.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance poetry, Sonnets, Fortune, Unrequited Love, Passage of Time, Human Nature, Short-sightedness</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of sonnets from Lorenzo de' Medici's "Rime." The previous sonnets (XXI and XXII) established the poet's struggle with the passage of time—specifically five years of unrequited love—and the capricious nature of Fortune. On this page, the tone shifts toward a deeper melancholy as the poet contemplates the total loss of hope and his resignation to a "fatal destiny."</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### XXIII

->[As hope fails, grief grows, and death becomes desirable.]<-

I feel growing more from day to day
that burning desire that set my heart ablaze,
and already the hope, which once defended it,
is failing, and with it all the adorned years of my life; <note>The "adorned time" (tempo adorno) refers to the poet’s youth and the time spent in the pursuit of beauty and love.</note>

life flees away without pause,
Fortune opposes all my undertakings,
wherefore to the days and nights spent in vain
I return, not without new tears.

However the grief, which was once so sweet to me,
and the soft lamenting—sustained by hope,
which once made sighs and weeping please me—

now that hope is missing, it must finally
increase, and the heart remain crushed in such sorrow,
that death would be among the lesser pains. <note>The poet employs a classic Petrarchan paradox: the suffering of love was "sweet" as long as hope existed. Without hope, the pain becomes unbearable, making death seem like a mercy.</note>

### XXIV

->[He will follow his sad fate, unless his lady<-
->or death makes him turn his path.]<-

Those beautiful, graceful eyes, which grant Love
power and lack of power, just as they please, <note>In Renaissance poetry, the lady's eyes are often described as the source of the God of Love's power over the poet.</note>
have made and make me so hate my peace,
that I consider it my primary affliction.

Nor, though I think of my eternal harm
and of time, volatile and fleeting,
or of the wicked hope, vain and fallacious,
do I yet perceive the manifest deception.

But I go on following my fatal destiny;
nor will I stop, unless my lady or death
should make me turn aside my path.

The hours of my life, whether long or short,
I have consecrated to her, because the wretched
heart has nowhere else to find comfort.

<vocab>Rhymes, sonnet, hope, grief, death, Love, Fortune, destiny</vocab>

<summary>In these two sonnets, the poet explores the psychological toll of unrequited love, documenting the transition from "sweet" suffering to a crushing despair as hope vanishes and he resigns himself to his fate.</summary>
<keywords>melancholy, unrequited love, Fortune, personification of Love, passage of time, Petrarchan paradox, resignation, mortality</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of sonnets from Lorenzo de' Medici's "Rime." The previous page concluded mid-sentence in Sonnet XXIV, where the poet lamented his "wicked hope" and "manifest deceit." This page introduces two new sonnets, XXV and XXVI, which further explore the psychological torment of the lover through classical allegories of Fortune and Love.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### XXV

->[He knows not which is the greater enemy: Love, Fortune, or Hope.]<-

I do not know well who is my greater enemy,
either wicked Fortune, or more cruel Love,
or excessive Hope which in my heart
maintains and grows the sweet ancient fire. <note>The "ancient fire" (dolce foco antico) is a literary nod to Dante and Petrarch, referring to a long-standing, burning passion.</note>

Fortune breaks every friendly thought;
Love doubles the fierce heat even more;
Hope helps the soul that does not die
because of the sweetness on which my heart feeds.

Nor was any harshness ever so bitter and wicked
as such sweetness is, or cruel death
as much as my life is through this kindled hope.

Either let Fortune be more favorable toward me,
or Love or Hope much less strong,
or let pious death take me, and these together. <note>The poet expresses a state of emotional paralysis where even "Hope" is an enemy because it prolongs his suffering by preventing the release of death.</note>

### XXVI

->[He is attracted by the charms of his lady, like a little bird by birdlime.]<- <note>The "visco" (birdlime) mentioned in the summary was a sticky substance smeared on branches to trap small birds—a common metaphor for the irresistible but dangerous traps of love.</note>

Not otherwise than a simple little bird,
seeing the snares set for its harm,
flees at first, and then returns to the first deception,
compelled by the sweet songs of other birds:

so I flee from the amorous sight,
where the snares are set for my distress;
then the sweet glances and words cause
me to run to my tears as if to delight.

And that which time is wont to do in others,
it undoes in me through various things,
for I recognize less than before the ill I now experience. <note>Typically, time heals or brings wisdom, but the poet claims his experience of love is making him more foolish and less aware of his own danger as time passes.</note>

Blind and without reason I let myself be led
by my blind enemy, and through a deceptive
path, I find myself at last in a blind pit. <note>The "blind enemy" is Cupid, the god of Love, often depicted with a blindfold, leading the poet toward a "blind pit" or spiritual abyss.</note>

<summary>In these two sonnets, Lorenzo de' Medici explores the paradoxical nature of desire, where hope and beauty act as traps that prolong the lover's suffering. He utilizes the classical metaphor of the trapped bird to illustrate the triumph of emotion over reason.</summary>
<keywords>Fortune, Love, Hope, Petrarchism, metaphors, birdlime, snares of love, Cupid, Renaissance poetry</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of sonnets from Lorenzo de' Medici’s "Rime." While the previous page (163) featured the poet’s struggle with Love, Fortune, and Hope in Sonnets XXV and XXVI, this page (164) focuses on Sonnet XXVII. It marks a shift from abstract psychological torment to a specific, vivid memory of the beloved in a pastoral setting.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### XXVII
#### [“Ah, how little every good lasts in the world!”] <note>original: "Ah quanto poco al mondo ogni ben dura"</note>

I saw my lady <term>madonna<gloss>literally "my lady," the traditional title for the beloved in Italian courtly poetry</gloss></term> by a cool stream
standing among green leaves and joyful women,
such that from the first hour that I burned with love,
I never saw her face more beautiful and kind. <note>The original word "pio" translates to "pious," but in this context, it refers to the lady showing "pietas" or mercy toward the suffering lover.</note>

This partly satisfied my desire,
and gave my soul cause to console itself;
but then, departing, I saw my heart remain behind:
my thoughts and my sorrow increased all the more.

For already the sun was sinking in the west,
and leaving the earth shadowy and dark,
wherefore my Sun <note>The "Sun" is a metaphor for the lady; just as the physical sun sets and leaves the world in darkness, the lady's departure leaves the poet in emotional darkness.</note> hid herself elsewhere.

That first joy made my mind much more sorrowful.
Ah, how little every good lasts in the world!
But the memory does not depart so soon.

<vocab>sonnet, my lady, desire, soul, west, memory, Rhymes</vocab>

<summary>This page features Sonnet XXVII, in which the poet recounts a beautiful but fleeting encounter with his lady by a stream, concluding with a lament on the temporary nature of earthly happiness.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Sonnet XXVII, courtly love, transience, memory, pastoral imagery</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---

# III - RHYMES

### CANZONE II <term>Canzone<gloss>A traditional Italian lyric poem consisting of a series of stanzas without a fixed number of lines, often dealing with themes of love, philosophy, or politics.</gloss></term>

->[Better death than staying far from her: yet he is happy
hearing that his exile displeases her.]<-

I thought, Love, that it was time by now
to put an end to the long, harsh, anguished weeping,
and to my sorrow,
not just wanting to continue so much in my misery,
<margin>5</margin> you and Fortune, too unjust and wicked;
for later, when you wish,
as befits such great lordship,
to keep that which you have already promised me
(ah, how many times and how much!),
<margin>10</margin> it will be difficult for you, though you can do all.
The soul, the spirits, and the bones
have been yours under this trust,
as much as you know, Love—and I, who experience it, know better—
that with this hope
<margin>15</margin> as a boy I was your servant, and now I am already an old man. <note>The poet emphasizes a lifetime of devotion, spanning from youth to old age.</note>
I lived content with such a fate,
and only fed my breathless heart
with the sight of her, the beloved;
those beautiful lights <note>original: "belle luce." A common poetic metaphor referring to the lady's eyes.</note> and the divine splendor
<margin>20</margin> quieted the soul, though it was afflicted and sad,
and because of this, every hardship
seemed sweet, which is acquired through loving.
The hope of greater happiness makes
every pain smaller,
<margin>25</margin> but wicked Fortune, envious of my well-being,
wanted to disturb everything,
both my tranquil state and happy fate,
and took from me the sight [of her] for which I always burn.
Alas! Death was better
<margin>30</margin> than staying far from her serene gaze.

<vocab>Love, Fortune, Song, hope, exile, weeping, sorrow, trust</vocab>

<summary>This page contains the opening stanzas of Canzone II, where the poet laments a lifetime of unrequited devotion and the cruelty of Fortune in separating him from the lady whose presence once made his suffering bearable.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Canzone II, courtly love, Fortune, exile, passage of time, devotion</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page concludes Canzone II. The previous page ended with the poet explaining how the hope of future happiness makes his current "martyrdom" or suffering more bearable. This page continues that thought, shifting toward the power of memory to sustain the soul during the poet's forced absence or exile from his beloved.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

Wherefore now, unable to do anything else, I feed my soul
on the memory of that adorned face,
and to her divine ways
with my thoughts a thousand times a day I return.
<margin>35</margin> If Fortune takes those lovely lights <note>original: "vaghi lumi." A poetic metaphor for the lady's eyes.</note> from me
and disturbs all my peace,
it is not because in woods, valleys, or rivers,
where the spirit carries its heavy frame, <note>original: "salma." Refers to the physical body as a burden or corpse carried by the soul.</note>
whether in dark night or day,
<margin>40</margin> my eyes do not always see their sun,
and her sweet words
do not still echo in my ears:
for remembering things beloved and worthy,
though a person may grow old,
<margin>45</margin> is not extinguished early in a noble heart. <term>Noble heart<gloss>original: "cor gentil." In the Italian poetic tradition, this refers to a heart refined by love and virtue, capable of deep spiritual feeling regardless of social rank.</gloss></term>
I go searching the highest hills,
and turn my weary eyes toward that place
where I left my treasure, <note>original: "mio bene." Literally "my good," a term of endearment for the beloved.</note>
there, from which the sad heart never departs;
<margin>50</margin> and with this I nourish it, and with a hope <term>Hope<gloss>original: "spene," an archaic poetic form of "speranza."</gloss></term>
that soon they shall be sated—
unless the coming of death breaks my thought—
my eyes, which for so long have been wet;
and with this, a part
<margin>55</margin> of my suffering I quiet, and I comfort my soul, <term>Soul<gloss>original: "alma," a poetic term for the soul or spirit.</gloss></term>
and in patience I bear
this unjust exile and the bitter, hard fate,
until a happier time returns;
and even if the suffering lasts,
<margin>60</margin> a single hour can restore the lost days.
Song, <term>Song<gloss>original: "Canzon." This final short stanza is the "congedo" or envoy, where the poet addresses the poem as a messenger.</gloss></term> there where my heart is
now you shall go, if the way is not already
barred to you, as it is to me; follow the trail:
say that my life is happy,
<margin>65</margin> hearing that this exile is displeasing to her.

<summary>This page concludes Canzone II, emphasizing how the poet uses memory and the hope of his lady's sympathy to endure the pain of exile and separation.</summary>
<keywords>memory, exile, noble heart, hope, personification of poetry, Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance love lyrics</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

### XXVIII

->[The memory of past happiness doubles the pain.]<-

If it happens that Love <term>Love<gloss>original: "Amor." Often personified in Renaissance poetry as the deity Cupid or the force of desire itself.</gloss></term> with some brief happiness
comforts the soul <term>Soul<gloss>original: "alma."</gloss></term> accustomed to long suffering,
the more that the desired good is prized,
the more I find myself joyful and content.

Thus, if by some prosperous event
hope <term>Hope<gloss>original: "speme."</gloss></term> rises to the summit of every height,
so that desire <term>Desire<gloss>original: "disio."</gloss></term> may grow, bitterness also increases
and doubles my heavy thoughts, a hundred for every one.

Therefore, if I had any comfort on that day,
when among green leaves and icy waters
and happy ladies, I saw those lovely lights, <note>original: "vaghi lumi." A recurring poetic metaphor for the lady's eyes, suggesting both beauty and a wandering, enchanting quality.</note>

being now far away and deprived of them, I return
to my first tears; and that which pleased me most
now seems to burn and consume my afflicted heart all the more. <note>This reflects a famous sentiment in Western literature—found in Dante and Boethius—that there is no greater sorrow than to remember happy times in misery.</note>

**Vocabulary:** Love, rhymes, sonnet, desire, hope.

<summary>Sonnet XXVIII explores the psychological torment of memory, where the recollection of a beautiful day spent with the beloved now serves only to intensify the poet's present grief and sense of isolation.</summary>
<keywords>Love, memory, suffering, sonnet, desire, hope, Petrarchan imagery</keywords>

While the previous sonnet explored how memory intensifies present sorrow, this poem shifts focus to the loss of "libertate" (freedom) and the poet’s total surrender to Love’s power. It maintains the recurring themes of memory and the "dolce tempo" (sweet time) of the first encounter.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### SESTINA II

->[“I utterly hate and flee from freedom.”]<- <note>original: "Al tutto ho in odio e fuggo libertate." This line sets the theme of the poem: the poet's paradoxical rejection of his own autonomy in favor of his obsession.</note>

->1<-

I feel that sweet time returning,
of which I cannot remember without weeping,
which was the beginning of my harsh life,
nor have I ever since known freedom; <term>Freedom<gloss>original: "libertate." This refers to the poet's independence before being captured by desire.</gloss></term>
and, because it is renewed in my mind,
Love <term>Love<gloss>original: "Amore." In this tradition, Love is not just a feeling but a personified master or tyrant who governs the poet.</gloss></term> wants me to create a record of it.

->2<-

Love remembers his victory,
and so he wants the season and the time <term>Time<gloss>original: "tempo." This refers specifically to the anniversary of the day the poet fell in love, usually associated with springtime.</gloss></term>
to be celebrated in verse and in the mind;
nor is he content with my sighs and weeping,
but, rejoicing in my lost freedom,
he insists that my life remain tearful.

->3<-

Since he has become the lord of my life,
I am compelled to do what Love commands,
without ever again exercising my former freedom;
which, if it allowed itself to be conquered at that time
when it was not yet subjected to weeping,
will surely yield now that my mind is enslaved.

->4<-

If I have given my body and my mind to another,
and because of this my life is afflicted,
I must only grieve for these tears
of mine, and not blame Love for this:
for if he has held me for such a long time,
it is because I gave him the freedom to do so. <note>This stanza introduces a moral argument common in Renaissance philosophy: the lover is responsible for his own suffering because his "freedom" (will) initially chose to submit to the "mind" (passion).</note>

<vocab>Sestina, Love, freedom, time, life, weeping, mind</vocab> <note>A <term>Sestina<gloss>A complex poetic form of thirty-nine lines. It does not use rhymes but instead repeats six specific words at the ends of lines in a mathematical rotation. The repetition of the words time, weeping, life, freedom, mind, and Love mimics the poet's obsessive, circular thoughts.</gloss></term> is a showcase of technical skill and emotional intensity.</note>

<summary>In this Sestina, the poet reflects on the anniversary of his "enslavement" to Love, arguing that he cannot blame the deity for his suffering since he voluntarily surrendered his freedom. The poem uses the rigid structure of the sestina form to reflect the inescapable nature of his obsession.</summary>
<keywords>Sestina, Love's tyranny, loss of freedom, memory, anniversary of love, poetic structure</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page concludes the second Sestina of the collection. While the first four stanzas established the poet's initial surrender to Love, these final stanzas (5, 6, and the envoy) depict a total psychological submission where the poet actively rejects his own freedom and embraces his suffering as a form of devotion.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

->5<-

Lost freedom is no longer its own,
because the mind has given away its first gift:
therefore, if he <note>Referring to "Amore" (Love) as a personified master.</note> wants me to celebrate that time
and if my life is to be content with this,
since he always conquered and I yield to Love,
then I am happy, as he wishes, with my weeping.

->6<-

Nor am I only content with long weeping,
but I utterly hate and flee freedom,
nor would I wish not to wish to serve Love;
and I hate every thought that in my mind
arises to make my life free,
and I call any other time lost.

->7<-

Happy and joyful was the time, and sweet the weeping,
in which life lost its freedom,
so the mind proclaims, and so Love wills. <note>This three-line concluding stanza is known as a "tornata" or envoy. In a sestina, it must incorporate all six of the poem's recurring keywords.</note>

**Vocabulary:**
Love <gloss>Amore</gloss>, freedom <gloss>libertate</gloss>, weeping <gloss>pianti</gloss>, mind <gloss>mente</gloss>, Rhymes <gloss>Rime</gloss>, any/whatever <gloss>qualunche</gloss>.

<summary>This page concludes Sestina II, illustrating the poet's complete renunciation of free will. He characterizes his obsession not as a burden to be escaped, but as a "sweet" and "joyful" state of servitude to Love.</summary>
<keywords>sestina, Love as tyrant, servitude, loss of freedom, courtly love, paradox of sweet pain, Renaissance poetry</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the formal structure of the Sestina (concluded on the previous page) to a sequence of Sonnets. While the previous text focused on the poet's total submission and the "sweetness" of his suffering, these poems (XXIX and XXX) explore the physical and psychological spaces where that suffering occurred, now transformed by the actual presence or favor of the beloved.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### XXIX

->[The mere sight of the serene face of his lady
turns ancient sorrows into sweetness.]<-

O fortunate house, that were once accustomed
to hearing my heavy sighs and laments,
keep within you the image of those holy eyes <note>original: "lumi santi"; literally "holy lights," a common Renaissance metaphor for a lady's eyes.</note>,
and despise all other things as worthless.
O waters, O clear fountains, full of sweetness,
which with your murmuring a short while ago
wept with me, now let your harshness
turn into song along with mine.
O bed, true witness of my ancient tears,
and full of my sighs,
O little study <term>studiolo<gloss>a small, private room or cabinet used for reading, writing, and contemplation</gloss></term>, refuge for my grief;
Love has turned our labors into sweetness
solely by the sight of her serene face,
and I do not know why I delay dying any longer. <note>The poet suggests that having reached this peak of happiness, life has no further goal, or perhaps that the joy itself is so intense it feels fatal.</note>

### XXX

->[Why the soul trembles and fears
when the time to achieve its reward approaches?]<-

When the long-awaited hour draws near
to give the reward <term>guidardone<gloss>a formal recompense or prize, often used in chivalric literature for a lover's success</gloss></term> to my faith,
when the attainment of mercy <term>merzede<gloss>the "favor" or "pity" a lady grants her suitor, often the goal of the entire poetic cycle</gloss></term> approaches,
 my wretched soul trembles and fears all the more.
And, like a stranger to itself,
it remains lost, and perhaps still does not believe it,
having been so often deceived; and even if it sees clearly,
it is always uncertain in the thoughts toward which it leans.
And this happens because it deems itself unworthy
of such great good, which is why it trembles pale,
comparing itself to that serene face.
Or perhaps, as Love shows and teaches it,
it stands in doubt, fearing that it might faint away
from excessive sweetness.

<vocab>Rhymes <gloss>Rime</gloss>, Love <gloss>Amor</gloss>, reward <gloss>guidardone</gloss>, mercy/favor <gloss>merzede</gloss>, sonnet <gloss>sonetto</gloss></vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets describing the poet's psychological state as his lady's favor turns his past sorrows into "sweetness." He expresses a paradoxical fear and unworthiness when finally faced with the "reward" for his long devotion.</summary>
<keywords>sonnet, Petrarchan imagery, the beloved's face, psychological anxiety, transition from grief to joy, courtly reward, studiolo</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

### XXXI
#### [“Your hope is foolish”.]

Love had led me to the very edge
of my hope, and the time for it had finally come:
that which is so greatly desired and hoped for was near,
for which I sigh so much and groan so much.
When I heard a voice, at which I still tremble,
rigid, harsh, cruel, unjust, and fierce:
— Your hope is foolish and your desire arrogant
to seek that which alone is supreme.
Let it suffice you to gaze upon my lovely eyes <note>original: "vaghi lumi"; literally "wandering" or "charming lights," a standard poetic metaphor for eyes.</note>,
and to hear the harmony of my words,
and to contemplate high divine virtues.
What you presume to have from me beyond this
is a vain thought, and, if your heart desires more,
let it complain not of me, but of its own end. — <note>The "end" here refers to the natural limit or boundary of the lover's reach; the lady (or the voice of Reason) reminds the poet that her physical or spiritual essence has a "supreme" quality that cannot be fully possessed by a mortal.</note>

### XXXII
#### Sonnet written for a friend.

He who was snatched up even to the third heaven <note>A reference to Saint Paul the Apostle, who according to the Bible (2 Corinthians 12:2) was "caught up to the third heaven" and saw paradise.</note>
never saw anything so excellent,
and Argus <note>In Greek mythology, Argus Panoptes was a giant with a hundred eyes; he was lulled to sleep and killed by Mercury’s music.</note> never heard such sweet melody,
though to his misfortune such a sound was heard;
and the phoenix, when its end is near,
does not gather so many scents for its mortal frame <note>original: "mortal telo"; literally "mortal cloth" or "shroud," referring to the phoenix's funeral pyre.</note>;
nor was that food and our veil <note>original: "nostro velo"; a metaphor for the human flesh or the earthly condition.</note> so sweet,
which our first parent <note>Adam.</note> tasted to our misfortune.
Nor did Love ever give such sweetness to anyone,
even when he wished to satisfy him fully,
as great as mine is, nor does he want me to think of anything else.
I bless the bow and the arrows
and the cause that took away my liberty,
since you reward me so well.

<summary>This page contains two sonnets: one where a divine or beloved voice rebukes the poet's "arrogant" hopes for total union, and another written for a friend celebrating the overwhelming sweetness of love through classical and biblical comparisons.</summary>
<keywords>Love, Sonnet, Argus, Phoenix, Hope, Saint Paul, Adam, Petrarchan imagery, divine virtue</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Rhymes" section, moving from the celebratory sonnet written for a friend on the previous page to two personal sonnets (XXXIII and XXXIV) that explore the bittersweet memory of lost love and the isolation of the lover's journey.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### XXXIII

->[It is better not to know the sweetness of Love,<-
->than to be entirely deprived of it later.]<-

It would have been better, Love, if I had never
experienced any part of your sweetness or your blessing:
for it is an easy thing to endure pain
for a soul long accustomed to suffering.
Thus, one desires more and prizes more
the good that one has known; from this comes
greater grief to the heart, if, when one possesses and holds it,
Fortune <note>original: "Fortuna"; personified here as the unpredictable force that governs human affairs.</note> forbids, interrupts, and shatters it.
That which I once desired without knowing it,
had led me quite close to death,
seeking that which was uncertain and new to me:
now that I have seen it, I know it and understand it;
think, Love, how hard my fate is,
since I find myself deprived of such a blessing.

### XXXIV

->[Even his sweet thoughts of love abandon him.]<-

Sweet thoughts, do not depart yet:
where, my sweet thoughts, do you leave me?
Do you act so well as a guide for my already tired feet
to the sweet dwelling where my beloved <note>original: "il mio ben"; literally "my good," a common term of endearment for a lover.</note> resides?
Here Zephyrus <note>original: "Zeffiro"; the west wind, associated with spring and gentle breezes.</note> does not blow, here Flora <note>The Roman goddess of flowers and the season of spring.</note> does not dance,
nor are the hillsides adorned with grass and flowers:
silences, shadows, terror, winds and frosts,
woods, rocks, and waters hinder my feet at every hour.
You depart nonetheless, and go to her,
your ancient refuge and that of my heart:
I remain all alone in the dark shadows.
Love teaches the blind path to my feet,
for I have him always within me, shining from one and the other star <note>A poetic metaphor referring to the lady's eyes as guiding stars.</note>,
nor do my eyes have any other light than their object.

<summary>In these two sonnets, the poet reflects on the cruelty of losing love's joy and describes a lonely, treacherous journey through a desolate landscape, guided only by the internal memory of his lady's eyes.</summary>
<keywords>Love, Fortune, Zephyrus, Flora, rhymes, sonnet, sweetness, shadows, loss, guidance</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Rhymes" section. While the previous poems explored the internal landscape of the lover's grief and his isolation, this sonnet (XXXV) shifts focus to a physical object—a small portrait—which serves as a silent companion and a catalyst for the speaker’s fluctuating emotions.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### XXXV

->Sonnet composed at the base of a small panel painting where a woman was portrayed.<-

You are the object of my every thought and care,
my dear image, my harbor and my rest:
with you I weep and with you I find comfort,
whenever I am seized by hope or by fear.
Sometimes, as if you were alive and pure,
I complain to you of every deceit and wrong,
and this vain thought makes me so unaware of reality,
that I would ask for nothing else, if only the illusion <note>original: "l’error"; in this context, it refers to a "sweet error" or a poetic delusion where the mind accepts a fiction as truth.</note> would last.
But then new sighs rise up again from my heart;
my eyes create a wide and tearful river,
and all my torments <note>original: "martíri"; literally "martyrdoms," a common Petrarchan term for the extreme suffering caused by love.</note> are renewed;
when my wretched soul finally realizes
that I scatter my prayers and words in vain:
whereupon I return once more to my original desires.

<term>sonetto</term> (sonnet), <term>rime</term> (rhymes/poems), <term>tavoletta</term> (a small wooden panel used as a support for a painting), <term>ritratto</term> (portrait), <term>martíri</term> (torments/suffering), <term>desiri</term> (desires), <term>miser’alma</term> (wretched soul), <term>prieghi</term> (prayers/pleas)

<summary>This sonnet describes the speaker's emotional interaction with a portrait of his beloved, moving from a comforting delusion that the image is alive to the painful realization that it cannot respond.</summary>
<keywords>sonnet, portrait, painting, unrequited love, delusion, grief, Petrarchan imagery</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

### CANZONE III

->Song composed while a lady was ill.<-

Through many paths and a thousand various ways
Love has tested whether my constancy is true,
as it seemed fit to him and as I have often said:
and, although he has bound me with a thousand knots, <note>In Renaissance poetry, "knots" (nodi) frequently symbolize the entanglements and obligations of love that bind a lover to their beloved.</note>
<margin>5</margin> he was still not quite certain of my faith,
wanting to bind me much more tightly.
And in the early years he formed a plan,
that, if that heavenly face, adorned and pure,
should show itself harsh toward me,
<margin>10</margin> frightened, I would abandon the quest:
wherefore never was a torch <note>original: "face"; a poetic term for the flame of passion or desire.</note>
lit in my lady's heart,
but in her appearance she was happy at my misfortune:
there is no greater sorrow
<margin>15</margin> than seeing another laugh at one's tears. <note>This echoes a famous sentiment by Dante Alighieri regarding the pain of memory and loss, here applied to the cruelty of an unrequited lover.</note>
In this way Love held me for a time,
without me ever experiencing any sweetness other
than contemplating a heavenly thing on earth.
This captured me, and this sustained me:
<margin>20</margin> I remained content under such sweetness
and joyful in peace in the midst of so much war.
Love, who sees that my heart does not stray,
but is firm, made a new plan within himself,
and the untamed, proud
<margin>25</margin> heart of my lady he kindled somewhat;
not much, but just enough
to add some hope to me
to keep me alive through so many troubles;
and then with more boldness
<margin>30</margin> he doubles his betrayals and deceits against me.

<term>canzone</term> (a complex Italian verse form consisting of several stanzas with a specific rhyme scheme), <term>costanzia</term> (constancy; the virtue of remaining faithful to one's beloved despite suffering), <term>sembianti</term> (outward appearance or facial expressions), <term>baldanza</term> (boldness or audacity).

<summary>In this opening of the third Canzone, the speaker describes how the personified figure of Love has tested his loyalty for years, first through the lady's indifference and then by giving just enough hope to prolong his suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Canzone, personification of Love, constancy, unrequited love, emotional suffering, Petrarchan tradition</keywords>

This page describes a shift where Love enlists Fortune to strike the lady with hardship, followed by a final, cruel reversal using the mythical arrows of lead and gold.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

How great my sufferings were then
and how hard and cruel my fate,
is difficult both to say and to believe:
my comforts were tears and sighs,
<margin>35</margin> and hope was already reduced to death,
where I believed I would find only mercy.

But my constancy and complete faith
do not fail because of pains nor are they lost,
but are reborn more green <note>original: "verde"; in Renaissance poetry, green symbolizes vitality, youth, and enduring hope, often associated with the laurel tree.</note>
<margin>40</margin> the greater my every torment was.
In the midst of so much hardship
her beauty always succored me,
and made me value every pain as little.
Love noticed this,
<margin>45</margin> and made a new plan and a new game.

And he sweetly begged Fortune
that she should seek out every new thing
that might be troublesome to my lady.
She [Fortune], who is always willingly troublesome,
<margin>50</margin> decided to make the final test,
and infested her heart with various sorrows.
And because of this my lady
was very pained and sad, and would have been more so:
but she was freed from it,
<margin>55</margin> as Love and Fortune willed together,
for they placed the ultimate remedies <note>original: "saluti estreme"; "saluti" carries the dual meaning of health/safety and salvation, suggesting the lover’s care was the lady's only cure during her time of distress.</note>
in the hands of her faithful lover.
Then she saw certain proof of it,
how constant he was
<margin>60</margin> and how much his faith deserved from her.

When he had done this, he put back
his golden arrow, and drew the leaden one that drives Love away,
and pierced the heart of my living light. <note>This refers to the myth of Apollo and Daphne; according to Ovid, a gold-tipped arrow kindles love, while a leaden one repels it. Here, the lady's affection is suddenly extinguished.</note>
Nor ever since that time has the sun
<margin>65</margin> shown its face kindly to the green laurel,
but the soul was deprived of every hope.

<summary>The poem describes how Love and Fortune conspired to test the lover by bringing hardship upon the lady, only to cruelly extinguish her love for him using a leaden arrow just as he had proven his devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Love (Amor), Fortune, constancy, golden arrow, leaden arrow, laurel, Petrarchan imagery, suffering</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

Wherefore the love that within my heart was boiling,
as it makes the soul noble and worthy,
almost turned into indignation,
<margin>70</margin> it could hardly bear such a wrong:
and such was the discouragement
that my heart felt from such ingratitude,
that it almost left the school of love <note>original: "amorosa scuola"; a common metaphor in Renaissance poetry where the lover is a student learning the disciplines of devotion and suffering.</note>;
but then it was rekindled once more,
<margin>75</margin> thinking upon that beauty unique in all the world.
Love, seeing all his attempts were in vain,
devised a new malice, and the cause
of all my constancy <note>original: "costanzia"; the stoic-like endurance of the lover despite rejection or hardship.</note> he wished to remove;
because, having removed that beautiful human countenance,
<margin>80</margin> it seemed to him that every reason
for my faith was gone, and to this he turned his thought,
and took a portion of beauty away from her
by making that sweet face grow pale,
the very evidence of paradise <note>original: "fede del paradiso"; the lady's face serves as a physical pledge or proof to mortals that heaven exists.</note>
<margin>85</margin> here among mortals, the dwelling-place of every good.
This increases my sorrows,
but it certainly does not diminish my long-held faith;
for from this I shall never be released by
grief, tears, or toil,
<margin>90</margin> nor can you truly take her beauty from her.
For if you indeed strip of your beauties
this noble and honored flower,
and take the feathers from so beautiful a phoenix <term>phoenix: a mythical bird that is one of a kind and reborn from its own ashes; in this context, it symbolizes the lady's unique and immortal nature.</term>,
you take away your own primary preeminence,
<margin>95</margin> you deprive and strip yourself of your sovereign honor,
and of the cause which made you happy.
She is the root of your kingdom;
she is your pride and your glory;
she will give eternal memory
<margin>100</margin> of you to future generations:
while she endures,
she is the guide and leader of this blind world,

<summary>Love attempts to break the poet's devotion by causing the lady to lose her physical beauty through illness or pallor. The poet responds that his faith is unshakable and warns Love that destroying her beauty would also destroy the source of Love's own power and fame.</summary>

# III - RHYMES

->LORENZO THE MAGNIFICENT, Works - I.<-

...your strength and your worth will endure;
but if that living light
<margin>105</margin> is extinguished on earth, Love itself will be extinguished.
Do not surrender, Love, to the power of another <note>The "power of another" refers to Death, who threatens to take the lady and thus strip Love of his dominion.</note>
that which is yours alone, which is your true honor;
ah, show your strength against death!
Love, bring aid to the suffering of us both,
<margin>110</margin> rescue your empire from ruin;
this time, overrule the harsh fates,
so that the noble soul and its mortal shell <note>original: "l'alma gentil e la sua scorza"; the "shell" or "husk" is a common Renaissance metaphor for the physical body which houses the spirit.</note>,
which make you worthily happy and joyful,
may be preserved in the world,
<margin>115</margin> and for me, the life that depends upon her.
Through you, it is clearly understood
that now my constancy <note>original: "costanzia"; the speaker refers to the stoic endurance of a lover who remains faithful despite the threat of loss.</note> is firm and complete.
Put me to no further tests, Love,
for my faith is true:
<margin>120</margin> reserve your strength and schemes for elsewhere.
Go, my song; pray to Love
that he no longer delay in rescuing himself,
for I see his empire in great danger;
and the illness is so far advanced <note>original: "suo mal sí presso"; refers to the lady's declining health which threatens Love's existence.</note>,
<margin>125</margin> that in a little while, no counsel will avail.

<summary>This page concludes a poem where the speaker pleads with Love to intervene against Death and Fate to save a dying lady. The speaker argues that his own loyalty is proven and that Love must act now to save the lady’s life, as her presence is the only thing that sustains Love's power in the world.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance poetry, courtly love, constancy, mortality, allegory of Love, Petrarchan style</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

### XXXVI

->Sonnet composed while traveling to the Maremma <note>The Maremma is a coastal region in Tuscany, which in Lorenzo's time was a wild, marshy, and rugged landscape, often used in literature as a setting for solitude.</note> along the coast.<-

With scattered steps and a wandering mind
I go searching through every harsh path
for the dwelling-place of wild forest beasts,
near where the Tyrrhenian sea bathes and floods;
only to test if the soul is quieted and satisfied
by new sights, for it seems to always
perceive and have before its eyes
those eyes that inflicted the ancient wound.
If I look to the left into some dark cave,
I see her there among the leaves and branches,
a new Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt and the moon, often associated with forests and chastity.</note> who brightens every shadow:
looking again to the right at the salt waves,
it seems she has taken her empire from Thetis <note>original: "Teti"; a sea nymph or goddess in Greek mythology, mother of Achilles, here representing the sea itself.</note>:
thus my sweet pain is always with me.

### XXXVII

->Sonnet composed for a dream.<-

More beautiful than ever and less fierce than before
Love <note>original: "Amor"; the personified deity of Love.</note> showed my dear enemy <note>original: "cara inimica"; a classic Petrarchan oxymoron referring to the beloved lady who, by resisting the lover's advances, acts as a "foe" to his desires.</note> to me,
when the thoughts and toil of the day
had been taken away by the sluggish sleep of evening.
She appeared to my eyes exactly as she was,
only her ancient harshness set aside,
and made radiant by the rays of love:
never did the truth seem so true to me.
At first, to speak, I was both fearful and slow,
as I used to be: then fear
was overcome by desire, and I began, saying:
— My Lady... <note>original: "Madonna"</note> — And in that moment she vanished like a wind.
Thus at one time, fleeing, she suddenly steals away
both sleep and herself and my mercy <note>original: "merzé"; in the courtly love tradition, this refers to the "reward" or "pity" a lover hopes to receive from his lady.</note>.

<summary>This page contains two sonnets by Lorenzo de' Medici: the first describes how the poet's obsession with his beloved transforms the rugged landscape of the Maremma into her image, and the second recounts a fleeting, bittersweet dream of her kindness.</summary>
<keywords>Maremma, Tyrrhenian Sea, Diana, Thetis, Petrarchism, courtly love, dreams, landscape, psychological projection</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

### XXXVIII

->[A vain vision of his lady’s pity.]<-

That haughty gaze, fatal to our mortal eyes,
which extinguishes every beauty that surrounds it,
had fled to take a truce for a few days
with Love <note>original: "Amor"; the personified god of desire.</note>, and to strip the strength from his arrow.
Then Love, or perhaps my fatal destiny—
envious that I might find any respite from my suffering—
showed me my basilisk <note>A mythical serpent whose gaze was said to be lethal; here, Lorenzo uses it to describe the lady whose beauty is both mesmerizing and "deadly" to his peace of mind.</note> adorned with pity:
ah, against Love no weapon is of any use!
At the hour when the chariot that Phaethon <note>In Greek mythology, Phaethon was the son of the Sun god who lost control of the solar chariot. Here, the "chariot furthest from us" refers to the Sun being on the opposite side of the world, meaning it is the middle of the night.</note>
once guided so poorly is furthest from us—
when thought sees most clearly that which it most hopes for—
her beautiful face had laid aside its disdain,
and that grace, which her brow previously lacked,
added pity to her haughty beauty.

---

### XXXIX

->[“I live only through the desire to die.”]<-

I am so certain, Love, of your uncertainty,
that I find my rest in never resting at all;
and I see that I am blind, yet you give me
all the stability of your own fickleness.
I find clarity only in doubts and suspicions;
I laugh at my own weeping, and I sing my laments;
nor do I feel any pleasures other than anxieties and woes,
either a sweeter bitterness or a more gentle harshness.
And only from my darkness do I have light.
I know that I do not know how to want what I indeed want,
and often I feel fear because of my excessive boldness.
An abundant river has left my eyes dry <note>The poet has wept so much that he is "dried out," yet the "river" of his grief remains.</note>:
I change my moods and desires just as I am accustomed to do,
and I live only through the desire to die.

<note>This sonnet is a classic example of Petrarchan antithesis, where the poet uses contradictory phrases—like "sweet bitterness"—to express the chaotic internal state of someone in love.</note>

<summary>In these two sonnets, Lorenzo de' Medici explores the deceptive nature of dreams where his "cruel" lady appears merciful, and reflects on the agonizing psychological paradoxes where pain becomes his only pleasure.</summary>
<keywords>sonnet, Phaethon, basilisk, Love, pity, paradox, Petrarchan style, dream imagery</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

### XL

->[He departs against his will from the sweet thoughts of Love.]<-

I depart, my sweet thoughts,
from you, and I leave every amorous care:
for my fortune, too unjust and harsh,
forces me to do exactly what I would not wish.
Sweet tears and sighs, both pleasant and cruel,
vain hopes and uncertain fear,
which troubled my fragile nature,
go to other hearts, and leave this one <note>The Italian "lei" (her/it) refers back to "fragil natura" (fragile nature), which is a feminine noun in Italian.</note>.
O verses, o rhymes, where every lament of mine
was sweet and I soothed such great distress,
while I lay in happy servitude <note>A hallmark of the Petrarchan style, "happy servitude" describes the lover who finds a paradoxical joy in being "enslaved" by his passion for the lady.</note>,
I leave you against my will, and yet I consent,
as if by force, to the foreseen deception.
But so be it, since I was born to such a fate.

---

### XLI

->[“The sweet is turned to bitter.”]<-

That which I once loved with the greatest desire,
is more troublesome to me now, and displeases me more;
that which was my joy and my peace,
is entirely my war and my sorrow.
The happy time is now more sorrowful and wicked;
that desire, which was ignited, now lies extinguished;
and my hope, once so vibrant,
has become fear; and what I feared has become desire.
That time, which took so long to arrive,
now flees away faster than a leopard <note>original: "pardo"; a common literary symbol for extreme speed and agility.</note>:
thus Fortune <note>original: "Fortuna"; often personified as a goddess turning a wheel, representing the unpredictable shifts in human life.</note> has turned all my fate.
The sweet is turned to bitter, the happy to weeping;
I have become entirely sluggish, slow, and late,
yet faster than ever toward death.

<summary>In these sonnets, the poet describes a painful departure from the "sweet thoughts" of love and the cruel reversal of his emotions, where former joys have transformed into bitter sorrows under the influence of Fortune.</summary>
<keywords>Fortune, Love, desire, grief, Petrarchan poetry, fate, death, transience</keywords>

The verse form changes here from the fourteen-line sonnet to the more complex, repetitive structure of the sestina.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

### SESTINA III <note>A sestina is a highly structured poem consisting of six stanzas of six lines each, followed by a three-line envoy. Instead of rhyming, the poem repeats the same six end-words in a specific rotating order, creating a sense of obsession or circling thought.</note>

->[A sad awareness of his amorous state.]<-

->1<-

Love <note>original: "Amor"; personified here as a deceptive lord or deity, common in the Petrarchan tradition.</note> has held me time after time
under false promises, long and vain,
so much that I am already tired of waiting
and now certain of his false deceits;
for the price of my long, harsh labor
is sorrow, and shame, anger and disdain.

->2<-

And that which most increases all my disdain
is that I have lost my youthful time,
nor can any price or labor win it back for me.
Now, how vain our desires are—
if ever I doubted it, now I am certain,
and from testing them too much, I am afflicted and tired.

->3<-

Not just of other things, I am already tired of thinking,
and I have come to hold myself in disdain,
standing in doubt of the good and certain of the evil;
but the only vengeance for one who loses time
is the repentance of vain undertakings:
shame is the fruit, then, of every labor.

->4<-

Every mortal labor of ours is vain:
but he who is never tired of following Love,
drawn by flatteries false and vain,
and like a wretched man holds other things in disdain,
more than any other, he loses his work and his time,
and is in error most manifest and certain.

| Term | Translation and Context |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Sestina** | <term>A complex poetic form using six repeating end-words (time, vain, tired, certain, labor, disdain) to weave a theme.</term> |
| **Amor** | <term>Love, often depicted as a powerful, sometimes cruel, mythological figure.</term> |
| **Tempo** | <term>Time, specifically the "lost time" of youth spent in unrequited passion.</term> |
| **Vane** | <term>Vain or empty; refers to the lack of substance in worldly desires.</term> |
| **Stanco** | <term>Tired or weary; the psychological state of the aging lover.</term> |
| **Certo** | <term>Certain; the movement from youthful confusion to the bitter "certainty" of old age.</term> |
| **Fatica** | <term>Labor or toil; the effort expended in love, which the poet now sees as wasted.</term> |
| **Sdegno** | <term>Disdain or scorn; directed both at Love and at the poet's own past self.</term> |

<summary>This page contains the first four stanzas of the third Sestina, in which the poet laments the wasted years of his youth spent pursuing a deceptive and unrewarding love. He expresses a deep sense of fatigue and self-scorn, realizing that his efforts have yielded only shame and regret.</summary>

<keywords>Sestina, Love's deception, wasted youth, repentance, moral fatigue, Petrarchan influence, poetic labor</keywords>

# III - RHYMES

->5<-

If I had been as certain as I am now
how much every labor is spent in vain
following Love, and how much time is lost,
perhaps I would have grown tired of the undertaking sooner;
but I hold the snares and chains in disdain <note>original: "sdegno"; a core concept in Michelangelo's poetry, representing a mix of scorn, indignation, and the struggle of the soul against worldly attachments.</note>
now, when my efforts to free myself are in vain.

->6<-

How vain our passions are,
how fixed and certain are weeping and grief,
and how every mortal disdain is in vain,
how much every human labor is lost,
is shown by that which is never tired of fleeing,
which carries away and steals everything: time. <note>Time is depicted here as a thief, a common motif in Renaissance art and literature (Tempus edax rerum).</note>

->7<-

Time passes away, and makes me recognize
my vain works; and now I am clear and certain
of my labor, and I hold myself in disdain. <note>This three-line concluding stanza is called a "tornata" or envoy. It serves as a final summary, weaving together the repetitive "keywords" that have cycled through the entire poem.</note>

| Term | Translation and Context |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Amore** | **Love** – Personified as a cruel master who traps the poet. |
| **Tempo** | **Time** – The ultimate consumer of human effort and physical beauty. |
| **Sdegno** | **Disdain/Scorn** – The poet's growing rejection of his own past desires. |
| **Fatica** | **Labor/Effort** – Specifically the hard work of living or loving without spiritual reward. |
| **Rime** | **Rhymes** – The poetic verses themselves. |
| **Michelangelo Buonarroti** | The author, reflecting here on the vanity of his long life and artistic pursuits. |

<summary>This page concludes Michelangelo’s third sestina, moving from a lament over wasted time to a harsh self-critique. The poet realizes that while he now recognizes the "snares" of romantic love, his age and the relentless passage of time make his efforts to change feel futile.</summary>

# III - RHYMES

### XLII

->[Only Death stands firm amidst the fleeting nature of human things.]<-

How vain our every hope is,
how deceptive every design,
how full of ignorance the world is,
Death, the mistress of all, reveals. <note>Death is personified here as the "maestra" or teacher who provides the ultimate lesson on the reality of existence.</note>
One man lives for songs and dances and jousting,
another turns his intellect toward noble things, <note>original: "cosa gentil"; referring to the pursuit of art, philosophy, or courtly virtues.</note>
another holds the world and its affairs in disdain,
another does not show what he carries within.
Vain cares and thoughts, diverse fates
arising from the diversity that Nature grants,
are seen at all times in this wandering world.
Everything is fleeting and lasts but a little,
so much is Fortune <term>Fortune: the personified force of luck or chance, often depicted in the Renaissance with a wheel to show how quickly she can raise someone up or cast them down.</term> inconsistent in the world;
only Death stands firm and lasts forever.

| Term | Translation and Context |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Morte** | **Death** – The only constant and "teacher" of truth in a world of illusion. |
| **Natura** | **Nature** – The creative force that provides human diversity and temperament. |
| **Fortuna** | **Fortune** – The fickle, ever-changing force of worldly luck. |
| **Caducità** | **Transience** – The fragile and temporary nature of all human things. |
| **Speranza** | **Hope** – Here described as "vain," reflecting the poet's shift toward spiritual resignation. |
| **Vanità** | **Vanity** – The emptiness of worldly pursuits (from the Latin *vanitas*). |

<summary>This sonnet reflects on the absolute transience of all human endeavors—whether they be pleasure-seeking, intellectual, or spiritual—concluding that only Death is a permanent and reliable reality in an inconsistent world.</summary>
<keywords>Death, transience, vanity, Fortune, human nature, mortality, Renaissance philosophy, Hope, Nature</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the shift from the previous sonnet's universal reflections on death toward the personal, internal conflict of the lover. While page 183 established that only Death is permanent, this Canzone explores how the poet remains trapped in the "claw" of Love despite his awareness of time's flight and the vanity of hope.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

# CANZONE IV

->[The uncertainties of love and life.]<-

Time flees and flies;
my youth passes and the happy age,
and long-held hope fails more and more;
nor yet does it find peace
<margin>5</margin>within me that fierce desire, which only death
can extinguish in the afflicted, tired soul:
but Love still holds me under his old claw <note>original: "antica branca"; depicts Love as a predatory bird or beast, emphasizing the poet's lack of agency.</note>
and makes it so that through long habit
I crave my own harm by a natural desire.
<margin>10</margin>Ah, fierce and cruel destiny,
that has given me boldness against myself,
from which I cannot save myself!
At least if hope were to fail entirely,
which I seem to see in her beautiful eyes;
<margin>15</margin>because Love wounds me with these weapons.
At least if no sign
of pity were seen in her beautiful face,
nor if her words were so sweet,
and if that gentle smile
<margin>20</margin>were hidden from my ears and eyes,
and my beautiful Sun <note>The "Sun" is a common Renaissance metaphor for the beloved woman, who provides light and life but can also scorch or dazzle the lover.</note> were concealed from me;
because the soul neither knows how, nor can, nor wants to
flee from that which keeps it alive,
but rather leads it to a more blessed death.
<margin>25</margin>Thus my doubtful fate
allows me neither to despair nor to hope for good;
so that I pray to Love
that he might completely remove this deceptive hope,
or else succor my afflicted heart:
<margin>30</margin>one would satisfy it, and the other would lead it out of error. <note>The poet presents a binary solution: either Love must help him succeed, or remove all hope so he can finally be free of the "error" of his obsession.</note>

| Term | Translation and Context |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Canzone** | **Song/Canzone** – A sophisticated lyric poem consisting of stanzas and a shorter concluding "envoy." |
| **Amor** | **Love** – Usually personified as the god Cupid, who rules the poet with "weapons" and "claws." |
| **Speranza / Spene** | **Hope** – Both terms denote hope; *spene* is a poetic variation. Here, hope is "deceptive" because it keeps the poet suffering. |
| **Disio** | **Desire** – The "fierce" internal force that persists even as youth and time vanish. |
| **Alma** | **Soul** – The seat of the poet's internal struggle between reason and passion. |
| **Rime** | **Rhymes/Verses** – The collective term for the poet’s lyrical output. |

<summary>The poet laments the passing of time and youth, yet finds himself unable to break free from Love's grasp. He feels trapped in a state of "doubtful fate," where the small signs of pity from his beloved prevent him from either finding fulfillment or giving up hope entirely.</summary>
<keywords>Time, youth, Love's claw, deceptive hope, desire, the Sun metaphor, internal conflict</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker’s internal struggle described in the first half of Canzone IV. While the previous section focused on the external beauty of the lady and the poet's lack of agency, this section examines the psychological mechanisms of habit and the "pious fraud" the mind plays on itself to justify continued suffering.</meta>

# III - RHYMES

Alas! For I believed
that a different age and different cares
would make me change my desire and my will:
    because it indeed happens
<margin>35</margin>that time brings and takes away other thoughts,
giving new impressions and removing the old.
Now this gathers even more grief in my heart;
for among a thousand thoughts that gather in it,
as the mind runs through various things,
<margin>40</margin>    it suddenly runs,
leaving others and even itself alone, to this one single thought,
    where it finds a tired rest,
and thus its fortune leads it;
and in this harsh and wearisome life of mine
<margin>45</margin>I rest my wandering thoughts and my afflicted soul.
    I would like to know, Love,
since you show me no help,
for what reason the tired soul still hopes.
    Perhaps through long error,
<margin>50</margin>habit has already turned into a natural course,
and has lost the straight and true path.
Nor do I believe the soul is what it once was:
it goes following desire wherever it leads.
And, because hope sustains it,
<margin>55</margin>it grows and comes along with desire.
Therefore, if this desire is never restrained,
this hope never departs,
although one does not see from where or from what vein
comes the water that might partially extinguish the fire;
<margin>60</margin>Love then has new wiles <term>versuzie: cunning tricks or subtle deceptions</term> and arts.
    Thus I deceive myself,
and from this the soul takes its comfort,
from which my long martyrdom <term>martíre: a standard poetic term for the suffering of a lover</term> takes its cause.
    Such sweetness those eyes have offered
<margin>65</margin>to the heart, that they make it hope:
this makes it consent to its own dying;

<vocab>Rhymes, Love, desire, martyrdom, wiles</vocab>

<summary>In this continuation of Canzone IV, the poet reflects on how aging has failed to cure his obsession, as his mind habitually returns to a single painful desire. He questions why he continues to hope despite receiving no help from Love, concluding that he is trapped in a cycle of self-deception fueled by the lady's gaze.</summary>

<keywords>habitual desire, psychological obsession, self-deception, Petrarchan martyrdom, passage of time, Love's cunning</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

and as vain desire leads it,
it follows that which it does not discern or see;
it does not yet know the evil it experiences,
<margin>70</margin>     and that, which is entirely outside
of its salvation <term>salute: in this context, it refers to both physical health and spiritual well-being</term>, it only desires and asks for,
    and, as Love invites it,
it believes it finds mercy <term>merzede: a lover's reward or the lady’s grace</term> in its death;
nor can it have help from itself any longer,
<margin>75</margin> since it has given the reins of its life to another.
    Therefore let it complain of itself,
rather, of the lovely light <note>The "light" refers to the lady's eyes or her beauty.</note> that induced it
to the blind error, from which its death was born;
    and, if this light led it,
<margin>80</margin> do not think that it will release it so soon,
for what once pleased cannot now displease:
rather, from the first day that there lay in it
that great desire, it drove out from the mind
every other thought, and desire took the mind for itself.
<margin>85</margin>     If it did not defend itself then,
it will not do so now, when it consents to its own harm.
    Now, if it is by my destiny
that it must be so, whether soon or late,
as that destiny wills, it is fitting I follow the path,
<margin>90</margin> until I have reached the final boundary.
    Canzone <term>Canzon: a formal Italian poetic song; it was traditional to address the poem in the final lines</term>, since you were born
at midnight, flee the sun and the day;
    weep for your ill within yourself;
flee the sight of the beautiful, adorned face;
<margin>95</margin>     let your fatal destiny take its course,
since doing anything else is in vain and of little worth.

<summary>This page concludes a poem about the total surrender of the self to an obsessive love, portraying the mind as being led by a "vain desire" it cannot resist. It ends with the poet's resignation to his fate and an instruction to the poem to hide from the world and the lady's presence.</summary>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### XLIII

->[Time flees and flies.]<-

I wept once, as Love <note>The poet personifies "Amore" as a powerful, often cruel deity, similar to Cupid.</note> willed,
at the lateness of his promises,
and at what happened to the both of us—
to me the loss, and to him his honor.

Now I weep, as my love wills,
for time flees, never to return,
and I see that what once was can be no more:
now this is what kills and destroys the heart.

This new sorrow is as much greater than the first
as that one still held some hope;
this one has nothing but vain repentance.

Thus I measure and judge my error within myself,
and I weep; and this weeping, the very condition
of our human life, exceeds every other.

---

### XLIV

->[Vain hope of escaping the torments of Love.]<-

Those sweet first thoughts of mine, with which I
nourished my heart in its most heavy losses,
I feel returning, and the first arts and deceptions,
and the sweet-bitter desire, gentle and wicked.

Alas! how foolish was my belief
that through more serious thoughts and more years
I thought to escape from amorous troubles,
not knowing my own desire well!

But, like a wild beast in some dark wood
that thinks to flee and yet runs to its death,
being wounded by the poisoned arrow,

so I believed I was escaping by running hard
toward an unknown evil; now that I recognize it,
I gladly consent to my hard fate.

<vocab>Love, time, sorrow, hope, error, thoughts, troubles, fate</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (XLIII and XLIV) exploring the themes of aging and the inescapable nature of desire. The poet reflects on how the pain of regret in old age is more acute than the hopeful pains of youth.</summary>
<keywords>sonnets, personification of Love, passage of time, regret, Cupid's arrow, human condition, fate</keywords>

<meta>Following the themes of aging and inescapable desire from the previous page, these sonnets (XLV and XLVI) further explore the cyclical nature of passion and the eventual shift toward spiritual resolution as life nears its end.</meta>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### XLV

->[From time to time, the flames of love are renewed within the breast.]<-

Just as from time to time green plants,
through the winter sun and the earth’s moisture,
produce new leaves and fresh flowers
when the earth takes on a different appearance;
so my Sun <note>The poet frequently uses "Sun" as a metaphor for his beloved, whose light governs his world.</note> and those holy lights <note>Referring to the lady’s eyes.</note>,
and the moisture of my eyes that comes from my heart,
cause Love <note>Personified as the god Amore or Cupid.</note> to put forth new leaves
when the time returns that is always before me.
Two shining stars return to my mind,
and the manners and the words that made me
vile against Love, yet bold against myself.
This doubles the old and the new flames,
and at once I fear and I hope.
Late is this mercy, for the ninth year has fled.

### XLVI

->[“Heaven draws me toward its worthy love.”]<-

Like a lamp at the morning hour,
when the oil <note>original: "umor," literally moisture or fluid, here referring to the fuel of the lamp.</note> that feeds the fire fails,
it seems extinguished, then rekindles, and the flame
becomes greater as it draws nearer to its end;
so, in my wandering and searching mind,
as the oil of every old hope fails,
if a greater fire is still maintained there,
it is because it is already near the end of its suffering.
Therefore, I do not fear this new assault of yours,
nor does the burning torch frighten me anymore,
having reached the end of desires, disdain, and anger.
My beautiful Medusa <note>In Greek mythology, Medusa turned onlookers to stone; here, a metaphor for how the lady's beauty once paralyzed or "petrified" the poet.</note> no longer turns me
to sculpted marble, nor does the Siren <note>Mythological creatures whose songs lured sailors to their doom; a metaphor for the tempting but dangerous power of beauty.</note> lull me to sleep,
because Heaven draws me to its worthy love.

<vocab>sonnet, Love, Medusa, Siren, flames of love, rhymes, lyric poetry</vocab>

<summary>In these two sonnets, the poet reflects on the cyclical resurgence of love after nine years and concludes that his intensifying passion is like the final flare of a dying lamp, ultimately leading him toward a more divine, spiritual love.</summary>
<keywords>desire, aging, memory, Medusa, Siren, spiritual love, Petrarchan sonnet, metaphor</keywords>

### XLVII

->Sonnet composed at Rimaggio.<- <note>Rimaggio is a small stream and rural area on the outskirts of Florence, a favorite setting for the poet's pastoral reflections.</note>

Leave your island so greatly beloved,
leave your delicate and beautiful kingdom,
O Cytherean goddess <note>original: "Ciprigna dea," an epithet for Venus (Aphrodite), who was famously born from the sea foam near the island of Cyprus.</note>, and come to the stream
that bathes the tiny green grass.
Come to this shade, to the sweet breeze
that makes every shrub murmur,
to the sweet songs of amorous birds;
let this place be chosen by you as your homeland.
And, if you come among these clear waters,
let your beloved and dear son be with you <note>Referring to Cupid, the personification of desire.</note>,
for here his power is not yet known.
Take from Diana <note>The goddess of the hunt and chastity.</note> her chaste nymphs,
who now wander free and without any danger,
holding the power of Love in little regard.

---

### XLVIII

->Sonnet composed at Rimaggio for some who were there celebrating.<-

A gentle nymph, graceful and beautiful,
more than Phoebus <note>original: "Febo," the sun god Apollo, known for his many loves, such as Daphne.</note> or any other god ever loved,
has increased the fresh stream with her tears,
where the poor girl was left behind.
There she grieves and often blames now this, now that
cause of her life so bitter and cruel:
since she left Diana, her desire
has turned to obey the third star <note>In the medieval and Renaissance view of the cosmos, the "third star" or third sphere is that of the planet Venus, which governs love and passion.</note>.
And nothing else comforts her sorrow,
except that he who has taken so much good from her,
should return her desired and dear treasure.
Only one doubt arises: that this sad heart,
which has so directly turned toward weeping,
might first become a fountain or a laurel tree <note>A nod to classical myths of metamorphosis, where intense grief or the need for escape leads to the transformation of humans into features of the landscape.</note>.

<summary>This page contains two pastoral sonnets set at the Rimaggio stream, invoking Venus and Cupid to bring the power of love to a place governed by Diana's chastity, while mourning a nymph's unrequited passion.</summary>
<keywords>Venus, Cupid, Diana, Rimaggio, pastoral poetry, mythology, metamorphosis, Phoebus, nymph, Florence</keywords>

## CANZONE V

->[The poet leaves the sad path of love and gives his sails to a better wind.]<-

Love <note>original: "Amor," personified here as a cruel lord or deity.</note>, you wish to make so many trials of me,
and you treat your servants so harshly,
the more faithful, long-standing, and constant they are;
for I no longer wish to serve your unjust laws,
<margin>5</margin>but through new ways 
I wish to go and seek new paths;
because it does not seem that I can hope
for any pleasure in the old way other than anxieties and weeping,
sighs, fear, shame, anger, and disdain.
<margin>10</margin>Had I only known your kingdom
and the life of lovers
that day when my chaste and holy
thoughts I entirely turned
toward you, who seemed to offer me peace,
<margin>15</margin>when I took myself away from myself—
which, the sooner it happened, the less it pleases me!
I had given myself without any reservation,
and as a truer sign
of my complete, pure, and true faith,
<margin>20</margin>I gave no price, but my heart as a pledge;
and the dominion and the state
of my free self he <note>Love.</note> took, where he still sits;
hoping that mercy <term>merzede: the reward or grace a lover expects from Love or their lady after long service</term>
I ought to have for my heavy anxieties,
<margin>25</margin>and that of a thousand promises, at least one
would be true, and that Fortune <note>The personification of luck or fate, often depicted as a fickle woman with a wheel.</note>
would sometimes change her face and clothes.
Now weariness and the years

<summary>In this opening of Canzone V, the poet renounces the painful service of personified Love, expressing regret over his youthful surrender and the empty promises of "mercy" and "Fortune."</summary>
<keywords>Love, Canzone, Rhymes, Fortune, mercy, heartbreak, poetic transformation, Renaissance lyric</keywords>

<page-num>191</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

I see myself having completely
<margin>30</margin> lost, and my age, once blooming and green,
is now without other flowers or fruit,
for time, more than any other thing, is never lost but once <note>The poet laments that youth and time, once spent, can never be recovered.</note>.
But it is no wonder if I was caught,
simple and young as I was:
<margin>35</margin> under such bait you set the hook for me;
because you gave me no mortal thing as an object <note>meaning the lady’s beauty seemed so transcendent it appeared divine, thus more easily deceiving him.</note>
at the hour and the moment
that you made me call myself a servant still,
because she who made me wretched
<margin>40</margin> appeared a divine thing to my eyes;
nor did I believe she could or would want to deceive,
whence the tears and the pain,
which I have suffered to follow her
for six solar cycles already <note>original: "solar sei," meaning six years have passed.</note>,
<margin>45</margin> were a pleasure to me; but now,
that I see every hope of mine to be false,
being completely free of it,
Love, I leave the snares and the chains;
and I give my sails to a better wind,
<margin>50</margin> for in such a cruel storm
navigation was not without peril.
I leave the tearful and sad life
and the tiresome hardship;
and I take a new path, another helm <note>original: "governo," referring to the steering or governance of a ship.</note>,
<margin>55</margin> and with better counsel
I guide my boat through the salty waves,
which was already so close to a reef.
Through another sea I wish to go:
I want to steer the tired prow elsewhere,
<margin>60</margin> where sure rest and a harbor
are not hidden,
which a short while ago were so far from me.
The past makes me wise,
and the hardship, and the time lost in vain.

<vocab>Rhymes, Love, poetry, navigation, metaphor, repentance</vocab>

<summary>In this concluding section of the poem, the speaker reflects on his wasted youth and the deceptive "divine" nature of his beloved. He renounces his devotion to Love, employing a classic maritime metaphor to describe steering his life toward a safer, more spiritual harbor.</summary>

<keywords>repentance, nautical metaphor, passage of time, lost youth, spiritual rebirth, Petrarchan influence</keywords>

<margin>65</margin> My blood freezes in my veins,
when I now reflect within myself
on that harsh, perilous, and wicked life,
and how almost every sense is lost
by one who treads upon a poisonous snake <note>original: "angue," a Latinate and poetic term for a serpent.</note>
<margin>70</margin> while passing along the middle of a path;
who then fears far more than before,
even though he is already out of danger,
having not recognized the evil while it was present;
and that cruel beast,
<margin>75</margin> which he had trodden with a bold heart <note>The "bold heart" suggests he was formerly brave only because he was ignorant of the true spiritual danger he was in.</note>,
he gazes upon with greater
dread, now that he is safe;
so I look back upon my past life,
rigid, impious, harsh, and hard,
<margin>80</margin> nor do I know well which I am more: fearful or glad.
Song <note>original: "Canzona." It was traditional to address the poem itself in the final lines, known as the "congedo" or "envoi."</note>, since we have changed our style,
do not take the usual path,
but comfort the noble soul <note>original: "alma gentile," a phrase signifying a person of refined character and spiritual sensitivity.</note> toward liberty.

<summary>The poet concludes his song by reflecting on the retrospective terror of his past sins, comparing himself to a traveler who realizes only after the fact that he has stepped on a venomous snake. He ends by commanding his poem to seek a new "style" focused on spiritual freedom rather than earthly love.</summary>
<keywords>repentance, snake metaphor, noble soul, spiritual liberty, poetic style, fear, relief</keywords>

### XLIX

->Sonnet written for a friend who has fallen in love anew,<-
->which he sent to the lady.<-

Never did I see the sky so quickly illuminated,
when Jove displays his weapons <note>original: "armi." This refers to the lightning bolts of Jupiter (Jove), the king of the gods in Roman mythology.</note>,
nor does a blink of an eye seem so fast to me,
as I, upon seeing you, suddenly burned;
   and, since your beautiful eyes <note>original: "be' lumi," literally "beautiful lights," a common poetic metaphor for eyes in Renaissance verse.</note> were no more stingy
in giving me peace than they were in binding me,
desiring to do for me what they showed they would,
I hope my bitter tears will become sweet.
   And, although Love <note>The poet personifies "Amor" (Cupid) as a fickle and often treacherous force.</note> is often deceitful,
and hope is vain, and such a path is full
of deceits for simple lovers,
   yet her eyes, which promised me peace,
I know will not keep me too long in these woes,
and will fulfill that which I alone crave and hope for.

---

### L

->Sonnet written to the Duke of Calabria in the name of a lady.<- <note>The Duke of Calabria at this time was Alfonso II of Naples, a close political ally of Lorenzo de' Medici. It was a common courtly practice to write poems in the voice of another person, often as a literary exercise or a social favor.</note>

It was enough to have taken my liberty from me
and to have separated and twisted me from the chaste path,
without also wishing to see me dead
in such torment and at such a tender age.
   You left me without having any pity
on me, who at your departure remained pale and wan,
a true omen of my short life,
no longer prizing my own beauty.
   Nor can I think of anything else but that hour
which was the cause of my sweet tears,
of my sweet martyrdom and sad blessing <note>original: "dolce martir e tristo bene." This use of oxymoron—coupling opposite concepts like "sweet" and "martyrdom"—is a hallmark of the Petrarchan style of love poetry.</note>.
   And if memory were not still
a consoler to distressed lovers,
Death would have already put an end to such great pains.

<vocab>Sonnet, Lorenzo the Magnificent, Duke of Calabria, Love, rhymes</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets by Lorenzo de' Medici: the first is a courtly poem written for a friend to woo a lady, and the second is a dramatic monologue written in the voice of a woman abandoned by the Duke of Calabria.</summary>
<keywords>sonnets, Jove, Alfonso II of Naples, Duke of Calabria, courtly love, Petrarchism, poetic personification</keywords>
