### LI

->Sonnet written regarding some petty poets who claimed Bartolomeo Coglioni<- <note>Bartolomeo Colleoni (1400–1475) was a celebrated mercenary captain (condottiero). His name, "Coglioni," is a pun on the Italian word for "testicles," which appeared on his coat of arms. Lorenzo uses this sonnet to mock those who expected great deeds from him that never materialized.</note>
->was meant to achieve great things, which in the end turned to smoke.<-

Impious Fury within the great temple of Janus <note>In Roman tradition, the doors of the Temple of Janus were opened during times of war and closed during peace. Here, "Fury" is personified as a caged beast of war.</note>
fearsomely rages, bloody and stained:
bound and conquered with a thousand knots,
it seeks to loosen both its hands.

And certainly it labors in vain,
for he who has girded the sword for it,
has already been overcome and defeated so many times,
that if he is cowardly, he will not wish to appear insane.

Therefore, Parnassus and the sacred spring <note>Parnassus was the mountain home of the Muses; the "sacred spring" refers to the Hippocrene, the source of poetic inspiration. Lorenzo suggests the poets praising Coglioni will find no inspiration there.</note>
will remain dry and parched for him,
nor will the green laurel be won by this.

The voice of Echo is known by now, <note>Echo, the nymph who could only repeat the words of others, symbolizes the hollow, repetitive praises of the "petty poets."</note>
nor will Phaethon ask for the chariot again, <note>In myth, Phaethon attempted to drive the chariot of the Sun and crashed; Lorenzo uses this to symbolize a disastrous failure of ambition.</note>
but it will be like the fairy and the treasure. <note>An allusion to folk tales where a "fairy's treasure" (fata Morgana) suddenly vanishes or turns to smoke and charcoal upon being found.</note>

---

### LII

->Sonnet written for the Duke of Calabria,<- <note>Alfonso II of Naples, the Duke of Calabria, was a close ally of Lorenzo. This poem adopts the Duke's voice.</note>
->when the Lady went to the Baths.<- <note>It was common for the nobility to travel to thermal baths for health and leisure. This departure leaves the city in mourning.</note>

— You were so happy just a short while ago,
now you are deprived of all your honor,
O fatherland named after the beautiful flower: <note>A reference to Florence (Firenze), whose name is derived from "Florentia" (the flowering city) and whose symbol is the lily.</note>
what fate now denies you such great fortune?
— Alas, she who makes me so unhappy
always maintained Love within my circle.
Now she has departed, and with her flees and dies
every good, nor am I permitted to be joyful anymore.

Thus I shall always be, until Fortune,
who has taken my treasure, returns it to me,
and restores me to my former state.

I have placed every good and every honor in this one woman:
she can make our days happy or sad,
nor do I hope for us to be happy without her. —

<vocab>Bartolomeo Coglioni, Janus, Parnassus, Phaethon, Echo, Duke of Calabria, sonnet, rhymes</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets: the first is a satirical piece mocking the unfulfilled military promises of Bartolomeo Colleoni and his praising poets, while the second is a courtly lament written on behalf of the Duke of Calabria regarding a lady's departure from Florence.</summary>
<keywords>Bartolomeo Colleoni, Janus, Florence, Duke of Calabria, Satire, Courtly Love, Mythology, Sonnets</keywords>

### LIII

->[The allurements of old love.]<-

If Love shows their beautiful sun to the eyes,
or if thought represents it to the heart,
if it happens that one hears, whether real or imagined,
the angelic harmony of words;
the soul, which still grieves for the past,
trembles and fears for its future harm,
because a flame, which has been recently extinguished,
is still prone to be easily rekindled.
And although the kindling of ancient hope
is no longer in the heart, there is that which is promised
by the gaze, the words, and the sweet smile.
But then disdain <note>original: "sdegno" — in the Petrarchan tradition, this refers to a righteous anger or a defensive pride that allows the lover to break free from desire.</note> still breaks the snares and the chains,
and snaps the bow and the arrows,
when I gaze fixedly upon the past harm.

---

### LIV

->To Feo Belcari.<- <note>Feo Belcari (1410–1484) was a prominent Florentine writer of religious dramas and a close friend of the Medici family. This poem begins a "poetic exchange" where Lorenzo asks a theological question for Belcari to answer in verse.</note>

The spirit, at times withdrawn into itself,
and from the tempestuous and troubled sea
having fled into a tranquil and peaceful harbor,
contemplating, feels doubt and wishes to draw a conclusion.
If it is true that all things proceed from God,
and without Him there is nothing—that is, sin <note>This reflects the Neoplatonic and Augustinian view that sin is not a "thing" created by God, but rather a "nothingness" or a privation of good.</note>—
if by His grace it is granted and given to us
to sow here so as to reap eternal fruit;
such grace only takes effect in one
who is turned toward it and well-disposed to receive it.
Therefore, what is it that disposes us?
I would like it explained to me which comes first,
whether such grace or the good inclination.
Answer now the doubt that has been proposed.

<vocab>Rhymes, Love, Feo Belcari, Grace, Sin, Sonnet, Allurements</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets: the first depicts the psychological danger of returning to a past love, while the second is a theological inquiry addressed to Feo Belcari regarding the priority of divine grace versus human will.</summary>
<keywords>sonnets, Petrarchism, Feo Belcari, divine grace, free will, Neoplatonism, Florentine poetry, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---

The poet returns to the theme of unrequited love, using the contrast between the "fire" of passion and the "coolness" of the natural world.</meta>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### SESTINA IV

->[In vain he seeks to forget the ancient flame among the beauties of Nature.]<-

->1<-

I flee the beautiful rays of my burning sun, <note>In the Petrarchan tradition, the "sun" is a common metaphor for the beloved, whose gaze provides light but also burns the lover.</note>
like a wild beast in the shadow of the foliage,
and I go searching for little brooks and springs
across slopes and valleys and over the highest hills,
where the chaste nymphs of Diana <note>Diana was the Roman goddess of the moon, the hunt, and chastity. By invoking her, the poet is attempting to trade his erotic passion for the cooling, virginal detachment of the wilderness.</note>
go following the animals through the woods.

->2<-

Although I often hide myself in the shade of the beeches,
seeking to defend myself from the sun,
nothing in Diana's world is able
to cover me among the green foliage
from the fire that fears no shadow of the hills,
nor is it extinguished by the water of the clear springs.

->3<-

But my tears create new springs,
which, by frequently soaking the green woods,
flow through the high and most lofty hills;
yet even then the fire of my bright Sun
does not diminish, and the amorous foliage
is reborn greener in the beautiful places of Diana.

->4<-

I believed that through the art of Diana
my pain might pass, and the living springs
might quench the fire, and the shadow of the foliage,
which I go seeking through so many woods,
might be an obstacle to the rays of the bright sun,
and that its power would be less in valley and hills.

<term>sestina</term> <note>A complex poetic form consisting of six stanzas of six lines each, followed by a three-line envoy. Rather than rhyming, it uses a pattern of "lexical repetition" where the same six end-words—here: *sun, foliage, springs, hills, Diana, woods*—rotate through every stanza in a mathematical sequence.</note>

<summary>In this sestina, the poet attempts to escape the burning passion of love by retreating into the cold, wild landscapes associated with the goddess Diana, only to find that his internal "fire" is immune to the cooling effects of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Sestina, Diana, pastoral, unrequited love, nature imagery, Petrarchism</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page concludes "Sestina IV." On the previous page, the speaker sought refuge from the "burning sun" of his passion by retreating into the cool, chaste wilderness of Diana. However, the transition here marks a psychological shift: nature no longer provides a sanctuary, as the speaker’s internal fire begins to consume the entire landscape.</meta>

<page-num>197</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

->5<-

The breeze that blows upon the high hills is fire;
my thoughts increase through the art of Diana, <note>In the hunt of Diana—representing the pursuit of a chaste or distant beloved—the poet finds no peace, only a more intense preoccupation with his own desire.</note>
and the further away it is, the more the sun burns;
and the water of the coolest springs is fire,
and the shadow of the dark woods is fire,
and fire are the waves and shadows, the trees and the foliage.

->6<-

For, although this mortal burden <note>The "mortal burden" (original: *carca mortale*) is a traditional poetic way to describe the physical body, which weighs down the soul and prevents it from finding true rest.</note>
is in the midst of the foliage and upon the hills,
and following the wild beasts through fields and woods
it travels into the beautiful lands of Diana
to seek its remedy in the shade and springs,
yet the heart is never far from the sun.

->7<-

As long as the sun shall light the foliage,
and the springs shall flow through the high hills,
I will follow my Diana through the woods. <note>In this final "tornata" or envoy, the poet resigns himself to his fate: he will continue his pursuit of the beloved (his "Diana") for as long as the natural world exists, even if it brings him only the "fire" of unrequited love.</note>

<vocab>Diana (goddess of the hunt), rhymes, hills, woods, sun, foliage, springs, mortal burden (the body)</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a sestina where the poet realizes that nature’s beauty and the goddess Diana's chaste wilderness cannot quench the "fire" of his love. He ends by resolving to continue his pursuit of his beloved, despite the suffering it causes.</summary>
<keywords>Sestina, Diana, unrequited love, Petrarchism, nature imagery, internal conflict, the body as a burden</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---

Here, the poet shifts focus to the dual external forces of Love and Fortune, personified as adversaries who strike and govern his soul.</meta>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

## SESTINA V

### [Love and Fortune strike the poet with arrows.] <note>The "sestina" is a highly structured poetic form consisting of six stanzas of six lines each, followed by a three-line envoy. Instead of rhyming, the same six words that end the lines of the first stanza are repeated in a specific rotating order throughout the poem.</note>

->1<-

From a thousand directions Love strikes me with arrows,
accompanied by cruel Fortune,
whereby in a single hour I feel a thousand deaths,
and a thousand times the afflicted soul rises,
which, drawn along by a vain desire,
lives and dies, as it pleases the one who rules it. <note>original: *chi la regge*. In the tradition of courtly love, the "one who rules" the soul is the beloved lady, who holds absolute power over the poet’s life and death.</note>

->2<-

But, if it happens sometimes that the one who rules it
does not disdain to obey Love,
and allows themselves to be governed by desire,
then Fortune arrives with a prosperous wind,
and if the sad soul rejoices somewhat,
it is then the cause of a much harsher death. <note>The poet suggests that even a moment of favor from his lady or "Fortune" is dangerous, as the inevitable loss of that favor causes a spiritual death more painful than constant suffering.</note>

->3<-

Thus living is more pleasing, when death
threatens at times, yet hope still sustains <note>original: *regge*. Used here in the sense of supporting or holding up the soul.</note>
the soul entire through every hard trial.
This hope has kept me a servant of Love,
nor ever, though wicked Fortune changes her ways,
have I changed, nor will I change my desire because of pain.

->4<-

Before my firm desire changes,
death will leave my limbs cold;
nor could cruel Fortune do so much
as to prevent the one who rules me from ruling me always.
Who, however, from that which pleases Love,
could lift their thought or change their soul?

<vocab>Love (Amore), Fortune (Fortuna), death (morte), soul (alma), desire (disio), rules/governs (regge), sestina (a complex verse form), strikes with arrows (saetta)</vocab>

<summary>This page begins Sestina V, where the poet describes being caught between the arrows of Love and the whims of Fortune. He expresses a paradoxical state where the "death" of his soul is constantly renewed by his unyielding desire and the governance of his beloved.</summary>
<keywords>Sestina, Love (Amore), Fortune (Fortuna), desire, spiritual death, courtly love, constancy</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues Sestina V from page 198. The previous stanzas established the poet's struggle against the arrows of Love and the whims of Fortune. Having previously argued that hope sustains his soul through trials, the poet now concludes his meditation on the endurance of his devotion beyond the physical realm.</meta>

<page-num>199</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

->5<-

Therefore, in vain the sad soul awaits its reward,
forced to make another’s desire its own;
but, even if Love should let me go free
and this fragile body were to fail through death,
she whom the world honors and who rules me <note>The lady, or "the beloved," is described as having a sovereign power over the poet's existence, mirroring the way God rules the universe.</note>
I shall follow always, in either good or ill fortune.

->6<-

Nor shall Fortune ever be able to boast
that she could make the soul change its will:
for He who rules heaven, the world, and Pluto <note>original: *Pluto*. Referring to the classical god of the underworld; the poet suggests that the Creator's authority spans from the highest heaven to the depths of the afterlife.</note>
made us free, and released from every desire. <note>A reference to the theological concept of "Free Will." While Fortune can affect the body, the soul's ultimate orientation remains under its own control.</note>
You [Fortune] may well hasten my death by some years,
but you cannot untie me from where I was bound by Love.

->7<-

Fortune shall never untie me from Love,
nor shall the soul ever change because of death,
if, after death, desire sustains itself. <note>In this final "envoy" (a short concluding stanza), the poet posits that his love is immortal. If the soul continues to feel desire after the body dies, then neither Death nor Fortune can ever truly end his devotion.</note>

<vocab>Love, Fortune, soul, death, desire, Pluto</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes "Sestina V," asserting the immortality of the soul and its devotion. The poet argues that while Fortune can destroy the body, the soul’s free will and its bond to Love are divinely protected and eternal.</summary>
<keywords>Sestina, Free Will, Fortune, immortality of the soul, Love (Amore), Pluto (underworld), courtly love</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the philosophical conclusion of "Sestina V" to a new lyric work, "Canzone VI." While the previous page focused on the soul’s endurance against Fortune and Death, this page shifts focus to a renewed experience of love. The poet describes a return to his "amorous snares," now viewed with a sense of peace and fortune rather than the previous misery.</meta>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

## SONG VI

->Song composed for Lauretta, lady of P. F.<- <note>The identity of "P. F." is debated, but "Lauretta" likely evokes the poetic tradition of Petrarch’s Laura, here addressed as a specific contemporary woman.</note>

Those charming sweetnesses that Love placed
in those two beautiful eyes where he still sits,
leaving the third heaven <note>In medieval cosmology, the "third heaven" is the sphere of Venus, the planet of love.</note> to come to you;
the lilies, the violets, and fresh roses,
<margin>5</margin>the modest and beautiful countenance that keeps mercy
hidden beneath its graceful veil,
now—when my habits and hair <note>A reference to aging; even as the poet grows older and should perhaps be beyond such passions, he is drawn back in.</note>
should be changing—make me return
to those amorous snares where Love once held me,
<margin>10</margin>until he felt such pity <note>original: *l'increbbe*. Literally, it weighed upon him or he felt sorrow for the poet's state.</note>
for me, weary and wretched; and perhaps now he wishes
to make amends for that past suffering,
as is fitting for a true lord;
and therefore he offered the bright Sun
<margin>15</margin>to my heart, nor does he wish me to think of any other.
Whatever beauty was ever found in a beautiful woman
he has placed in her, and in me as much love
as can be borne for so graceful a being.
Nor did a flame ever burn as brightly as that
<margin>20</margin>which burns and consumes my fortunate heart,
which, happy in the fire, finds its quiet and its rest.
That amorous life,
which once made me hate myself,
I feel returning, but its fate has changed;
<margin>25</margin>for a most happy death
would seem so sweet to me then—sweeter than life—
when, staying near my treasure,
I feel neither pain nor any sorrow.
I only grieve in that hour
<margin>30</margin>when the eye is deprived and fasting from its joy <note>original: *suo ben*. Literally "its good" or "its wealth," referring to the sight of the lady.</note>.

<summary>In this opening of Song VI, the poet describes being drawn back into the service of Love by the beauty of a woman named Lauretta. Unlike his past suffering, he now finds a paradoxical peace and "fortune" in the burning passion of his devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Love, Lauretta, third heaven, amorous snares, beauty, devotion, desire</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page continues "Song VI" (Canzone VI) from page 200. The poet moves from describing the lady's physical features to the landscape that surrounds her, specifically a valley and the Zambra stream. The theme shifts from the poet's internal fire to the external peace found in nature and the paradoxical power dynamics between the Lover, the Lady, and the god Love.</meta>

<page-num>201</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

How much that little valley satisfies my heart,
where either out of wonder the sun often comes
to linger, or as Love draws it there!
How much a gentle breeze contents my soul,
<margin>35</margin> which fills my heart with kindled hope
so sweetly, and breathes so softly,
that it would calm the storm and the wrath
of the sea, even when it rages most!
The wave, clearer than crystal or amber,
<margin>40</margin> of the fortunate Zambra <note>The Zambra is a small stream near Florence, close to the Medici villa at Poggio a Caiano; its mention suggests the author is Lorenzo de' Medici.</note>,
with its sweet murmur sometimes gladdens me,
and sometimes sweetly groans,
and weeps and laughs, just as my heart does.
It calms the rages and the scorns
<margin>45</margin> through this Love, from which I have such peace.
And I well believe he <note>Referring to the god Love.</note> would be, as he once was,
toward my heart, and his cruelty
he would demonstrate by ancient habit;
were it not that she, with her words,
<margin>50</margin> moves him to have greater pity for me—
she, whose beauty surpasses his powers;
and already such great mastery
has Love given her, that not only does she compel me,
but she has encircled him <note>Love himself.</note> with such wonder,
<margin>55</margin> that in the end he has conquered himself.
I see now by proof that every great power
is subject to a greater force:
she conquered me, and Love was her victor;
yet then Love offered no resistance
<margin>60</margin> to her strength and to her worth.
Just as upon beautiful hair of gold a green garland
makes the gold appear clearer and more shining,
and the golden locks make the green seem much more graceful,
so that pity <note>original: *pietate*. In this context, it refers to a blend of mercy, compassion, and refined devotion.</note> which Love sends to her heart
<margin>65</margin> makes her beauty more excellent,
and her beautiful countenance makes that pity more welcome;

<vocab>Love, Zambra (stream), pity/mercy, wonder, rhymes</vocab>

<summary>The poet describes a serene pastoral landscape where the sun and wind reflect his internal peace. He concludes with a philosophical meditation on how the lady's beauty and mercy have even managed to conquer the god of Love himself.</summary>
<keywords>pastoral, Zambra stream, Florentine landscape, power of beauty, mercy, personification of Love, golden hair</keywords>

...for the one through the other is what
makes each more dear and worthy for its own sake:
<margin>70</margin>because that pity <note>In the courtly love tradition, *pietà* (pity or mercy) refers to the lady’s kindness or her willingness to return the poet’s affection; beauty without this kindness was considered cruel.</note> is worth little in the end
where beauty is not present;
beauty without pity is a living death,
and every other disdain is surpassed
by that good which one desires, if it is kept apart from it.
<margin>75</margin>Pity and beauty as companions
Love and Nature have joined together in her.

This union creates a harmony
so sweet that it surpasses every other sweetness:
I cast into oblivion not only pain, but my very heart.
<margin>80</margin>These excellences of my lady
make the soul joyful even when it is most weary,
for great contentment follows great desire.

Love, since you are so merciful
toward me—for whatever reason it may be—
<margin>85</margin>I thank you for such a happy fate:
I only fear that the span
of my life may be shorter than I would wish,
and that this excessive sweetness may extinguish
through death the very root of my well-being within me;
<margin>90</margin>yet the end will not seem
grievous to me, dying so happy.

Song <note>The *Canzona* (Song) concludes with a "tornata" or envoi, a short final stanza where the poet speaks directly to the poem, giving it instructions on where to go.</note>, you shall go into that valley
where my heart dwells, which is always sunny,
above the cool stream:
<margin>95</margin>there you will remain, happy and alone;
take care not to speak a word:
stay where a gentle breeze <note>The Italian *auretta* (little breeze) is likely a poetic play on words, referencing the "aura" or spirit of the beloved, a common stylistic device in Renaissance poetry.</note> blows.

<summary>The poet concludes his song by celebrating the perfect balance of beauty and mercy in his lady, expressing a joyful acceptance of his fate even if the intensity of his happiness leads to an early death. He ends by sending the poem to the sun-drenched valley of the Zambra stream.</summary>
<keywords>Pity, Beauty, Harmony, Love and Nature, Envoi, Valley of Zambra, Lorenzo de' Medici, Courtly Love</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LV

->[Love shines in the eyes of his lady.]<-

What is it that I see inside the beautiful eyes
of my lady? Alas! It is perhaps Love himself.
Even my blinded sight perceived him there,
although the splendor of those eyes overcomes it.
— "Love, why do you not speak to me through them?" —
He replied, noticing my error:
— "Because she wrenched the bow and arrows from my hand,
and bound me with her blond hair.
This lady, with willful violence,
has made me turn my own arrows <note>original: *saette*</note> against myself;
because of her, I hate my ancient star <note>The "ancient star" likely refers to the god Love's own celestial influence or his traditional power, now superseded by the lady.</note>.
I have two stars for one, and each of them
is much more beautiful; and I tremble, for the fountain
of all her mercy is taken away and dried up." —

### LVI

->[ "Ah! Return to see that beautiful countenance." ]<-

Sometimes Love sweetly begs me,
speaking before my breathless heart:
— "Ah! Return to see that beautiful countenance <note>original: *sembiante*</note>,
there where once I accompanied your heart.
It departed because of excessive pain:
I remained behind in those holy lights [her eyes],
where I am still a good witness to so much
hardness before, and now to a merciful ardor.
Return to your ancient, clear, faithful stars:
for through its influence, one pours Love into you,
and the other Nobility <term>gentilezza: a refinement of soul and moral character</term> together.
Just mercy <note>original: *pietà*</note> has made them much more beautiful." —
My sad heart does not respond to this,
but remains silent in uncertainty and fears everything.

<vocab>Love, lady, arrows, star, clemency, countenance, heart, mercy, nobility</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (LV and LVI) depicting the god Love as a captive of the lady's beauty. The poet explores themes of visual captivation, the transition from the lady's "hardness" to "mercy," and the heart's lingering fear of past rejection.</summary>
<keywords>Love, eyes, beauty, mercy, Petrarchan imagery, personification, heart's fear, nobility</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LVII

->Sonnet written at Volterra.<-

If to some sunny <note>original: *aprico*; a literary term for a place pleasantly exposed to the sun</note>, sweet and beautiful place
my soul transports my weary and tired body,
Love is always with me at every step,
with whom alone I weep and speak of my suffering.

Whether in a shady wood or on a rugged and cruel mountain,
I see you, Love, sitting there upon a stone;
if in a valley or a dark and low place,
I see, hear, or think of nothing but him.

Nor does my sad heart know what to do anymore:
either flee to its death in those beautiful eyes,
or else die constantly far from them.

It says to itself: — "If once I burned in those eyes,
my dying was sweet, my fate happy,
so it is better that I die in those beautiful eyes." —

---

### LVIII

->[Love returns as sovereign to the afflicted heart.]<-

— "How do you return, Love, inside the afflicted
heart, which was peaceful because of your departure?"
— "I return to the seal I impressed <note>original: *impresso mio sigillo*; a common Renaissance metaphor where Love 'stamps' his image or authority onto the lover's heart like wax</note>
within the heart pierced by those beautiful eyes."

— "Alas, I believed that it had expired <note>original: *prescritto*; suggesting that the 'legal' term of Love's rule had run out</note>,
so much time has passed since it obtained its liberty."
— "Do not say so, for the first arrow that opened it—
the eyes that drew it there have always left a trace."

— "Indeed, I felt in my heart—turned to ash
by the fire that the moisture from those eyes drips—
a small sign of the ancient love." —

— "You shall see that this tiny spark
will ignite eternal heat in you,
the fault and misfortune of your own sight <term>pupilla: literally 'pupil,' referring here to both the poet's eye and his capacity for vision</term>." —

<vocab>Sonnet, Volterra, Love, Rhymes, sonnets, Italian poetry</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (LVII and LVIII) exploring the poet's inability to escape his obsession. The first finds the image of Love mirrored in the landscape of Volterra, while the second uses a dialogue between the poet and Love to show how passion is rekindled from a lingering spark.</summary>
<keywords>Volterra, landscape, internal dialogue, personification of Love, Cupid’s seal, Petrarchan imagery, unrequited love, desire</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LIX

->[“Love, in how many ways you retake the heart!”]<-

If someone tunes two instruments
to the same pitch <note>original: *nella medesma voce*; referring to the musical concept of unison or sympathetic resonance</note> with sweet harmony,
by plucking one, the other string sounds
the same notes because of their conformity:

so it seems the impressed image vibrates
within my heart—though deaf to our sighs—
if it is reminded by similarity
of that face, which is beyond human minds.

Love, in how many ways you retake the heart!
For while fleeing the sight of that beautiful face,
and feeding the heart on a hollow painting <note>original: *vana pittura*; "vain" or "hollow" because the painting lacks the life and soul of the actual person</note>,

whether our eyes see nothing else,
or the painter were already in paradise,
I saw her very self: now he goes fleeing from Love.

### LX

->[The infinite power of Love.]<-

I used to mock Endymion <note>A beautiful youth in Greek mythology who was granted eternal sleep so that the Moon goddess, Selene, could love him forever.</note>,
accuse the folly of the handsome Narcissus <note>A youth who fell in love with his own reflection in a pool and transformed into a flower.</note>,
and feel wonder that Pygmalion <note>A sculptor who fell in love with an ivory statue he had carved, which was later brought to life by the goddess Venus.</note>
gazed so intently upon his own creation.

Alas! my own delirium is even less rational,
led as I am to love a painted face so much
that it cannot, with words or with a smile,
calm that great desire it places in the heart.

At least those eyes I flee and those graceful tresses
could have given me some aid;
this the hollow likeness cannot do.

Love, your power is infinite:
he who denies it is a fool: for I have now seen how
the wretched heart can love without hope.

<vocab>Love, painting, Endymion, Narcissus, Pygmalion, power, sonnet, instruments</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets exploring the psychological power of imagery; the first uses a musical metaphor for how a memory resonates in the heart, while the second compares the poet's devotion to a painting with famous mythological figures who loved inanimate or unattainable objects.</summary>
<keywords>sonnets, sympathetic resonance, painting vs reality, mythology, Narcissus, Pygmalion, Endymion, unrequited love</keywords>

### LXI

->[The paleness of the face that loves.]<-

> If, when I am nearest to that lovely face,
> the cold blood retreats to my heart,
> and if a sudden paleness assails me,
> I know what it is: that every virtue <note>original: *virtù*; in this context, it refers to the "vital spirits" or the inner strength that animates the body according to Renaissance medical theory.</note> has been taken from me.
> That face, in which every good is gathered,
> through the rays of its glittering <note>original: *micante*; a Latinism from *micare*, meaning to flash or vibrate with light.</note> splendor,
> scatters and diffuses its beautiful power
> into the heart that is entirely turned toward loving it.
> and it dwells so long within the sad heart,
> that the feigned image <note>The mental representation or "portrait" of the beloved held in the lover's memory.</note> completely destroys
> the true form with its very presence.
> Then that vital power which came from her—
> is it any wonder if it flees from me,
> since it returns to her, as if to its own beginning?</p>

### LXII

->[Near torment, far desire.]<-

> How do I leave you, or how are you with me,
> O face, from which my every fate proceeds?
> How do I die here, or how do I live elsewhere?
> Love, you tell me, for I would not know how.
> Who forces my departure, if I do not wish it?
> If I flee a sun, how do I flee it or where?
> Alas! what shadow ensures it does not find me,
> if it is never night unto my eyes?
> This much is certainly true: that if I see
> the true form, she seems most beautiful and proud,
> graceful beyond measure and disdainful;
> but if I am far away, a new springtime
> clothes the meadows with small flowers and grass:
> so beautiful do I see her then, and so full of pity <note>original: *pietosa*; meaning merciful or compassionate. The poet experiences a cruel paradox: she is cold when near, but his imagination makes her kind when he is far away.</note>.

<summary>Two sonnets exploring the physical and psychological effects of the beloved's presence and absence; the first describes the lover's paleness as his vital spirits flee toward the lady, while the second contrasts her real-life disdain with the idealized kindness he imagines from a distance.</summary>
<keywords>sonnets, Petrarchism, vital spirits, paleness, presence and absence, idealized love, unrequited desire</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LXIII
->Sonnet composed in Naples.<-

My wandering thoughts from hour to hour
speak together of my lady
so sweetly, that my heart goes astray
to go to her, and then my soul follows.
Love, who always dwells within my heart,
seeing that the soul is already departing,
moved to pity, shows me one most graceful and pious
who honors his kingdom.
Love has taken the eyes, the hands, the mouth,
and the beautiful countenance of my lovely lady
and has clothed another noble lady <note>original: *gentil donna*; in Renaissance poetry, nobility (*gentilezza*) refers to a refined soul capable of feeling true love, rather than just social rank.</note> in them;
such that, seeing her, I seem to see
the holy lights <note>A poetic metaphor for the eyes, suggesting they are windows to the divine.</note>: thus Love restrains the heart,
so that it does not flee away with my life.

---

### LXIV
->[*"Love only makes those who have nobility and faith strong enough to gaze upon high beauty."*]<-

He who has a sight so powerful
that he can gaze steadily upon my lady,
sees such beauties in her face
that they would make all souls content.
But Love has placed there a shining splendor
that denies paradise to mortal eyes:
so that for those excluded from such great good,
only wonder remains.
Love only makes those who have nobility and faith
strong enough to gaze upon that high beauty,
lifting away part of the shining rays.
He who sees that beauty once
and is worthy to taste its sweetness,
cannot help but love her forevermore.

<vocab>Sonnet, Naples, Love, nobility, faith, rhymes, beauty</vocab>

<summary>In these two sonnets, the poet describes how Love acts as a mediator: first by recreating the image of the beloved in another form to keep the poet's soul from departing, and second by shielding the "blinding" divine beauty of the lady so that only the noble-hearted may behold it.</summary>
<keywords>Petrarchism, Naples, Love as mediator, divine beauty, nobility of spirit, spiritual ecstasy, Renaissance poetry</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of sonnets focusing on the personal experiences and mythological transformations associated with Love. <header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LXV

->[Night meeting.]<-

O truly happy and blessed
night, which was present at such great goodness;
O blind steps, guided gently
by that smooth and delicate hand;
   you, Love, and my heart, and my beloved
lady alone know, and no one else,
that sweetness which conquers every human mind,
never before experienced by man.
   Oh, more than any other harmony of sounds and songs,
sweet silence; oh blind shadows, you who possessed
the privilege of tearful light! <note>Possibly a reference to moonlight or the shimmering reflection of tears in the dark.</note>
   Oh happy sighs and worthy tears!
Oh lofty desire, which presumed
to wish to hope for such high reward! <note>original: *pregio*; literally "prize" or "value," referring here to the favor or presence of the lady.</note>

---

### LXVI

->*Sonnet composed spontaneously, for a stone found in a grove.*<-
<note>original: *Sonetto fatto ex tempore, ad saxum in lucu repertum.*</note>

Once I was a wretched lover, now transformed
by the charm of two beautiful eyes
by a nymph among green shrubs;
from a lover I have become a hard stone.
   If any noble heart <note>original: *gentil cor*; referring to a soul refined by Love.</note> has passed this way,
let him be wiser by my example;
nor let him make his eyes rebellious to reason,
because snares are stretched on every side.
   Although a rigid stone, there still remains in me
so much pity <note>The poet plays on the words *pietra* (stone) and *pietá* (pity/mercy), a common device in Italian verse.</note> that I can warn others
and make them wise through my own danger.
   Cautiously, with eyes and head lowered,
let anyone who is as I once was pass by here,
for Love is still the god in these places.

<vocab>Rhymes, sonnet, Love, nymph, Petrarchism</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets: the first celebrates the sensory and emotional intensity of a secret nighttime encounter, while the second uses the classical trope of metamorphosis to warn others of the "stony" dangers of unrequited or overwhelming passion.</summary>
<keywords>night, secret love, silence, metamorphosis, stone, warning, nymph, Petrarchan tropes</keywords>

## LXVII

->[The Deceptions of Love.]<-

Eyes, you are indeed, as you appeared,
the most beautiful eyes I ever saw:
the other lovely beauties I gazed upon
and her manners are most beautiful and virtuous.
Nor can I complain, alas! of these,
but rather thank and honor them greatly,
but only of you, O false Love, who knew
that my heart was <term>adamant</term> <note>original: *adamante*; a substance of legendary hardness, like diamond, used here to show the poet thought his heart was unbreakable and immune to love.</note> and did not say so.
Already I asked those eyes where you were:
you formed words in that mouth
to make mountains move, let alone a captured heart.
Already through sighs I recognized her
loving thoughts, and that pity touches her heart,
but I did not know by what fire I was inflamed. <note>The "fire" of love is a standard metaphor in Renaissance poetry for intense passion that consumes the lover.</note>

## LXVIII

->[Love puts the thought of death to flight.]<-

A bitter thought sometimes holds me
and takes lordship over all others:
if it lasts, I die; and if I drive it away,
it returns another time with more force.
It tells me that every hope of mine is deceptive,
the love, the faith of my lady;
it recounts the various thoughts she had before
Love placed all my well-being within her.
Thinking of this, I call upon Death as a remedy,
and she would hear me then with pity:
but Love, who knows how wrongly I grieve,
shows me those beautiful eyes, and before them
every evil thought flees, every sorrow of mine,
like shadows before the dawn.

<note>original vocabulary: Lorenzo the Magnificent, rhymes, sonnet, Love, Death, Renaissance poetry</note>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (LXVII and LXVIII) exploring the internal psychological conflicts of the lover, specifically the themes of Love’s deception and the redemptive power of the lady's gaze against thoughts of despair.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, sonnets, Love's deception, personification of Death, Petrarchism, Renaissance poetry, emotional struggle, beauty</keywords>

### LXIX

->[Sweet is the weeping, "since I saw those beautiful eyes weep so copiously and intensely."]<-

So sweet an example of weeping have those tear-filled lights <note>In Renaissance poetry, "lights" (*lumi*) is a common metaphor for eyes.</note> given to my eyes, that two perennial rivers shall always flow from mine: such a desire to weep has been born in me.

Alas, how beautiful they were, and in what a miserable state I left them! Now consume me, O tenacious memory, and yet you still presume to promise worse: O too-adverse fate!

For so great a fault, a sweet weeping is but little punishment; and sweet is the weeping, since I saw those beautiful eyes weep so copiously and intensely.

Wherefore my eyes, which presumed so much, wished then, by weeping, to resemble those; and I hope and burn, that Death may soon close them.

---

### LXX

->[Love converts every thought into the thought of her.]<-

Alas, my thought always looks solely and fixedly upon the last glances of my lady: my eyes first feel envy and anger at this, for they are slower to reach their prize. <note>The poet suggests that while the mind can instantly visualize the beloved, the physical eyes are frustrated by distance and time.</note>

But then, even if I look at diverse things, my strong thought, which draws them to itself, converts them all into her; and from this it breathes a brief sweetness into my lying eyes. <note>The eyes are "lying" because they see the physical world, but the poet's mind perceives only the lady.</note>

And as the sun, without <term>accident</term> <note>original: *accidente*; in the philosophy of the time, an "accident" was a property (like heat) that could be separated from the essence of a thing. Here, it refers to the sun’s power being amplified by reflection.</note> or form of heat, then takes on new power through reflection, and sets the world on fire;

so, after various things have come to my thought through my eyes, Love transforms them, and returns only the image of my lady to my eyes.

<vocab>Rhymes, sonnet, weeping, Love, memory, Death, power, reflection</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets exploring the psychological effects of love: the first focuses on the poet's desire to mirror the lady's tears, while the second uses the physics of sunlight to explain how love filters the poet's entire perception of reality.</summary>
<keywords>Petrarchism, memory, reflection metaphor, Neoplatonism, weeping, transformation of sight</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LXXI

->[« Remembering the first time ». ]<-

Love guided my lady’s sacred feet <note>original: "sacre piante"; "piante" literally means soles of the feet, used here metonymically for her footsteps or presence.</note>
to that beautiful place, as it pleased him,
where she first offered me her white hand
as a pledge of her faithful and constant heart.

Having arrived in that place, turning her holy eyes,
since she did not find me there,
such great pity for me ran to her heart
that she made a sweet and beautiful show of weeping.

Then, remembering the first time and that
amorous pledge, and looking to see where I might be,
there, all alone, she drew a deep sigh;

with which her soul moved to depart:
but, her calling out the graceful and beautiful name,
she held back the soul that wished to flee. <note>The poet suggests that the lady was so moved by the memory of their love that her soul almost left her body in a sigh, only to be drawn back by the sound of a beloved name—likely the poet's.</note>

---

### LXXII

->[To a flower intended for his lady.]<-

Let that power <note>original: "virtú"; in this context, it refers to the life-force or generative power of nature.</note> which produced and nourished you,
wild and lovely flower, not grieve now,
nor fear, if I pluck or gather you from it,
that you will lose your natural vigor.

You shall be a gift to my lady, such that,
should it happen that she receives you in her white hand
and turns her amorous eyes upon you,
their power prevails over every other.

If, while she weeps, the amorous stream
of tears bathes your languid leaves,
you shall be among the flowers of the earthly paradise.

Nor take wonder or grief from this,
for I too, being now separated from her here,
alas, nourish myself and live on tears alone.

<vocab>sonnet, Love, Rhymes, flower, soul, weeping</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets: one imagining the lady returning to the site of their first romantic pledge and nearly dying from grief in the poet's absence, and another addressing a wildflower that the poet plucks as a gift, claiming the lady's tears will grant it eternal life.</summary>
<keywords>memory, tokens of love, nature imagery, weeping, soul, devotion, Petrarchan sonnet</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LXXIII

->[The legend of the red roses.]<-

We do not come into your white hand, my lady,
from green gardens, adorned and cultivated
in the sunny and sweet air of Paestum; <note>original: "Pestano"; refers to the ancient city of Paestum in southern Italy, famous in antiquity for its roses that bloomed twice a year.</note>
but gathered in harsh forests and shaded valleys:

where Venus, afflicted and burdened by many thoughts
due to the danger facing Adonis, <note>In Greek mythology, Venus (Aphrodite) rushed to save her lover Adonis from a wild boar. The poem references the legend that roses were originally white but turned red when stained by the goddess's blood.</note> ran in vain,
and a sharp thorn pricked her bare, rustic foot,
shedding her divine blood through the thick woods.

We then held up our white flowers,
so that the divine blood would not reach
the earth; from this, our purple color was born.

No summer breezes or distant streams
have nourished us; rather, the sighs of Love
were our breezes, and tears were our waters.

---

### LXXIV

->[A flattering dream.]<-

After my soul departed from that beautiful countenance,
full of laments, as is its custom,
Love—who truly grieves for my sighs—
seeing my eyes fixed in weeping,

with a sweet and longed-for oblivion, put an end
to the cries, the sad sighs, and the words;
and, while I slept, he then made it so that my beautiful Sun <note>The "Sun" is a common Renaissance metaphor for the beloved lady, representing her beauty and the light she brings to the poet's life.</note>
came to me, more joyful and beautiful than ever.

She offered me her left hand,
saying: — "Do you not recognize the place? This
is the place where Love first wished to give me to you." —

Then, as we moved onward, step by gentle step,
to another part, I was awakened by the sweetness;
and I remained full of desire, my chest wet with tears. <note>original: "col petto molle"; literally "with a soft/moist chest," likely referring to the poet waking up to find his chest dampened by the tears shed during the emotional dream.</note>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets: the first recounts a mythological origin story for red roses involving Venus and Adonis, while the second describes a vivid, bittersweet dream in which the poet’s beloved visits him and recalls the beginning of their love.</summary>
<keywords>sonnets, Venus and Adonis, Paestum roses, mythology, dreams, courtly love, Renaissance poetry</keywords>

## LXXV

->[« My thought lifted me... »]<- <note>original: "Levommi il mio pensiero..."; This opening is a direct homage to the famous sonnet 302 by Petrarch, where the poet’s mind ascends to heaven to seek his beloved.</note>

Through a long, steep, and harsh way, shrouded in shadows,
with Love guiding my blind thought,
I moved my feet along an unknown path,
having already turned my desire toward heaven.

Through a thousand errors at last with much sweat
I arrived at the horizon of our hemisphere;
thence into a lofty and more noble
place, already lifted and removed from the earth.

Having reached the third step <note>In the Renaissance understanding of the cosmos, the "third step" or third sphere of heaven belongs to Venus, the planet of Love.</note> of the great stair,
the dear son <note>Cupid (Amor).</note> handed me over to his mother, <note>Venus.</note>
although he appeared then confused to us.

Then, taken up into a more luminous part,
I could gaze upon the sun with mortal eyes,
nor did any mortal thing ever please me again.

---

## LXXVI

->[The miracle of the violets.]<-

The young leaves and the small trees
are accustomed to reclothe themselves in the new season, <note>Spring.</note>
and Flora <note>The Roman goddess of flowers and springtime.</note> opens her beautiful breast to Phoebus, <note>A name for Apollo, representing the sun.</note>
and produces you along with the other new flowers.

Now the mature season <note>Late summer or autumn.</note> has made those others
convert into seeds or into sweet fruits:
what marvel now makes you alone appear,
O amorous flowers, so fresh and beautiful?

This alone I believe, O sweet violets, <note>original: "mammole viole"; specifically referring to *Viola odorata*, known for its deep color and fragrance.</note>
that you are destined by Nature
to warm yourselves in the rays of my Sun. <note>As established on page 212, the "Sun" is the poet's metaphor for his lady.</note>

Let all wonder cease, if you will come
into that hand, if she wishes to accept you:
such a new and beautiful miracle you will see.

<vocab>Amor (Love), Flora (Goddess of flowers), Febo (Phoebus/the Sun), Sole (the Sun/the beloved), sonetto (sonnet), rime (rhymes), mammole viole (sweet violets)</vocab>

<summary>This page presents two sonnets: one chronicling a spiritual journey to the celestial sphere of Love, and another marveling at violets blooming late in the year, which the poet attributes to the divine presence of his lady.</summary>
<keywords>Petrarchan influence, celestial ascent, Venus, Cupid, Flora, Phoebus Apollo, violets, seasonal metaphors, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LXXVII

->*[[ “The undertaking is now late and the work is in vain.” ]*<- <note>original: "L’impresa omai è tarda e l’opra vana"; This line emphasizes the futility of trying to escape Love's influence once it has taken hold.</note>

My afflicted soul has become distant
from those beautiful and perilous lights; <note>original: "luci"; a standard Petrarchan metaphor for the beloved's eyes, which provide light but also lead to the "danger" of infatuation.</note>
therefore, though quite timid, it resolved
to make itself free, and more estranged from Love.

It calls its thoughts, and in a soft, low voice,
hiding its Love, it proposed its desire.
From so many sorrows, <note>original: "omei"; literally "oh-mes" or sighs of lament.</note> one replied for them all:
— The undertaking is now late, and the work is in vain. —

So saying, it perceives this afflicted soul
in the abandoned place where the heart used to be,
which has fled away with the rebel spirits. <term>spirits: In Renaissance physiology, "vital spirits" were subtle fluids that carried life and sensation through the body. Here, the poet’s life-force has abandoned him to follow the beloved.</term>

Then the wretched soul, realizing
it is alone, also takes its own path;
and I live only through the power of Love.

---

### LXXVIII

->*[[ The sad fate of Love. ]*<-

A thought that often speaks of Love
lives alone in me, and I listen to it willingly:
and if any other rises in my mind,
like a traveler, it does not stay for long.

My wretched soul—which feels,
beyond these thoughts, every one of my spirits turned
against my life—very timidly,
withdrawn into itself, laments that beautiful face.

And he, <note>The beloved, or perhaps Love personified.</note> having an inkling of such grief
from the spirits of Love, with true and pious
speech excuses himself to the sad soul, and says:

— It is the proper and graceful duty of beauty
to please: soul, blame your own desire,
if I please everyone and make only you unhappy. — <note>The beloved argues that beauty is a neutral force whose "job" is to be beautiful; the suffering of the lover is a result of their own uncontrollable desire rather than any malice on the part of the beautiful object.</note>

<vocab>Love, soul, rhymes, sonnet, desire, virtue/power, duty</vocab>

<summary>These two sonnets detail the internal conflict of the lover, where the soul attempts to flee from Love's pain only to find itself abandoned by its own vital spirits and heart. The second poem features a personified dialogue where Beauty defends its nature, suggesting the lover's suffering is a product of their own desire.</summary>

<keywords>Petrarchism, psychology of love, vital spirits, personification, internal dialogue, Renaissance poetry, desire vs. beauty</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LXXIX

->[His heart sighs within the beautiful breast of his lady.]<-

Alas, how much desire Love has placed
inside my anguished and sorrowful breast!
and, because the place is too narrow for such a heavy burden,
it often comes out in the form of sighs.

My wise heart, which feels itself oppressed,
in order to make room, in its turn, for such great emotion,
has gone up onto that beautiful hillock, <note>original: "poggetto"; literally a small hill, here used as a delicate metaphor for the lady's breast.</note>
where my lady is, and stays close to her.

And, though the source of my great desire is missing,
by the heart's departure, Love, they have established a custom,
that everything living within me desires her alone.

My heart informs me from that proud mountain
through a messenger of Love, who comes to me swiftly,
that it sighs out of pity within that beautiful breast.

### LXXX

->[The eyes, too, would like to follow the path of the heart and go to her.]<-

My wet and weary eyes often say to me:
— We would like to follow the heart’s path
and go to those eyes, where every sight dies,
and dying, becomes clearer and more beautiful. — <note>The poet suggests that physical sight is blinded or "dies" when faced with the lady's brilliance, yet this leads to a higher, more beautiful spiritual perception.</note>

The way is well known to our slow steps;
for just as a burning vapor <note>original: "acceso vapore"; likely referring to a meteor or "will-o'-the-wisp" that lights the night sky.</note> illuminates
the night, so spirits of Love <term>spirits: In Renaissance medical theory, "spirits" were vapors that mediated between the soul and the body. Here, the lady's radiance generates these spirits to guide the lover.</term>
light the beautiful path where one goes to my lady. —

And I, for whom it is a burden both to satisfy and to deny them,
lead them to the top of the highest hills,
and show them, though far away, that place.

Like a thirsty man, if he washes his mouth,
his desire increases, if you only moisten his lips;
so then the weeping in my eyes increases, and the fire in my breast.

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (LXXIX and LXXX) exploring the physical separation of the lover's faculties—his heart and eyes—as they seek to be near the beloved lady.</summary>
<keywords>Love, heart, lady, sighs, eyes, sonnet, Petrarchan imagery, desire</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LXXXI

->["Love lives forever."]<-

— When will this sweet enemy die,
this Hope, who sustains a bitter life,
and who dies when that sweet and clear light, <note>A common metaphor for the lady's eyes or her presence.</note>
returning to my eyes, nourishes my happy heart?
Noble Faith, sister and friend
to tearful and dear Hope,
Faith so noble, today so rare in the world,
when will she die? Love, let me be told.
Love, you are silent, and you are the reason I die;
these [Hope and Faith] cause me to live: then I desire death,
life to you, oh amorous error. —
Then Love responds, smiling:
— Death is sweet, and their life is a martyrdom:
they shall die soon, and Love lives forever. —

### LXXXII

->[In vain he asks the beautiful river for news of his distant lady.]<-

O clear river, you carry away
in your rapid, whirling waves
the sad tears of my lady,
from those beautiful eyes that Fortune now hides.
Your mournful murmur, which I heard
responding to my miserable laments,
tells me this for certain; to your green banks
a stream that wanders into you brings the weeping.
Ah! Restrain your swift course for a moment:
so may the Dog Star <note>original: "Sirio can"; Sirius, the brightest star in the constellation Canis Major. Its "poisonous bite" refers to the "dog days" of summer when the star's heat was believed to cause rivers to dry up or become stagnant.</note> never offend you,
rapid river, with its poisonous bite.
Contend with the Phison, with the Euphrates: <note>original: "Frison, Eufrate"; two of the four rivers of the Garden of Eden. The poet suggests the river should take pride in rivaling these legendary waters.</note>
yet you still flee and deny me your help,
nor do you wish for me to hear news of my beautiful Sun. <note>The poet refers to his lady as his "Sun," the source of his light and life.</note>

<summary>In these two sonnets, the poet explores the endurance of Love despite the "martyrdom" of Hope and Faith, and fruitlessly seeks news of his absent beloved from a passing river.</summary>
<keywords>Love, Hope, Faith, personification, Sirius, biblical rivers, lady as Sun, Petrarchan imagery</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LXXXIII

->[To the little violet given to him by his lady.]<-

O beautiful violet, you were born
where my first beautiful desire was born:
sad and beautiful tears were the waters
that nourished you and bathed you many times.
Pity in that fortunate land
nourished the desire where the beautiful flower-clump <note>original: "cesto"; while it can mean a basket, in this botanical context it refers to the tuft or cluster where the flower grew.</note> lay:
the beautiful hand picked you, and then it pleased her
to make mine blessed by such a beautiful gift.
And it seems to me that you always want to flee
back to that beautiful hand; so I hold you
sweetly pressed to my bare chest;
to my bare chest, because desire and sorrow
take the place of the heart, which scorns the chest,
and stays where you came from, O violet. <note>The poet uses a common Petrarchan conceit: his heart has physically left his body to stay with his lady, leaving his chest empty of everything but his longing for her.</note>

---

### LXXXIV

->[Effects of the amorous gaze.]<-

If it happens that my gaze, all intent,
gazes fixedly upon the flame of those beautiful eyes,
my chest, lit with desires, sighs—
the smoke of the fire that torments my heart.
Thus the path becomes quite ready
from fire to fire through my sighs;
just as it seems a new flame draws the smoke
of a candle that has only just been extinguished. <note>The poet refers to a common observation: if a candle is blown out, a flame held in the trail of rising smoke can "travel" down the smoke to relight the wick.</note>
Visibly then, whoever wishes can perceive
in that beautiful smoke the spirits of Love,
which offer the sweet fire from one to the other.
They go and come from one heart to the other;
neither one nor the other notices their own suffering,
so sweetly and so willingly do they die.

<summary>This page contains two sonnets: the first focuses on a violet given as a gift, symbolizing the lady's presence, while the second uses the scientific metaphor of a guttering candle to describe how sighs transport love between two people.</summary>
<keywords>violet, gift, longing, heart's journey, amorous gaze, sighs, spirits of love, candle metaphor</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

## LXXXV

->[The Sighs of Love.]<-

The lofty sighs from that amorous breast,
bringing me news of my lord <note>The "lord" here likely refers to Love (Amor) personified, who dwells within the lady's heart.</note>,
as soon as they are released from her beautiful lips,
they find a refuge, still warm, within my heart.

They recount to it the words that Love
has spoken to them, in sweet and silent speech;
all the spirits <note>In Renaissance medicine and philosophy, "spirits" were subtle substances that moved through the nerves and blood to facilitate sensation and movement.</note> then run to hear them,
leaving the heart oppressed and constricted.

Against its own will, the heart is forced to drive
the spirits out with its own sighs, and pushes elsewhere
this amorous troop from whence they had first emerged.

Life and death seem to happen where they depart:
thus a single spirit alternates and moves between two
a sweet way of living, which dwells between death and life.

---

## LXXXVI

->[His lady triumphs over Love, the Graces, and the virtues.]<-

Proud hill, though humble in appearance,
far more worthy and more fortunate than those others:
the Esquiline, Caelian, Aventine, and their brothers, <note>The "brothers" are the other of the Seven Hills of Rome: the Palatine, Quirinal, Viminal, and Capitoline.</note>
though they have been sung of in a more elevated style:

these hills once saw the more lowly triumphs
of the Emilii, the Scipios, and the Marcelli: <note>Famous Roman families and generals celebrated for their military victories.</note>
but you see triumphing before those beautiful eyes
Love himself, bound, and every noble heart.

The Graces come in chains and with robes loosened, <note>original: "catenate e scinte"; the "Graces" are the three goddesses of charm and beauty, here depicted as captives in the lady's victory parade.</note>
Pity and Beauty walk before the chariot, along with those
virtues that are distinguished within a noble heart.

They are joyful, though triumphed over and conquered—
all the more joyful as they appear more beautiful,
painted upon the face of my lady.

<summary>This page contains two sonnets: the first describes the physical and spiritual impact of the lady's sighs on the poet, while the second compares the lady's moral and aesthetic power to the historical military triumphs of ancient Rome.</summary>
<keywords>sighs, vital spirits, Roman hills, Triumph, Graces, Scipio, virtues, Petrarchan imagery</keywords>

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of sonnets centered on the poet's internal emotional state and his exaltation of the lady. Following the "triumph" imagery of the previous page, Sonnet LXXXVII describes a symbolic transfer of power from Cupid to the lady, while Sonnet LXXXVIII explores the paradoxical pleasure found in the "sweet pain" of love.</meta>

<page-num>219</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LXXXVII

->[Love’s New Inventions.]<-

Love, on that victorious day
which reminds me of the first sweet evil <note>original: "dolce male" — a classic oxymoron used by Petrarchan poets to describe the experience of falling in love, where the pain of longing is experienced as something pleasurable.</note>,
joyfully climbs the proud mountain:
the Graces and his dear brothers <note>The "brothers" of Love (Amore/Cupid) are the Erotes or Amores, the winged infant deities who accompany him.</note> went with him.
Having set aside the noble attire in which he was adorned,
he gave me his blindfold and his wings:
to her, he gave the bow in her right hand, and an arrow
in her left, and the quiver around her.
The bright, thin, tucked-up robe
of my amorous Diana <note>The poet identifies his lady with Diana, the goddess of the hunt, emphasizing both her beauty and her chaste, formidable nature.</note> reveals
her bare limbs, or expresses them through the fabric.
Phoebus <note>Phoebus Apollo, the sun god, associated with light, music, and poetry.</note> adorned her eyes and head with his rays;
thus, those first blessed and sweet sights
were not the product of human arts or mortal works.

---

### LXXXVIII

->[Sweet Miracles.]<-

It seems a thousand harsh thoughts, born of suffering <term>martíri: literally "martyrdoms," a common poetic term for the spiritual and physical agonies of unrequited love</term>,
move the sad soul within the heart:
if they die, they turn into sighs,
and yet immortal grief renews them.
Nor do I know how it can be, except through experience,
that the heart always ignites new desires
for its own death, and draws nourishment
from such harsh thoughts, which helps it to live.
Tell me, Love, how it is that even as they die,
these sad thoughts have made that beautiful image
in my heart sweet and immortal.
Love then answers me, smiling:
— No blessing is as sweet as my pain.
I perform these sweet miracles. —

<summary>This page contains two sonnets: one depicting a mythological scene where Love transfers his weapons to the lady (transforming her into a huntress-goddess), and another exploring the psychological paradox of finding life and sweetness within the "martyrdom" of love.</summary>
<keywords>Amore, Diana, Phoebus, sonnet, Petrarchism, suffering, Graces, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of sonnets exploring the poet's internal emotional landscape. While the previous page (219) focused on the symbolic transfer of Love’s power to the lady and the paradoxical "sweet suffering" of the lover, these two sonnets (LXXXIX and XC) deepen the theme of finding spiritual salvation and sweetness even within sorrow and tears.</meta>

<page-num>220</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### LXXXIX

->[“So beautiful is my lady.”]<-

So beautiful is my lady, and in herself she gathers
so many sweet and unseen beauties,
that it is a better state to find no favor <term>salute: a complex term in Italian poetry meaning both "salvation" and the "well-being" derived from a lady’s greeting</term>
in her, than to satisfy all other desires.
Therefore the tears, desires, hopes, and pains,
which have come from such a beautiful thing,
bring with them a noble well-being
that lives forever, and from which life takes its leave <note>original: "a cui la vita toglie" — suggesting a state of spiritual ecstasy so high that physical life seems to fade away by comparison.</note>.
Oh beautiful death and oh sweet sorrows!
Oh thoughts, which carry in your sighs
such sweetness to the heart, unknown to others!
How can it be that any pain weighs down,
however afflicted, any heart that always gazes
with eyes or with thought upon supreme beauty?

---

### XC

->[The weeping of his lady has made even Love compassionate.]<-

You will never again be a cruel god,
Love <note>Amore: the personified god of love, Cupid.</note>, since in that beautiful and holy gaze
the weeping of my lady has bathed you—
beautiful weeping, sweet weeping, and pious weeping.
That pity, which moved the beautiful desire,
I believe will have made you so compassionate,
and those pious tears; for a happy song
lovers can now make out of my sorrow.
My pain makes you happy and secure:
fear no more, oh pale lovers <note>The "pale lover" was a common literary trope; it was believed that the intensity of love drained the blood from the face.</note>,
that through love, by weeping, the heart might dissolve.
Even if I were to weep, my noble lord
has made the tears so sweet by weeping,
that every noble heart <term>cor gentil: "gentle heart," a central concept in Italian poetry referring to a soul refined by love and virtue</term> wants to weep forever.

<vocab>Rhymes, sonnet, Love, lady, beauty, sorrow, weeping, favor/salvation, noble</vocab>

<summary>In these sonnets, the poet explores the redemptive power of beauty and sorrow, suggesting that even unrequited love and tears provide a spiritual "well-being" superior to earthly satisfaction.</summary>
<keywords>Petrarchan tradition, spiritual love, noble heart, Cupid, aesthetic of sorrow, beauty and salvation</keywords>

--- Page 227 ---

While page 220 established that the lady's weeping made Love himself more compassionate, sonnet XCI provides a vivid, sensory description of those tears, and XCII shifts to a reflective state, where Love reveals the "invisible" history of the poet's devotion.</meta>

<page-num>221</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### XCI

->[The tears.]<-

Alas, how beautiful were those tears
that the cloud <term>nimbo: literally a rain cloud or halo; here, the "cloud" of emotion or desire</term> of desire moved as they fell,
when the just grief that struck the heart
then rose up into the amorous stars <note>In the poetic language of this era, "stars" is a frequent metaphor for the lady’s eyes.</note>!
They streaked across the delicate skin
of the white cheeks, sweetly flushed with red,
just as a clear stream would flow through a meadow
of white and red flowers; such were those beautiful tears.
Happy Love <note>Amore: personified as the god Cupid.</note> stood within that amorous rain,
like a bird that, after the sun <note>original: "dopo il sol" — likely referring to the cool relief of rain after a hot sun.</note>, happily receives
the dewy drops it had longed for so much.
Then, weeping in those eyes where he dwells,
he made sweet sparks visibly fly out
from that beautiful and sorrowful weeping.

### XCII

->[The sweet deceptions of Love.]<-

Our eyes behold a beautiful and pleasing work,
such as no one else sees or believes besides you,
made by the hand of him who sees without eyes <note>A reference to the traditional depiction of Cupid as blind or blindfolded.</note>,
not painted or sculpted or written in dark inks.
It seems I see Love, happy, showing me
that first sweet time from which proceeds
so much love, so much nobility <term>gentilezza: a core concept in Renaissance poetry, referring to an innate refinement and moral excellence of the soul</term>, and faith,
our high desires and our sweet troubles.
That first happy fear shakes my heart;
towards me you move your happy and ready steps,
your hand, your mouth, and those merciful stars.
Even if Love shows them in every place,
your weepings in those lofty mountains <note>original: "altèri monti" — likely a reference to the lady's place of origin or a specific symbolic landscape.</note>,
where they were born, make them more true and beautiful.

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (XCI and XCII) that idealize the lady’s sorrow. The first compares her tears to refreshing dew and a stream through flowers, while the second describes Love as an invisible artist who recreates the history of the poet's affection.</summary>
<keywords>tears, Cupid, eyes as stars, nobility, spiritual love, memory, poetic landscape</keywords>

Sonnet XCIV transitions into a meditation on the agonizingly slow passage of time for a lover in distress, utilizing the classical figure of Phoebus Apollo.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### XCIII

->[ "Love moves new sighs from my heart." ]<-

My Lady, feigning a sweet anger,
somewhat troubled, said to Love <note>Amore: the personified god of Love, or Cupid.</note>:
— No more fire now: the breast burns too much
out of pity for my heart, which sighs within her. —
Love laughs at this, and the heart, which desires to burn,
feels greater delight in the largest fire,
and, like gold already perfect in the furnace,
it becomes more beautiful, and the fire does not torment it.
Love moves new sighs from my heart:
with these sweet bellows <term>folli: literally bellows used to stoke a fire; here, the sighs that feed the poet's passion</term> he stokes the fire,
as much as it can burn in that beautiful furnace.
This fire, which then shines through the eyes,
and the burning speech, when she speaks,
kindles new flames wherever it reaches.

---

### XCIV

->[ The long days of amorous suffering. ]<-

When blind desire, as a greater punishment,
counts the hours, now long and once so short,
just as a snake, crushed by a wheel by chance,
moves but cannot follow its own uncoiling back;
so slowly does Phoebus <note>Febo: Phoebus Apollo, the sun god, who drives a golden chariot across the sky to mark the day.</note> lead his golden chariot,
in which he seems to carry with him, out of envy,
the sweet death of bitter desires
and the end of my hope that suffers so much.
Neither does the heart admit any new sweet thought,
nor do these tear-dampened eyes find any soft oblivion
by which the sun might be driven faster;
and what pains me most in this waiting
is that Phoebus, now so slow, promises me
he will then rapidly carry away every joy of mine. <note>The poet fears that while his time of suffering feels endless, his future moments of happiness will pass in an instant.</note>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets: the first uses the imagery of a furnace to describe the refining power of passion, while the second uses the image of a wounded snake to depict the agonizingly slow passage of time during emotional suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Love, desire, fire, Phoebus, time, suffering, sonnet, Petrarchan imagery</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### XCV

->[Deceptive hopes and eternal torments.]<-

O short and clear nights, o long and black
days; o shining shadows, o dark light;
light that steals the brightness from open eyes,
shadow that cheers closed eyes with clear light!
O dark sleep, who turns blind and sickly thoughts
into visions of pure light!
O image of death <note>original: "immagin del morir." The Renaissance trope of sleep as the "brother of death" is used here to show how dreams feel more "alive" than waking reality.</note>, which while it lasts
I see, hear, and feel, and have my desires whole!
O my excessive sweetness, mortal enemy
to your own self, who places my good 
before my desire and then flees, for which I grieve!
O unhappy sleeps of lovers!
Since, when I have most of what I most want,
I lose it, and it flees just when it draws most near.

---

### XCVI

->[May Love defend his eyes against the proud gaze of his lady.]<-

Who will make my eyes steady and strong
against the power of that new, proud, and merciful
shining gaze, from which they desire,
wretched and joyful, to be conquered and killed?
Love, why do you not comfort these foolish eyes?
Through them you first entered my breast <note>In the Neoplatonic tradition of Michelangelo’s era, love was thought to enter the soul through the eyes of the beloved.</note>;
these made me yours, and you my god:
why do you not bring some help to them?
Leave the anguished breast, where you reside
as if in a clear mirror—a noble imprint
of the beauty that lives with you in her.
Leave my breast, and climb up into my eyes
armed with yourself; and let my eyes, now beautiful,
safely face her beautiful eyes.

<summary>This page features two sonnets by Michelangelo: the first explores the painful contrast between the fulfillment found in dreams and the emptiness of waking, while the second asks the god of Love to move from the poet's heart to his eyes to protect them from the lady's overpowering gaze.</summary>
<keywords>Michelangelo Buonarroti, sonnets, Renaissance poetry, dreams and sleep, paradox, Neoplatonism, Cupid, eyes and gaze, unrequited love</keywords>

<page-num>224</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### XCVII

->[The eyes.]<-

If my lady sometimes looks into my eyes,
she does not gaze at them, but rather cherishes herself within them;
and she appears so beautiful to herself that she admits
that my heart sighs for a noble thing <note>original: "gentil cosa". In the poetic tradition Michelangelo followed, "gentle" refers to nobility of spirit and moral refinement rather than just kindness.</note>.
Therefore she often turns her beautiful eyes
toward mine, where she is expressed so truly,
that a beautiful thing—or anything resembling it—
she can neither see nor desire outside of them.
When she makes herself appear so beautiful to herself,
there goes in company with her honored face
a beautiful crowd of ardent, loving spirits <note>In Renaissance thought, "spirits" were believed to be subtle vapors that carried life and emotion between the heart and the eyes.</note>.
Having reached my heart, which is further inflamed by her,
she drives out lazy hope and long-endured pity:
thus she sees my spirits then contented.

---

### XCVIII

->[A doubt of Love.]<-

When the light of those beautiful eyes reaches me,
there flees before those loving lashes
the great family of my various thoughts,
along with pity and half-alive hope.
There departs from my fleeting memory
every impression that resembles the truth,
and only sweetness and wonder remain,
which kills every other thing, wherever it lives.
The spirits, meeting that sweet splendor,
go joyfully, fleeing from me, toward her in whom
(and they know it well) Love kills and consumes them.
Whether my vision remains or whether it flees—
for it is dead in me then, yet lives in another—
let this lover's doubt be resolved by the noble heart.

<vocab>lady, eyes, heart, spirits, hope, Love, memory, wonder, doubt</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (XCVII and XCVIII) exploring the mystical connection between the lover's eyes and the beloved's beauty. Michelangelo describes his eyes as a mirror in which his lady sees her own perfection, and the overwhelming sensory experience of her gaze which displaces all other thoughts.</summary>

<keywords>Neoplatonism, mirror imagery, spiritual transformation, beauty, Italian Renaissance poetry, eyes, internal spirits</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### XCIX

->[The harsh memory of the pains of Love.]<-

Harsh memory, why do you not fade,
you who, so inflamed, torment my sad heart?
Harsh memory, who presents to me,
in my mournful thoughts, deceptions, angers, hatreds, and resentments.
Alas, unhappy day that you strive
to disturb my sweet and pleasant desires!
And you, Love, consent to such great evil,
so that no one should despise your total goodness <note>original: "perché al tuo bene intero alcun non sdegni." Lorenzo suggests that Love allows suffering so that the ultimate "good" or reward of love is not undervalued by the lover.</note>.
My sorrowful thought shows me
something I dare not speak; but every spirit <note>In the medical and poetic theory of the time, "spirits" were vital vapors that moved through the body to facilitate sensation and emotion.</note> of mine
that sees it flees to the heart:
and, finding that sad thought even stronger and more fierce
within the heart, it destroys them one by one:
my weary heart trembles and asks for its spirits in vain.

---

### C

->[To Paolo Cortese <note>Paolo Cortese (1465–1510) was a renowned humanist and papal secretary, known for his writings on language and the Roman Curia.</note>.]<-

What wonder, my noble Cortese,
if Love kindled a new <term>Mongibello</term> <note>original: "Mongibello," the local name for Mount Etna in Sicily. It represents a paradox: a mountain covered in snow (white fleece) that contains a burning volcanic fire.</note>—
cold, confined, and born of the silent, white, drifting fleece—
within your frozen breast?
Some, oppressed by poison, defended
their life with a mortal and wicked poison <note>This refers to the ancient medical concept of "similia similibus curentur"—the idea that like cures like, or that one can become immune to poison by consuming small amounts of it.</note>;
and thus the ice of the external snow
drove out the ice that was in your heart and caught fire.
This fire sometimes freezes the blood
in every vein; one man loves another and hates himself;
another lives without a heart and begs for death.
This amorous life is entirely full
of noble wonders, and the lover often experiences
within himself what he would not believe possible in others.

<sig>LORENZO THE MAGNIFICENT, Works - I. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 15</sig>

<vocab>Lorenzo the Magnificent, Paolo Cortese, sonnet, Rhymes, Mongibello, love, memory</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets by Lorenzo de' Medici exploring the psychological torment of romantic memory and the paradoxical nature of love's "burning ice." Sonnet C is specifically addressed to the humanist Paolo Cortese, using the metaphor of Mount Etna to describe the coexistence of cold indifference and passionate desire.</summary>

<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Paolo Cortese, sonnets, Italian humanism, Mount Etna, Mongibello, metaphors of love, Renaissance psychology, spirits, poetic fire and ice</keywords>
