<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

## CI

->[He invokes his lady in the places already gladdened by her presence.]<-

Why is my life <note>A common term of endearment for the beloved lady.</note> and my dear lord <note>original: "signor mio." In the courtly tradition, the beloved is often addressed with masculine titles of feudal authority, or this may refer to Love personified.</note> not here
together with my thoughts,
in the sweet shade and by this stream,
which laments and groans with my weeping?
Why does her noble foot not press this grass?
Why does she not hear my cruel lament,
and the sighs that are moved by the desire
which too-bitter hope kindled in us?
Perhaps that pity, which Love once promised me
so much, and still promises me now
(for he wrote it in the center of my heart with his arrow),
would come before my final hour:
if she were to hear my sweet lament,
she who makes me fall in love would show beautiful pity.

---

## CII

->[He does not know how to live far from his beloved.]<-

Alas! Every happy place brings to my heart
a thousand bitter sighs, harsh thoughts:
because it does not seem I can, know, or hope
to live happily far from my light <note>The "light" refers to the lady, whose presence illuminates the poet's world.</note>.
But to find more peace, my soul leads me
into dark woods, mountainous and wild,
fleeing from footprints and well-trodden paths:
this sometimes moves the soul to find consolation.
So among the shrubs I stand all alone,
yet never less alone <note>original: "né mai men sol." This is a famous literary trope (dating back to Cicero and Petrarch) suggesting that in solitude, the mind is most crowded with thoughts.</note>, for I have in my company
a thousand sweet and worthy thoughts of love.
There, with sweet tears, I bathe
my breast, and I nourish the heart that desires nothing
except that death or better times should come.

<summary>In these two sonnets, Lorenzo de' Medici explores the Petrarchan themes of pastoral solitude and the bittersweet memory of the beloved. He depicts a natural world that mirrors his internal grief, where the presence of the lady is felt only through her absence.</summary>

<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, sonnet, courtly love, pastoral landscape, solitude, Petrarchan influence, longing</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

# CIII

->[Far from her eyes, he sadly recalls his journey in love.]<-

I often sit upon a hard stone,
and with my arm I make a support for my cheek,
and I think to myself, retracing
my journey in love step by step;
and first the hour and the day that Love
made me weary, when he wanted me in his kingdom;
then every happy event and every disdain <note>original: "sdegno." In courtly poetry, this refers to the lady’s coldness or rejection, which paradoxically fuels the poet's devotion.</note>,
up to the time through which I currently pass.
Thus thinking of my so-long suffering
and of the days and the nights, as Love
wills, which I have already consumed in tears,
and seeing no end yet to such harm,
I accuse my fate: and what pains me most
is finding myself far from those holy lights <note>The "holy lights" (lumi santi) are the eyes of the beloved lady.</note>.

# CIV

->[The poet, content with his torments, takes pleasure in the servitude of Love.]<-

I thank you, Love, for every torment,
and, if I ever called you a cruel lord,
like a man who was guided by fury,
I repent for every ancient failing.
Because she for whom I feel my fortunate heart
burning in sweet fire,
is worthy of human and celestial honor,
and if I languish for her, I am content.
Oh fortunate and truly happy fate,
if it happens that one serves a noble and worthy
lord <note>A reference to the "feudalism of love," where the lover is a vassal to either the personified god Love or the lady herself.</note> and seeks his peace in him!
Already a thousand times I have desired death;
yet then I remain content with such disdain,
so much does being his servant please me in the end.

<note>Vocabulary from the original manuscript: rhymes, sonnet, Love, suffering, torment, servitude.</note>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (CIII and CIV) by Lorenzo de’ Medici. The first reflects on the history of his love while in exile from his lady's presence, while the second expresses a refined "courtly love" sentiment, where the poet thanks Love for his suffering.</summary>

<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, sonnets, courtly love, memory, servitude of love, melancholy, Petrarchan tradition</keywords>

<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### CV

->[Love has little glory, if he has lit the tinder of such a great fire.]<-

It brings you no honor, Love, to have captured
and deceived me in my tender years,
when my age was prone to deceptions,
and there is little glory if you have ignited the tinder.
And, if I surrendered, you have wronged me,
.  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  . <note>These dots indicate a missing line or lacuna in the original manuscript.</note>
against hard armor and not Venusian clothes <note>original: "venerei panni." Clothes associated with Venus, implying soft, courtly attire rather than the steel armor of a soldier.</note>,
reserve your arrows and your drawn bow:
for more glory usually results for the defeated,
if he is weak and the victor is powerful:
thus your glory fades bit by bit.
Once I saw you surrounded by a divine prison;
you held heaven and the world in a tremor,
and the Stygian marsh <note>The Styx, the river/marsh of the underworld in Greek mythology, over which even the gods swore oaths.</note>: now you merely burn the fire.

---

### CANZONE VII

->[The heart, conquered by the eyes of his lady,<-
->awaits death or reward for its sufferings.]<-

When a ray of sun,
through a tiny crack
entering the dark house of the bees,
warms and awakens them to the sweet season,
<margin>5</margin>they emerge ignited by a new care <note>original: "novella cura." In this context, "cura" refers to the diligent work or task of the bees as spring begins.</note>
through the lovely forest,
plundering greedily now this or that
species of flower, with which the earth is adorned:
one goes out, another returns
<margin>10</margin>laden with beautiful and fragrant prey;
one urges and presses on,
if she happens to see another idle at her work;
another drives away the vile drone <note>original: "fuco." The male bee that does not gather nectar, often used as a metaphor for idleness.</note>,

<vocab>Love (Amore), Song (Canzone), Rhymes (Rime), Styx (Stige), new care (novella cura), reward (guiderdone), drone (fuco), the tinder (l'ésca)</vocab>

<summary>This page contains Sonnet CV, a critique of Love for preying on the young and defenseless, and the opening of Canzone VII, which uses the naturalistic imagery of bees awakening in spring to describe the soul's devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Love, Styx, Venus, bees, allegory, spring, honor, servitude</keywords>

### SONG VIII

->Song composed while I was one day where certain ladies were,<-
->not without danger to myself.<-

To renew the ancient wounds of Love,
which either cold desire or its own little worth
had kept locked within the heart,
the ancient object <note>The beloved woman, the "object" of his affection.</note> and those lovely eyes
<margin>5</margin> suffused with pity
were offered to my eyes, and through them to my heart.
Her pious countenance <term>sembiante</term><note>From the Italian "sembiante," meaning outward appearance or facial expression; in Renaissance poetry, the face was thought to speak a silent language of the soul.</note> seemed to show that she felt grief,
not so much for my own hard fate,
but with humble and clever
<margin>10</margin> voices she seemed to ask mercy <term>merzede</term><note>A multifaceted term implying a reward, a favor, or an act of grace from the beloved to the lover.</note> for the harm done,
as is appropriate for such an unjust offense;
persuading the heart that it is too heavy a burden
to deny pardon to one who asks for it humbly.
This the beautiful face said, while remaining silent:
<margin>15</margin> none could hear it but a lover.

<summary>The first poem concludes with the poet's thoughts seeking the lady in a metaphorical meadow, while the second poem describes a silent, emotional exchange where the lady's expression seems to plead for forgiveness for the pain her beauty causes.</summary>

since, when he had me in his power,
he did not wish to keep me, let him lose all hope of me.

### BALLADE

#### [The Thoughts of Love.]

Light and ready, they depart
from within my breast, my thoughts,
which my sad soul sends as its messengers
to the mountains of love,
<margin>5</margin>to that noble breast where my heart dwells.
On the path of love,
each of them at every step finds
some piteous thought,
which seems to move from my lady’s breast
<margin>10</margin>to comfort my soul from time to time.
They stop together, and, then being questioned,
they all say one thing, ever new,
of the pity that sends them
out from that fair breast,
<margin>15</margin>within which the handsome lord <note>Amor (Love), often personified in medieval poetry as a lord or deity inhabiting the beloved's heart.</note> dwells;
and the heart would stay there
all alone, but Pity and Love are there as well.
From their ancient caverns,
the sun’s fervent and clear flame draws
<margin>20</margin>the little ants:
some clever and diligent one learns
and tells the others where the thrifty peasant
has hidden, with cunning, a little mound of grain:
whence the black, greedy swarm emerges.
<margin>25</margin>Hand in hand, all of them
go and come from the mound,
carrying the precious prize in mouth and in hand:

<summary>This page concludes a poem about the poet's liberation from love's "error" and begins a Ballade that uses the metaphor of ants gathering grain to describe his thoughts traveling to and from his lady's heart.</summary>
<keywords>Cupid, Love, Pity, personification of thoughts, ant simile, courtly love, messengers of the heart</keywords>

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Ballade of Thoughts," extending the extended metaphor of ants gathering grain to describe the poet’s thoughts traveling to his lady. It concludes the poem by describing the "sweet poison" of love—the bittersweet mixture of joy and sorrow felt when the poet realizes his lady suffers out of pity for him.</meta>

<page-num>236</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

they go out light and ready,
and return from abroad heavy and burdened.
<margin>30</margin> They pause their tiny footsteps
meeting on the path; and while one
rests, the other informs
her of the other prey <note>In this metaphor, the "prey" is the grain the ants gather, which represents the comforting thoughts the poet's mind "collects" from his lady.</note>, and with more desire
invites her always to the sweet labor.
<margin>35</margin> The long and worn path is crowded and busy.
And even if they all bring back the same thing,
it is always more dear and more welcome,
as anything must be
without which life would fail.
<margin>40</margin> The heavy <note>original: *ingiusto*, literally "unjust," here meaning a burden disproportionate to the ant's small size.</note> bundle is light,
if the little creature would die without it.
So my thoughts
fly more lightly to my beautiful lady:
encountering hers,
<margin>45</margin> they stop, and then speak with one another:
a sweet prey—though heavy—they bring
with them from that dear and immortal treasure:
it is always one thing, yet always more beautiful:
which from that noble breast
<margin>50</margin> where Love and Pity reign,
were driven out by sorrowful sighs.
Hence the soul rejoices and feels disdain <note>The "disdain" or "indignation" arises from the soul's conflict: it is happy to be loved but pained that this love causes the lady to sigh and suffer.</note>
at the same time, and feels a sweet sorrow.
It feels sweetness, if it senses
<margin>55</margin> Love and Pity reigning in her white breast;
the afflicted mind grieves
that those beautiful thoughts should be driven out
by harsh thoughts, and that my lady
should be sorrowful and sad because of me; and thus mixed,
<margin>60</margin> pain and desire create a sweet poison:
from which the soul either acquires a bitter life
or a sweet death,
for it enjoys its ill and is saddened by its own good.

<summary>This page concludes the Ballade, using the industrious ant as a metaphor for how the poet’s thoughts "gather" comfort from his lady's heart. It ends with the classic Petrarchan paradox of "sweet poison," where the lover's joy is inextricably linked to the lady's sympathetic suffering.</summary>
<keywords>metaphor, ants, thoughts, Love, Pity, sweet poison, paradox, Italian lyric poetry</keywords>

This is the dear burden <note>original: *salma*, literally "corpse" or "remains," but here used metaphorically to mean the heavy load or "prey" the thoughts/ants are carrying.</note>,
<margin>65</margin> burdened with which my thoughts give me strength.

<margin>70</margin> following the example of the cruel and wise heart;

forgetful of the soul, dwelling there with Love.

<margin>75</margin> help in such great distress

<margin>80</margin> each one remains confounded:
for he who sets out prizes only the goal:
they move toward this, and, not finding the end <note>The poet suggests her beauty is so vast it is an "infinite end," a goal that cannot be fully reached or exhausted.</note>,

<margin>85</margin> in the infinite end of such sweetness.
I give great thanks to you,
merciful and beautiful thoughts <note>Referring to the lady's thoughts mentioned in line 76.</note>,
who come to the aid of the heart in its wanderings:
and, were it not for those,
<margin>90</margin> in this too-lofty undertaking the heart would die.

<summary>This concluding section of the poem describes the poet's thoughts becoming "lost" in the infinite beauty of the beloved, choosing to remain with her rather than return to his soul. The poet expresses his dependence on the lady's own compassionate thoughts to sustain his heart in this state of abandonment.</summary>

### CVI

->*[A New Wound of Love.]*<- <note>The bracketed titles in this section were likely added by later editors to summarize the theme of each poem.</note>

The green of all my hope had already,
as Love willed, been reduced to white <note>In the color symbolism of the time, "green" represented youthful hope and vitality, while "white" represented the pallor of old age or the fading of passion.</note>:
my heart seemed lacking in its strength,
whereby it had lost all its boldness;
when that power which surpasses every other,
Love, drew a golden arrow <note>original: *stral d'òr*. In classical mythology, Cupid’s golden arrows inspired love, while his leaden ones repelled it.</note> from his side,
and pierced the unconquered heart, proud and frank,
with force enough to break any constancy.
.  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  . <note>These dots indicate missing lines in the original manuscript or damaged text that cannot be recovered.</note>
.  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  . and he would have been more captured by it
if not for the fact that he fears amorous deceptions.
Amidst the grass, he seemed to see
a snare laid out and hidden; now I know not which has
grown more in me, the fear or the hope.

### CVII

->*[“A great flame is not extinguished all at once.”]*<-

I am not satisfied with a single farewell
to depart from the banners of Love <note>original: *amorose insegne*. This uses the common metaphor of "militia amoris," where being a lover is compared to serving as a soldier under a lord's banner.</note>:
for a great flame is not extinguished all at once,
nor can a long grief be healed in a short time.
.  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .
.  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .  .
sweet desire, wise and worthy words,
now I steal away from my first thoughts.
My tears full of every sweetness,
sweet sighs and changed fates,
for another destiny, other thoughts lead me;
yet let this one blessing be granted to me,
to remember, at least until death,
my angelic, living, and bright light <note>A traditional reference to the lady, often comparing her eyes or her soul to a divine light that guides the poet.</note>.

<vocab>Love, hope, virtue (or strength), tears, grief, farewell, rhymes</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two sonnets (CVI and CVII) concerning the persistence of love. The first describes a heart being unexpectedly wounded by Love's golden arrow after hope had faded, while the second reflects on the impossibility of ending a long-term devotion with a single goodbye.</summary>
<keywords>Love, Cupid's arrow, hope and fear, farewell, poetic metaphors, Stil Novo, memory of the lady</keywords>

<page-num>239</page-num>
<header>III - RHYMES</header>

### CVIII

->*[The Power of His Lady's Gaze]*<- <note>This title was likely added by a later editor to summarize the poem's theme.</note>

What wonder is it, if that noble fire <note>original: *gentil foco*. In the medieval and Renaissance poetic tradition, a "noble" or "gentle" fire refers to a love that can only be felt by a refined and virtuous heart.</note>
in which I sweetly burn flares up ever more?
If I look upon that beautiful face a thousand times,
it returns a thousand new sweetnesses to my eyes.
   The heart, to which this beauty descends,
is filled with marvel, and it accuses the dull and slow eye
of laziness, reproaching it
for failing to see the power <term>Virtú (Power/Virtue): Referring to the inherent spiritual or miraculous strength believed to reside in a lady's eyes.</term> of that beautiful gaze.
   Love sees my weary eyes
through the eyes of my lady, and he speaks to my heart
through the sweet rays of her merciful gaze <note>original: *vista pia*. The word *pia* denotes a combination of pity, kindness, and spiritual devotion.</note>:
   — “Infinite is the worth from which proceeds
to your eyes this ever-new sweetness:
the eye is mortal; let the fire be eternal.” — <note>This concluding line reflects the Neoplatonic idea that while the physical body (the eye) is subject to death, the spiritual passion (the fire) ignited by beauty is immortal.</note>

---

<vocab>rhymes, sonnet, gaze, love, lady</vocab>

<summary>A sonnet exploring how the poet's love is renewed every time he looks at his lady, concluding with a message from Love itself regarding the eternal nature of his passion.</summary>
<keywords>sonnet, gaze, noble fire, immortality of love, beauty, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 247 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of a major new section in the collected works of Lorenzo de' Medici. The preceding section contained his "Rhymes" (Rime), specifically concluding with Sonnet CVIII. This page introduces the "Selve d'Amore," shifting from the compact sonnet form to the more expansive "ottava rima" stanzas.</meta>

## IV

# FORESTS OF LOVE <note>The title *Selve* (literally "woods" or "forests") is a translation of the Latin *Sylvae*. Following the tradition of the Roman poet Statius, this term was used during the Renaissance to describe a collection of varied or "miscellaneous" poems, often characterized by a rich, descriptive, and pastoral style.</note>

### STANZAS <term>Stanze (Stanzas): This refers specifically to the "ottava rima" form, a poetic structure consisting of eight-line stanzas that Lorenzo used for his longer narrative and descriptive poems.</term>

<summary>This page serves as the section header for the "Selve d'Amore" (Forests of Love) by Lorenzo de' Medici, signaling a transition from his shorter lyric poems to longer, narrative stanzas.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Forests of Love, Selve d'Amore, Stanze, Stanzas, Renaissance poetry, Italian literature</keywords>

--- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Selve d'Amore" (Forests of Love) introduced on the previous page. Having transitioned from the sonnet form, Lorenzo now employs the "ottava rima" (eight-line stanzas) to explore the philosophical and emotional paradoxes of being "bound" by love. This first "Forest" begins with a celebration of his captivity.</meta>

# FIRST FOREST <note>original: "Selva Prima". As established in the section header, the term "Forest" refers to a miscellaneous collection of poetic thoughts, modeled after the Latin *Sylvae*.</note>

->1<-

O sweet servitude <term>Servitú (Servitude): A standard trope in Renaissance poetry where the lover describes his devotion as a form of voluntary enslavement to the beloved, which paradoxically brings him higher status.</term> that liberated
the heart from every low and vile service,
when to such beautiful service you bound me
you released the heart from a hundred lowly cares!
O beautiful hand, when today you bound me,
you made me free and noble!
Blessed be those first knots,
Love, with which you bound me in so many ways!

->2<-

O sweet and beautiful sovereign <note>original: "signore". Though the word is masculine (lord), it is used here to address the beloved woman or the personification of Love that governs the poet.</note>, in whom gather
beauty and nobility, such that they exceed
all others in any other person, and then among themselves each
claims the highest rank within my lady!
How sweet and blessed is Fortune,
who gave me as a servant to such a noble sovereign;
and I am a servant more free and worthy than any other,
serving one for whom to serve is to reign! <note>The idea that "to serve is to reign" (servir è regno) reflects the belief that true freedom is found in submitting to a virtuous and noble power.</note>

->3<-

Thus, if both banks restrain
the river, it happily maintains its slow course;
sweetly to the eyes it leads its clear wave,
and keeps the fish within its quiet bed;
it bathes the green bank, full of lovely flowers,
and so seems to serve joyfully;
it stays in the dark cave, from which the tranquil wave
presses and flows with a sweet murmur:

<summary>The opening stanzas of Lorenzo's "First Forest" celebrate the paradox of love, where being "bound" to a noble beloved provides more freedom and dignity than being unattached. He uses a nature metaphor, comparing his restrained heart to a river that flows beautifully only when held within its banks.</summary>
<keywords>courtly love, servitude, freedom, nature metaphor, river, nobility, ottava rima, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

--- Page 250 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Forest" of Lorenzo de' Medici's *Selve d'Amore*. Stanza 4 completes the metaphor of the river begun on the previous page, contrasting the controlled, "bound" river with a flooded, unrestrained one. The following stanzas extend this theme through other natural elements: wind and fire.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->4<-

but, if a stormy and thick cloud <note>original: "nimbo". A Latinate term for a rain cloud or a localized storm.</note> rains down,
removing the light of the sun from us,
little by little you see it swell then,
until at last it no longer fits within itself,
and it carries away the labors of the already tired oxen,
dragging forests and pushing stones within its path;
the grassy bank in the middle and the curved bridge
are submerged, and the clear spring becomes a turbid lake.

->5<-

When a soft little wind breathes
with a sweet law, it bends the flowers to the ground,
and playing with them it circles around;
sometimes it knots them, now loosens, now reties them;
it impregnates the grain; the grass near
to the future scythe <note>An image of hay or wheat ready for harvest.</note> waves high and tosses;
the young branches give forth
a sweet sound, and not a single flower falls to the ground.

->6<-

But, if Aeolus <term>Aeolus: The ruler of the winds in Greek mythology, who kept the winds imprisoned in a cave on the island of Lipari.</term> gives liberty from the cave
to the tempestuous and fierce winds,
not only does he prune the green branches,
but the whole ancient pines fall to the ground:
the angry sea threatens the wretched ships
with their hooked prows, and seems to despair:
the air takes on a veil of thick mists;
thus the earth, the sea, and the sky grieve.

->7<-

A small spark, struck from the stone,
nourished by leaves and small dry branches,
provides warmth; but, struck by the rapid wind,
it burns the brush first, the shoots and sticks;
then, near the thick and dense forest,
it sets fire to the oaks and the tall, old holm-oaks:
its wrath fulfills a cruel enmity toward the woods:
smoke and sparks and a strange screeching fill the air.

<summary>Lorenzo uses vivid natural allegories—a flooding river, a rising gale, and a spreading wildfire—to illustrate how gentle, life-giving forces turn destructive when they break their natural "bounds." This serves as a metaphor for the power of love and human passion.</summary>
<keywords>nature, allegory, Aeolus, storm, wildfire, Lorenzo de' Medici, pastoral poetry, Renaissance imagery</keywords>

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Forest" (Selva Prima) of Lorenzo de' Medici’s *Selve d’Amore*. Following the descriptions of wind and the start of a fire from the previous page, these stanzas conclude the metaphor of elemental destruction before shifting into a Neoplatonic meditation on the "sweet chain" of love that binds the universe and the poet's own heart.</meta>

<page-num>245</page-num>
<header>FIRST FOREST</header>

->8<-

The shady homes <note>The poet refers here to the dens and burrows of animals.</note> are in flames, and the sweet nests
are gone, along with the ancient, high forest stalls;
nor does any wild beast seem to trust the woods,
but flees in terror, turning its back to the fire:
various bellows and screeches fill the sky:
the dark valley echoes back the sound:
the careless shepherd, from whom the fire 
escaped, weeps, astonished and disheartened.

->9<-

A benign law has set the limit for the water
so that it does not cross it and cover the earth:
in the middle of the great body <note>The Earth.</note> the center is hidden,
heavy and contrary to the fire, which is above:
diverse things have composed a whole;
though contrary to one another, they create a harmonious work:
a benign law orders and moves the heavens:
a sweet chain binds and rules the whole.

->10<-

That happy day, a delicate hand placed
a sweet and beautiful chain around my neck,
which opened my breast and wrote within my heart
that name, and sculpted that beautiful human countenance.
Since then, my eyes <note>original: "luci". Literally "lights," a common poetic term for eyes.</note> have always gazed fixedly
upon such beautiful eyes, that every other object is in vain.
This unique beauty now alone contents
 my sight, which was previously intent on a thousand things.

->11<-

Not valleys adorned with sunny leaves;
not the clear stream that waters the grass,
painted with colors of white, red, and yellow;
not great cities or magnificent buildings,
fierce spectacles <note>original: "ludî fèri". Likely a reference to ancient gladiatorial games or hunts.</note>, strange games, or soft dances;
not ships <note>original: "legni". Literally "woods," used metonymically for ships.</note> at sea accompanied by the west wind;
not pretty birds, new animals, or monsters;
not sculpted stone or gems please our eyes.

<vocab>Forest, shepherd, fire, benign law, chain, countenance, buildings</vocab>

<summary>Lorenzo describes the devastation of a forest fire caused by a shepherd's negligence before transitioning into a philosophical praise of the "sweet chain" of cosmic order, which he then connects to the personal experience of being captivated by his beloved.</summary>
<keywords>forest fire, pastoral imagery, Neoplatonism, Great Chain of Being, courtly love, landscape, elements</keywords>

--- Page 252 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Forest" of Lorenzo de' Medici’s *Selve d’Amore*. Following the previous page’s description of the "sweet chain" that binds the universe and the poet's initial sight of his beloved, these stanzas explore the total devotion of the lover and contrast the "sweet bonds" of love with the violent chains of mythology.</meta>

<page-num>246</page-num>
<header>IV - FORESTS OF LOVE</header>

->12<-

In these things, without any set rule,
my eyes went wandering, searching for their hidden
peace; they did not know it was found in this one woman,
who, once known, pleases them so greatly.
Secretly, my happy fortune
guided the desire that lies within my heart:
my heart was led, though it did not know it,
to see again the one it had already seen.

->13<-

That day, then, when that amorous hand
painted the beautiful image within my heart,
it constrained my eyes with a voluntary bridle
to gaze only at her, and not at this or that;
it gathered a thousand various thoughts into one;
nor does my tongue speak now of anything else,
nor do my amorous footsteps seek anything else:
my heart remains bound to her forever.

->14<-

Savage <term>Fury: personification of uncontrolled rage or war</term> stays bound
in the great temple of Janus <note>original: "tempio di Giano". In Roman tradition, the doors of the Temple of Janus were closed during times of peace, symbolizing that War/Fury was locked inside.</note>
with a thousand and a thousand knots:
he tries to loosen one hand and then the other:
he seethes, stained with blood and full of horror.
Cerberus <note>The three-headed hound of the underworld.</note> in the low kingdom, blind and vain,
terrifies the mournful shadows with his barking:
held fast by three chains, he seems possessed by ire;
rage, foam, and venom fall from his teeth.

->15<-

My beautiful chain does not bind
my gentle heart, full of sweetness, in such a way:
composed of three knots, she leads it joyfully
with her hands: beauty made the first,
pity <note>original: "pietá". In this context, it refers to the lady's mercy or compassion toward the suffering lover.</note> the second—for such sweet suffering—
and Love the third; no time shall ever break them:
the beautiful hand then pulled them tight together,
and with such a sweet bond entwined my heart.

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici contrasts the terrifying chains of war and the underworld with the "sweet chain" of love that binds his heart. He describes love as a trinity of beauty, pity, and desire that focuses his entire being on his beloved.</summary>

<keywords>Neoplatonism, allegory, Temple of Janus, Cerberus, chains of love, beauty, pity, Lorenzo de' Medici, Petrarchan imagery</keywords>

--- Page 253 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Forest" of Lorenzo de' Medici’s Selve d’Amore. The previous page concluded with the poet describing the "sweet chain" of his love, contrasting it with the violent chains that bind the mythological figure of Fury and the hound Cerberus. Stanza 15 was interrupted while explaining how beauty and grace began to weave this voluntary bond.</meta>

<page-num>247</page-num>
<header>FIRST FOREST</header>

->16<-

Love showed me on that blessed day
her serene eyes <note>original: "luci serene". Literally "serene lights," a common poetic metaphor for the eyes.</note> more beautiful than ever,
with all the Graces <note>In mythology, these are the three goddesses of charm, beauty, and creativity who attend to Venus.</note> gathered around my lady;
nor did he use any other chains to bind me again.
Is it any wonder if I do not return to myself?
Or what desire flees from its own good?
Supreme beauty, love, and sweet clemency
do a voluntary violence to my heart.

->17<-

When this chain was woven,
the air, the earth, and the heaven happily joined in:
the air was never so serene,
nor did the sun ever offer such beautiful light;
the happy earth was full of young leaves and flowers
where a clear stream ran;
Venus <note>original: "Ciprigna". An epithet for the goddess Venus, referring to her mythical birth on the island of Cyprus.</note> sat in her father’s <note>Jupiter, representing the sky.</note> lap that day,
looked down happily from heaven upon that place, and smiled.

->18<-

From her divine head and amorous breast,
she took various roses with both hands
and scattered them through the quiet, serene sky:
she covered my lady with these flowers.
Benign Jove <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods.</note>, full of joy,
opened human ears on that beautiful day
to hear the celestial melody
which came from heaven in songs, rhythms, and sounds.

->19<-

Beautiful women moved their feet to the sound,
dancing, lit by a noble <term>gentile: in the courtly tradition, this refers to nobility of spirit and refinement of character rather than social rank</term> love:
you could see the lover near his lady,
taking her desired hands in his;
glances, nods, sighs—the remedies of love;
brief words understood only by them;
he gathers the flowers that have fallen from the lady,
and after kissing them, replaces them on her head and in her breast.

<vocab>First Forest, Love, The Graces, Venus, Jove, melody, dance, poetry</vocab>

<summary>In these stanzas, Lorenzo describes a cosmic celebration of love where the gods Venus and Jupiter bless the union of the lovers, manifesting as a perfect spring day filled with celestial music. The scene transitions from divine intervention to a courtly dance where lovers exchange secret glances and gestures.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Selve d'Amore, courtly love, Venus, Jupiter, the Graces, celestial music, spring imagery, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Forest" of Lorenzo de' Medici’s Selve d’Amore. The previous page established a scene of divine harmony, with Venus and Jupiter watching over a landscape where the poet's lady is the central figure. The narrative now shifts from the external setting to the intimate exchange between the lover and the beloved, specifically focusing on the "theft" of the poet's heart.</meta>

<page-num>248</page-num>
<header>IV - THE FORESTS OF LOVE</header>

->20<-

Amidst so many pleasing and beautiful things,
my most beautiful and noble lady,
surpassing all others, adorned them all:
clothed in a white and delicate dress,
speaking in new and silent words
with her eyes to my heart, while her mouth is silent <note>original: "sile". A Latinism for "is silent," emphasizing the sophisticated, classical tone of the poem.</note>:
— Come here — she said to me, — my dear heart:
here is the peace for your every desire. —

->21<-

This sweet voice opened my breast,
and compelled my happy heart to depart:
her beautiful hand offered itself to meet it
halfway, and gently squeezed it:
though it was once rough, she transformed it into nobility <term>gentilezza: in the Renaissance, this referred to a "nobility of soul" and refined character rather than just social rank</term>;
then she painted her own beautiful name and face upon it:
thus adorned with such beautiful things,
she hid it away within her own breast.

->22<-

There it remains; from there it cannot depart;
it cannot depart, because it does not wish to leave:
a sweeter object for its high desire
it neither has nor can have, and therefore has no such will:
the heart is a law unto itself; it chooses and desires
to serve this noble law:
with its own hand it has fashioned its own bonds,
nor does it wish to even have the capacity to be pleased by another.

->23<-

Let whoever wishes gaze upon different things,
constantly renewing various objects before their eyes;
if it happens that now one and then another attracts him,
it seems he finds true beauty in none;
but, like a greedy and wandering bee <note>original: "pecchia". An archaic Italian word for a bee, often used in poetry to describe the restless search for beauty.</note>, let him circle
always seeking new flowers to nourish himself;
he would not change his slow flight so often,
if that which is found in many flowers were contained in only one.

<summary>The poet describes how his lady's silent gaze and sweet invitation lead to his heart being "captured" and refined by her, ultimately finding total contentment in her alone. He contrasts this singular devotion with the restless, unsatisfied wandering of those who seek beauty in many different places.</summary>
<keywords>courtly love, the heart, nobility, constancy, bee metaphor, Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance poetry</keywords>

--- Page 255 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 248, the poet transitions from the moment his heart was taken by his lady to a reflection on the nature of her beauty. In the preceding stanzas, the lady was established as a semi-divine figure. Now, the poet describes the process of recognizing her as the ultimate source of all earthly beauty, using the metaphor of a hunter's dog tracking its prey.</meta>

<page-num>249</page-num>
<header>FIRST FOREST</header>

->24<-

In the first moment when Love <note>original: "Amor". Here personified as the god of love, Cupid.</note> opened his eyes,
he placed this beauty before my desire:
and after he offered her to me, beautiful as she is,
laughing—alas!—he hid her from my eyes.
With how many tears were different beauties
then sought, for how much time, and in how many things!
At times my sorrowful eyes would see
something that resembled this beauty.

->25<-

Then (just as an eager dog on the hunt,
trying to find the hidden beast among the leaves,
if he sees the ground impressed with tracks,
knows by the sign that she had passed that way—
because the resemblance seems to provide
a certain proof of true beauty),
so, searching through this thing and that,
Love finally showed me my beautiful lady.

->26<-

Then my joyful eyes said to my heart: — This
is the one whom Love showed for the first time,
desired and sought by us alone,
shown and returned after she was taken from us.
Her true sweetness reveals
how much grace and virtue <term>virtute: in the Renaissance, this often referred to an inherent "power" or "excellence" that produces a spiritual or physical effect, as well as moral goodness</term> she has gathered.
Among many others we never found this one,
who alone unites every beauty within herself.

->27<-

Rather, she is always found in every part;
for whatever is beautiful to the eyes comes from her.
The living fountain of all good gives to the world
various beauties scattered among various things;
and that which other things show only in part,
is contained in her entirely and perfectly. <note>This reflects Neo-Platonic philosophy, popular in Lorenzo de' Medici's Florentine court, which held that all beautiful things on earth are merely imperfect reflections or shadows of a single, divine Source of Beauty.</note>
And, if a mere resemblance pleases the eyes,
how much more perfect is their peace here!

<summary>Lorenzo describes the poet's search for his lady's unique beauty, recognizing it in other objects only as faint "tracks" or reflections of her perfect form. This culminates in the realization that she is the "living fountain" and the perfect sum of all earthly beauty.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, Amor, beauty, hunting metaphor, Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance poetry, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 249, where the poet describes his lady as the singular source of all earthly beauty. He has just explained that while other things possess beauty only in part, she unites all perfections within herself, acting as a "living fountain."</meta>

<page-num>250</page-num>
<header>IV - FORESTS OF LOVE</header>

<note>original: "Selve d'Amore". The title refers to the classical genre of "Sylvae," meaning a collection of diverse poems or "timbers" gathered to build a larger work.</note>

->28<-

Opposing voices create a sweet sound,
and different colors a new beauty:
the high pitch is pleasing because of the deep:
white finds its grace within the black.
Wonderfully has the highest beauty
made it so that one enemy <note>In this context, "enemy" refers to opposites in a state of tension or contrast.</note> benefits the other;
that highest beauty, which every heart desires
and which I see only in my lady.

->29<-

This one alone I long for: and my burning eyes <note>original: "luci". Literally "lights," a common poetic metaphor for eyes in Renaissance verse.</note>
do not make their stay in any other thing.
And, just as the intent and blessed spirits
stand forever around the holy face,
nor can the pure celestial minds
look at anything else, for everything else is less adorned;
so that first moment and that beautiful place
placed a similar sweet yoke <note>A "sweet yoke" is a traditional metaphor for the voluntary and pleasant servitude of being in love.</note> upon my neck.

->30<-

I feel my heart within the loving breast
of my noble lady, which wishes to sing,
and, in praising that blessed time,
uses the beautiful mouth (as is its custom)
of my lady for such a pleasing effect,
a sweet instrument for song and for words.
The happy and joyful heart cannot contain itself:
so, singing within her mouth, it says:

->31<-

— O blessed day,
day that was the first for our eyes!
You who with the true light
chase away every shadow, and show that they were but shadows!
An invisible shadow it was,
that was always surrounding our eyes:
and yet we saw this,
and the high and serene light,

<vocab>Selve d'Amore: Forests of Love, ottava rima: an eight-line poetic stanza, beauty, lady, love, rhymes, Lorenzo de' Medici</vocab>

<summary>In these stanzas, the poet explores the Neoplatonic idea that beauty arises from the harmony of opposites and describes his total devotion to his lady, whose presence is compared to the divine vision enjoyed by angels. The poet’s heart has metaphorically moved into the lady's breast, and he begins a song of praise for the day they first met.</summary>

<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Neoplatonism, harmony of opposites, divine beauty, courtly love, Petrarchan imagery, mystical union</keywords>

# FIRST FOREST

->32<-

...[shadows which others] could not see: oh sad eyes!
Oh, fortunate time for me,
who opened my eyes to such a beautiful sun! <note>original: "sol". In Renaissance poetry, the "sun" is a standard metaphor for the beloved, representing both her physical beauty and her role as a source of spiritual guidance.</note>
Perhaps I shall seem ungrateful,
sweet time, since from you
comes all my happiness,
if my heart, made happy through you, now only desires
that this blessing might exist without any time at all. <note>The poet expresses a classic lover's paradox: he blesses the specific moment in time he met his lady, yet his joy is so great that he wishes the experience could be removed from the flow of time and made eternal.</note> —

<term>Selva Prima</term> (First Forest), <term>occhi</term> (eyes), <term>tempo</term> (time), <term>bene</term> (well-being or "my good"), <term>cor</term> (heart), <term>sanza</term> (without).

<summary>The poet concludes a song of praise regarding the moment he first saw his beloved, acknowledging time as the source of his joy while simultaneously wishing for that joy to transcend time and become eternal.</summary>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page marks the beginning of the "Second Forest" (Selva Seconda). The previous page concluded the "First Forest" with a meditation on time and eternity. Here, the tone shifts to a formal lament of separation and longing, utilizing the "ottava rima" (eight-line stanza) form typical of Italian Renaissance epic and lyric poetry.</meta>

# SECOND FOREST <note>The title "Selva" (literally "Forest" or "Wood") refers to a literary collection of varied poems. In the Renaissance, this genre suggested a "natural" gathering of different themes and styles, much like the diverse trees found in a wild wood.</note>

->1<-

After so many sighs and so many cries of "alas," <note>original: "omei". This is a pluralized form of the exclamation "omè" (alas), used here to quantify the lover's repeated expressions of grief.</note>
Love, I do not see that beautiful, adorned face:
after so many sorrows and wretched weepings,
those beautiful eyes, alas, do not return.
O deceptive hope, O thoughts of mine,
held for so long now, from day to day!
When will it be that I gaze upon those beautiful eyes?
I know not: let it be when it will, for it will be late.

->2<-

My beautiful eyes, O clever little words,
I see you no more, alas! and I hear you no more!
O hours, now long, though you were once so short,
enemies then and now to my contentment! <note>The poet explores the paradox of time: when he was with his lady, the hours felt too short; now that they are apart, they feel agonizingly long. In both cases, the passage of time seems to work against his happiness.</note>
O my destiny, O cursed fate,
have pity at last upon my torment:
return those beautiful eyes to my eyes,
for without them I could no longer live.

->3<-

Alas! I do not live, and yet I cannot die,
far, alas! from those beautiful, holy lights: <note>original: "lumi santi". It was common in Renaissance love poetry to refer to the beloved's eyes as "lights" or "stars," suggesting their power to provide spiritual and physical direction.</note>
I do not live, for my life is with her;
here remains only the body, sighs, and tears:
a blind hope nourishes my sorrows,
and does not permit the thread to snap. <note>The "thread" refers to the classical mythological concept of the thread of life spun by the Fates. The poet suggests that even though he feels he should die from grief, a flickering hope keeps him tethered to the world.</note>
Love, to whom I have given myself forever,
keeps me miraculously in this state.

<summary>This page begins the "Second Forest" with a sequence of stanzas lamenting the absence of the beloved. The poet describes a state of "living death," where he is physically present but spiritually absent, kept alive only by a painful, "blind" hope.</summary>

<keywords>Second Forest, separation, eyes as lights, time's relativity, hope, Love (personified), living death, ottava rima</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the "Second Forest" (Selva Seconda) from the previous page. Having established his grief over being separated from his beloved, Lorenzo now moves into a series of "if only" wishes, drawing heavily on the classical tradition of Ovidian metamorphosis—imagining himself transformed into objects or plants just to be near her.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->4<-

Why are the wild beasts, the woods, the mountains, and the rivers more happy than you, my weary eyes? Why are the stones more happy than you, since they at least occasionally see those lovely lights? <note>original: "vaghi lumi." In Renaissance lyric poetry, the eyes are frequently called "lights" or "stars." The word "vago" carries a double meaning of "beautiful" and "wandering" or "desirous."</note> My life, which remains here without them, must consume itself in weeping. At least let the end come quickly, if I must remain for so long without seeing that loving face.

->5<-

If only, upon that beautiful mountain where she now stays all alone without me, those beautiful lights had, with their ready power, turned me into one of those hardest stones! Perhaps she would have sometimes looked upon me with a piteous face, or touched me with her light steps: if I could feel it, I would have every desire fulfilled; if not, I would at least be beyond such great sorrow. <note>The poet suggests that even as an unfeeling stone, his suffering would end, but as a sentient stone, he might feel the touch of her feet.</note>

->6<-

If only that holy light had turned me into the leaves from which I take my name! <note>Lorenzo is making a sophisticated pun on his own name. "Lorenzo" (Laurentius) is derived from the "Lauro" or Laurel tree. This references the myth of Apollo and Daphne, where the nymph transforms into a laurel to escape the god, who then wears her leaves as a crown.</note> Perhaps, passing then by that plant, she would have piteously plucked some branch; and while she now speaks or sings with Love, perhaps her hand, which I love so much, would have made a little garland and placed it on her head. At least let me be the grass crushed by that beautiful foot!

->7<-

If only she had, with her steady gaze, turned that human look into a fountain upon the beautiful mountain where my paradise is! Perhaps sometimes her white hand would have bathed there, and her beautiful face would have been mirrored in the waters, from which I am so far away. If only she had at least turned me into a wild beast: seeing her, I would not have fled. <note>The poet reverses the natural order; while wild animals usually flee from humans, he would remain rooted by her beauty.</note>

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici employs the "ottava rima" form to express a lover's longing, wishing to be transformed into stones, trees, or water to achieve proximity to his lady. These stanzas demonstrate the Renaissance fascination with Ovidian metamorphosis and the poet's playful use of his own name (Lorenzo/Lauro).</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance poetry, Selve d'Amore, metamorphosis, courtly love, laurel, Ovidian influence, pastoral landscape</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the "Second Forest" (Selva Seconda) from page 254. Having previously imagined himself transformed into a stone or a laurel tree to be near his beloved, Lorenzo now shifts into a direct lamentation of his current state, addressing Love (Amor) as both his tormentor and his only companion.</meta>

# SECOND FOREST

->8<-

I still sigh: and my sighs vanish into the wind;
I call out your beautiful name, and no one responds;
I weep in vain, I grieve and I lament;
a sweet sleep no longer covers my weeping eyes <note>original: "umide luci." Literally "moist lights," a common Renaissance metaphor for eyes.</note>;
and I feel a fire within,
which burns me always and confounds my thoughts.
I can do no more, O my false hope!
Nothing pleases me now but her, or death.

->9<-

O sweetest nights, O happy days,
amorous sighs, O sweet weeping!
O Love, witness to our beautiful secrets,
long vigils, O sweet words, O songs!
O cruel destiny, why do you now forbid me these things,
and shatter the beautiful desire of wretched lovers?
You gave me such great bliss, then stripped it away,
only to make the sorrow in which I live even greater. <note>This reflects a common theme in Renaissance poetry: that the memory of past happiness only serves to increase present suffering.</note>

->10<-

If I am never to see those beautiful eyes again,
let my own be closed, and never see the light again;
for everything else that I might see, besides them,
brings only greater sorrow to my heart.
Love, you who speak with me of my suffering
and who are my escort and guide <term>duce</term> <note>From the Latin "dux," meaning a leader or guide, specifically one who shows the way through a difficult journey.</note> through these pains,
return my peace to me through those eyes,
or cut short my life, if it please you.

->11<-

I know well, my dear and sweet lord,
the pain you feel for my torments;
even from here, I see that pious face
bathed in tears, and I hear your laments;
your words, your pity, your desire,
and your amorous thoughts are ever-present to me,
along with a thousand other signs of burning passion:
and all this only increases my immense sorrow. <note>The "dear and sweet lord" addressed here is Love (Amor/Cupid). Lorenzo personifies Love as a companion who suffers alongside the lover, witnessing and sharing in his grief.</note>

<vocab>sighs, Love, sorrow, hope, secrets, vigils, escort, guide, desire</vocab>

<summary>Lorenzo expresses the deep agony of separation from his beloved, addressing Love (Amor) as a personified companion who shares in his grief. He reflects on lost happiness and concludes that if he cannot see his lady's eyes again, he would prefer the darkness of death.</summary>
<keywords>despair, Amor, Cupid, courtly love, memory, grief, Renaissance lyric, longing</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the "Second Forest" (Selva Seconda) from the "Forests of Love" (Selve d'Amore) collection. The poet continues his lamentation, shifting from a general address to Love toward a specific exploration of the physical and emotional distance between himself and his beloved.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->12<-

Love and my own habit still lead me
to the place where our last glances were exchanged,
the end of my bliss, and the beginning of such great pain;
nor do I see those beautiful eyes, wherever I look;
wherefore, sorrowful and sad and barely alive,
I depart, and move my steps slowly and late
to some other place, so that I might see then,
at least from afar, where my beloved dwells.

->13<-

There I speak with Love and with myself,
and I say a thousand times: — Alas, weary me!
There is my beautiful lord <note>original: "bel signor." In the courtly love tradition, the poet often refers to his lady using masculine titles of feudal lordship to signify his total devotion and her power over him.</note>, perhaps resting
under the shade of trees or by a rock;
some coarse peasant speaks with her
or someone else, who does not care or move a step away:
and I, who live only by the sight of her,
am so far distant: now weep, sad soul. —

->14<-

I do not know what to say, nor what I should think,
without a swarm of infinite sighs:
for perhaps someone else gazes longingly <note>original: "vagheggia." This term implies a lingering, admiring, and desire-filled gaze.</note> at those beautiful eyes,
and it seems they look fixedly and closely at them,
and touch and hold that beautiful hand:
and, to fully increase my torments <term>martíri</term> <note>From the Greek "martys," meaning witness; in this context, it refers to the "martyrdom" or extreme suffering endured by a devoted lover.</note>,
Love finally shows her to me as the prey of others—
her beauty and our sweetness.

->15<-

Alas! what pain I feel, when I remember
who enjoys such beauty of hers in peace,
and sees and touches those polished limbs <note>original: "pulite membra." In Renaissance aesthetics, "pulite" (literally "clean" or "polished") describes limbs that are smooth, elegant, and perfectly formed, often compared to ivory or marble.</note>
at every hour, whenever he wishes, and does not even prize them!
Fortune <term>Fortuna</term> <note>The personified force of chance or fate that governs human affairs, often depicted as a fickle goddess with a wheel.</note> divides me, stretches and dismembers me
from her beautiful face and from such sweetness:
nor do I desire or value anything in the world except those
limbs, and I cannot even hear news of them.

<vocab>Forests of Love, Love, Fortune, torments, sad soul</vocab>

<summary>In these stanzas, the poet wanders to the site of his last meeting with his beloved, tortured by the physical distance between them. He expresses intense jealousy, imagining indifferent strangers or rivals enjoying the presence and touch of the woman he idolizes.</summary>
<keywords>longing, courtly love, jealousy, Fortune, physical absence, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

### SECOND FOREST
->LORENZO THE MAGNIFICENT, Works - I.<-
->17<-

### 16

And if I hear any news at all,
it is only what my own thought represents to me
among so many of my sufferings <term>martír</term> <note>original: "martír." Literally "martyrdoms," a common Renaissance metaphor for the intense emotional pain of unrequited or distant love.</note>, a thousand fears;
and longing and jealousy still torment me,
desire, spite, envy, and sad cares;
and Fortune <note>In the Renaissance, Fortune (Fortuna) was often personified as a fickle goddess who actively pursued individuals to bring about their downfall.</note>, ready and attentive to my harm,
pursues me always; Love <term>Amor</term> kills me,
then rejoices and laughs at my great misfortune.

### 17

While my heart thus afflicts itself and groans
and grieves with me for my great misfortune,
at the moment when it most desires and most fears,
weeping has it as its prey, and death demands it;
there rises a sweet and desired hope,
which comforts me with its words,
and says: — You shall yet see that beautiful face
happy, sweet, and more loving than ever.

### 18

Those beautiful eyes, happy and loving,
those few clever and sweetest words,
will quiet your longing thoughts
and your afflicted soul, which rightly grieves.
Those eyes, which are now hidden from you, will act
as the sun does among thick mists:
your weeping and your sorrowful sighs will flee
before those burning, loving lights <note>original: "luci." A poetic term for eyes, comparing them to celestial bodies or lamps.</note>.

<margin>
Description
of summer.
</margin>
### 19

As soon as there appears on your blind horizon
the light that always shines in your heart,
and from the summit of that sacred mountain
that loving ray descends to your eyes,
there is no need to place your hand over your brow,
for this sweet light does not offend the sight.
Oh, what a beautiful dawn! Oh old Tithonus <note>In Greek mythology, Tithonus was the mortal lover of Aurora (the Dawn). Lorenzo suggests that his beloved's "dawn" is so much more beautiful that he does not envy the mythological one.</note>, then
keep your Aurora without my envy.

<summary>The poet transitions from a state of jealous despair and "martyrdom" to a vision of hope, as he imagines the healing power of his beloved's eyes, which he compares to a dawn more beautiful than that of myth.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance poetry, courtly love, personification of Fortune, Tithonus and Aurora, metaphors of light, martyrdom of love</keywords>

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the "Second Forest" (Selva Seconda). In the preceding lines, Hope was comforting the narrator, promising that his "sun"—his beloved—would soon reappear. This page transitions into a vivid "Description of Spring," using the physical renewal of nature as a metaphor for the emotional renewal the lover hopes to experience.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->20<-

You will see the shores covered in diverse colors,
as is the custom in springtime;
nor will the earth grieve any longer over the weather,
but dress herself in roses and violets.
And the signs in the sky, once hostile to the sweet season <note>This refers to the zodiac signs of winter, such as Capricorn or Aquarius, which the "new sun" of spring overcomes.</note>,
the new sun will make sweet and benign:
and the harsh season, cold and slow,
will not be recognized, should one look for it.

->21<-

Happy and wonderful, you will see the dry branches
clothed again with new leaves,
and the sharp thorns turn into lovely flowers,
and Procne and Philomela <note>original: "Progne e Filomena." In classical mythology, these sisters were transformed into a swallow and a nightingale. Their return is a traditional literary marker for the start of spring.</note> return to us;
the bees <term>pecchie</term> <note>An archaic Italian term for bees.</note> leave their old dwellings,
happily buzzing from flower to flower;
and the wise ants renew their abandoned labors
with their tiny steps.

->22<-

In the sweet season, the good shepherd prepares
to leave the winter quarters with the herds;
the happy flock, which, bleating in a troop,
returns to the high mountains and fresh waters.
The lamb, trotting along, follows its mother’s path;
and any that were just now born,
the loving shepherd carries in his arms:
the faithful dog acts as a guard for them all.

->23<-

Another shepherd carries on his shoulder
a sheep that has gone lame on the journey:
another has the nets, the mallet, and other things
on the back of a pregnant mare,
to pen them in when the sun descends <term>avvalla</term> <note>original: "avvalla." Literally "to go into the valley," a poetic way to describe the sun setting behind the horizon.</note>;
in this way, none of them encounter the wolf:
then they happily eat milk cakes and snowy-white ricottas,
and snore the whole night through.

<summary>This page depicts a classic "Description of Spring" (topos of the locus amoenus), detailing the rejuvenation of flora, the return of birds and insects, and the seasonal migration of shepherds to the mountains.</summary>
<keywords>springtime, pastoral life, Procne and Philomela, nature renewal, shepherds, transhumance, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the "Second Forest" (Selva Seconda) by Lorenzo de’ Medici. Following the previous page's description of a shepherd tending his flock and the return of migratory birds, these stanzas deepen the pastoral allegory. The poet describes how the arrival of the beloved (the "new sun") transforms the natural world, causing even mythological figures like Clytie to shift their devotion and taming the wildest beasts.</meta>

<page-num>259</page-num>
# THE SECOND FOREST

->24<-
The birds will break their silences, now much shorter,
singing through the leafy branches:
some seem to add to their old nests
certain stalks and little twigs.
Mushrooms will stand out in the green meadows:
happy women will run here and there to gather them;
the dormouse will leave its sleep and the place where it was,
and the scops owl will be heard in the evening.

->25<-
You will see, no longer seen in her own realms,
Flora <note>The Roman goddess of flowers and spring.</note> wandering with her nymphs:
her dear lover, Zephyr <note>original: "Zefiro." The west wind, associated with the beginning of spring; in mythology, he is the husband of Flora.</note>, has held her in his arms;
and together they both play.
Winter will crown its white hair
with leaves for the sake of this new power <term>virtúe</term> <note>In this context, it refers to the life-giving, generative force of nature returning in spring.</note>:
fierce tigers, bears, and lions will become tame:
and from hard ice, the waters will become liquid.

->26<-
Clytie <note>original: "Clizia." In Greek mythology, a nymph who pined for the sun god Helios and was transformed into a flower (the heliotrope or sunflower) that always turns toward the sun.</note> will leave her ancient lover,
turning away her weary, pale face.
Toward this new sunrise of love
the host of all other flowers will be turned,
intent on gazing fixedly at the radiant
light of her eyes and worshiping it greatly.
The dew upon the grass and in every branch
you will no longer believe is nourished by the rays of Phoebus <note>original: "febei raggi." Phoebus is an epithet for Apollo, the sun god. The poet suggests the beloved's eyes are now the source of light and life, replacing the sun.</note>.

->27<-
You will hear through the shady and green valleys
horns and bagpipes made from the bark
of willow or chestnut: and you will see dances
in the shade of the elms, when the sun is most intense.
The fish beneath the liquid crystals <note>A poetic metaphor for clear water.</note>
will feel the power of those beautiful eyes:
Nereus <note>An ancient Greek sea god, the "Old Man of the Sea."</note> and his daughters <note>The Nereids, or sea nymphs.</note> will have calm seas;
the happy world will show another face.

<summary>This page concludes a lyrical description of spring where the beauty of the beloved surpasses the power of the sun, taming wild animals and diverting the mythological flower Clytie from her devotion to Apollo.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Italian Renaissance poetry, Flora, Zephyr, Clytie, pastoral imagery, mythology, allegory of spring</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the "Second Forest" (Selva Seconda) by Lorenzo de’ Medici. Following the previous page's description of Nature's awakening and the transformation of the nymph Clytie, the poem now focuses on the personified figure of Winter (Bruma) reacting to the arrival of the lady, who acts as a "new sun" that rejuvenates the world.</meta>

<page-num>260</page-num>
# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->28<-

Just as a sapling, gracefully grafted,
is amazed when it later sees
new flowers and new green leaves within itself,
nourishing and ripening seeds that are not its own:
such amazement will the frosty winter <term>bruma</term> <note>Literally the winter solstice or the coldest part of the year; here personified as a figure of winter cold.</note> feel,
when the earth shows itself to us so beautifully
dressed in its new garments,
saying to itself: "Now I have returned to childhood" <note>original: "rimbambita." This literally means to become a child again or to enter a "second childhood"; here it suggests the earth feels rejuvenated and reborn by the spring.</note>.

->29<-

This new wonder will endure
until the light of those beautiful eyes appears
and presents itself to the frost-covered lashes:
when Winter sees those sweet, clear lights,
it will either be transformed into its daughter <note>Likely referring to Spring, the seasonal successor and "offspring" of Winter.</note>
or be forced to flee to the antipodes <note>The direct opposite side of the globe.</note>:
whoever gazes fixedly upon this noble face
must either become noble themselves or be undone.

->30<-

If this noble power draws near to her,
if that beautiful face is seen all around,
the first wonder quickly ceases—
the one brought by the desired and new day:
then, silent, Winter will say to itself:
"I marvel more that her adorned face,
which steals all power from the rays of Phoebus <note>original: "febei rai." Phoebus is a name for Apollo, the sun god; the poet is saying the lady's beauty outshines the sun itself.</note>,
does not perform even greater feats still."

->31<-

Then the enamored winter, as it departs,
will leave behind the light of those beautiful eyes:
the path is already marked by many flowers,
happily waiting for her lovely foot to touch them:
the air she parts is radiant and blessed:
a cloud <term>nembo</term> <note>A storm-cloud or vapor; used here to describe a thick, falling shower of petals.</note> of love seems to snow down
fragrant flowers of a sweet scent upon her:
everywhere, the spirits of Love shine bright.

<summary>These stanzas personify Winter's amazement at being transformed into Spring by the lady's presence, eventually fleeing to the other side of the world as she passes through a landscape of miraculous flowers.</summary>
<keywords>personification of winter, rejuvenation, Phoebus, Apollo, spirits of love, allegory of spring, grafting metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the second "forest" poem by Lorenzo de’ Medici. Following the personification of Winter's retreat in the face of the lady's beauty, the poem now describes a mythological and geographical procession as the lady enters the city of Florence.</meta>

<page-num>261</page-num>
# SECOND FOREST

->32<-

To honor my beautiful sun <note>The poet refers to the lady as a sun whose presence brings light and life.</note>,
leaping satyrs come, agile and crowned;
Pan comes playing his pipes, and in his company he wants
fauns, who hold green mountain boughs <note>original: "mái alpestri." These are flowering branches carried during May Day festivals, symbolizing spring.</note> in their hands:
the nymphs carry white roses and pale violets
in their laps and in their baskets:
the rivers arrive, adorned with soft marsh-grass,
filling their twisted horns <note>In classical art, river gods were often depicted with horns, symbolizing the strength and branching of a river’s flow.</note> with flowers and leaves.

->33<-

The joyful Arno leaves his old mother Falterona <note>Mount Falterona in the Apennines is the source of the Arno River.</note>
and the caverns of that ancient mountain,
and he covers his blue brow
with a green crown of poplar leaves:
in his murmuring he speaks to himself,
and the Arno laments having such a beautiful bridge;
the Arno who, as much as he can, strives and longs
to have eternal fame, just like his brother <note>The "brother" is likely the Tiber river in Rome, suggesting a rivalry for glory between Florence and Rome.</note>.

->34<-

Behold, appearing at the widowed walls <note>original: "vedove mura." The city walls are described as "widowed" or lonely, perhaps implying they were desolate before the arrival of the lady or the return of spring.</note>,
we see the sweet light of those beautiful eyes:
the low-born hearts tremble and are afraid
that this noble fire might touch them:
but in those others of a high and noble nature,
love and refinement seem to overflow:
women and young maidens already run to see her;
they feel no envy, but rather, they become more beautiful themselves.

->35<-

Once she has entered within that beautiful circle <note>Refers to the circular walls of the city of Florence.</note>,
how much sweetness will those people feel
who have awaited her with such great desire,
seeing then their own sweet peace!
O dear homeland, may you never again
envy the first Golden Age,
the Fortunate Isles in the West,
or the place where the first parent once sinned <note>A reference to the Garden of Eden.</note>.

<summary>This page describes a mythological procession of satyrs and river gods accompanying the lady into Florence, concluding with a celebration of the city as a new Eden or a return to the Golden Age.</summary>
<keywords>Arno River, Falterona, Pan, satyrs, Florence, Golden Age, Eden, mythology, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>
