<meta>Continuity: This page continues the second "Forest" poem by Lorenzo de’ Medici. Following the lady’s arrival at the walls of Florence (the "widowed walls"), the poet now describes her entrance into her home and the allegorical figures that dwell there, contrasting with a personification of Jealousy.</meta>

<page-num>262</page-num>
# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->36<-

Everyone applauds her, everyone greets her,
pointing her out to one another with a finger.
Gentle hearts say: — Welcome,
our sweetness, our peace, and our life! —
The base folk will stand sorrowful and mute,
and will flee the joust <note>original: "giostra." Here used metaphorically to describe the overwhelming power and competition of her beauty’s gaze.</note> of her beautiful eyes.
Behold, my noble lady is already in her home;
a happy house, though somewhat humble.

->37<-

No marble columns of great height
support the small and low walls
of this building; it is not given beauty
by stones of great solidity, clear and hard:
neither the work of a sculptor prized by the common crowd,
nor any mosaic, nor is there any painting,
no oriental gems, silver, or gold;
but a much more noble and beautiful work.

->38<-

At the door stand Beauty and Grace <note>original: "Leggiadria." A refined quality of elegance, charm, and lightness central to Renaissance courtly behavior.</note>,
sweet amorous Glances and beautiful Countenances:
Pity shows herself within, and in her company
Hope and Mercy <note>original: "Merzé." In the tradition of courtly love, this refers to the favor or compassion a lady grants her suitor.</note> seem to sing sweetly
(oh what a sweet and divine melody!):
ornate manners, and honest and holy ways,
sweet speech, witty sayings upon the stairs:
Faith, Love, and Nobility <note>original: "Gentilezza." This denotes nobility of soul and refined character rather than merely inherited rank.</note> are with her in the hall.

<margin>
Description of Jealousy.
</margin>
->39<-

Only an old woman in a dark corner,
pale, fleeing the sun, sat there,
sighing in silence; and a mantle
of an uncertain, changing color she wore:
a hundred eyes she has in her head, and all shed tears,
and a hundred ears has the malignant goddess:
what is, and what is not, the wretched one hears and sees:
she never sleeps; and, obstinate, trusts only herself. <note>This description of Jealousy, with her many eyes and ears, draws on classical descriptions of Fame or Rumor (Fama) from Virgil’s Aeneid, adapted here to represent the hyper-vigilance of a jealous mind.</note>

<summary>The lady enters her home, which is described not as a physical palace of gold and marble, but as an allegorical space inhabited by virtues. The passage concludes with a striking personification of Jealousy as a multi-eyed, sleepless monster hiding in the shadows.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, allegory, Jealousy, personification, courtly love, Florence, Renaissance poetry</keywords>

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the second "Forest" poem by Lorenzo de’ Medici. After describing the virtuous personifications dwelling in the lady's home, the poet turns to the origin story of Jealousy, the "malignant goddess" briefly introduced at the end of the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>263</page-num>
# SECOND FOREST

->40<-
In the primeval time when ancient Chaos
gave birth to her beloved son, Love,
this malignant goddess I speak of <note>The poet is referring to Jealousy, characterizing her as a twin or dark counterpart to Love.</note> was also born;
she came forth in that very same birth.
Jove, the kind father and friend to the world,
banished her among the lower shadows
with Pluto and the Furies; and she stayed with them
while Saturn reigned during the Golden Age <note>original: "etá d'oro." A mythical period of primordial peace and harmony when Jealousy did not yet exist among men.</note>:

->41<-
Later, as the immortal gods were frequently
and gravely offended by fierce Cupid—
caught now in one snare, now in another—
they brought her back from the underworld
by divine decree, burning with indignation,
ruling that wherever Love is, she should be also.
Thus this enemy encumbers the world:
she follows Love always, as the shadow follows the body.

->42<-
The high father Jove feared greatly
that the beautiful and sweet son of Chaos
might, with his new-found strength,
make himself the ruler of the Great Council in Jove’s place,
transferring the scepter and the kingdom elsewhere;
therefore, he recalled her from exile,
swearing then by the Stygian marsh <note>original: "palude stigie." The River Styx in the underworld, by which the gods swore their most binding and unbreakable oaths.</note>
that she must always follow in Love’s footsteps.

->43<-
The high father thought that through her
he could take much power away from the arrows of love,
and loosen the hard knots and all the snares;
for when the immortal gods see
how much pain whoever loves must incur—
in what tears, sighs, and so many evils—
they would cast off every thought of love,
fleeing the heavy yoke and the harsh empire.

<vocab>Chaos, Love, Jove, Pluto, Furies, Saturn, Cupid, Stygian</vocab>

<summary>Lorenzo de’ Medici presents a mythological origin for Jealousy, describing her as the twin of Love whom Jove released from the underworld to temper Love's power over the gods.</summary>
<keywords>Jealousy, Chaos, Love, Jove, Golden Age, Cupid, Styx, mythology, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the second "Forest" (Selva II) by Lorenzo de’ Medici. On the previous page, Jove had just decreed that Jealousy must forever follow Love as a punishment and a restraint, swearing a binding oath by the River Styx.</meta>

<page-num>264</page-num>
# IV — FORESTS OF LOVE

->44<-

Once the law and the oath were established
and consented to by the divine senate,
little time passed before Jove was unhappy with it
and then repented in vain for having sworn;
feeling this mortal torment within himself,
Love had previously been secure, joyful, and blessed;
and, had it not been for the faith already pledged,
Jove would have sent her back to the **Tartarean seat** <note>original: "tartarea sede." Referring to Tartarus, the deepest pit of the Underworld where the wicked are punished.</note>.

->45<-

Born of **Chaos** and nurtured by **Pluto**
on the milk of the **Furies** (oh, wicked deity!),
she makes mortals feel, while still in life,
the pains of the great kingdom without light.
Her immortal wound never heals.
She carries a sword stained with the foam
of **Cerberus** <note>The three-headed hound that guards the entrance to the Underworld.</note> down there in that low place;
she turns good into evil, and always believes the worst.

->46<-

She feeds on vain shadows and sad thoughts:
her unhappy mouth is always gnawing a heart;
and as soon as it is consumed, it is reborn:
oh, miserable is he who meets such a fate!
Even in her first cradle and in her swaddling clothes,
envy and hatred overflowed in her wicked breast.
She always flees whenever my beautiful **Sun** <note>A common poetic metaphor for the beloved lady, whose presence brings light and clarity.</note> arrives,
yet that **living death** <note>original: "morte viva." A classic oxymoron describing the agonizing state of jealousy.</note> does not truly depart.

->47<-

Oh, how many times has my **Sun** attempted
to drive this terrible monster away from her,
sometimes with threats, sometimes with kind words!
**Love** and **Faith** say, weeping: "This is our enemy,"
and each one grieves in vain.
In vain does our lowly human will oppose itself
to the decree that is already fixed and holy in heaven.
She flees from one corner, only to go into another.

<summary>Lorenzo describes the terrifying nature of Jealousy, a monstrous deity born of Chaos who torments lovers and persists despite the efforts of the poet's lady to drive her away.</summary>
<keywords>Jealousy, Chaos, Pluto, Furies, Cerberus, Jove, divine decree, living death, Underworld mythology</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, the poet described the terrifying nature of Jealousy, born of Chaos and Pluto. Jove (Jupiter) had previously sworn a binding oath by the River Styx that Jealousy would always accompany Love, a decision he now deeply regrets as he sees the torment it causes.</meta>

<page-num>265</page-num>
# SECOND FOREST

->48<-

O poisonous monster, an affront to heaven,
O living fountain of every human torment;
mortal enemy of love and of delight,
of hope, of faith, and of every contentment;
you set the wretched breast ablaze with fury.
Break, O **Jove** <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods.</note>, this unjust oath,
send this unhappy creature back to the eternal fire:
though perhaps even Hell will not accept her.

->49<-

Men and gods alike pray with hands clasped
that you might utterly exterminate and extinguish her;
let pity for the laments of heaven and human tears
enter into your noble heart.
Alas, let not so many righteous prayers be in vain!
And let that oath be maintained no longer,
sworn to the common harm, as I clearly see:
it was an error to make it, and to keep it is worse.

->50<-

Just as you were once rightly persuaded
to release the wise son of **Iapetus** <note>The Titan Prometheus, who was chained to a rock in the Caucasus Mountains as punishment for giving fire to humanity. Jove eventually allowed him to be freed.</note>,
bound eternally in the **Caucasus**,
to show some merit for his good counsel:
why do you hesitate now, O supreme father,
to return this wretched woman to her first exile?
To her first exile—and these are not new things:
you can do all; and that which pleases Jove is just.

->51<-

Like an ancient oak standing on high,
when it is struck by the fury of the winds,
assailed now from one side, now from the other,
its leaves fall, and its hanging branches
bend so low that some touch the earth;
yet the trunk stands firm and seems to feel no fear,
setting little prize on the war of **Aeolus** <note>The god and ruler of the winds.</note>,
holding its roots fast in the ground;

<summary>The poet appeals to Jove to break his sacred oath and banish Jealousy back to the Underworld, citing the precedent of Prometheus's release. The page concludes with a simile of an oak tree representing resilience against emotional storms.</summary>
<keywords>Jove, Jealousy, Prometheus, Iapetus, Aeolus, resilience, mythological precedent, prayer, oath-breaking</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, the poet continued his address to Jove (Jupiter), comparing the god's unyielding oath to an ancient oak that resists the winds. He pleaded with the king of the gods to reconsider the "hard law" that forces Jealousy to accompany Love, despite the misery it causes both mortals and immortals.</meta>

<page-num>266</page-num>
# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->52<-

Thus, benign and just father <note>Referring to Jove (Jupiter), the king of the gods.</note>, let yourself be moved somewhat, if our harsh and nearly eternal weeping reaches your holy ears. You would wish to show pity, provided you do not sin; but when you think of your holy oath <note>An oath sworn by the River Styx, which even the gods could not break.</note>, the fountain of pity must run dry; for the divine will is never corrected: and so this cruel law remains firm.

->53<-

O my blind hope, where have you led all my desires, escorting them from thought to thought with sweet flattery! While you falsely comforted them with lovely flowers and beautiful foliage, you now bear fruit that is sour, hard, acrid, and bitter: showing me my lady in vain, I see in her place only Love and Jealousy.

->54<-

Woe is me! When you first entered my mind, I saw such true and lovely lights cover the path of love with flowers; I saw nymphs, **Pan** <note>The god of the wild, shepherds, and flocks, often depicted with the hindquarters of a goat.</note>, satyrs, and rivers running, just as anyone sees who beholds the truth. O fallacious hope, now you consume me! Now the truth flees, and the sweet deception steals away! And only Jealousy remains here with Love.

->55<-

Love, who treats my every suffering as a game, laughs without pity at the deception: if Hope shows herself even for a moment, all my vain thoughts follow after her. Yet the amorous fire does not fail; instead, these deceptions make it grow much greater. Jealousy glares at me with fierce eyes, and my heart suffers for it and grows angry in its grief.

<summary>The poet laments Jove's inability to break his divine oath, which keeps Jealousy bound to Love. He describes how his initial hopeful visions of a pastoral paradise have been replaced by the bitter reality of Jealousy's constant presence.</summary>
<keywords>Jove, Jupiter, Hope, Jealousy, Love, Pan, Satyrs, divine law, pastoral imagery, ottava rima</keywords>

--- Page 273 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page’s lament over the "hard law" of Jove that binds Love to Jealousy, the poet now shifts his focus to the physical and spiritual "lofty places" where his love began. He transitions from a mythological complaint to a personal, reflective memory of his lady (Madonna) in their shared landscape.</meta>

<page-num>267</page-num>
# SECOND FOREST

->56<-

My Lady <note>original: "Madonna." In the tradition of Italian lyric poetry, this is a respectful title for the beloved, meaning "My Lady."</note> remains in those lofty parts where my fair desire was first born; where Love tore every other thought from my heart and planted that which has remained evergreen. He chose my lady from among all women and gave her to me; nor since then has anything else pleased me. This amorous place now steals her away from me: there she stays, without me, pensive and alone.

->57<-

In this place where my Lady turns her beautiful and tearful eyes—alas!—she gazes sorrowfully upon the mysteries of love and remembers our first sweet encounters. At every step she calls for me and sighs; and he who is called hears and responds from afar. She weeps, and in weeping her torment only grows, and I seem to hear her speaking to herself in this way:

->58<-

— "Here I waited for him, and from here I first caught sight of him. From here I heard the approach of his light feet, and there I reached out my timid hand to him. Here, with a trembling voice, I said: 'Sit now.' Here he wished to place himself all alone by my side, and there I gave myself to him entirely. There Love bound both of us with a knot <note>A common Renaissance metaphor for the unbreakable bond of mutual love.</note> that never since has been untied."

->59<-

"When I felt him among the shadows and saw him close by, my heart trembled fearfully in my breast. My desire was both hesitant and perplexed; by a joyful fear and a timid delight my longing heart was oppressed all at once. Nor do I know, in that moment, what I would have chosen. While Love urged my steps forward and fear held them back, I was filled with a full, yet uncertain, joy."

<summary>The poet imagines his beloved standing alone in the place where they first met, recounting her internal monologue as she remembers the "sweet knot" of their first encounter and the conflicting emotions of fear and joy.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Madonna, memory, first love, desire, longing, Italian Renaissance poetry</keywords>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page where the poet's Lady (Madonna) began a monologue reflecting on the site of their first meeting and the "knot" of love that bound them together, she now describes the intimacy of their union and the sorrow of their eventual parting at dawn.</meta>

<page-num>268</page-num>
# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->60<-

"Here," I said to him, "lie down contented now: let your heart be glad, since it has what it desires." O sweet words, O tender embraces, O kisses! O sighs that issued from both our breasts! O shifting time, O brief and fleeting hours, how much goodness you carried away! Here he left me full of longing, when, as day approached, he said: "Farewell."

->61<-

Alas for me! The day was already near; Phoebus <note>A poetic name for the Sun, derived from the Greek god Apollo. In Renaissance poetry, the arrival of the sun often signals the "alba" or dawn song, a traditional poem about lovers forced to part at daybreak.</note> had almost reached the horizon, so that the sweetness of that beautiful stay made the time seem like but a brief moment. He said: "Whether alive or dead, I shall return to you." Thus he departed, and was separated from me. Guiding this hand through the dark <note>original: "cieco," literally "blind." It refers to the path being obscured by the early morning shadows.</note> path, he clasped it and kissed it, and carried my heart away with him.

->62<-

From this place, I followed him with my tearful eyes and my gaze as long as I could: he walked alone, kindled in a sweet fire, with his footsteps leading away but his face turned back toward me. The shadowy night allowed this final sweetness to last but a little while, and then he was taken from me. To my eyes no further power <note>original: "virtú." In Renaissance philosophy, this refers to the physical or spiritual faculty of a sense—here, the eyes' ability to perceive at a distance.</note> was granted; but his image remained impressed within my heart.

->63<-

Thus speaks my Lady; and those who are near her do not hear it; but I, who am so far away, hear her. This memory has brought back to my thoughts that first time when the beautiful knot <note>A recurring metaphor in this work for the unbreakable bond of mutual love.</note> was tied, and it so alienates me from my own self that I almost see that beautiful time and the manner in which her white hand bound me then; but that brief, vain pleasure lasts only a moment.

<summary>The Lady concludes her monologue by describing the painful parting of the lovers at dawn, after which the poet reflects on how his soul hears her voice across the distance, vividly reliving the moment they first fell in love.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, separation, dawn, Phoebus, memory, the knot of love, longing, Italian Renaissance poetry</keywords>

--- Page 275 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page where the Lady described the sorrow of the poet's departure at dawn, she now shifts to an internal reflection on the persistence of memory and the deceptive nature of Hope.</meta>

<page-num>269</page-num>
# SECOND FOREST

->64<-

O stubborn and hostile memory,
that places those beautiful times before my eyes!
O even more cruel and deceptive hope of mine,
which promises the heart even better things yet!
But I do not see the only thing that pleases me,
nor do those blessed lights <note>original: "luce," a poetic term frequently used by Renaissance poets like Petrarch and Lorenzo to refer to the beloved's eyes.</note> return:
one eye looks backward and the other forward,
and the heart, angry and weary, sighs forever.

->65<-

Why do you follow this woman still, O vain and foolish thoughts,
having been deceived so many times?
And I, more foolish still, why did I choose to follow you?
Oh, stay your path, my weary thoughts!
I choose instead to remain with weeping eyes,
and cry out "alas" <note>original: "omei," an archaic exclamation of grief.</note> a thousand times in an hour,
spending the time that remains to me in sorrow and fire,
and then to die, rather than to live in hope.

->66<-

At least, if memory stings desire,
let it represent the truth before the heart:
but this vain memory flees far off in an instant,
for if you approach, it moves further away.
It flees from one moment to the next and never arrives:
it torments the heart with hoping and desiring.
Love, who always leads her in his company,
thus depicts <note>original: "dipinge." Lorenzo often uses the language of painting to describe how emotions and mental images are "drawn" onto the heart or mind.</note> this sweet pain.

<margin>Description of Hope.</margin>
->67<-

She is a woman of immense stature:
the tips of her hair seem to reach the heavens;
she is formed and clothed in dense mist;
she dwells upon the summits of the highest mountains.
Just as one, looking at the clouds, forms and imagines
seeing new shapes of swift animals,
which the wind changes and then fashions anew;
thus Love paints this vain image.

<summary>In these stanzas, the poet laments the cruelty of memory and hope, which only serve to prolong his suffering. He concludes with a striking allegorical description of Hope as a giant woman made of mist, as shifting and unsubstantial as clouds in the wind.</summary>
<keywords>memory, Hope, allegory, Love, suffering, mental imagery, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

--- Page 276 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page’s introduction of Hope as a colossal figure made of mist dwelling on mountain peaks, Lorenzo continues to describe her elusive and frustrating nature through a series of vivid metaphors.</meta>

<page-num>270</page-num>
# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->68<-

From a distance, she seems very beautiful and grand;
she captures almost the entire world within her shadow:
but if it happens that one draws near with desire,
little by little she fails and thins away:
and, as usually happens when the North Wind <note>original: "Borea," the personification of the cold north wind in classical mythology.</note> seems to sting,
you see the clouds vanish from before your eyes;
so she is not to be found where you believe she is,
yet you always see her right there before you.

->69<-

Just like a dog that believes its hungry mouth
is about to be bathed in the blood of a wild beast
that flees before him, and he almost touches it already,
yet he never reaches it, though he never stops hoping to:
so this desirous and foolish longing
is never satiated, and remains as empty as it was before;
she flees even faster ahead of him,
while he, full of rage and desire, consumes himself:

->70<-

Or as it is when the sun warms one's back,
and a person wants to catch the shadow cast before them;
if they wish at least to match its pace with their steps,
the shadow must necessarily advance by that same distance:
though he runs as a deer is accustomed to run,
the shadow remains as far ahead in the end as it was before;
now he seems to press upon it, now to overtake it for a moment;
but at the end of the race, he always remains at the rear <note>original: "sezzo," an archaic term meaning the last or final position in a sequence.</note>.

->71<-

The rolling wheels can never overtake
the ox or horse that pulls the cart forward;
in the same way, this woman can never be touched:
no mortal eye gazes upon her vain forehead.
She has a single eye in her head, and she stares <note>original: "guata," a forceful verb meaning to watch or peer intently, often with a sense of vigilance.</note>
at high and distant things ahead, never turning it back.
Only Minerva <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom and strategic warfare.</note> with her shield <note>original: "egida" (Aegis), the protective shield or breastplate associated with Minerva/Athena, often bearing the head of Medusa.</note> has ever seen
that face, and she laughs at our miserable lot.

<term>Selve d'Amore</term> — "Forests of Love," the title of this collection of poems by Lorenzo de' Medici, written in "ottava rima" (eight-line stanzas).
<term>Borea</term> — The North Wind, often used in Renaissance poetry to symbolize a force that clears away mists or clouds.
<term>Minerva</term> — Goddess of Wisdom; her ability to see Hope's face suggests that only through wisdom can one see Hope for what it truly is: a deception.
<term>Egida</term> — The Aegis, a mythical shield; here it represents the divine protection or clarity of mind that shields the wise from the torments of false hope.

<summary>This page concludes the personification of Hope, using similes of a chasing dog, a moving shadow, and a cart's wheels to illustrate how Hope always remains just out of reach. Lorenzo posits that only through Wisdom (Minerva) can one see through Hope's illusions.</summary>
<keywords>Hope, allegory, North Wind, Minerva, Aegis, desire, frustration, Lorenzo de' Medici, Selve d'Amore</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Lorenzo continues his allegorical description of Hope, portraying her as a monstrous, winged creature dwelling in the turbulent middle atmosphere, surrounded by deception and false sciences.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

<page-num>271</page-num>

->72<-

Upon her misty shoulders grow
two feathered wings of extraordinary size.
She flies through high places, from which those fall
who believe she is lifting them upward.
Wind and vain shadows feed this beast <note>original: "fera," referring to the personification of Hope as a predatory or wild animal.</note>,
and she rarely tastes other food.
She flies by night, and always tends to flee
the light of the sun, just as the dawn does.

->73<-

Heaven pushes her away, and Pluto <note>original: "Pluton," the god of the Underworld; here signifying that Hope has no place in the divine realm or the land of the dead.</note> pushes her away:
she flies through this middle region <note>In Renaissance cosmology, the "middle region" of the air was thought to be the site of meteorological phenomena like storms and clouds.</note>,
where Juno <note>Juno was often associated with the element of air in allegorical poetry.</note> freezes the liquid moisture into ice and stone
and dissolves the clouds into water:
there Vulcan <note>The god of fire and smithing.</note> forges his quiver;
from there Aeolus <note>The ruler of the winds.</note> moves the South Wind and the North Wind <note>original: "Austro" and "Aquilone."</note>,
fires, comets, and falling vapors,
and the beautiful Iris <note>The personification of the rainbow.</note> of a thousand colors.

->74<-

Following this wretched woman everywhere are
Dream, Augury, and Lies;
and palm-readers <note>original: "chiromanti."</note> and every deceptive art,
lots, diviners, and false prophecy,
the spoken word and the writing on foolish papers,
which say what will be only after it has already happened;
alchemy <note>original: "l'archimia."</note> and those who measure the heavens from the earth;
and conjectures made according to one's own will.

->75<-

Under the blind shadow of her great wings
the vain world finally finds shelter.
Oh, the blindness of miserable mortals!
Oh, ignorance too vain and poor!
And whoever could count all her evils,
let them number the stars in heaven and the fish in the sea,
the birds that cross the sea in autumn,
or the leaves that leave the branches bare.

<summary>Lorenzo describes Hope as a winged monster dwelling in the chaotic middle atmosphere (the realm of weather and shifting winds), accompanied by various forms of superstition and false divination. He concludes that the number of sorrows caused by Hope is as infinite as the stars or the leaves in autumn.</summary>
<keywords>Hope, allegory, mythology, Juno, Vulcan, Aeolus, winds, divination, alchemy, human ignorance</keywords>

--- Page 278 ---
<meta>Continuity: Lorenzo continues his critique of Hope, transitioning from her physical description to her role in the mythological origins of human suffering through the story of Prometheus and Pandora.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

<page-num>272</page-num>

->76<-

**But** what evil is there that mortal man suffers,
which does not proceed from your cursed self?
Or what heavy pain do you not nourish?
How many sad souls have you given as prey to Love?
What great peril is there that the heart 
does not dare, if it happens that the wretched man believes you?
You were given from heaven to us mortals 
as the life and preservation of all evils. <note>Lorenzo reverses the usual view of Hope as a comfort, suggesting instead that Hope is what keeps suffering alive by preventing people from accepting their fate.</note>

->77<-

O son of Iapetus <note>original: "figlio di Iapeto," referring to Epimetheus, the brother of Prometheus.</note>, utterly foolish,
it was of no use that your wise brother <note>Prometheus, whose name means "foresight."</note> warned you
not to look upon Pandora’s beautiful face
or accept a gift that came from her.
Return the theft, Prometheus, which, being taken,
sent so many diseases into the wretched world.
You can hardly discern who was more foolish:
he who took the gift <note>Epimetheus, who accepted Pandora.</note> or he who once stole the fire.

->78<-

Foolish prudence and blind foresight <note>A critique of Prometheus; even his "foresight" could not prevent the eventual suffering of humanity.</note>
was yours, and your brother’s was mad stupidity.
Ah, return the theft, if Jove <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods.</note> is content
to withdraw diseases and malice from the world.
You did not yet know that repentance
always follows that which is badly begun:
you thought to deceive Jove: oh, grave errors!
Thus the wisest men make the greatest mistakes.

->79<-

If you had not been there, Jove would not have given the task
to Vulcan to fashion Pandora;
Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, goddess of wisdom and weaving.</note> would not have added the fine arts
and the practice of them to make her more decorative;
with every beauty in her face, and vice in her mouth,
Venus <note>original: "Vener," the goddess of love.</note> would not yet have given her grace
and sweet glances and a lovely human countenance;
nor would Jove then have placed our death in her hand. <note>Referring to the "pithos" or jar (often called a "box") Pandora carried, which contained all the world's evils.</note>

<summary>In these stanzas, Lorenzo de' Medici links the deceptive nature of Hope to the myth of Prometheus and Pandora, arguing that human suffering was born from the theft of divine fire and the subsequent creation of the first woman.</summary>
<keywords>Prometheus, Epimetheus, Pandora, Jove, Vulcan, Pallas Athena, Venus, original sin, Greek mythology, human suffering, Hope</keywords>

--- Page 279 ---
<meta>Continuity: Lorenzo continues his philosophical critique of Hope, using the myth of Prometheus and his brother Epimetheus to illustrate how Hope serves as the final, lingering trap in Pandora's jar, keeping humanity bound to suffering.</meta>

# SELVA SECONDA

<page-num>273</page-num>
<header>SECOND FOREST</header>
*LORENZO THE MAGNIFICENT, Works - 1.*

->80<-

She would not have been so graceful and beautiful
when she offered the jar to that fool, as she did. <note>The "fool" refers to Epimetheus, whose name means "afterthought," for ignoring his brother's warnings.</note>
He, as you know, although cautioned beforehand,
took the jar and immediately opened it.
Instantly, there issued from the jar and fled
throughout the world all diseases and various passions:
of that jar fashioned by the celestial smith, <note>original: "celeste fabro," referring to Vulcan (Hephaestus), who created Pandora from clay.</note>
Hope alone remained upon the lip.

->81<-

And so, it was too harmful and costly,
that fire which you stole within the reed: <note>original: "ferula," the giant fennel stalk Prometheus used to smuggle fire from Olympus.</note>
from then on the world became cruel and greedy,
the mind always restless and complaining,
wars, fires, wrongs, and bitter weeping:
from then on ships furrowed the cerulean waves: <note>In classical literature, the invention of seafaring was often seen as a sign of human greed and the end of the peaceful Golden Age.</note>
lies, deceit, and the breaking of faith—
from this vain hope every evil proceeds.

->82<-

You remained there all alone upon the rim,
because hope never falls to the ground:
she is born of desire, and she promises it to you;
one wandering thought seems to give birth to the next:
from evil she expects good, and from good, better,
just as a bird flits from branch to twig:
never certain: therefore, neither inside nor out
did she remain in the jar that Pandora gave.

->83<-

She forces mortals too much, she presumes too much,
this enemy of the human mind;
even in the blind kingdom without light <note>The "blind kingdom" (cieco regno) refers to the Underworld or Hades.</note>
she wishes to extend her hidden power:
it seemed to some a worthy and noble custom
to abandon the present sweet life:
some despise sweet life and crave death,
hoping to live on afterwards through fame.

<summary>Lorenzo recounts the myth of Pandora's jar, arguing that Hope is a deceptive "enemy of the mind" that prolongs human suffering and even drives people to seek death in pursuit of future glory.</summary>
<keywords>Pandora's Box, Prometheus, Epimetheus, Hope, human suffering, stolen fire, fame, mythology, Vulcan</keywords>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

<margin>
Description of the Golden Age.
</margin>

->84<-

Before the harmful thought of that sorrowful theft <note>Referring to the theft of fire from the gods by Prometheus.</note>
entered the mind of the son of Iapetus, <note>Prometheus, the Titan who created man and stole fire for them.</note>
Saturn ruled the world under his just empire 
during the quiet Golden Age. 
Human life was then longer and more joyful: 
truth was, and appeared to be, one and the same: 
every desire was restrained and content, 
nor did the world know the concepts of "yours" or "mine." <note>The absence of private property is a hallmark of the Golden Age in classical literature.</note>

->85<-

The generous earth gave life 
in common to all in that beautiful time. 
Not yet wounded by the plowshare or the hoe, <note>original: "vomero o marra."</note>
she produced grains and various fruits; 
clothed in fragrant herbs and flowers 
never destroyed by the sun or the frost: 
the running waters, sweet, clear, and bright, 
then quenched a moderate thirst.

->86<-

Through the grassy fields, happy and free, 
the herds wandered without any fear, 
without the timid shepherd suspecting 
that they might be taken by bear or wolf. 
The untamed bulls were then many, 
not yet deprived of their procreative heat, 
nor, through the exhaustion of long intervals, 
having the hard callus of the yoke upon their necks.

->87<-

One could see, within a field of stubble, 
the little sheep stay happily with the wolf, 
paired together without suspicion of one another; 
the wolf was not then fierce, nor she timid. 
Nor was the fox malicious or deceitful: 
and there was no need for the farm-girl 
to keep a watchdog <note>original: "botol," a small common dog or cur used for guarding.</note> to chase her away; 
but it seemed that if the fox did come, it was to join the feast.

<summary>Lorenzo describes the mythical Golden Age under Saturn's rule, depicting a lost paradise of communal property, effortless agriculture, and peaceful coexistence between predators and prey.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Age, Saturn, Prometheus, Garden of Eden trope, pastoral poetry, absence of private property, harmony of nature</keywords>

--- Page 281 ---
<meta>Continuity: Lorenzo continues his description of the Golden Age under the reign of Saturn. Having previously described the bounty of the earth and the peaceful coexistence of wolves and sheep, he now focuses on the harmony among birds, predators, and even mythical creatures.</meta>

# SECOND FOREST

->88<-

The hare and the hound lie in a single bush together:
the one does not bark, and the other does not yet moan in fear.
Between the greyhound, the roe deer, and the stag, there is peace,
nor does any rely on or fear swift feet:
they play among themselves, and it pleases them to provoke 
one another at times; and if they run together,
they do not run to escape a fierce bite,
but only to outdo each other in the race.

->89<-

Simple and white, without a single blemish,
the dove nests happily wherever she pleases,
without fearing that her companion
or the male might destroy the eggs out of envy;
she does not fear the falcon in the open fields,
nor the ambush of the goshawk among the branches.
Now the heron <note>original: "aghirone," an archaic term for the heron.</note> goes about crying out happily,
neither fearing the strike nor the talons of the falcon.

->90<-

The partridge does not fear the tiercel <note>original: "terzuolo," the male of certain birds of prey, which is usually smaller and more agile than the female.</note>
grasping her like iron pincers;
nor does the fat quail fear being caught
by the sparrowhawk as she takes flight.
The merlin enjoys watching the lark rise
from the low ground toward the sky, singing:
nor do snakes need to worry
about being food for the stork’s chicks.

->91<-

You can walk barefoot through the meadow without risk
of angering the cold serpent <note>original: "frigid'angue," a poetic, Latinate term for a snake.</note> by treading on it.
And snakes have neither venom nor hiss
that makes the blood flee from the face to the heart.
It is safe to look directly at the basilisk, <note>original: "bavalischio." In medieval folklore, the gaze of the basilisk was thought to be instantly fatal.</note>
nor does anyone languish in sadness because of its deadly gaze.
Nor do the animals at the spring need to wait
for the unicorn to perform the tasting. <note>original: "facci la credenza." According to legend, the unicorn would dip its horn into poisoned water to purify it before other animals could drink; in the Golden Age, this "credence" or tasting ritual is unnecessary because the world is already pure.</note>

<summary>Lorenzo describes the absolute harmony of the natural world during the Golden Age, where predators and prey play together and even the most venomous or mythical creatures are harmless.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Age, Saturn, harmony, birds of prey, basilisk, unicorn, nature, peace</keywords>

--- Page 282 ---
<meta>Continuity: Lorenzo de' Medici continues his description of the Golden Age, a mythical era of peace under the rule of Saturn. Following the description of birds and serpents, he now moves to the larger predators and the absence of human violence—specifically hunting and meat-eating—which he identifies as the source of the modern rift between humanity and nature.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

### 92

->The tiger, the fierce lion, and the panther,<-
->like rabbits, are tame and slow;<-
->and every lowly and gentle beast<-
->appears as fierce as lions or tigers.<- <note>Lorenzo suggests a state of total equality where the concepts of "predator" and "prey" no longer define an animal's appearance or behavior.</note>
->Nor does any animal flee the human face.<-
->The birds—white, scarlet, yellow, and black—<-
->no longer hide among the thick brush:<-
->on the hand, the head, or the shoulder of man they perch.<-

### 93

->It was not yet within the breast of mortals<-
->to satisfy a savage craving for meat.<-
->We become like beasts through what we eat,<-
->which strips human blood of its true nature;<- <note>This reflects a common Renaissance philosophical idea that eating animal flesh "corrupted" the refined nature of human beings.</note>
->hence there is war between man and animal:<-
->hence the bird flees from leaf to leaf,<-
->and laments with a piteous cry<-
->when it cannot find its dear chicks in the nest.<-

### 94

->The painful bitterness was not felt<-
->by the mother who loses her dear lamb:<-
->the cow did not fill the heavens with lowing,<-
->returning from the slaughterhouse without her calf:<-
->nor to protect human limbs from the frost<-
->do wild beasts die to give up their pelts:<-
->the path was safe for the animals,<-
->and no one hunts for food or for pleasure.<-

### 95

->The birds go singing from branch to branch<-
->without suspicion of nets or snares;<-
->the partridge finds her chicks at her call,<-
->should she happen to gather them or count them.<-
->Nor beneath the bait did the hook find<-
->the fish yet, nor nets, nor other entanglements.<-
->The purple-murex is safe from deceptions:<- <note>original: "porpora," the sea snail used in antiquity to create purple dye.</note>
->nor does blood stain precious cloths.<- <note>A reference to the fact that ancient "Royal Purple" was made by crushing thousands of living shellfish; in the Golden Age, even this form of "slaughter" for luxury did not exist.</note>

<summary>In these stanzas, Lorenzo describes the Golden Age as a vegetarian utopia where humans and animals lived in harmony, and no creature was killed for food, clothing, or luxury dyes.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Age, Lorenzo de' Medici, vegetarianism, hunting, nature, harmony, birds, animal rights in literature</keywords>

--- Page 283 ---
<meta>Continuity: Lorenzo de' Medici continues his vivid depiction of the Golden Age. Having previously described the harmony among land animals and birds, he now turns his gaze to the sea and the interior of the earth, emphasizing a world before the "corruption" of mining, navigation, and warfare.</meta>

# SECOND FOREST

### 96

->Safe now and no longer fearing, the octopus<-
->approaches the moray eel with its hundred tails;<- <note>original: "cento code" (hundred tails). Lorenzo uses poetic license to describe the octopus's many tentacles.</note>
->nor does the lobster close both its claws,<-
->nor does the lobster bite the moray eel<-
->upon its back, as the eel is unprepared to defend itself:<-
->nor does one take revenge for the pain of the other.<-
->Today one overcomes the other, but there it seems to yield<-
->to the conquered, and the first victor is himself the prey.<-

### 97

->Thus, full of labor and light, the day<-
->chases away the pale and rosy dawn;<-
->then the night chases her, until at last<-
->the day must make the night its prey;<-
->and, while the hunter sounds his horn,<-
->he remains conquered in this eternal hunt:<- <note>This "eternal hunt" refers to the celestial cycle of time, which Lorenzo contrasts with the violent, literal hunting of his own era.</note>
->so it happens among these beasts in the sea,<-
->if one ought to compare these things to those.<-

### 98

->The earth kept hidden within its belly<-
->the wretched veins of every metal;<-
->nor did the fierce desire for gold<-
->seize mortal hearts; and the heart was not yellow with fear,<- <note>Lorenzo makes a pun here: the heart is not "yellow" from the greed of gold, nor "yellow" from the pallor of fear.</note>
->nor was iron found and made ready for war;<-
->nor did the horse ring out with bit or hoof;<-
->nor did bronze spread the memory of men;<- <note>A reference to the practice of casting bronze statues or tablets to celebrate "mortal glory" and military victors.</note>
->nor was there any thirst for mortal glory.<-

### 99

->Quiet <term>Nereus</term> <note>A Greek sea god, often called the "Old Man of the Sea," representing the calm ocean.</note> and every one of his daughters<-
->had not yet seen, full of wonder, the first shadow<-
->of the <term>Argo</term> <note>The first ship in Greek mythology, built by Jason to find the Golden Fleece. Its construction was often seen by Renaissance poets as the end of the Golden Age and the start of human interference with nature.</note> within their realms,<-
->moved by either oar or wind;<-
->nor were the sea and the shores measured in miles,<-
->with a thousand other harmful and strange inventions.<-
->The name of "islands" had not yet been heard:<-
->it seemed the world ended wherever there was a shore.<-

<summary>Lorenzo describes the Golden Age's maritime peace and the absence of mining and navigation. He portrays a world where the earth's resources remained untouched and the sea was a vast, unmeasured mystery, free from the "harmful" advancements of human technology and ambition.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Age, Nereus, Argo, mythology, marine life, mining, peace, nature vs technology, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Lorenzo de' Medici continues his description of the Golden Age in the "Second Forest." Following the peace among the creatures of the sea and the earth's hidden metals, he now describes the physiological and environmental perfection of this lost era, focusing on the harmony of the human body and the eternal spring of nature.</meta>

# IV - THE FORESTS OF LOVE

### 100

->In the plants were the flower, the leaf, and the fruit,<-
->neither time nor place confuses their order.<- <note>In this mythical Golden Age, the seasons coexist; trees bear flowers and ripe fruit simultaneously.</note>
->In every place, nature brings forth<-
->every animal on land, in the air, and in the waves.<-
->Everything, when called by its name,<-
->responds according to its natural essence.<-
->There was nothing old or new;<-
->nor was any wonder to be found in that time.<- <note>Because everything was already perfect, there was no "novelty" or "miracle"—perfection was the daily standard.</note>

### 101

->The human body was so well disposed,<-
->its <term>humors</term> so balanced and divided,<- <note>humors: The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile) believed in the Renaissance to determine health and personality.</note>
->that desire was restrained and composed:<-
->no hope, no envy, anger, or sorrows:<-
->nor had nature provided an appetite<-
->such that, through the common channels, hair, or pores,<-
->anything superfluous should come; and nothing remains in excess<-
->despite the sweetness of food or the abundance.<- <note>Lorenzo describes a state of perfect metabolism where the body consumes exactly what it needs without waste or gluttony.</note>

### 102

->Thus beautiful, robust, healthy, and clean,<-
->they do not feel—for it did not exist—heat or frost;<-
->they do not flee from rime or rain under roofs;<-
->nor does the bolt of Jove make the heart tremble.<- <note>original: "il telo" (the weapon/bolt). This refers to Jupiter's lightning, representing both storms and divine wrath.</note>
->Sweet sleep upon grassy beds<-
->comes when our sky is without the sun:<-
->when the sun's rays purge the mists,<-
->they rise together with the animals and the flowers.<-

### 103

->Burning with love without passion,<- <note>original: "passione." In a philosophical sense, this refers to "suffering" or emotional turmoil that disturbs the soul.</note>
->hope or jealousy does not accompany them:<-
->it is always one love, as heaven ordains<-
->and nature, which is without flaw.<-
->With this shared temperament,<-
->they wander through the countryside alone and joyful:<-
->their age is never that of a child or an elder.<- <note>Residents of the Golden Age exist in a state of perpetual, vigorous youth.</note>
->Their clothes are leaves, and flowers are their garlands.<-

<vocab>Selve d'Amore, ottava rima, nature, Golden Age, humors, Jove</vocab>

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici describes the physiological and environmental perfection of the Golden Age, where the human body exists in a state of humoral balance and nature provides eternal sustenance without the harshness of seasons or aging.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Age, humors, perpetual youth, harmony, Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance philosophy, nature, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 285 ---
<meta>Continuity: Lorenzo de' Medici continues his description of the Golden Age in the "Second Forest." Having previously described the physical perfection of the body and the environment, he now moves to the psychological and spiritual harmony of this era, where human desire and intellect were perfectly aligned with the natural world and divine will.</meta>

# SECOND FOREST

->104<-

What <term>purple</term> <note>original: "porpora." In the Renaissance, this referred to a prestigious, deep red-violet dye.</note> does not lose its value against those colors?
What <term>kermes</term> <note>original: "chermisí." A brilliant red dye derived from insects, highly prized in the Florentine textile industry.</note> or crimson, whether in wool or in silk?
What silver, what gold equals the flowers?
Thus they lead a life always joyful.
Oh sweet time! Oh sweetest loves!
Oh life always desiring and yet quiet!
Because burning desire never torments,
nor does the body become weary when it is spent.

->105<-

Desire is only as much as nature requires,
and it wants what it has, and what it has does not harm it;
nor does it ever grieve for having or not having;
nor does it fail or take on excessive force.
That which pleases today, is wont to please forever;
it does not sate to the point of boredom, nor does it leave regret behind;
it fulfills itself and restrains itself,
and feels no pain from either fulfillment or restraint.

->106<-

Every appetite that might harm others sleeps;
ambition did not occupy the kingdoms.
Nature was then in perfect harmony
between the blessed man and the <term>celestial signs</term>. <note>This refers to the belief that human life was once in total sync with the movements of the stars and planets.</note>
These properties, those <term>high forms</term>, <note>A reference to Platonic Ideals—the perfect versions of things that exist in the divine mind.</note>
were seen by the eyes and seen by the intellects;
thought has no doubt, no fatigue;
it understands the truth without confusion.

->107<-

The intellect was matched with desire,
the will with the power of understanding;
they were content to know of God
only the part that man is able to comprehend;
nor did the presumption of our vain and wicked
intellect seek to ascend any higher,
nor to search with such useless care
for the causes that nature hides from us. <note>Lorenzo contrasts the Golden Age with his own time, where "vain" intellectual curiosity leads to spiritual unrest and the questioning of God’s mysteries.</note>

<summary>In these stanzas, Lorenzo describes the psychological and spiritual state of the Golden Age, emphasizing a perfect balance where desire is naturally self-regulating and the human intellect is satisfied with divine truth without the "wicked" urge to overreach.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Age, desire, intellect, nature, harmony, kermes, purple, Platonic forms, divine will, Florentine Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 286 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the description of the physical and emotional harmony of the Golden Age, Lorenzo de' Medici now contrasts that ancient perfection with the "modern" condition of the human intellect, which struggles with doubt, over-ambition, and the limits of knowledge.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->108<-

Today, mortal <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "ingegno." In the Renaissance, this referred to the innate capacity for reasoning, creativity, and understanding.</note> still presumes
to seek a hidden good, toward which it aspires:
human <term>desire</term> <note>original: "dïsio." A central theme in Lorenzo’s poetry, often representing the soul's longing for the divine.</note> stirs its base sharpness,
yet finds nowhere to rest; thus it grows angry
and grieves that the mind has too much light,
merely assuming that good exists; and, if it does not gaze upon it,
it mourns for the little it has and sees that it cannot see;
it asks either to be blind or to have perfect sight.

->109<-

It lacks in excess, and seems to overreach in the little it has;
let it see the excess less, and let the little presume much;
and, just as a weak fire in green wood
does not shine clearly, but fills weeping eyes with smoke.
Nocturnal birds are the sport of others
when seeking the sun: and <term>Icarus</term> <note>The mythological son of Daedalus who flew too close to the sun with waxen wings; he is a classic symbol of "hubris" or overreaching ambition.</note>
loses his unaccustomed feathers if he climbs too high,
and remains a wingless bird in the middle of the sky.

->110<-

Like a migratory bird that leaves its beloved shore
because of the cold and crosses the sea in flight,
already weary halfway through the waves,
it sees only water on every side and is burdened with sorrow;
no branch or rock halts its flight;
if it should see a boat furrowing the waves,
it fears the hands of man and the tempest of the sea,
and remains hesitant in the midst of the waves:

->111<-

so if the mind leaves its native place,
it becomes confused by its own doing;
if it wishes to seek an unknown shore,
it remains weary and doubtful at last among the waves.
Back then <note>Referring to the people of the Golden Age described in the preceding stanzas.</note>, the nimble intellect saw
that truth which corresponded to its own powers:
nor did <term>presumption</term> <note>original: "prosunzion." The act of overstepping one's natural limits or claiming knowledge one does not possess.</note> spoil this good;
they desired only what they had, and what they understood was enough.

<summary>Lorenzo contrasts the restless, overreaching intellect of the modern age with the contented wisdom of the Golden Age, using the metaphors of Icarus and a lost migratory bird to illustrate the pain of human doubt.</summary>
<keywords>intellect, Golden Age, Icarus, desire, human limits, allegory, Renaissance philosophy, doubt</keywords>

--- Page 287 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the philosophical reflection on the limits of human intellect and the hubris of Icarus, Lorenzo now contrasts the effortless clarity of the natural world with the restlessness introduced to humanity by Prometheus's theft of fire and the pursuit of excessive knowledge.</meta>

# SECOND FOREST

### 112

What the heavens reveal of themselves and what nature shows, they understand without doubt or toil; neither overly subtle nor vain concern stirs the bile or nourishes <term>burnt humors</term> <note>original: "adusti umor." In Renaissance medicine, "burnt" or "combust" humors were believed to be overheated bodily fluids that caused melancholy, anger, or madness.</note>. Pure and noble truth, in its nakedness, does not beg for long vigils or intense study; the mind sees this true and beautiful sweetness and, content here, asks for nothing more.

### 113

<term>Prometheus</term> <note>The Titan from Greek mythology who stole fire from the gods. Lorenzo interprets this act not as progress, but as the beginning of human anxiety and the loss of natural "bliss."</note> took this happy age from the world and robbed man of his true bliss, because he wished to know too much. From knowing too much, restlessness is born. Through knowing too little, his <term>vain brother</term> <note>Epimetheus, whose name means "afterthought." Unlike his brother "foresight," he was foolish and, in many versions of the myth, was responsible for accepting Pandora and her box of evils.</note> then released death and diseases in a multitude. Knowing too much and too little both sadden life, for "too much" and "too little" are equally distant from the middle path.

### 114

Foolish foresight, senseless worry, and the presumption of vain intellect drew fire out of its proper nature; its powers then extended beyond its own kingdom. From this, the war was born that still lasts among the elements, which took offense at it; the earth trembles, the sky flashes with lightning and pours rain: every <term>intemperance</term> <note>original: "distemperanza." A state of imbalance where the harmony between the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) or the bodily humors is destroyed.</note> originates here.

### 115

This evil fire ignited the fierce desire for the elements to overcome one another; this wretched longing then descended lower into mortal bodies and human minds. It drew all its strength from <term>Hope</term> <note>In the myth of Pandora, Hope was the only thing left in the jar. Here, Lorenzo views Hope skeptically, as a force that fans the flames of impossible desires.</note>, who blows upon the evil fire with her winds. Thus the world and every mortal life exist through a war that is not yet finished;

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici uses the myth of Prometheus and Epimetheus to explain the origin of human suffering and environmental chaos, arguing that the loss of the Golden Age resulted from a disruption of the "middle path" of knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Prometheus, Epimetheus, Golden Age, human intellect, four humors, four elements, Pandora's box, temperance, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 288 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous section where Lorenzo critiqued Prometheus for bringing restless ambition and "burnt humors" to humanity, these stanzas transition into a longing for the "Golden Age"—a state of natural grace and emotional equilibrium where love replaces the agony of intellectual overreach.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->116<-

Just like a ship on the high seas, struck by rapid and opposing winds, it labors but does not move from its spot if it happens that the winds are equally powerful. But if one exerts more force and prevails over the other, the ship grows weary and, finally defeated, follows the losing side. Oh, miserable world! Or rather, foolish to those who find it pleasing, or who believe they can find peace in such a great war! <note>Lorenzo uses the nautical metaphor of a "deadlocked" ship to describe the internal conflict of the human soul caught between competing passions.</note>

->117<-

Let this fire burn, at least; let it burn so brightly that it consumes all other <term>sad humors</term> <note>Referring back to the "burnt humors" mentioned on the previous page; here, the "fire" of love is seen as a purifying force that cleanses the body of the fluids thought to cause melancholy.</note>. Then let us return to that first holy place, and let no one else presume to steal fire again. Let sweet leisure return without hope or sorrow; let the oaks drip honey, let the rivers run with nectar and milk. Let all sorrows be driven away, and let blessed hearts burn with sweet love. <note>This stanza invokes the classic imagery of the "Golden Age," a mythological era of plenty where nature provided for man without labor, and honey flowed from trees.</note>

->118<-

In these sweet places, in these times, place me, <term>Love</term> <note>original: "Amor." The personification of the god of love.</note>, with my beautiful lady, in the greenness of youth, in those first simple years, without hope and without jealousy. May time never bring us to a mature age, but let our sweet love be eternal. Let there be no more beauty in her, nor any other fire in us, but only that sweet time and place.

->119<-

That sweet place and <term>earthly paradise</term> <note>original: "basso paradiso," literally a "low paradise," referring to a state of heaven-on-earth.</note>, that beautiful time has no other flaw than the need to see my Lady’s beautiful face. This makes it most sweet and perfect, if one hears her words or her gentle laughter, which is beyond all true love and delight. The gold of that near-divine age is refined in the sweet fire of my lady.

<summary>Lorenzo longs for a return to the mythological Golden Age, where the purifying fire of love replaces human restlessness and internal conflict. He envisions an eternal youth spent with his beloved in an "earthly paradise" of natural abundance.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Age, earthly paradise, purification, Love, eternal youth, nautical metaphor, pastoral poetry</keywords>

--- Page 289 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page where the narrator yearned for the restoration of the "Golden Age"—a mythological era of peace and natural abundance—these stanzas shift the focus to the narrator's lady. He argues that her presence is so divine that she creates a "paradise" wherever she goes, surpassing even the ancient myths.</meta>

# SECOND FOREST

->120<-

And, if high law forbids even this, <term>Love</term> <note>original: "Amor." Referring to the personified god of Love, often depicted as Cupid.</note>, at least drive away that great hope—a hostile, intimate, and secret enemy that kills the heart with its sweet poison. Return to me the joyful, loving light and that sweet, angelic, and serene gaze; grant a sweet look to this cruel and sad woman, just as the <term>basilisk</term> <note>original: "bavalischio." A mythical creature whose gaze was famously lethal. Lorenzo uses this comparison to suggest that his lady's eyes have a similarly total, though "sweet," power over his life.</note> is to mortal sight.

->121<-

If you return my noble lady to me, as beautiful and loving as when I left her, I will never ask you, Love, for that <term>Golden Age</term> <note>The "Etá d'oro," a recurring theme in Renaissance poetry representing a lost state of innocence and perfection.</note>—be it real or a fable—nor for any other paradise or any other thing. For wherever my lady is, as you know, every virtue and every sweetness gather: and all that is beautiful is contained within her beauty.

->122<-

Alas for me! Now, in that high and sylvan place where she finds herself sorrowful and sad, the age is golden; it is an <term>earthly paradise</term>, and there the first age of man is renewed. If, even while sad and weary in those alpine heights, she moves with such sweetness and grace—if she carries so many blessings while in pain—what will she do when she is finally happy with me?

->123<-

What she will do then—if the sad heart even contemplates it, such great desire inflames the wretched soul that, struck then by immense and cruel pain, it can hardly defend itself from death. Even if Love promises or permits the heart to think of something else, this offends it even more. It cannot live without the thought of love; yet, even in thinking of his lady, he dies. <note>Lorenzo concludes with a classic "lover’s paradox": the thought of the beloved is the only thing that sustains his life, yet the intensity of that thought is so overwhelming it leads to a symbolic death.</note>

<summary>In these stanzas, Lorenzo de' Medici elevates his beloved to a divine status, claiming her presence creates a "Golden Age" even in a desolate wilderness. He explores the psychological torment of the lover, who is caught in a cycle of life-sustaining and heart-breaking desire.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Golden Age, basilisk, courtly love, earthly paradise, sylvan landscape, paradox of desire</keywords>

--- Page 290 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page where the narrator described his lady's presence as creating an "earthly paradise" in the mountains, these stanzas focus on the poet's desire to be reunited with her through the intervention of Love (Cupid), utilizing the metaphor of the soul as a purifying fire.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->124<-

<term>Love</term> <note>original: "Amor." Here, Lorenzo addresses Cupid, the personified god of Love, as a messenger and cosmic force.</note>, you who see her wretched state,
moved to pity, I believe, by her suffering,
fly swiftly to that beautiful place;
bring my lady to me: or place your wings
upon my shoulders, and grant me your flight,
so that I may go to her: if you are my rival,
as I suspect, and inflamed by her beautiful eyes,
I am jealous if you touch her as you carry her.

->125<-

If you transform me into a loving bird,
I shall burn, as the <term>phoenix</term> <note>The mythical bird that dies in fire and is reborn from its own ashes; a common Renaissance symbol for the transformative and eternal nature of love.</note> is wont to do
in <term>Phobean rays</term> <note>original: "febei rai." Referring to Phoebus Apollo, the god of the sun. The "sun" here is a metaphor for the lady's eyes.</note>, and I shall become more beautiful
reborn from my bright sun.
If your wings should burn in that
noble fire, you are wrong to complain;
and it is not just that you call yourself offended,
because you yourself lit that noble fire.

->126<-

This fire stole from you the gaze
of my lady, and with it, ignited the heart.
You were indignant; I suffer for it, and burn
with a diverse desire that took hold.
Between the swift heart and the heavy, slow body
the fire and the thought pull toward that beautiful land;
here the body remains, and the thought follows her;
I neither go, nor stay, nor am I divided or whole. <note>This "neither go nor stay" is a classic Petrarchan paradox, describing the spiritual tension of a lover whose mind is with his beloved while his body is elsewhere.</note>

->127<-

This fire is of a noble nature;
it resides in the heart, at its highest peak:
and the matter, which was coarse and hard,
it first consumed with some pain:
at last the fire became pure light,
which seems to express itself in the <term>diaphanous</term> <note>original: "diafano." Transparent or translucent. In Neoplatonic philosophy, the soul becomes "clear" or "transparent" as it is purified by divine love, allowing the light of the beloved to shine through it.</note> heart;
thus in the heart, not that it has light of its own,
shines the light of two beautiful eyes.

<vocab>Love (Amor), Forests of Love (Selve d'Amore), phoenix (fenice), Phobean rays (febei rai), noble fire (gentil foco), eight-line stanza (ottava rima)</vocab>

<summary>The poet pleads with Cupid to facilitate a reunion with his lady and describes a Neoplatonic transformation where love acts as a purifying fire, turning the heart into a transparent vessel for the lady's light.</summary>
<keywords>Cupid, Phoenix, Neoplatonism, purification, spiritual light, paradox of love, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

--- Page 291 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page where the poet's spirit was caught in a paradoxical state of wanting to fly to his lady while being tied to his body, these stanzas explore the physical and psychological toll of this internal conflict through various classical and natural metaphors.</meta>

<header>SECOND FOREST</header>

### 128

With great effort within my weary breast
I hold my spirit, lest it flee along with my life:
this noble thing can only remain so low
if its path is forcibly blocked:
like a stone stopping in the middle of the sky,
invited by both the center <note>In the Aristotelian physics of the time, "the center" refers to the center of the Earth, the natural resting place for heavy objects like stones.</note> and the heavens:
nature denies it any rest,
unless it returns to the beautiful thief <note>The "thief" is a poetic reference to the lady who has "stolen" the poet's heart.</note>.

### 129

Thus I am like a spread net,
which the hollow wood holds above the wave:
the heavy lead, weighing from below,
pulls it toward the deepest part:
in the end, each of them fails in their task:
the wood becomes soaked and the lead does not sink:
neither desire seems to be fulfilled:
meanwhile, the net is worn away and torn.

### 130

The beautiful image, which her white hand
stamps upon my heart as if it were alive,
so deceives the amorous flame <note>The "amorous flame" represents the poet's burning desire or his very soul.</note>
that it stays there, and is the reason I live.
That sweet deception saves my life;
and, if it were not so, my life would have fled with her:
the flame sees its beautiful thief in my heart,
and so grows quiet, believing it is with her.

### 131

Just like the hunter, who cleverly steals
the dear cubs from the fierce tiger,
and, though he gains a great lead on her,
the beast, faster by nature,
almost reaches him and bloodies her claws;
but, seeing her own reflection
in the mirror that he places on the sand,
she believes it is her cub and restrains her course; <note>This draws on a popular medieval legend (found in bestiaries) that a tigress could be distracted from a hunter by a mirror; seeing her own reflection, she would stop to "nurture" the image, thinking it was her stolen offspring.</note>

<vocab>Second Forest, eight-line stanza <note>original: "ottava rima." A classic Italian verse form.</note>, amorous metaphors, hunter, tiger, mirror</vocab>

<summary>In these stanzas, the poet uses vivid natural metaphors—a stone suspended in air, a fishing net, and a distracted tiger—to illustrate the agonizing psychological tension of being separated from his beloved.</summary>
<keywords>soul's ascent, Aristotelian physics, internal conflict, amorous deception, tiger metaphor, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the metaphor in stanza 131 where the poet compares himself to a tiger distracted by a mirror, this page continues the exploration of that deception and the poet's subsequent plea for the relief of sleep or the return of his beloved.</meta>

<header>IV - FORESTS OF LOVE</header>

->132<-

just so, within the mirror of my heart,
this beautiful fire of love grows quiet.
But, once it recognizes the empty error,
this fierce tiger rises up in fury;
and, if it does not reach the thieving hunter,
in its anger it finds no moment of rest.
O Love, who sees my pain and my peril,
either help me or give me counsel.

->133<-

If you will not return the beautiful woman to me,
let a peaceful sleep close these tearful eyes:
if I see her while sleeping, you defend
my life with these wandering and foolish thoughts.
O Sleep, who always contends with weeping
to take hold of the eyes, flatten the high hills,
remove the rough path, the woods, rocks, and rivers,
and show me those lovely lights <note>In the poetic tradition of the time, "lovely lights" (vaghi lumi) refers to the lady's eyes.</note> up close.

->134<-

I see I know not what in the dark shadow,
a fire that falls from heaven to the earth,
like a vapor; and its pure light
reaches the ground, and seems to be reborn there:
the flame turns back toward the heavens and endures,
without any new fuel to feed it.
Some propitious deity shows to my eyes
that we shall soon see our lady again.

->135<-

I feel a sweet little breeze, which blows
from the glowing and red aurora <note>The Roman personification of the dawn.</note>.
Every animal, which is blinded when it gazes
upon the Phoebéan light <note>The light of the sun, named for Phoebus Apollo, the god of the sun.</note>, I believe can now flee.
The lover doubles his kisses and sighs
that every shadow of the night is already shaken off;
filled with even greater desire, with great effort
he leaves the arms of his sweet friend.

<summary>These stanzas conclude a sequence of metaphors regarding the poet's internal struggle, moving from the fury of a deceived heart to a prayer for prophetic dreams and the eventual arrival of dawn.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Selve d'Amore, amorous fire, Sleep, Phoebus, Aurora, courtly love, metaphors of desire</keywords>

--- Page 293 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page where the poet anticipated the arrival of dawn and the return of his beloved after a night of restless dreams, these stanzas describe the actual sunrise and the allegorical appearance of the Lady accompanied by Love and Beauty.</meta>

<header>SECOND FOREST</header>

->136<-

Already some of the most diligent birds
call the sun with certain sweet verses,
and they set the song; and then follows them
the chorus of a thousand different birds.
The flowers, which without the sun become less beautiful,
can no longer hold themselves within the bud:
at first of one color and then painted by the sun,
they become of a thousand shades, surpassed by no art.

->137<-

Hunted, the dawn <note>Original: *aurora*.</note> flees before it:
the air already sheds its changing garment,
and dresses itself in light that gilds it;
that which remains without Phoebus <note>Original: *Febo*, a classical name for the sun-god Apollo, often used to represent the sun itself.</note> stays in black.
Behold my sun <note>A common poetic metaphor where the beloved is compared to the sun because she brings light and life to the poet.</note> coming out from the mountain,
leaving behind that shadowy and dismal part:
I see the light, and already I feel the heat—
the light is beauty and the heat is love.

->138<-

This light comforts and does not offend
the eyes, but takes from them every desire
to see anything else: and the fire does not burn,
but warms with a gentle and pious heat.
My Lady <note>Original: *Madonna*.</note> takes these two by the hand:
on the left she leads the blind god <note>The "blind god" refers to Cupid, or Love, who is traditionally depicted as blindfolded.</note>,
and Beauty she holds on the right;
and she, even more beautiful, comes between them.

->139<-

Love, who gazes fixedly into those two beautiful eyes,
doubles the fire with which he burns himself:
Beauty <note>Original: *Beltá*.</note>, who mirrors herself in that lovely face,
makes herself even more beautiful and gives herself to herself.
My Lady moves in a sweet smile,
from which the world takes all its beauty:
this beauty alone makes the world fall in love;
in various things, the world ignores the beautiful origin <note>The poet suggests that while people are attracted to many different beautiful things, they fail to recognize that all beauty stems from this single, divine source.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the transition from dawn to sunrise, serving as a metaphor for the arrival of the poet's beloved. The Lady is personified as a divine figure who leads both Love and Beauty, acting as the source of all earthly grace.</summary>
<keywords>dawn, Phoebus, sunrise, Cupid, allegory of Beauty, My Lady, Neoplatonism, nature imagery</keywords>

--- Page 294 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page, the poet described the dawn and the arrival of his Lady, who walks between the allegorical figures of Love (Cupid) and Beauty. This page continues the description of their arrival and introduces a song performed by these figures.</meta>

# IV - FORESTS OF LOVE

->140<-

They come singing joyfully together:
everyone hears the sweet melody;
the heart understands it, but fears to repeat it
to others. It happens with this beautiful harmony
as it does with the celestial music <note>The "celestial harmony" refers to the "Music of the Spheres," an ancient philosophical concept that the movements of the planets and stars create a divine music, which is inaudible to human ears because we are accustomed to it from birth or because our souls are too "low" to perceive it.</note> in these distant
parts of the world, which seems to be silent,
because the lowly human ear cannot tune itself to that sound:
likewise, the common people are deaf to this beautiful song.

->141<-

My heart tells me secretly
that Beauty composed the words
of this song, and then it hears
that Love gently intones the melody;
and, although the mind keeps it in secret,
not every noble person <note>Original: *gentil persona*. In the tradition of Italian courtly love, "gentle" or "noble" refers to a nobility of spirit and heart, rather than just social rank.</note> is excluded:
repeating it to them is not burdensome for the heart,
and, as the heart recalls it, this is the song:

->142<-

> — O lovely, amorous eyes,
> who in this face and that,
> when you gaze intently,
> see a thousand different beauties;
> 
> while these two lovely lights <note>Original: *dua vaghi lumi*, a common poetic metaphor referring to the eyes of the beloved.</note>
> are hidden from you,
> let no foolish man presume
> to have seen beauty in its entirety.
> 
> Here is true beauty
> all gathered into one face:
> from here the others have taken their example,
> those which are scattered among various things.
> 
> Whoever gazes upon this beauty,
> sighs forever with eternal and sweet love. —

<summary>In these stanzas, the poet describes a divine song performed by Love and Beauty, comparing its sublime nature to the music of the spheres. The song itself celebrates the Lady’s face as the singular source and template for all beauty in the world.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, music of the spheres, allegorical song, eyes as lights, Platonic beauty, noble heart</keywords>

--- Page 295 ---
