Having established the allegorical presence of Love and Beauty accompanying the Lady, the poet now introduces a song that defines the nature of true beauty and the elite spiritual capacity required to perceive it.</meta>

### V

# AMBRA

### LITTLE POEM <note>The title *Ambra* refers to Lorenzo de' Medici's favorite villa at Poggio a Caiano, built on an island in the Ombrone river. In this poem, "Ambra" is personified as a nymph pursued by the river god Ombrone.</note>

->140<-

They come singing joyfully together:
everyone hears the sweet melody;
the heart understands it, but fears to repeat it
to others. It happens with this beautiful harmony
as it does with the celestial music <note>The "celestial harmony" refers to the "Music of the Spheres," an ancient philosophical concept that the movements of the planets and stars create a divine music, which is inaudible to human ears because we are accustomed to it from birth or because our souls are too "low" to perceive it.</note> in these distant
parts of the world, which seems to be silent,
because the lowly human ear cannot tune itself to that sound:
likewise, the common people are deaf to this beautiful song.

->141<-

My heart tells me secretly
that Beauty composed the words
of this song, and then it hears
that Love gently intones the melody;
and, although the mind keeps it in secret,
not every noble person <note>Original: *gentil persona*. In the tradition of Italian courtly love, "gentle" or "noble" refers to a nobility of spirit and heart, rather than just social rank.</note> is excluded:
repeating it to them is not burdensome for the heart,
and, as the heart recalls it, this is the song:

->142<-

> — O lovely, amorous eyes,
> who in this face and that,
> when you gaze intently,
> see a thousand different beauties;
> 
> while these two lovely lights <note>Original: *dua vaghi lumi*, a common poetic metaphor referring to the eyes of the beloved.</note>
> are hidden from you,
> let no foolish man presume
> to have seen beauty in its entirety.
> 
> Here is true beauty
> all gathered into one face:
> from here the others have taken their example,
> those which are scattered among...

<summary>This page introduces the mythological poem Ambra, beginning with a lyrical passage where the figures of Love and Beauty perform a song. It highlights the Neoplatonic idea that true beauty is a divine harmony perceptible only to those with a noble spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Ambra, Lorenzo de' Medici, Music of the Spheres, courtly love, Neoplatonism, personification of Beauty, personification of Love</keywords>

--- Page 297 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from the introductory songs of Love and Beauty to the main body of the poem "Ambra." Having established a spiritual and courtly context, the poet now shifts to a vivid, naturalistic description of the winter season, which serves as the setting for the upcoming myth of the nymph Ambra and the river god Ombrone.</meta>

<page-num>I</page-num>

# DESCRIPTION OF WINTER.

Fled is the season that had turned
the flowers into fruits <note>Original: *pomi*. While literally "apples," in this context it refers generally to the ripened fruit of the harvest.</note> now ripe and gathered:
on the branch, no leaf can hold itself any longer,
but scattered through the much thinner woods
they make themselves heard, if it happens that
the hunter crosses them, and the few seem like many:
the wild beast, though it hides its faint tracks well,
cannot move in secret through the dry leaves.

->2<-

Among the dry trees stands the joyful laurel
and the fragrant shrub of Venus <note>Original: *Ciprigna*. An epithet for Venus, "The Cyprian," referring to her legendary birth on the island of Cyprus. The "fragrant shrub" is the myrtle, which was sacred to her.</note>:
the fir tree grows green in the white mountains <note>Original: *bianche alpe*. While it can refer specifically to the Alps, here it likely means any high, snow-covered peaks.</note>
and bends its branches already burdened with snow:
the cypress holds some hidden bird;
and the strongest tree fights against the winds:
the humble juniper with its sharp leaves
does not prick the hand of one who picks them carefully.

->3<-

The olive tree on some sweet, sunny slope
appears now green, now white, according to the wind <note>The undersides of olive leaves are a silvery-white, creating a shimmering effect when the wind blows through the trees.</note>:
nature preserves and nourishes in this tree
that greenness which is lacking in other foliage.
Already the migratory <note>Original: *pellegrini*. Meaning "pilgrim" or "traveling," describing birds that migrate with the seasons.</note> birds with great effort
have led their tired family
from across the sea, and along their path were shown
Nereids, tritons, and other sea-monsters <note>Nereids and Tritons are Greek sea deities. In Renaissance poetry, "monsters" often refers to any wondrous or large sea creature rather than something purely malevolent.</note>.

<summary>This opening passage of the poem provides a sensory description of early winter, contrasting the withered deciduous forests with the resilient evergreens and the arrival of migratory birds.</summary>
<keywords>Winter, Lorenzo de' Medici, nature imagery, evergreen trees, mythology, Venus, migration, hunting</keywords>

--- Page 298 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the description of the winter season begun on the previous page. Having described the changing flora and the behavior of animals, the poet now shifts to a cosmic and psychological perspective, describing the long winter nights and the mythological figures that personify the stars and the dawn.</meta>

<page-num>292</page-num>

# V — AMBRA

->4<-

Night has fought for the empire and won,
and leads the brief day as her prisoner:
in the serene sky, ringed by eternal flames,
she joyfully leads her starry chariot around:
nor does she rise before the other adorned golden chariot <note>The "golden chariot" is that of the Sun (Phoebus Apollo), which sets in the ocean as the night begins.</note>
is seen dipped into the ocean:
Cold Orion threatens Phoebus with his blade <note>The constellation Orion is often depicted as a hunter with a sword or club. In winter, Orion dominates the sky, seemingly "threatening" the Sun (Phoebus) who stays hidden longer.</note>,
should he show us his beautiful face.

->5<-

Following this burning nocturnal chariot are
vigils, night watches <note>Original: *escubie*. From the Latin *excubiae*, referring to the act of keeping watch or standing guard at night.</note>, and anxious cares,
and Sleep (and, although he is very powerful,
these troublesome things often overcome him too),
and sweet dreams, which deceive the mind
when it is oppressed by hard fortunes:
someone who wakes up sick and poor
celebrates health and great treasure in his sleep.

->6<-

Oh, miserable is he who, in such a long night,
does not sleep and waits for the desired day:
especially if some great and sweet desire pricks him,
such as the coming day might promise!
And, although he joins both his eyelids together,
excludes sad thoughts, and admits sweet ones,
whether sleeping or awake, so that he might cheat the time,
the night seems to him a century of a hundred years.

->7<-

Oh, miserable is he who finds himself out among the waves
during such a long night, far from the shore!
The wind breaks the path of the blind prow <note>The "blind prow" suggests a ship unable to see its way through the darkness and storm.</note>,
and the sea trembles with a fierce bellowing;
with many prayers and vows Aurora <note>The Goddess of the Dawn.</note>
is called upon, but she stays with her old husband <note>In Greek mythology, Aurora (Eos) loved Tithonus and obtained immortality for him, but forgot to ask for eternal youth; thus, he became a withered old man. The poet suggests the dawn is slow to arrive because she is lingering in bed with him.</note>:
sadly he counts, and longingly he watches,
the slow steps of the late night.

<summary>Lorenzo de’ Medici personifies the winter night as a conqueror that holds the sun captive, exploring the psychological toll of long nights on the anxious, the poor, and the imperiled sailor.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, winter, night, Orion, Phoebus, Aurora, Tithonus, dreams, maritime danger, personification</keywords>

--- Page 299 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s descriptive poem "Ambra," moving from the psychological experience of long winter nights to the physical changes in the landscape, focusing on the migration of birds and the freezing of the rivers.</meta>

<page-num>293</page-num>

# V — AMBRA

->8<-

How different, even opposite, is the fate
of happy lovers in the freezing winter frost <note>Original: *algente bruma*. From the Latin *algere* (to be cold) and *bruma* (the winter solstice), signaling the deepest part of winter.</note>,
for whom the nights are both bright and short,
while the dark and late-coming day consumes itself.
In this season, so icy and harsh,
having already grown their new plumage,
the little birds have somewhat set aside
what I know not whether to call happy verses or weeping.

->9<-

Shrieking in the sky, cranes are seen from afar
marking the air with various and beautiful shapes,
and the last one, with neck outstretched, reaches
where the one in front leaves its fleeting tracks:
and once it reaches the sunny places,
one keeps a vigilant watch, while the rest of the flock sleeps.
Thousands of species of colorful and lovely birds
cover the meadows and move lightly across the lakes.

->10<-

The eagle often with its slow flight
threatens them all, and flies above the pond:
they rise together and drive it away with the wind
of their clattering feathers; and yet, if a single one
remains outside the winged herd,
the swift bird suddenly snatches it away;
the poor creature remains deceived, if it believes
it is going to Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus, who took the form of an eagle.</note> like Ganymede <note>In Greek mythology, Ganymede was a beautiful Trojan prince abducted by Zeus in the form of an eagle to serve as cupbearer to the gods.</note>.

->11<-

Zephyr <note>The West Wind, associated with spring.</note> has fled to Cyprus, and dances
idly with the flowers through the joyful grass:
the air—no longer serene, beautiful, and golden—
is broken and disturbed by Boreas <note>The North Wind.</note> and Aquilo <note>The North-East Wind.</note>.
The running and murmuring water is crystallized by
the ice, and, exhausted, now rests quietly.
The fish, caught in the hard and clear wave,
remains like a mosquito in golden amber <note>This striking image provides the title for the poem, *Ambra*, comparing the fish frozen in a river to an insect preserved in resin.</note>.

<summary>Lorenzo de' Medici describes the arrival of deep winter through the migration patterns of cranes, the predatory behavior of eagles, and the freezing of the landscape. The passage concludes with a famous metaphor of a fish trapped in ice like an insect in amber, evoking the poem's title.</summary>

<keywords>Winter, birds, cranes, eagle, Ganymede, Zephyr, Boreas, ice, amber, Lorenzo de' Medici, pastoral poetry</keywords>

--- Page 300 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s descriptive and allegorical poem "Ambra." Having previously described the arrival of winter and the movements of birds, the poet now focuses on the personification of the landscape—specifically Monte Morello and the personified winds—as they prepare for the winter floods.</meta>

<page-num>294</page-num>

# V — AMBRA

->12<-

That mountain which stands against the fierce Northwest Wind <note>Original: *Cauro*, the Latin *Caurus*, a cold wind from the northwest.</note>,
so that it may not harm the noble flower grown
within its lap—a flower of honor, wealth, and power—
now encircles its already white head with mists:
the shoulders, falling away from that haughty head,
cover the white hair, and the shaggy chest
is hidden by a horrible beard, stiff with ice:
the eyes and nose form a fountain, and the frost turns it to ice.

->13<-

The misty garland that encircles 
his high temples is placed on his head by the South Wind <note>Original: *Noto*, the wind that brings moisture and rain.</note>;
then the North Wind <note>Original: *Borea*, the cold wind from the north.</note> chases and drives it from the alp;
and the old head remains bare and white:
the South Wind, on damp and malicious wings,
carries the mists back, and seems to clothe him once more.
Thus Mount Morello <note>A prominent mountain north of Florence, overlooking the Medici villa at Poggio a Caiano.</note>, in its anger, now burdened and now light,
threatens the subject plain below with either rain or snow.

->14<-

The hot and tinted South Wind <note>Original: *Austro*, often described as coming from the south (Ethiopia) and bringing heavy rains.</note> departs from Ethiopia,
and sates his thirsty sponges
dipped in the salt waves of the Tyrrhenian Sea:
as soon as he reaches the destined places,
heavy with water and girded with clouds,
and weary, he then clenches both his fists:
the joyful rivers, meeting the friendly waters,
then emerge from their ancient caverns.

->15<-

They give thanks to Father Oceanus <note>In mythology, the personified source of all the world's fresh water.</note>, their temples
adorned with marsh grass and river foliage;
their hoarse and twisted horns sound in celebration:
their swollen bellies, now proud, are filled:
the indignation, conceived for many days
against the timid banks, is fulfilled;
the foaming water has already broken the enemy dike,
and the old margin can no longer enclose its course.

<summary>This page describes the personification of Monte Morello and the various winds (Northwest, North, and South) as they bring heavy mists and rains, causing the rivers to swell and violently overflow their banks.</summary>
<keywords>Monte Morello, Winds (Boreas, Notus, Auster), personification, winter flooding, Poggio a Caiano, Italian landscape poetry</keywords>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s descriptive and allegorical poem "Ambra." Having previously described the arrival of winter and the personification of Mount Morello and the winds, the poet now shifts focus to the swelling of the rivers and the violent power of the winter floods, comparing the roar of the water to the geothermal activity near Volterra.</meta>

<page-num>295</page-num>

<header>V - AMBRA</header>

### 16

Not through twisted ways or oblique paths,
in the manner of snakes, in great coils,
do they hasten their way to the ancient father <note>The sea, often personified in classical poetry as the father of all waters.</note>:
distant rivers join their waves together;
and one tells the other, like a friend,
news of his country and its customs:
thus speaking together in a strange voice,
they seek, but do not find, their lost mouth <note>The floodwaters are so vast that the usual river mouths are submerged or unrecognizable.</note>.

### 17

When, swollen and wide, it constricts itself
between the high mountains of a closed valley,
the waves shriek, held back, turbid and malignant,
and mixed with earth they appear yellow:
it piles heavy stones upon stones,
angry at the rocks of the narrow path:
it swirls foaming waves and roars horribly;
the shepherd sees it from above and, though safe, fears.

### 18

Such a roar, weeping, makes the earth sad,
parched within its hollow, burnt belly:
it drives out flame and mixed water along with smoke,
crying out as it exits through the narrow mouth,
terrible to the ears and to the sight:
high and robust Volterra <note>An ancient hilltop city in Tuscany.</note>, nearby, fears the thunder
and the turbid pools <note>Original: *lagoni*, referring to the geothermal springs or "soffioni" characteristic of the region around Volterra, known today for its borax and steam vents.</note> that foam:
and it expects rain if the smoke rises higher.

### 19

Thus angered, the fierce torrent <term>frende</term> <note>From the Latin *frendere*, meaning to gnash one’s teeth or roar with rage.</note>
proudly, and gnaws at the opposing banks:
but, after it spreads out into the wide plain,
almost content, it is then barely heard:
uncertain whether it turns back up or continues down,
it has reached the edge of the distant mountains;
already a victor, it marches toward the quiet lake,
full of branches and trunks, its mountain prey.

<summary>Lorenzo describes the violent transformation of rivers during winter floods, comparing the sound and fury of the water to the geothermal volcanic activity near the city of Volterra.</summary>
<keywords>Ambra, Lorenzo de' Medici, Volterra, lagoni, geothermal, floods, Tuscany, Ombrone river, personification, nature poetry</keywords>

--- Page 302 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s "Ambra," following the description of the violent torrent as it breaks its banks. The focus shifts from the natural power of the water to the human tragedy of the flood and the beginning of the mythological transformation of the landscape.</meta>

<page-num>296</page-num>

<header>V - AMBRA</header>

### 20

->20<-

The peasant woman <term>villana</term> <note>A woman of the rural working class.</note> barely had time, 
fearing for her life, to open the stable for the beasts: 
she carries her crying son in a wicker basket <term>zana</term>; 
the eldest daughter follows, her shoulder 
heavy with humble clothes, linens and wool: 
other old household goods <term>masserizia</term> go floating by; 
the pigs swim along with the frightened oxen; 
the little sheep will never be shorn again <note>A poetic way of saying the sheep have drowned or been lost to the flood.</note>.

### 21

->21<-

Some of the family have retreated 
to the tops of the houses; and from the roof 
they see their poor wealth sinking below— 
their labor, their hope; and out of fear 
for themselves, they do not grieve or speak a word; 
the heart in their sad breast fears for life itself, 
nor do they seem to care for their dear possessions: 
for thus the greater worry chases every other away.

### 22

->22<-

The familiar green bank no longer restrains 
the happy fish, who now have wider spaces: 
their old and rightful desire is somewhat satisfied 
to see new shores: and, not yet sated, 
this new pleasure leads them wandering 
to see the ruins and the great destruction 
of the buildings; and beneath the water 
they see the walls, joyful and yet not quite secure.

### 23

->23<-

Then, in the manner of a tiny island, 
Ombrone <note>The Ombrone Pistoiese, a river that flows near Lorenzo’s villa at Poggio a Caiano.</note>, the proud lover, encircles Ambra; 
Ambra, no less beloved by Lauro <note>Lauro is the poetic pseudonym for Lorenzo de' Medici himself, punning on the laurel tree.</note>, 
who is jealous if his rival touches or embraces her; 
Ambra, a dryad <note>A tree nymph.</note> pleasing to her Delia <note>The goddess Diana (Artemis), often called Delia after her birthplace, Delos.</note> 
as much as any who ever loosed an arrow from a bow; 
so beautiful and noble that it eventually harms her; 
light of foot and faster than any other.

<summary>Lorenzo describes the tragic loss of a peasant family's livelihood during a flood before transitioning into a mythological allegory where the river Ombrone, personified as a lover, pursues the nymph Ambra.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Ambra, Ombrone, flood, Tuscan landscape, mythology, peasant life, Poggio a Caiano</keywords>

--- Page 303 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Lorenzo de’ Medici’s mythological poem "Ambra," following the description of the flood. Having established the setting of the Ombrone river, the narrative now introduces the backstory of the nymph Ambra, her admirer Lauro, and the river god Ombrone who pursues her.</meta>

<page-num>297</page-num>

<header>V - AMBRA</header>

### 24

->24<-

From her early years, this nymph was loved
by her noble Lauro <note>A poetic pseudonym for Lorenzo de' Medici himself; "Lauro" is the Italian word for laurel.</note>, an alpine shepherd,
with a chaste love; nor had any
lecherous flame ever entered her innocent breast.
To flee the heat, one day she entered naked
into the cold waves of the Ombrone <note>The Ombrone Pistoiese river.</note>, son of the
Apennines, proud in his appearance and his ways,
on account of his ancient father and hundred brother-rivers.

### 25

->25<-

As her virginal limbs entered
the dark and icy waters, he felt her;
and moved by that graceful, adorned body,
the proud god emerged from his cavern:
with his left hand he took the twisted horn <term>torto corno</term> <note>The horn is a traditional symbol of river deities, representing the branching channels or the power of the current.</note>,
and naked in all else, burning with desire,
he shielded his wild head from the sun’s rays,
crowned with fir trees and mountain beeches.

### 26

->26<-

And toward the place where the nymph remained,
he went, very softly, hidden by the foliage;
he was not seen, nor did the murmur 
of the clear waves allow his footsteps to be heard.
He came so close to the nymph
that he believed he could reach her blonde tresses,
and hold that beautiful nymph in his arms,
holding her beautiful, naked body against his own.

### 27

->27<-

Just like a fish, when the fisherman 
unwittingly covers it with a fine and thin mesh,
flees the net as soon as it feels it above,
leaving behind some scales in order to escape;
so the nymph, when she realized she was discovered,
fled from the god as he threw himself upon her:
she was not fast enough—or rather, he was so fast—
that she left some of her hair in his hand.

<summary>This page describes the river god Ombrone's sudden infatuation with the nymph Ambra as she bathes, and her narrow escape as he attempts to seize her, leaving only a lock of hair in his grasp.</summary>
<keywords>Ambra, Lauro, Lorenzo de' Medici, Ombrone, Apennines, river god, nymph, mythology, pursuit, pastoral poetry</keywords>

--- Page 304 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the narrative of the nymph Ambra fleeing from the river god Ombrone. On the previous page, Ombrone attempted to seize her while she bathed, but she managed to slip through his fingers, leaving him with only a few strands of her hair.</meta>

<page-num>298</page-num>

<header>V - AMBRA</header>

### 28

->28<-

Leaping from the waves, she quickens her pace; full of fear, she flees naked and barefoot. She leaves behind her clothes, her arrows, and her quiver <note>original: "turcasso"</note>; she cares not for the sharp thorns or the rugged cliff. The god remains behind, grieving, afflicted, and weary: in his pain he wrings his hands and lifts his eyes to heaven, cursing his cruel and slow hand as he looks upon the blonde hairs he has torn away.

### 29

->29<-

And, following her then, he said: "O hand, so swift and fierce to tear those beautiful locks, but alas! too slow to hold that more than human body and make me happy." Thus vainly weeping for his first error, hoping at least to reach her with his voice where his slow step could not arrive, he cried out: "O nymph, I am a river, and yet I burn <note>The poet uses the classic "oxymoron"—a contradiction in terms—of a water deity suffering from the "fire" of passion.</note>."

### 30

->30<-

"In the midst of the cold waters, you kindled a blind, burning desire in my breast. Since your body lay within the waves, why does it not lie with me? It would be better so. If my shade and my clear water pleased you, my cave has more beautiful shade and more beautiful waters. You take pleasure in my things, yet you do not like me; and yet I am a son of the Apennines <note>The mountain range in central Italy where many rivers, including the Ombrone, have their source.</note>, and a god."

### 31

->31<-

The nymph flees, and makes herself deaf to his prayers; fear adds wings to her white feet. The god urges his steps forward as he runs, made swift in his pursuit by love; he sees her white feet wounded with great pain by thorns and sharp stones; his desire grows—making him both freeze and sweat—as he watches her flee, so beautiful and naked.

<summary>Ambra flees naked and terrified from the river god Ombrone, who pursues her while pleading for her to return to his waters. The god laments his failure to capture her, even as he watches her sustain injuries from the rough terrain during her escape.</summary>
<keywords>Ambra, Ombrone, Lorenzo de' Medici, river god, nymph, pursuit, Apennines, Ovidian transformation</keywords>

--- Page 305 ---

On the previous page, Ombrone lamented his slow pace compared to her swift flight, despite his divine nature. The chase has now moved from the rugged mountains down into the open plains near Florence.</meta>

<page-num>299</page-num>

<header>V - AMBRA</header>

### 32

Timid and bashful, Ambra still runs; in her flight, she does not yield to the rapid winds; she could place her light soles upon the ears of grain, and they would support her gentle feet <note>This is a classical literary trope, famously used by Virgil to describe the swiftness of the warrior-maiden Camilla, who could run over a field of corn without bending the stalks.</note>: Ombrone sees her taking more ground at every moment, while the nymph sees less of him with every step: already in the wide plain her pace advances so much that he loses all hope of catching her.

### 33

Earlier, through the high, harsh, and steep mountains, he came among the stones with a rapid course: her less nimble and slow steps gave him hope for some success: but having reached, alas, down into the open plains, a bit <note>original: "morso" — a metaphor comparing the slowing of the river's current in the flatlands to a horse being restrained by a bridle bit.</note> was almost placed on the tired river: since he cannot follow with his feet, he accompanies her across the countryside with his desire and his eyes.

### 34

What should the enamored god do, since he can no longer reach the beautiful nymph? The more she is denied to him, the more desire kindles and goads his enamored heart. The nymph was already near where my Arno <note>Lorenzo de' Medici often refers to the Arno as "his" river, reflecting his pride in Florence and his family's dominion over the Tuscan landscape.</note> receives the Ombrone and joins its waves: Ombrone, seeing the Arno, takes comfort, and his dead hope rises somewhat.

### 35

He cries from afar:
> — O Arno, to whom the greater part of us Tuscan rivers <note>original: "fiumi tòschi"</note> flee for refuge, the beautiful nymph, who flees like a bird, followed by me through so many mountains and woods, breaks my heart without any mercy, nor does it seem that her hard heart knows love: return her to me and my lost hope, and break and block her swift course.

<vocab>Ambra (the nymph), Ombrone (the river-god), Arno (the primary river of Florence), nymph (a minor nature deity), Tuscan rivers, <term>ottava rima</term> (the eight-line poetic stanza used here), Lorenzo de' Medici (the author and ruler of Florence)</vocab>

<summary>The pursuit of Ambra by the river god Ombrone moves from the mountains to the plains, where Ombrone slows down and calls upon the river Arno to help him capture the fleeing nymph.</summary>
<keywords>Ambra, Ombrone, Arno, river gods, pursuit, Tuscan landscape, classical allusion, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

--- Page 306 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the mythical narrative of the nymph Ambra’s flight from the river god Ombrone. Having reached the plains where the Ombrone river meets the Arno, the pursuer calls out to the larger river for assistance in capturing his prey.</meta>

<page-num>300</page-num>

<header>V - AMBRA</header>

->36<-

“I am Ombrone, who for you gathers my azure <note>original: *cerule* — a poetic term for sky-blue or sea-green, often used in classical literature to describe water deities.</note> waves; I save them all for you, and once they become yours, they grow so deep that you may scorn both banks and bridges, tall and proud. This nymph is my prey; and these blonde tresses, which I carry in my hand with bitter grief <note>This implies that Ombrone has come close enough to snatch at her hair, or that he holds a lock of it as he pleads his case.</note>, are a clear sign of it: in you alone is my hope. Help me quickly, for the nymph is flying away!”

->37<-

Arno, seeing Ombrone and moved by pity—because there was no time to give a spoken answer—held back his waters; and, already swollen and large, he blocked the path of the beautiful Ambra from afar. The virgin breast was struck and chilled by a new fear the closer she approached: behind her she hears Ombrone, and before her she sees a lake <note>The Arno has flooded its banks to create an impassable barrier, trapping the nymph between the two rivers.</note>; her frozen and wandering heart knows not what to do.

->38<-

Like a hunted beast that has been chased and then cornered, fleeing the greedy mouths of the dogs, away from the immediate danger, yet seeing the stretched net before its eyes; fearful, and almost certain it must be caught, it dares not flee forward nor turn back; it fears the dogs and does not trust the net; it knows not what to do, and screams in terror;

->39<-

such was the fate of the beautiful nymph: oppressed by fear on every side, she knows not what to do but long for death. She sees one river and then the other approaching; and in her despair, she then cried out loudly: “O chaste goddess <note>This is an invocation of Diana, the virgin goddess of the hunt, to whom nymphs traditionally pledged their chastity.</note>, to whom I was entrusted by my dear father and my ancient mother, be my only help in this final struggle!”

<summary>The river god Ombrone enlists the help of the Arno, which floods its banks to trap the fleeing nymph Ambra. Caught between two waters, Ambra despairs and prays to the goddess Diana for salvation.</summary>
<keywords>Ambra, Ombrone, Arno, Diana, metamorphosis, hunt, river gods, Ovidian style, Lorenzo de' Medici</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the flight of the nymph Ambra. Trapped between the flooded Arno river and her pursuer, the river god Ombrone, she turns to prayer. This section depicts her miraculous metamorphosis into stone to preserve her chastity, a classic mythological trope used here by the poet to celebrate the landscape of his estate at Poggio a Caiano.</meta>

<page-num>301</page-num>

<header>V - AMBRA</header>

->40<-

“Beautiful Diana <note>The goddess of the hunt and protector of virginity.</note>, this chaste breast was never stained by foolish desire: look upon it now, for I, a mere nymph, am not strong enough against two enemies; and each of them is a god <note>She refers to Ombrone and the Arno, or perhaps Ombrone and Love itself.</note>. Along with the desire to die, there remains in my heart only the chaste love for my Lauro <note>original: *Lauro* — This is a poetic double-meaning. It refers to the laurel tree, but also serves as a signature for the author, Lorenzo de' Medici, whose name in Latin/poetic form was Laurentius or Lauro.</note>: carry, O winds, this final cry to my Lauro, so that he may mourn my death.”

->41<-

Hardly were these words out of her mouth when her white feet were seized by a strange stiffness: you see them grow and turn into a rock; her limbs and beautiful body change color, yet you would still believe she was once a woman: the limbs appear like a figure usually does when it is sketched <note>original: *bozzata* — a technical term in sculpture for a rough-hewn or unfinished work.</note> and not yet finished in hard stone.

->42<-

Ombrone, weary and exhausted from the chase, takes new strength from the hope of his precious prey and quickens his pace; it seems he almost believes he has her in his arms. Seeing the rock grow before his eyes, and still unaware of what is happening, he knows not where it comes from: but then, seeing all his desire is in vain, he stops, full of wonder and grief.

->43<-

Just as in a park a doe or other wild beast, enclosed by a thicket or a small wall, finding herself overwhelmed by dogs and without hope of escape near the wall, leaps up out of fear and rises light as air before the dog; the wretched, deluded dog remains inside; unable to follow where she has climbed, he stops, and stares at the place where she fled;

<summary>The nymph Ambra prays to Diana for protection and is transformed into stone just as Ombrone is about to capture her. The poem concludes the chase with a simile comparing the frustrated river god to a hunting dog left behind by its prey.</summary>

<keywords>Diana, Ombrone, Lauro, metamorphosis, chastity, Lorenzo de' Medici, Ambra, sculpture, nymph, river god</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the poem "Ambra" by Lorenzo de' Medici. After the nymph Ambra was transformed into stone to escape the pursuit of the river god Ombrone, the god now stands before the geological monument of his own making, filled with regret and the burden of his own immortality.</meta>

<header>V - AMBRA</header>

->44<-

Thus the god stops his swift tracks;
he looks pitifully at the beautiful growing stone,
the stone, which still preserves some shape
of a beautiful woman, and still feels a little;
and as love and pity move him,
he bathes the stone bitterly with tears,
saying: — “O my Ambra, these are the waters
where you once enjoyed bathing your beautiful body.”

->45<-

“I would not have believed, in such great sorrow,
that my own self-pity, overcome by that
for my nymph, would flee somewhat
because of the greater pity for my beautiful Ambra:
this pity for her, not my own, moves me to tears.
Life is still sad and wretched,
even if it is eternal: when I reflect,
it is worse for me to feel than for her to have no feeling.” <note>Ombrone realizes the tragedy of his divinity: because he is a god, he cannot die to escape his grief, whereas Ambra is now a stone, spared from further suffering.</note>

->46<-

“Alas! In my high ancestral mountains <note>The Apennine mountains, where the Ombrone river originates.</note>
there are so many nymphs, and each one is safe:
among a thousand beauties I chose the most beautiful,
I know not how; and loving only this one,
as a first sign of love I tore at her hair,
and I chased her from the cool, dark water,
tender and naked: then, fleeing pale,
her sacred blood stained the thorns and the rocks.”

->47<-

“And finally transformed into a stone
solely because of my cruel desire,
I do not know how I have lost her, since she was never mine,
nor can I lose this wretched life:
in this my fate is too adverse,
being a miserable and immortal god;
for if only I could at least die,
this just and immortal sorrow could end.”

<term>Ambra</term> <note>Both the name of the nymph and the name of the small island/outcropping at Lorenzo de' Medici’s villa at Poggio a Caiano.</note>
<term>ninfa</term> <note>Nymph: a minor nature goddess, often associated with water or forests.</note>
<term>iddio</term> <note>God: referring here to the river god Ombrone.</note>
<term>sasso</term> <note>Stone or rock; refers to the physical landmark into which the nymph was transformed.</note>
<term>metamorfosi</term> <note>Metamorphosis: a common theme in Renaissance poetry, inspired by the Roman poet Ovid, where humans transform into plants, animals, or stones.</note>

<summary>The poem concludes with the river god Ombrone’s lamentation as he stands before the stone that was once the nymph Ambra. He expresses the profound irony of his divine nature, realizing that his immortality is a curse because it forces him to endure eternal grief for a love he destroyed.</summary>

<keywords>Ombrone, Ambra, Lorenzo de' Medici, Poggio a Caiano, metamorphosis, grief, immortality, river gods, nymphs</keywords>

->48<-

I have learned how one may please
the beloved woman and win her love:
by causing the most displeasure to the one you love most. <note>Ombrone reflects bitterly on the paradox of his love: his intense desire for Ambra was the very thing that caused her suffering and eventual loss of humanity.</note>
O freezing Boreas, <term>Boreas: the personification of the cold North Wind</term> who lingers in ice,
make the flowing water harden and freeze,
so that, having become stone, it may accompany the nymph.
Nor may the sun ever, with its bright yellow rays,
dissolve these rigid crystals back into water. —

<summary>In this final stanza, the river god Ombrone concludes his lament by calling upon the North Wind to freeze his waters forever, so that he may remain joined in stone with the transformed Ambra.</summary>

<sig>LORENZO THE MAGNIFICENT, Works - 1.</sig>

### VI

# ECLOGUES <note>original: "Egloghe"</note>

<vocab>Lorenzo the Magnificent, Eclogues, Italian literature, pastoral poetry</vocab>

<note>An "Eclogue" is a classical form of pastoral poetry popularized by the Roman poet Virgil. Lorenzo used this form to blend refined philosophical ideas with the rustic imagery of the Tuscan countryside, often using shepherds as masks for himself and his friends in the Medicean circle.</note>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Section VI of the collection, dedicated to the "Eclogues." It signals a thematic shift toward pastoral literature and rural dialogue.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Eclogues, pastoral poetry, Italian Renaissance, rustic literature</keywords>

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>This page continues the section of "Eclogues" introduced on the previous page. Having established the shift to pastoral poetry, this text presents the opening of "Corinto," one of Lorenzo de' Medici's most famous works in this genre. It follows the classical tradition of a shepherd’s lament, written in "terza rima" (triple rhyme), the same poetic structure used by Dante in the Divine Comedy.</meta>

# I

## CORINTO <note>Corinto is a fictional shepherd. In the pastoral tradition, authors often used such characters as alter-egos to express their own feelings on love and philosophy.</note>

The moon, amidst the lesser stars,
shone brightly in the quiet and serene sky,
almost hiding their splendor;
and sleep had every earthly animal
<margin>5</margin> released from their daily labors;
and the world is full of shadows and silence.
Only the shepherd Corinto, in the thick woods,
sang for the love of Galatea <note>Galatea is a sea-nymph from classical mythology, famously pursued by the Cyclops Polyphemus. Lorenzo reimagines her here as the object of a shepherd's unrequited desire.</note>
among the beeches, and there is no one else to hear him;
<margin>10</margin> nor to his tearful eyes <note>original: "luci," literally "lights," a poetic term for eyes.</note> had he
given any rest; rather, all alone,
with these verses he wept for his love:
— O Galatea, why do you hold
the shepherd Corinto in such contempt, he who loves you so much?
<margin>15</margin> Why do you wish for the poor wretch to die?
What my sighs and sad weeping are,
the woods hear it, and you, Night, feel it,
since I am beneath your starry mantle.
Without suspicion, the well-fed herds
<margin>20</margin> rest happily in their stillness,
perhaps ruminating on pale grasses.
The little sheep, still within the fold <note>original: "rete," literally "net" or "mesh," referring to a portable sheepfold.</note>,
watched over by the vigilant dog, remain
sleeping and joyful in the cool breeze.

<summary>This page introduces the shepherd Corinto, who wanders through a moonlit forest lamenting his unrequited love for the nymph Galatea while the rest of the world sleeps.</summary>
<keywords>Corinto, Galatea, pastoral poetry, terza rima, Lorenzo de' Medici, unrequited love, nocturnal imagery</keywords>

--- Page 314 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Corinto" eclogue. The shepherd Corinto persists in his solitary lament, moving from describing his environment to addressing the unresponsiveness of his beloved Galatea. He invokes the classical power of poetry and the elements of nature—wind and echo—to carry his message to her.</meta>

<header>VI - ECLOGUES</header>

<margin>25</margin>I weep, unheard, for my harsh suffering,
my tears, prayers, and words are cast into the shadows:
for if they are not heard, what fruit do they bear?
Alas, how she flees before our eyes!
Yet she does not flee from my thoughts,
<margin>30</margin>for there, more than if she were present, she consumes my heart.
Oh, do not have a heart so severe!
For three lustres <note>A "lustre" is a period of five years; Corinto notes she has remained chaste for fifteen years.</note> already of your chaste life
you have served the harsh empire of Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt and virginity; Galatea has remained unreachable and dedicated to her independence.</note>:
is this not enough? Now give me some help,
<margin>35</margin>nymph, you who are without any pity.
But, alas for me! my voice is not heard.
If only one word out of a thousand were heard!
I know that verses have the power, if heard,
to make the moon come down from heaven to earth.
<margin>40</margin>Verses once caused the people of Ithaca <note>A reference to the companions of Odysseus (from Ithaca), who were transformed into swine by the enchantress Circe.</note>
to be transformed into beasts in the green meadows:
verses can break the cold serpent.
Therefore, these rough and plain verses
<margin>45</margin>we shall give to the wind; and now I have seen how
my weeping will be carried to her.
The breeze moves the high crowns of the trees,
which, when moved, give off a sweet murmur
that fills the air and the woods with her name:
<margin>50</margin>if the breeze brings this to me, it will not be a burden
to carry my weeping to this harsh woman
through the high mountains and the deep valleys,
where Echo dwells, who doubles my laments:
either she, or the wind, shall carry it with them:
<margin>54</margin>I know that weeping is not sown in stone <note>A metaphor meaning his tears are not wasted on something entirely lifeless, hoping for some spark of humanity in her.</note>.
<margin>55</margin>Perhaps she hears me nearby in some cave <note>original: "speco," a poetic term for a cave or grotto.</note>.
I do not know if you are near: I know well that,
flee where you will, I am always with you.
If your cruel will were more merciful,
if I could see you here, if I could touch
<margin>60</margin>your white hands and your beautiful face, oh God!

<summary>In this passage from Lorenzo de' Medici’s pastoral poem, the shepherd Corinto laments his unrequited love for the nymph Galatea, invoking the magical power of poetry to influence nature and pleading for the wind to carry his voice to her.</summary>

<keywords>pastoral poetry, Corinto, Galatea, Diana, Ithaca, Circe, Echo, magical verses, longing, nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the eclogue "Corinto," a pastoral poem likely written by Lorenzo de' Medici. The shepherd Corinto transitions from his solitary lament to a vivid daydream of a shared life with the nymph Galatea. He imagines domestic and pastoral bliss, attempting to persuade her to soften her heart and join him in the activities of the countryside, such as music and hunting.</meta>

# I - CORINTO

->If you were to rest with me upon the grass,<-
->from the bark of a flexible willow I would make<-
->a <term>zampogna</term> <note>original: "zampogna"; a traditional Italian bagpipe or reed pipe used by shepherds.</note>, and I would wish for you to sing.<-

->I would see your white foot move through the grass<-
->dancing and kicking at the wind.<-

->and I would make them rain down upon your face:<-

<margin>70</margin>

->where the first ones were picked.<-

->I would make, mingled with leaves and little blossoms!<-

->The murmur of clear little streams<-

<summary>Corinto envisions an idealized pastoral life with Galatea, attempting to woo her by offering protection during hunts and celebrating her beauty. He expresses intense anxiety over her safety as she runs away from him, comparing his helpless watching to an archer who has already lost control of his shot.</summary>

# VI - ECLOGUES

# I - CORINTO

But you are so light, that I have faith
that your lightness could run across the liquid
waters without wetting a foot.
<margin>100</margin> But what fear was born within my heart,
that you might do as Narcissus <note>A figure from Greek mythology who fell in love with his own reflection in a pool and wasted away.</note> did,
to whom his own beauty was too pleasing;
when you washed your face at the beautiful spring,
then, once the storm you stirred was quieted,
<margin>105</margin> you stared fixedly into the tranquil mirror!
Ah, the foolish and dull mind of lovers!
Once you left, I ran there, not believing
the beautiful image would have been removed from there.
I looked into the water, and, not seeing you there,
<margin>110</margin> I saw myself; and it seemed to me I was such
as not to be rejected, when seeking you.
If I am not white <note>original: "bianco"; meaning fair-skinned or pale. Corinto argues that his tanned skin is a sign of his hard work in the sun.</note>, it is the sun’s doing, nor does it suit me poorly,
being a shepherd so strong and robust:
but tell me: a man who is not dark <note>original: "brun"; tanned or swarthy.</note>, what is he worth?
<margin>115</margin> If my shoulders and chest are covered in hair,
this should not displease you,
if you have as much wit and taste as you have beauty.
You perhaps do not know how great my power is:
if I take a wild bull by the horns,
<margin>120</margin> despite his resistance, I bring him to the ground.
The other day, in a dark and hollow cave,
I went to take a pair of bear cubs,
clinging with my hands as I went.
I reached the den; and after I had pulled them out,
<margin>125</margin> the rabid and proud mother bear sensed me,
and began to act aggressively toward me.
I grabbed a hard branch, and upon the grass
I left her dead, and brought away the prey;
which, if you wish, is being kept for you.
<margin>130</margin> To my arms everyone must yield:
I won the other day, for the feast of Pan <note>original: "Pana"; the Greek god of the wild, shepherds, and flocks.</note>,
a cow, who had her calf <note>original: "reda"; an archaic term for offspring, specifically a suckling calf.</note> behind her.

<summary>Corinto boasts of his physical strength and rugged appearance to Galatea, comparing himself favorably to his own reflection and recounting his victory over a mother bear to win her cubs as a gift.</summary>
<keywords>Corinto, Galatea, Narcissus, Pan, pastoral strength, bear hunting, shepherd's life, masculinity</keywords>

--- Page 317 ---
<meta>This page continues the pastoral poem "Corinto," where the shepherd Corinto is attempting to woo the nymph Galatea. On the previous page, Corinto boasted of his physical strength and his ability to wrestle bulls; here, he pivots to displaying his material wealth, his desirability to other women, and a warning about the fleeting nature of youth.</meta>

# I - CORINTO

With bow in hand, I wish to contend with Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt, known for her skill with the bow.</note>:
as a prize, I won a four-horned ram
<margin>135</margin> with white fleece reaching down to the level ground:
it shall be yours, though Neifile <note>A woman's name, likely representing a rival for Corinto's affections who is being ignored.</note> is scorned by it,
for whose sake I am all too ungrateful because of my love for you:
she constantly adorns herself just to please me.
If I am rich, you know it; for on every side
<margin>140</margin> you hear the valleys resound with the lowing
of oxen, and the bleating of sheep.
I have fresh milk at every hour, and in the flowery
meadow, gathered strawberries, beautiful and red,
which look pale compared to your blushing face;
<margin>145</margin> fruits, mature and large in every season;
I nourish many, many thousands of bees,
nor would you believe there were more in the world;
they make a honey so sweet that it resembles
the ambrosia that some say feeds Jove <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods in Roman mythology.</note>;
<margin>150</margin> nor is it surpassed even by the sugar canes of Sicily <note>original: "canne di Sicilia." During the Renaissance, Sicily was a well-known source of sugar cane, a luxury product.</note>.
O nymph, if my song does not move you,
let at least the song of various birds move you,
who sing with pitiful and strange voices.
Do you not hear wretched Philomela <note>In mythology, Philomela was a woman transformed into a nightingale; her song is a classic symbol of poetic lament.</note> grieving
<margin>155</margin> for love with me, lamenting her lot
as I do yours, in sweet verses?
This song alone accompanies me while I lie sleepless.
But I believe I move you to pity;
yet you laugh, while my tears soak the ground.
<margin>160</margin> Where there is extreme beauty and cruelty,
there is living death; yet I comfort myself:
your beauty <note>original: "beltate"</note> shall not last forever.
The other morning, I was walking in a small garden of mine,
and the rising sun was already appearing
<margin>165</margin> with its rays, though I could not yet see it clearly.
Some rosebushes are planted inside,
to which I turned my longing eyes,
to see that which I had never seen before.

<summary>In this passage, Corinto attempts to woo Galatea by listing his agricultural riches and his desirability to others, before concluding with a warning that her beauty is temporary. The poem shifts toward a contemplative scene in a garden, setting up the famous "carpe diem" (seize the day) theme common in Renaissance literature.</summary>
<keywords>Corinto, Lorenzo de' Medici, pastoral poetry, Galatea, Diana, Philomela, transience of beauty, honey, agriculture</keywords>

--- Page 318 ---
<meta>This page continues the pastoral poem "Corinto" by the Renaissance scholar and poet Angelo Poliziano. In the previous section, the shepherd Corinto was boasting of his wealth and his desirability to other women. Here, the tone shifts to a philosophical reflection on the fleeting nature of time, using the metaphor of a rose to encourage the nymph Galatea to accept his love before her beauty fades.</meta>

# VI - ECLOGUES

There were roses there, both white and crimson:
<margin>170</margin> some unfold their petals one by one to the sun;
tightly closed at first, then they seem to open and spread out:
another, younger still, barely releases itself
from the bud; there was also one
who still denies its closed petals to the air;
<margin>175</margin> another, falling, carpets the ground at its feet with flowers.
Thus I saw them being born and dying,
and saw their loveliness pass in less than an hour.
When I saw the leaves fall to the earth,
fading and pale, it then came to my mind
<margin>180</margin> what a fleeting thing is the flowering of youth.
Every tree has its flowers: and immediately
the tender branches unfold to the sun,
whenever the air is felt to renew itself.
The tiny, still-unformed fruits <term>set</term> <note>The Italian term "allegano" refers to the botanical process of "fruit set," where a flower transforms into a berry or fruit after pollination.</note>;
<margin>185</margin> which little by little sometimes grow so large
that the strong branches bend under the great weight,
nor can they carry their own weight
without great danger; they can barely hold themselves up
as they grow, taking the burden upon themselves from hour to hour.
<margin>190</margin> Autumn arrives, and the sweet fruits
are gathered when ripe: and, once the fair weather has passed,
they are finally stripped of flowers, fruits, and foliage.
Gather the rose, O nymph, while the season is fair. <note>This is a famous early expression of the "carpe diem" (seize the day) theme in Italian literature, which would later influence English poets like Robert Herrick ("Gather ye rosebuds while ye may").</note>

<summary>In this concluding section of the shepherd's song, Corinto uses the lifecycle of roses and fruit trees as a metaphor for human youth and beauty. He ends with a poignant plea to Galatea to "gather the rose" and enjoy life's prime before it inevitably withers.</summary>
<keywords>Angelo Poliziano, Corinto, pastoral poetry, carpe diem, metaphors of nature, roses, passage of time, youth and aging</keywords>

--- Page 319 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the shepherd Corinto’s lament on the previous page to a new section titled "Apollo and Pan." While the previous section focused on the fleeting nature of youth and beauty, this passage serves as an introductory "locus amoenus" (a literary term for an idealized "pleasant place"), describing the perfect climate and geography of the Vale of Tempe in Greece, where the divine competition between Apollo and Pan takes place.</meta>

<header>II - APOLLO AND PAN</header>

### II

## APOLLO AND PAN

There is a mountain in Thessaly called Pindus, <note>Pindus is a major mountain range in northern Greece, historically considered the home of the Muses.</note>
more celebrated in the past by sacred bards <term>vates</term> <note>From the Latin "vates," meaning a divinely inspired poet or prophet.</note>
than any other that exists from old Atlas to the Indus. <note>A poetic way of saying "from the furthest West (the Atlas Mountains) to the furthest East (the Indus River)."</note>
At its base, well-born grass and flowers
<margin>5</margin> are bathed by the waters of a spring, clear and lively,
watering then the little blooms and green meadows.
Then, not content with such narrow banks,
they spread through a place that never saw
the sun more beautiful, or of which more is written.
<margin>10</margin> Peneus is the river, and the land that smiles
around it is called Tempe, a plain
which the river divides equally.
A shady—though not dark—forest encircles
the place, full of woodland beasts,
<margin>15</margin> not hostile to our human nature.
Various colors of flowers can be seen,
so charming that one must slow
their pace, overcome by a new pleasure.
There the nights are not sluggish or late,
<margin>20</margin> nor does cold winter hide or conceal the green,
or delay the tender branches.
Nor does the cloudy air freeze there
from the cold North Wind <note>original: "Aquilon," the Roman name for the chilling north wind Boreas.</note>, nor do the flowing
waters slow with ice or veil the fish.
<margin>25</margin> The rage of the Dog Star <note>original: "Sirio can." Sirius, the brightest star in the constellation Canis Major, was associated in antiquity with the "dog days" of extreme summer heat.</note> is not felt,
nor does it seem that the parched sand,
panting and thirsty, bows the languishing flowers to the earth.

<term>Pindus</term> <note>A mountain range in Greece.</note>, <term>Thessaly</term> <note>A region in northern Greece.</note>, <term>Atlas</term> <note>The mountain range in North Africa.</note>, <term>Indus</term> <note>The river in modern-day Pakistan/India.</note>, <term>Peneus</term> <note>A river in Thessaly.</note>, <term>Tempe</term> <note>A famous valley in Greece.</note>, <term>Aquilon</term> <note>The North Wind.</note>, <term>Sirius</term> <note>The Dog Star.</note>, <term>Terza rima</term> <note>A rhyming verse form consisting of an interlocking three-line scheme (ABA BCB CDC), famously used by Dante.</note>

<summary>This page describes the idyllic Vale of Tempe in Thessaly, a mythological paradise of eternal spring untouched by harsh winters or scorching summers. This setting serves as the backdrop for the upcoming story of Apollo and Pan.</summary>
<keywords>Pindus, Thessaly, Vale of Tempe, Peneus River, Apollo, Pan, Pastoral Poetry, Nature, Terza Rima</keywords>

--- Page 320 ---
<meta>This page continues the vivid description of the Vale of Tempe, shifting from the landscape's physical beauty to its mythological significance. It describes the eternal spring of the valley and recounts how Apollo came to dwell there as a shepherd after being exiled from heaven by Jupiter.</meta>

<header>VI - ECLOGUES</header>

Nor does the earth crack open so that her prayers
might reach the ears of Juno, <note>Juno, queen of the gods, was often associated with the air and the governance of rain and weather.</note>
<margin>30</margin> so that she no longer denies the longed-for waters.
A season of eternal spring
is always present in these delightful and beautiful places,
nor do they change with the turning of the heavens.
The leaves are always green and the flowers ever fresh,
<margin>35</margin> just as spring is accustomed to produce
the song of birds in springtime.
Phoebus <note>A common name for Apollo, the sun god, meaning "bright" or "radiant."</note> still loves this place, and still cherishes
his laurel <term>Daphne</term>, <note>Apollo’s first love, who was transformed into a laurel tree to escape him. The Italian text uses "laur," referring both to the tree and the nymph.</note> if it still exists; what wonder is it
if the winter is mild and the sun less scorching?
<margin>40</margin> Daphne occupies and takes hold of both the banks
of her father, <note>The river god Peneus was the father of Daphne.</note> and sometimes, as he weeps, the waves rise
so high that they actually touch his beloved daughter.
In the waters, in the shade of the sacred leaves,
snow-white swans sing sweetly:
<margin>45</margin> the water receives the song, and then echoes back.
Because Phoebus always loved those ever-green leaves,
the swans left the spring of Pegasus <note>The Hippocrene spring on Mount Helicon, created by the hoof of the winged horse Pegasus; it was sacred to the Muses and Apollo.</note>
and their song is now heard here.
More than any other place on earth, the god Apollo
<margin>50</margin> loved this one, from the rising spring
to where the river Peneus loses its name.
But he loved it even more after the death of Phaethon, <note>Apollo's son, who died while unsuccessfully attempting to drive the chariot of the sun.</note>
whom he avenged by sending Steropes <note>One of the Cyclopes who forged the thunderbolts for Jupiter.</note>
<margin>55</margin> to the Acheron. <note>original: "Acheronte," one of the rivers of the Underworld, here signifying death.</note>
Because of this, the ruler of the great council <note>Jupiter/Zeus.</note> was angered,
and to justly punish the grave error,
he exiled him from heaven for a time.
Then he took on the clothing of a shepherd;
<margin>60</margin> but little difference can be perceived
between his pastoral form and his former honor.
Only the bow, which hangs from his sacred shoulders,
which used to be made of gold,
is now made of yew <note>original: "nasso," likely referring to the yew tree (taxus) or wood from the island of Naxos, commonly used for bows.</note> and no longer gives off a golden glow.

<summary>This passage describes the eternal spring of the Vale of Tempe and explains Apollo's presence there as a shepherd following his exile from heaven by Jupiter.</summary>
<keywords>Apollo, Daphne, Peneus, Phaethon, Vale of Tempe, Pastoral, Greek Mythology, Juno, Steropes</keywords>

--- Page 321 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Apollo's exile in the Vale of Tempe. Having been stripped of his celestial rank by Jupiter, Apollo has taken the form of a shepherd. The text describes his rustic transformation and the beginning of his musical rivalry with the wild god Pan.</meta>

## II - APOLLO AND PAN

<margin>65</margin> Thus the golden lyre, which once hung
at his side in his own beautiful kingdom,
was now weathered, and no longer shone.
The ivory plectrum <note>A small flat tool used to pluck the strings of a lyre or lute.</note> is now made of wood;
yet his eyes still breathed a divine light:
<margin>70</margin> this cannot be taken by any who deemed him worthy.
His blond hair kept its usual beauty;
but where once a crown of gems pressed upon it,
there were now the leaves of his river. <note>A reference to the laurel tree, which was once the nymph Daphne, daughter of the river god Peneus.</note>
In this state, the shepherd now sings, now plays;
<margin>75</margin> at times he joined both sweetnesses together,
sometimes speaking with Daphne, sometimes with Peneus.
Pan heard him one day, and, when he reached
the place where he was, said: "That you should sing so well—
no shepherd who ever watched over herds or milked cows could do so.
<margin>80</margin> It would be fitting for me to compete with you one day;
but for me, a god, it would be a shame to compete
with one who follows the steps of the cattle."
The Cynthian <term>Cinthius</term> <note>A title for Apollo, derived from Mount Cynthus on the island of Delos where he was born.</note> shepherd said to him: "You need not
have this concern, for my lyre
<margin>85</margin> is as worthy as your panpipes.
If you do not recognize the song, look at my eyes."
Pan recognized him—the one whom Delos adores—
by the splendor that breathed from his holy eyes.
"And now with much more burning zeal
<margin>90</margin> I sing," he said, "of him whom Arcadia worships,
more than any other inhabitant of heaven." <note>Pan is referring to himself; Arcadia was the center of his cult.</note>
And the Delian <term>Delio</term> <note>Another name for Apollo, meaning "of Delos."</note>: "This gives me great pleasure:
I am content." So they each sat down
upon the flowery grass, green and tender.
<margin>95</margin> Pan placed himself in the shade of Syrinx, <note>The nymph Syrinx was transformed into reeds to escape Pan; he then used those reeds to invent his pipes.</note>
who still remembers their ancient love:
she moved and almost smiled at the song.
Apollo then tunes and runs over every string
in the shade of his sacred laurel:
Pan accords his joined panpipes <term>zampogne</term>. <note>Italian: *zampogna*. While often referring to bagpipes in modern Italian, in this classical context it refers to the *syrinx* or panpipes made of multiple reeds bound together.</note>

<summary>Apollo, living as a humble shepherd, is challenged to a musical duel by the god Pan. After Pan recognizes Apollo's divinity through the light in his eyes, the two prepare to compete amidst the symbolic shade of the laurel and the reeds.</summary>
<keywords>Apollo, Pan, musical contest, lyre, panpipes, Syrinx, Daphne, Vale of Tempe, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 322 ---
<meta>This page continues the scene in the Vale of Tempe. Following Pan’s recognition of Apollo’s divinity despite his shepherd’s rags, Apollo begins his song, invoking a nymph for inspiration before the landscape itself reacts to the divine music.</meta>

# VI - ECLOGUES

### APOLLO'S SONG

<margin>100</margin> O beautiful nymph, whom I called for so long
under that old beech tree in the shaded valley,
though you did not deign to hear our song;
oh, do not keep your beautiful face hidden,
if these bold desires are not already foolish
<margin>105</margin> in wanting to recite such a lofty thing.
I pray you by the grassy hills,
by the pleasing shadows and by the rising springs,
which have often dampened your white feet;
by the high ridges of the alpine mountains,
<margin>110</margin> by your graceful, modest beauties,
by those eyes, which at times you match against the Sun;
by the white tunic, which clothes
your ivory limbs, by your blonde hair,
by the happy grass crushed by your bare feet;
<margin>115</margin> by the shady caves, where you sometimes hide,
by your beautiful bow, which if it were of gold,
you would seem like Delia <note>A name for Diana (Artemis), the goddess of the hunt and Apollo's twin sister, named for their birthplace on the island of Delos.</note> among the green leaves;
nymph, recall to me what verses were
sung by the gods, for it was necessary
<margin>120</margin> for every nymph to come and hear them.

The Peneus <note>The river god and the river itself flowing through the Vale of Tempe.</note> held back its rapid course,
the herds forgot their grazing,
the song cut short the birds' light wings. <note>A poetic way of saying the birds were so entranced that they stopped both their singing and their flight.</note>
The fauns, to honor their god,
<margin>125</margin> and every satyr came to that harmony;
the murmuring of the leaves grew still.
Pan then gave his sweet verses to the wind.

<summary>Apollo invokes a nymph to inspire his singing, resulting in a song so powerful that the river stops flowing and all of nature falls silent to listen. The god Pan then prepares to respond with his own music.</summary>
<keywords>Apollo, Pan, invocation, nature's silence, Peneus, Delia, Satyrs, Eclogues, pastoral poetry</keywords>

--- Page 323 ---
<meta>This page continues the musical competition between the gods Apollo and Pan in the Vale of Tempe. While the previous page featured Apollo's invocation to a nymph and the calming effect of his music on nature, this page begins Pan's response: a mournful song centered on the tragic death of the shepherd Daphnis and the cruelty of Love (Cupid).</meta>

II - APOLLO AND PAN

### PAN’S SONG

Goddess <note>Venus (Aphrodite), the goddess of love, who according to myth was born from the sea foam.</note>, created in the restless sea,
were you the cause of the Sicilian shepherd's <note>Daphnis, a legendary hero of pastoral poetry said to have been blinded or killed due to a tragic love affair.</note>
<margin>130</margin> death, or was it the impious offspring born of you?
Certainly it was you—or rather, your son, Love <note>Cupid.</note>;
or rather, you were the impious one, and you made him cruel:
you, a vain hope, and he, a blind fire.
And from which of the Furies did you take
<margin>135</margin> the poison, Cupid? Perhaps you dipped your arrow
in the foam of Cerberus <note>The three-headed hound that guards the gates of the Underworld; his foam was often associated with madness and poison.</note>?
Cruel boy, how could you watch such evil,
and a death so bitter and wicked,
with dry eyes, and you an immortal god?
<margin>140</margin> If Cytherea <note>Another name for Venus, derived from the island of Cythera, where she was famously worshipped.</note> gave her consent to this,
I now consider her divine powers to be vain;
if they are not, then you are not the son of a goddess.
Rather, the strange ridges of the snowy
Caucasus <note>The Caucasus Mountains, traditionally viewed by classical poets as a remote, savage wilderness.</note> gave birth to you, and among hard rocks
<margin>145</margin> you were nourished on the milk of Hyrcanian tigers <note>Tigers from the region of Hyrcania (near the Caspian Sea), used as a poetic symbol for extreme cruelty.</note>.
Cruel nurses! And yet you let yourself be surpassed
in mercy by such cruel nurses!
They wept for him, yet your heart remains hard.
Their hairy cheeks were then streaked
<margin>150</margin> by the first wails, and new tears fell
from fierce eyes that had never tasted them before.
But where were you, oh beautiful nymphs,
when Daphnis gave his final laments,
calling out to the cruel stars?
<margin>155</margin> Daphnis, lover of the green woods,
Daphnis, the honor of my kingdom, more pleasing to me
than any shepherd who ever watched over herds.
Ah Daphnis, Daphnis, how well you have watched
the herds, and how poorly yourself! But who can
<margin>160</margin> flee, nonetheless, from inexorable fate?

<summary>Pan begins his song by blaming Venus and Cupid for the tragic death of the shepherd Daphnis, contrasting their divine cruelty with the surprising grief of wild tigers.</summary>
<keywords>Pan, Cupid, Venus, Daphnis, Sicilian shepherd, Hyrcanian tigers, pastoral lament, Greek mythology</keywords>

--- Page 324 ---
<meta>This page continues Pan's mournful song from the musical contest. Having previously questioned the cruelty of Cupid and Venus in the death of the shepherd Daphnis, Pan now reflects on the inevitability of Fate and the universal mourning of nature and the Arcadian ancestors for the fallen youth.</meta>

# VI — ECLOGUES

->Who can resist the constant wheels <note>Likely referring to the wheel of Fortune or the inescapable cycles of Destiny.</note>,<-
->and by praying bend the pitiless sisters <note>The Fates (the Moirai or Parcae), who spin and cut the thread of life and are famously indifferent to human pleas.</note>,<-
->or by bathing one's cheeks with tears?<-

->Who can escape, Cupid, your fury?<-
<margin>165</margin> ->Syrinx <note>The nymph Syrinx, who fled Pan's advances and was transformed into reeds; Pan used those reeds to create his namesake "Pan pipes."</note>, you know how light in pursuit<-
->Love once made my feet, though fear did more for yours.<-
->Since Daphnis <note>The archetypal hero of pastoral poetry. In myth, Daphnis died young, often as a result of a conflict with the god of Love or a broken vow of chastity.</note> did not make the harsh commands merciful<-
->through his death, let no other lover<-
->ever hope to find pity in him <note>Referring to Cupid (Love), who remained unmoved even by Daphnis's tragic end.</note>.<-

<margin>170</margin> ->All the caves were filled<-
->with the bellows of fierce lions, and with sad weeping<-
->the stones and the woodland plants broke into a sweat.<-
->Lycaon <note>The first King of Arcadia, who was turned into a wolf by Zeus. Here, even the wild beast-king weeps.</note>, weeping as never seen before,<-
->mourned him, as did she whose form she took<-
<margin>175</margin> ->along with her son: the cold Callisto <note>Callisto was a nymph transformed into a bear and then into the constellation Ursa Major. Her son became Ursa Minor. They are "cold" because they reside in the northern sky as stars.</note>.<-

<summary>In this passage from Pan's song, the speaker laments the absolute power of Fate and Cupid, noting that even the tragic death of Daphnis could not soften the god of Love. The poem describes a world in mourning, where wild animals, plants, and even the stars (Callisto) weep for the lost shepherd.</summary>
<keywords>Cupid, Daphnis, Syrinx, Lycaon, Callisto, Fates, pastoral poetry, mourning, Arcadia</keywords>
